# Brainstorm: 270 ideas for Anvil

Ideas from seventeen lenses. The ranked plan built from them is in [EPIC.md](EPIC.md). Each idea gives
what it is, what to build, a flagship demo, what it builds on, the main risk, an effort
estimate, and a wow score from 1 to 10.

## type-systems (16)

### Scaling laws read off the source: symbolic `anvil cost`

*wow 9/10 · weeks*

`anvil cost` already counts FLOPs and bytes per line for concrete constants. Made symbolic, it prints parameters, FLOPs per step and peak memory as exact polynomials in D, L and T. A class can watch Kaplan's C ≈ 6·N·tokens rule fall out of their own 60-line GPT, along with the 12·L·T²·D attention term the rule leaves out. A cheap version works within days; the principled version uses the symbolic dimensions idea.

- **Build:** anvil/cost.py --symbolic CONST,... Phase 1 (days): probe the program over a grid of `--set` values and rebuild the exact multivariate polynomial by Newton interpolation in fractions.Fraction. The degree bound comes from probing until finite differences vanish. Floordiv and stride terms are detected as non-polynomial and reported per residue class. Phase 2: take each kernel's cost directly as a SymDim polynomial from the symbolic elaborator. Output: formulas per source line, leading-term simplification (≈ 6·N·B·T), and `--solve 'memory <= 8GB'` to pick the largest D/L under a budget. Also a predicted time per step from measured GFLOP/s per kernel schedule, which finishes IDEAS.md's 'estimated time per step'.
- **Demo:** `anvil cost --symbolic D,T,L examples/transformer.anvil` prints `params = 12·L·D² + … + 256·D + T·D` and `train FLOP/step ≈ 6·params·B·T + 12·L·B·T²·D`. Then plot predicted against measured step time as D varies on the M3 Max, live in class.
- **Builds on:** anvil/cost.py, the CLI's `--set NAME=VALUE` overrides, experiments/run.py grids
- **Risk:** Interpolation needs a polynomial. Programs whose structure changes with a constant (compile-time `if`, strides) give piecewise answers that must be explained, not hidden. Each grid point costs an elaboration, which takes seconds for unrolled programs.

### Semiring-polymorphic index notation via type classes

*wow 9/10 · months*

Add type classes with laws (Semiring, Monoid, CommutativeMonoid, Idempotent) and let `sum a[i,k] * b[k,j]` run in any semiring. MaxPlus is Viterbi, MinPlus is shortest paths, LogSumExp is the forward algorithm and the CRF partition function, Bool is reachability, and dual numbers give forward-mode AD as a dtype. Differentiating a LogSumExp program yields marginals (Eisner 2016: forward-backward is just backprop). The laws also tell backends what is legal: max is idempotent and commutative, so `atomicMax` stays bit-deterministic on CUDA, while an f32 sum needs an ordered tree. Today's CUDA atomicAdd scatter quietly breaks Anvil's determinism promise.

- **Build:** anvil/types/classes.py: `class Semiring a: zero, one, add, mul` and `instance Semiring MaxPlus …` in parser.py, with dictionaries resolved at compile time by the elaborator (monomorphized like Rust traits, zero run-time cost). `sum[S] …` or `with semiring S:` in index notation. ir.Reduction.op generalizes from {sum, max, min, prod, argmax} to (combine, identity, laws). kernel.py red_identity/combine and the cuda.py/metal.py reductions emit from the instance. LogSumExp uses an online max-rescaled reduction (the flash-attention trick). Atomics or ordered reductions are chosen from the laws. QuickCheck-style property tests in tests/ spot-check each instance's laws.
- **Demo:** Replace the Python beam search in examples/draw/text_reader.py with an Anvil Viterbi over letter segmentations in the MaxPlus semiring. Then train a CTC/CRF line reader whose loss is the same code in LogSumExp, with autodiff producing forward-backward for free. Bonus: Floyd–Warshall in one line on the Metal GPU.
- **Builds on:** Index-notation reductions, ir.Reduction, kernel.py red_identity/combine, the CUDA atomicAdd scatter path, the handwriting app
- **Risk:** AMX, MPS and cuBLAS only accelerate (+,×), so semiring matmuls fall back to hand-written NEON/Metal kernels that may be much slower. Type classes are a large addition to a Python-like surface.

### Algebraic effects: randomness, IO and probabilistic programming as handlers

*wow 9/10 · months*

Give Anvil a Koka-style row-polymorphic effect system (Rand, IO, Param, Train, Abort) with user-defined handlers. Random streams get keys derived from the static call path instead of today's global counter (`self.stream += 1`), so adding a print or reordering lines no longer changes every random number. Pure code can be fused and hoisted freely. `~` becomes `perform Sample(d)`, so one model can run under reparam, score (REINFORCE), trace, condition or enumerate handlers. That is Pyro's poutines, resolved at compile time with zero run-time cost.

- **Build:** anvil/types/effects.py: effect inference by row unification over the AST, with optional annotations (`fn dropout(x, p) -> … ! Rand`). `handle … with H:` syntax in parser.py. In the elaborator, handlers are compile-time dynamically scoped clauses. Tail-resumptive handlers are plain calls. The multi-shot `enumerate` handler resumes the continuation once per value by re-elaborating the rest of the block, which works only because Anvil stages, and gives exact marginalization of small discrete latents. interp.py and runtime.s key the RNG by a hash of (call path, site), which retires optimize.py's 'still use up its stream' hack. The IO effect tells metal.py which regions can run with no host sync.
- **Demo:** One model with a discrete latent (a mixture/VAE over 10 clusters) runs three ways: `handle with reparam` (relaxed), `handle with score` (REINFORCE with a baseline) and `handle with enumerate` (an exact sum over the clusters), with gradient variance printed side by side. Then insert a print mid-model: the random numbers and the loss curve stay bit-identical, where today's Anvil, PyTorch and NumPy would all shift.
- **Builds on:** The counter-based RNG (ir.Rand, interp.rand_bits), DistVal and the `~` builtins, the staging elaborator, dead-draw handling in optimize.py
- **Risk:** Re-elaborating continuations is exponential under nested enumerate. Inference must never break existing programs, so effects are inferred by default and never required.

### Certified kernels: bounds, race-freedom and derivative rules checked by Lean 4

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

Every Anvil memory access is `buffer + affine offset`, so bounds safety is Presburger arithmetic, which is decidable. `anvil certify` emits a Lean 4 file with theorems for each kernel: every load and store is in bounds (closed by `omega`), every parallel split writes disjoint memory, the arena never overlaps live buffers, and each derivative rule in simplify.deriv is a Mathlib theorem. 'Every access in this 4,200-line assembly file is proved in bounds', with Lean's green check in front of a class.

- **Build:** anvil/certify.py walks the optimized ir.Program and emits per-kernel obligations such as `theorem k86_bounds : ∀ i < 64, ∀ k < 784, i*784 + k < 50176 := by omega`. It adds injectivity lemmas for parallel loops (the argument autodiff.injective already makes), disjointness of arena intervals from arena.py's plan, and Check-relative lemmas for gathers. A Lean library, AnvilCert/Deriv.lean, proves each simplify.deriv rule once using HasDerivAt. Translation validation of optimize.py's fusion on the affine fragment: each fused kernel is shown equal to its sources by symbolic substitution. `lake build` runs in CI.
- **Demo:** `anvil certify examples/mnist.anvil && lake build` checks a few hundred theorems, all green. Then flip a sign in a derivative rule or introduce an off-by-one in a `where`, and the exact Lean theorem that names the kernel and the source line fails.
- **Builds on:** Affine offsets in ir.py, the bounds proofs in solve_ranges, autodiff.injective, backend/arena.py, simplify.deriv
- **Risk:** The certificate covers the IR, not the NEON emitter (aarch64.py / kernel.py); validating the generated assembly is research-scale. Gathers have data-dependent indices, so they can be certified only relative to their run-time checks.

### Check once, for all shapes: symbolic dimensions and ∀-typed functions

*wow 8/10 · months*

Anvil checks types the way C++ templates and Zig comptime do: shapes are concrete ints (eval_dim insists on them), and a fn body is checked only when some call instantiates it, so an uncalled prelude function is never checked. Make dimensions symbolic (n, k+1, 2*k, (h+2*pad-k)//stride+1) and check every fn and model once, generically. Hover would then show `Conv2d.forward : ∀ nb h w. f32[nb, c_in, h, w] → f32[nb, c_out, (h+2·pad−k)//stride+1, …] requires h+2·pad ≥ k`, with the `requires` clause inferred. That gives tensor code the guarantees of Rust generics, which jaxtyping (runtime), Dynamo symbolic shapes (guards at trace time) and Anvil's own instantiate-then-check cannot.

- **Build:** New package anvil/types/: dims.py (canonical multivariate polynomials over shape variables with exact floordiv/mod atoms and Fraction coefficients); solver.py (linear integer arithmetic by the Omega test, i.e. Fourier–Motzkin with integer tightening, for = and ≤; polynomial identities by normalization; anything undecided becomes a residual obligation). An 'abstract elaboration' mode in anvil/elaborate.py: CVal gets a SymVal sibling, Var.extent and TVal.shape may hold SymDims, and unify_shape / eval_dim / solve_ranges send their comparisons through the solver. Undecided obligations become inferred preconditions stored on the FnVal. `anvil check --generic` verifies the whole prelude, and anvil/ide.py prints ∀-signatures and the inferred `requires`. Code that needs concrete values (compile-time `if` on a dim, unrolled recursion) falls back to today's per-call checking, and its hover says 'checked per call'.
- **Demo:** Hover over `Conv2d.forward` in VS Code to see the generic signature and the inferred `requires h + 2·pad ≥ k`. Then plant a bug in a prelude function nothing calls (layer_norm reducing the wrong axis) and get a red squiggle with no call site anywhere. PyTorch, JAX and today's Anvil all miss that bug until the code runs.
- **Builds on:** elaborate.py's unify_shape / check_param_type / eval_dim, the affine range solver in solve_ranges with Affine.var_bounds, ide.py's Recorder hovers
- **Risk:** The elaborator is a staging interpreter that branches on concrete values in hundreds of places, so abstracting it is invasive. Nonlinear dims (n*k, floordiv) make the solver incomplete, so some functions get only partial generic guarantees.

### Refinement types and a static NaN prover

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

`--check` finds the first NaN at run time; this idea shows most NaNs are impossible before running. Refinements like `f32{> 0}`, `f32{0 ≤ _ ≤ 1}` and `i32[n]{0 ≤ _ < k}`, plus an interval/sign abstract interpreter over the kernel IR, let `anvil prove` show that log, sqrt, division and gathers get legal arguments, and squiggle `log(x - 0.5)` in the editor with the reason. Obligations it cannot prove become run-time checks that blame the annotation (gradual refinement with Findler–Felleisen contracts).

- **Build:** anvil/types/refine.py: abstract domains over ir.Expr (intervals with ±∞, sign, 'finite', and a small octagon domain for correlated terms like x − mean(x)), transfer functions for every Unary/Binary op and reduction, and widening at run-time loops. Obligations come from the prelude: log needs > 0, sqrt ≥ 0, / ≠ 0, cross_entropy labels < classes, and gather bounds. parser.py accepts `{…}` refinements on params, fn arguments and loader results. Data refinements are checked once when idx/csv/npy data is loaded (this covers IDEAS.md's 'labels outside the number of classes' warning). Unproved obligations become ir.Check statements carrying the refinement's span. Stan-style constrained parameters (`param sigma: f32{> 0}[d]`, `param p: simplex[k]`) are stored unconstrained and passed through softplus or softmax bijectors by the elaborator.
- **Demo:** The README's --check example becomes a compile-time error: "`x - 0.5` ∈ [−0.5, 0.5] may be ≤ 0, so `log` can return nan". layer_norm is proved safe, with the note 'because of + eps'. EMNIST labels are checked against 62 classes when loaded. `anvil prove examples/vae.anvil` reports that no NaN is reachable except through overflow of `exp(0.5 * logvar)`, exactly where VAEs blow up.
- **Builds on:** ir.Check run-time checks, the --check machinery, simplify.py, the compile-time bounds proofs in solve_ranges
- **Risk:** Interval analysis is coarse: correlated expressions cause false alarms, and weights are unbounded during training. It must report three tiers (proved, definitely bad, unknown) and stay quiet on unknown by default.

### Named axes that free the memory layout

*wow 8/10 · months*

Make axis names part of tensor types (`f32[B=64, T=128, H=4, E=16]`), so reductions, softmax, broadcasting and contraction go by name. The transformer's four `.reshape(-1, T, HEADS, DH)` calls and every transpose disappear from user code. The bigger payoff: once axis order is logical, the compiler owns the physical layout and picks NCHW, NHWC or blocked NCHWc per buffer from a cost model. That targets the 5× Metal-convolution gap by choosing the layout MPS and simdgroup kernels want, with no permutes in the source.

- **Build:** parser.py and the elaborator: named-axis type syntax (`axis T = 128`, or inferred), and TVal gains an axis-name tuple. When a tensor is named, index notation matches subscripts to axes by name, so subscript order is free. Prelude softmax / layer_norm / cross_entropy take `over=` axes. New anvil/backend/layout.py chooses a physical permutation and blocking per Buffer by scoring candidates against kernel.py's schedules and arena.py. Views still compose, because every access is already `buffer + Affine`. Type errors read like: "axis `T` of q meets axis `S` of k in a contraction: rename with k.rename(T→S)".
- **Demo:** examples/transformer.anvil rewritten with named axes has zero reshapes and zero transposes. `anvil ir` shows the compiler chose a head-major layout for q, k and v. The EMNIST CNN on Metal speeds up only because the compiler switched to channels-last; the source is unchanged.
- **Builds on:** Index notation and its range inference, zero-copy views (TVal.tmpl over Affine), kernel.py schedule selection, backend/metal.py
- **Risk:** Layout choice interacts with fusion, in-place reuse and file formats (save/npy must stay row-major, which needs transposes at the boundary). A bad cost model could lose some of the CPU lead over PyTorch.

### Rank polymorphism with typed frames (Remora-style) and variadic shape variables

*wow 8/10 · months*

Turn `...` from an index-notation trick into a type. Signatures get variadic shape variables (`fn attention(q: [...b, t, d], k: [...b, s, d]) -> [...b, t, d]`), and functions get Remora's frame/cell typing: each has a cell rank, and applying it to bigger arrays lifts over the frame with a static prefix-agreement check. vmap becomes a one-line type rule, so per-example gradients get a checked type: `vmap(grad(loss))(X) : f32[64, 784, 128]`.

- **Build:** anvil/types/dims.py gains shape-list variables: `...b` binds a sequence of dims, and shape lists unify like TypeScript variadic tuples / PEP 646 TypeVarTuple. elaborate.py lifts any call whose arguments exceed the declared cell rank by elaborating the body under fresh frame index variables, reusing the comprehension machinery that already handles `...`. FnVal (already a compile-time value) becomes usable as an argument, so `vmap(f, over=axis)` and `grad(f)` work as staged higher-order functions. Per-example gradient kernels are fused so per-example clipping uses per-example norms without materializing the [n, …] tensor when possible.
- **Demo:** DP-SGD (Abadi et al. 2016) on MNIST in about 12 lines: `g = vmap(grad(loss))(X, y)`, per-example clipping and Gaussian noise. The editor shows every intermediate's type, and a table shows the privacy/accuracy trade-off. The same attention function also runs unchanged on [t,d], [b,t,d] and [b,h,t,d].
- **Builds on:** `...` in index notation, FnVal compile-time functions, grad in autodiff.py, broadcasting in ops.py
- **Risk:** Implicit lifting can bring back NumPy-broadcasting surprises, so it needs clear errors and maybe an opt-in. Per-example gradients are huge if fusion fails to avoid materializing them.

### The numeric tower on real silicon: f16, bf16, fp8, int8, complex, with typed scales and precision policies

*wow 8/10 · months*

parser.py currently rejects f16 and bf16 with 'not supported yet'. Add them, plus f8e4m3/f8e5m2, i8 with a scale-carrying quantized type `q8<s: f32[c]>`, c64, and a type-level precision policy (`precision(compute=bf16, accum=f32, master=f32)`) whose type rules insert loss scaling. These lower to hardware Anvil already targets: NEON FP16 `fmla .8h` (2× the lanes), BF16 `bfmmla`/`bfdot` and I8MM `smmla`/`sdot` on M2/M3, Metal half/bfloat with simdgroup_matrix, and CUDA mma.sync (fp8 wgmma on Hopper).

- **Build:** ir.Buffer gets an itemsize, and every hard-coded `4 *` in kernel.py, aarch64.py, cuda.py, arena.py and runtime.s becomes `itemsize *`. anvil/types/numeric.py: a dtype lattice that widens implicitly but requires narrowing to be written (`f16(x)`), using the refinement intervals to flag f16 overflow (exp above 11.09). New .8h/.16b lane classes in kernel.py's register allocator. Complex as a pair dtype with `fft` in the prelude (an open IDEAS.md §5 item). `quantize(net, i8, calib=X)` produces a typed int8 model for export.py. MSL half/bfloat in metal.py, and __half / __nv_bfloat16 with mma in cuda.py.
- **Demo:** `anvil export --dtype i8 examples/digits_model.anvil`: the drawing app's reader becomes 4× smaller, runs on sdot kernels and keeps about 99% accuracy. A naive f16 softmax without the max subtraction is rejected at compile time with the overflow bound. A bf16 GPT trains on Metal at about twice the f32 speed.
- **Builds on:** The dtype field on Buffer/Expr, the npy loader's f16 decoding in anvil_host.h/runtime.s, kernel.py schedules, metal.py/cuda.py, export.py
- **Risk:** The 4-byte assumption runs through offsets, the arena, the assembly runtime and checkpoint formats. Mixed lane widths complicate register allocation and tiling, and quantized accuracy needs calibration work.

### Typed holes: the compiler proposes and trains the architecture

*wow 8/10 · months*

Write `fn classify(x: [n, 784]) -> [n, 10] = ?`, and the hole's type drives a search over well-typed compositions of prelude layers, with shapes as the spec (type-directed synthesis in the style of Synquid and Myth). Anvil compiles in about 0.2 s and trains an MNIST epoch in under 0.1 s, so the compiler can actually train dozens of candidates and fill the hole with the best one. Typed program synthesis meets architecture search, live in the editor.

- **Build:** `?` and `?name` holes in parser.py. ide.py reports each hole's expected type and the in-scope terms of matching type (Hazel-style live holes, useful without any search). anvil/synth.py enumerates typed pipelines (`x |> Linear(784, ?d) |> relu |> …`), treats symbolic dims as unknowns for the dims solver, and prunes by `anvil cost` FLOP/memory budgets. It trains each candidate for a fixed FLOP budget through the existing driver with successive halving (Hyperband) and writes the winner back into the source. A VS Code code action triggers it.
- **Demo:** In class, type `= ?` after a signature and invoke the code action. A terminal table of about 40 candidate architectures trains in parallel, showing params, FLOPs and accuracy, and within a minute on the M3 Max the hole fills with the winner, e.g. a small conv net.
- **Builds on:** The fast compile/train loop, `anvil cost`, experiments/run.py grids via `--set`, ide.py and the VS Code extension, the prelude's layers
- **Risk:** The search space explodes, and the winner after a minute is often what a human would have written. The holes must be useful on their own so this is a type-system feature and not just a NAS gimmick.

### Sharding as types: GSPMD-style placement inference with checked collectives

*wow 8/10 · moonshot*

Put placement in the type: declare `mesh(data=4, model=2)` and write `f32[B @ data, D @ model]`. Sharding propagation becomes type inference over a sharding lattice. Contracting over a sharded axis yields a 'partial sum' type that must be reduced, so a missing all-reduce is a compile error, and `anvil cost` reports communication bytes per line. The same types can place work across Apple Silicon's CPU, AMX and GPU on unified memory.

- **Build:** anvil/types/sharding.py: a lattice (replicated ⊑ sharded(axis) ⊑ partial(op)) propagated through kernels by unification on named axes and reduction variables. Collectives (all_reduce, all_gather, reduce_scatter) become new ir statements. Backends: cuda.py calling NCCL, and a multi-process 'virtual mesh' runtime on macOS with a shared-memory ring all-reduce in anvil_host.h, so it can be demoed on one machine. cost.py gets a communication model. Optional auto-sharding by ILP over per-tensor choices (Alpa, Zheng et al. OSDI 2022), compared with GSPMD (Xu et al. 2021) and JAX shard_map.
- **Demo:** The GPT trains with 4-way data × 2-way tensor parallelism on an 8-process virtual mesh on the M3 Max, with losses bit-identical to the single-process run. Deleting the `@ model` on Wo gives a type error: "partial sum over D reaches layer_norm unreduced".
- **Builds on:** Named axes, cuda.py and anvil_host.h, cost.py, Anvil's fixed reduction order (determinism)
- **Risk:** There is no multi-GPU hardware, and the CUDA backend has never run on an NVIDIA GPU. A virtual mesh on one Mac proves correctness and type safety, not speed.

### Differentiability as a type: tangent spaces and the dead-gradient tracer

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Give every type a tangent type (f32[s] ↦ f32[s], i32 ↦ Zero, a model ↦ a record of its parameters' tangents) and a smoothness qualifier. The qualifiers: C∞; C⁰ for relu/max/abs; piecewise-constant for round, floor, argmax and comparisons; detached. Today the compiler only says the objective does not depend on a parameter. With these types it shows the exact edge that killed the gradient and suggests a fix: a straight-through estimator, a softmax relaxation, or moving the detach.

- **Build:** anvil/types/diff.py: a qualifier lattice the elaborator computes for each value from simplify.deriv's rules, carried on TVal. `@differentiable(wrt: x)` and `@nodiff` annotations on fns and model members are checked at definition (Swift for TensorFlow's Differentiable protocol, but static). When warn_untrained or grad finds no path, a backward search over kernel loads (reusing autodiff.diff_loads and the active-set computation) reports the cut edge with spans. ide.py sends the gradient path to VS Code as decorations. Warnings for i32 casts on the loss path and for Hessians through C⁰ ops.
- **Demo:** 'Why isn't it learning?': five broken student models (accuracy as the loss, an i32() cast, round() quantization, a comparison used as a mask, a misplaced detach). Each one lights up in the editor with the dead edge and a suggested fix. The quantization model takes the suggested straight-through estimator and trains.
- **Builds on:** autodiff.py (diff_loads, active/need sets), warn_untrained in elaborate.py, simplify.deriv, ide.py and the VS Code extension
- **Risk:** The analysis mostly exists already. The real work is making the explanations clear and the suggested fixes correct, and keeping the lattice in sync with every op.

### Units of measure, all the way through the gradient

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

F#-style units on tensors (`f32<m/s>[n]`) with Kennedy's unit inference, extended to gradients: `grad(loss<J>, x<m>)` has type `f32<J/m>`. The checker then explains optimizers. In sgd, `w -= lr * g` forces lr to have units w²/loss. In adam, m/√v is dimensionless, so lr has the units of w. That is the type-level reason Adam is scale-invariant and SGD must be retuned when features are rescaled.

- **Build:** anvil/types/units.py: units as integer exponent vectors over base units, with unification over the free abelian group (Kennedy, ESOP 1994; solved by Hermite normal form) and unit variables generalized per fn. Rules: +/− need equal units, * and / add exponents, sqrt halves them, exp/log/sin/softmax need dimensionless arguments, sum/mean keep units, grad divides them. Optimizer blocks are inferred like functions, so each hyperparameter's unit can be shown. ide.py hovers show inferred units. Everything defaults to dimensionless, so existing ML code type-checks unchanged.
- **Demo:** A physics-informed net learns projectile motion: input t<s>, output x<m>, and a residual d²x/dt² + g<m/s²>. A unit slip in the loss is a compile error. Then the class hovers over `lr` in sgd and in adam and sees the inferred units. They run the experiment: rescale the inputs by 1000 and only SGD breaks, as the types predicted.
- **Builds on:** Type annotations in the parser and elaborator, grad in autodiff.py, optimizer blocks in prelude.anvil, ide.py hovers
- **Risk:** The PINN residual needs second derivatives, and higher-order gradients are not supported yet. Annotations may feel like noise outside scientific ML, so inference must do almost all the work.

### Uniqueness types: write it functionally, run it in place

*wow 7/10 · months*

Replace the heuristics that decide in-place updates (owned_target, aliased, used_outside, the optimizer's reuse_dying_inputs) with Futhark-style uniqueness types and borrows for views. PyTorch's most-googled autograd error ('a variable needed for gradient computation has been modified by an inplace operation') becomes a compile-time error with two spans. Pure-functional updates (`x.at[i].set(v)`) are guaranteed to compile to in-place stores, rather than relying on JAX-style donate_argnums.

- **Build:** anvil/types/unique.py: a uniqueness/borrow checker over the elaborated program. `*[n]` marks unique types in signatures. Views (slices, transposes, gathers: TVals over a shared Buffer) are borrows with lifetimes. `x[i] = …` and optimizer `step` consume their target. autodiff.py's mutation-hazard check is rephrased as a borrow conflict: "`W` is borrowed by `loss` (line 10) until the backward pass, but consumed by the update at line 12". arena.py uses consumption facts to hand a consumed buffer's storage to its successor (gradient buffers across steps, KV-cache rings). `.at[].set/.add` lowers to in-place writes when unique, and `--strict-inplace` turns any hidden copy into an error.
- **Demo:** An autoregressive GPT sampler with a KV cache, written in pure functional style (`cache = cache.at[t].set(k_t)`). `anvil ir` shows zero copy kernels and the arena report shows a single cache buffer. Then introduce an alias bug and show Anvil's two-span borrow error next to PyTorch's run-time traceback for the same mistake.
- **Builds on:** elaborate.py setitem / owned_target / aliased / used_outside, optimize.py reuse_dying_inputs, the autodiff.py mutation hazard, backend/arena.py
- **Risk:** Uniqueness types are hard for students, so inference must keep them invisible until there is a real conflict. Borrows of views across inlined function boundaries need lifetime inference.

### Bounded dynamic dimensions: gradual shapes that keep the static arena

*wow 7/10 · months*

Static-only shapes are Anvil's biggest language limitation. Add dims known only at run time but bounded (`x: [n ≤ 64, 784]`, or `[?, 784]`), with gradual-typing casts that check at the boundary and blame the cast's line (Siek–Taha; Wadler–Findler blame). The arena stays static, sized for the bound in the style of XLA's bounded dynamism, and kernels take extents in registers. The last partial batch, variable-length lines and a growing KV cache then just work.

- **Build:** SymDims (from the symbolic dimensions idea) with an upper-bound refinement. AffVal (run-time affine integers, already used for slices) carries a dim's run-time value. kernel.py/aarch64.py take loop bounds from registers (`cmp x8, x_n` instead of `#784`), with vector tail loops and a run-time choice between the full register tile and remainder tiles. arena.py plans at the bound. ir.Check guards casts like `x as [64, 784]`. `batches(..., drop_last=false)`. pyapi.py compiles once per bounded signature instead of once per concrete shape. Metal and CUDA kernels take extents as arguments.
- **Demo:** The char-RNN trains on whole README lines of varying length with no padding waste. The drawing app's reader is called from Python at batch 1, 7 and 64 against one compiled library. A failing cast reports "this tensor has 65 rows, but line 12 promised ≤ 64".
- **Builds on:** AffVal, ir.Check, backend/arena.py, kernel.py schedules, pyapi.py
- **Risk:** The NEON/AMX schedules and fusion decisions were tuned for static extents. Run-time extents add tail loops and may give back part of the CPU lead on small kernels.

### C++ and Python see Anvil's types: shape-typed bindings

*wow 6/10 · days*

Carry Anvil's shapes across the language boundary. `anvil export --cpp` emits a C++23 header whose functions take `std::mdspan<const float, std::extents<size_t, 1, 28, 28>>`, with concepts for bounded dims, constexpr parameter counts and an RAII model class, so clang rejects a wrong-shaped call. `anvil.function` writes .pyi stubs with PEP 646 / jaxtyping shapes, so pyright underlines shape bugs in the Python that calls Anvil.

- **Build:** anvil/export.py: a `--cpp` mode that generates a header-only C++23 wrapper over the existing C ABI (mdspan extents, `requires` clauses, static_asserts, std::span overloads, constexpr metadata such as parameter count and FLOPs from cost.py), plus a CMake snippet. anvil/pyapi.py: stub generation (`Float[np.ndarray, "n 784"]`, or `np.ndarray[tuple[int, Literal[784]], np.dtype[np.float32]]`). Tests in tests/test_export.py compile a deliberate misuse with clang++ -std=c++23 and expect failure, and run pyright on a stub misuse.
- **Demo:** Call the exported digit reader from a 15-line C++ program. Pass a 28×27 image and get a clang compile error naming the mismatched extent. Do the same in VS Code Python and see pyright flag it before anything runs.
- **Builds on:** anvil/export.py (C library + header with baked weights), anvil/pyapi.py, ide.py's type rendering
- **Risk:** Low. mdspan needs a recent libc++ (Xcode 16 has it). Static shape typing in Python is still clunky, so the Python half is best-effort.

## functional (16)

### Fusion by algebra: equality saturation over kernel IR, and the compiler derives FlashAttention

*wow 10/10 · months*

Replace part of optimize.py's hand-written fusion heuristics with an e-graph (egg, Willsey et al., POPL 2021) driven by algebraic laws written as rules: map fusion, fold fusion, the banana-split (tupling) law that fuses two reductions over the same index into one pass, and the online-softmax law (Milakov & Gimelshein 2018), which rewrites max-then-sum-of-exp as a single pass over a (max, scaled-sum) monoid. Applied to the 3-line attention in examples/attention.anvil, these laws derive FlashAttention (Dao et al. 2022): one reduction over keys carrying (m, l, acc[d]), O(n) memory and no score matrix. That fixes the README's 'attention materializes the score matrix' weakness by derivation instead of a hand-written kernel.

- **Build:** anvil/egraph.py: hash-consed e-nodes over ir.Expr plus reduction nodes, union-find, saturation with node and time limits, run per kernel neighborhood. `rule` declarations in a prelude file (`rule exp(a) * exp(b) => exp(a + b)`), parsed by parser.py and compiled into matchers. Reductions over user monoids (idea 6), so the online-softmax combine `(m1,l1,a1) ⊕ (m2,l2,a2)` is an ordinary reduction the backends can tile. Extraction scores candidates with cost.py (FLOPs + bytes moved) plus a register-pressure estimate from kernel.py's tile model. `anvil ir --explain` prints the chain of laws applied, with source spans. kernel.py: a multi-accumulator reduction schedule (tile over query rows, stream over key blocks); metal.py: the threadgroup-tiled version. Each rewrite is differentially tested against interp.py.
- **Demo:** Show the naive attention source, then run `anvil ir --explain attention.anvil`. It prints the derivation: softmax∘matmul ⇒ banana split ⇒ online normalizer ⇒ tiling ⇒ flash attention. Then run causal attention at t=8192, d=64. The 256 MB score matrix per head disappears, and the target is to beat both the materialized version and PyTorch's scaled_dot_product_attention on MPS. The class watches a compiler rediscover a 2022 systems paper from two algebraic identities.
- **Builds on:** optimize.py (inline/fuse/CSE passes), simplify.py folding, cost.py static FLOP/byte model, ir.Reduction, kernel.py register tiling
- **Risk:** E-graphs blow up on large unrolled programs, so saturation must stay local and bounded. The backward pass of flash attention needs a recompute schedule: either the derivation covers it too, or it falls back to the materialized version. NEON has 32 vector registers, so 64-wide accumulators per query row force careful tiling.

### vmap in one rule (and pmap is the same rule over devices)

*wow 9/10 · weeks*

JAX needs a hand-written batching rule for each primitive. Every Anvil operation is already an index-notation kernel `Y[I] = ⊕_J body`, so vmap is a single IR-to-IR rule: add one domain variable `b` to each kernel the function emits, and add `b·stride` to each load and store of a batched buffer. That rule is enough for per-example gradients, ensembles and batched Jacobians, all fused and compiled ahead of time; Dex showed the pointful-index version of this idea (Paszke et al., ICFP 2021). pmap is the same rule with `b` split across devices instead of iterated by a loop.

- **Build:** New anvil/transform/vmap.py. Elaborate f once at unbatched shapes into an isolated block. Find the batched buffers by forward dataflow from the batched inputs; params with in_axes=None stay shared. Clone each kernel: prepend Var b to its domain, re-allocate produced buffers with a leading B dimension, and add b·numel to Affine offsets. Gather loads get batched too (`x[ids[b, i]]`), and ScalarRef runtime offsets become per-b gathers. A runtime `if` on a batched condition becomes a select (masking, as in JAX's cond batching). The Rand salt is mixed with b so each example gets independent noise. in_axes/out_axes are TVal view templates, so they cost nothing. backend/blas.py: a vmapped `x @ W` with a shared W folds b into M, giving one bigger AMX GEMM instead of B small ones; per-example weights use the existing stack-of-products path. pmap: `pmap(step, devices=[cpu, gpu])` splits the b axis between the AArch64 backend and metal.py over unified memory (zero copies). `psum` is a sum reduction over b, i.e. the gradient all-reduce. cost.py picks the split ratio.
- **Demo:** DP-SGD on MNIST (Abadi et al., CCS 2016). `per_ex = vmap(grad(loss), in_axes=(None, 0, 0))(params, x, y)` computes per-example gradients; the program clips each one, adds Gaussian noise and prints the privacy budget ε. The target is private training within ~2x of non-private, the bar set by JAX in Subramani, Vadivelu & Kamath (NeurIPS 2021), where Opacus and TF-Privacy are far slower. Second beat: `vmap(jacrev(net))` draws saliency maps for ten digits as ASCII art in one call.
- **Builds on:** the uniform comprehension form of ir.Kernel (domain + Affine offsets), TVal zero-copy views, blas.py stack-of-products detection, the counter-based RNG salt, Metal's unified memory and host-side loop
- **Risk:** Runtime while-loops with batched conditions need masking until every lane finishes. B× temporaries can blow up the arena unless arena.py keeps reusing them. For small models, CPU/GPU synchronization in pmap may cost more than it gains.

### You Only Linearize Once: autodiff rebuilt as jvp + transpose, giving every derivative of every order

*wow 9/10 · months*

Rebuild autodiff.py the way JAX and Dex do it (Radul, Paszke, Frostig, Johnson & Maclaurin, 'You Only Linearize Once', POPL 2023). A forward-mode `jvp` transform emits tangent kernels, and a `transpose` transform turns any linear kernel into its adjoint; reverse mode is transpose ∘ linearize. Both map kernel IR to kernel IR and compose, so Hessians, Hessian-vector products, Jacobians and grad-of-grad all follow, and today's 'higher-order derivatives are not supported' error goes away.

- **Build:** anvil/jvp.py: for each kernel `Y[I] = ⊕_J body`, emit `Ẏ[I] = ⊕_J Σ ∂body/∂X · Ẋ[f(I,J)]` with the existing simplify.deriv. It needs rules for max/min (select on equality, as Backprop already does) and prod; Rand has a zero tangent, while reparameterized samples get tangents through mu and sigma. Forward mode needs no scatter and no tape, and tangents fuse into the primal kernel via optimize.py's horizontal fusion. anvil/transpose.py: generalize Backprop.emit_grad (injective → reduction kernel, otherwise scatter-accumulate) into transposition of any kernel that is linear in buffers marked 'tangent', with Dex-style linearity checking and an 'unzip' step that separates primal from tangent kernels. New transforms: `grad(grad(f))`; `hvp(f, x, v)` as forward-over-reverse; `jacfwd`/`jacrev` = vmap(jvp)/vmap(vjp) via idea 2; `hessian` = jacfwd(jacrev). tests/test_gradcheck.py: finite-difference checks of second derivatives for every prelude function.
- **Demo:** One lecture, three beats. (1) The Hessian spectrum of the MNIST MLP, computed with Lanczos over HVPs (Ghorbani, Krishnan & Xiao, ICML 2019). An ASCII density plot shows roughly ten outlier eigenvalues, one per class, emerging during training. (2) A physics-informed neural network (Raissi et al. 2019) solving Burgers' equation. Its loss needs ∂²u/∂x² of the network with respect to its input; it trains in seconds and is drawn with `show`. (3) Newton's method on logistic regression converging in 6 steps where SGD takes 1,000.
- **Builds on:** autodiff.py Backprop (injective/covers/scatter logic, mutation-hazard checks), simplify.deriv symbolic derivatives, interp.py for gradient checks
- **Risk:** The kernel IR has mutation (loop-carried writes, `+=`, item assignment), and linearization must keep the existing hazard rules. Symbolic derivatives grow quickly at second and third order, so tangent kernels need CSE. Forward-over-reverse roughly doubles the kernel count, which hurts compile time.

### Differentiable run-time loops: scan, fold and while_loop with tapes and optimal checkpointing

*wow 9/10 · months*

Today `static for` is the only way to backpropagate through time: the 32-step char-RNN unrolls into 1,376 kernels and takes 3.3 s to compile. Add `scan(f, carry, xs)`, `fold` and `while_loop`, whose gradients run a real reversed loop over a tape of carries, using a constant number of kernels whatever the sequence length. Because T is static, the compiler can choose an optimal checkpointing schedule at compile time: binomial 'Revolve' (Griewank & Walther 2000) or √T (Chen et al. 2016). A user-facing `checkpoint(f)` rematerialization transform comes with it.

- **Build:** Lower to ir.For plus tape buffers `tape[T, …]` written at the top of each iteration (or add a new ir.Scan statement). autodiff.py: the gradient of a loop seeds the adjoint of the final carry, then emits a reversed ir.For whose body is Backprop over the single-iteration region; ad_region already computes exactly that region. Carry adjoints are loop-carried values. Params read inside the loop accumulate their gradients across iterations, which accumulating stores already support. while_loop: a static maximum trip count sizes the tape, plus a runtime count. New anvil/checkpoint.py: a Revolve schedule for a given memory budget, emitted as nested For loops that recompute segments. arena.py: tapes are static allocations; `anvil build` reports tape memory, and `anvil cost` reports the memory/recompute-FLOPs trade-off. metal.py/cuda.py: counters stay on the host, tapes become device buffers. interp.py: reference semantics.
- **Demo:** (1) The char-RNN at SEQ=1024 instead of 32. It compiles in well under a second, which unrolling cannot do. `anvil build` prints the tape memory, and `--checkpoint=sqrt` cuts it ~32x for about 1.3x recompute. (2) Differentiable physics, DiffTaichi-style (Hu et al., ICLR 2020): 'learn to throw'. Gradient descent tunes a ball's launch velocity through 2,000 simulated steps with drag and a bounce until it lands in a target cup, and the trajectory improves live in `show`.
- **Builds on:** autodiff.ad_region/Backprop, ir.For, loop-carried SSA write-backs (elaborate.setup_loop/write_backs), arena.py static planning, Metal's host-side loop counters
- **Risk:** Params have reference semantics and loops can contain `+=` scatters; the backward loop must respect those mutations. `break` and data-dependent exits need recorded trip counts. Gradients through bounces are poor, so the physics demo must be chosen with care.

### Monoids, semirings and parallel scans: user-defined reductions with laws

*wow 9/10 · months*

Let users declare `monoid` and `semiring` in Anvil and use them anywhere index notation uses sum or max: `reduce[LogSumExp]`, `scan[M]`, semiring einsums. Swapping (+,×) for (max,+) turns matrix multiplication into Viterbi or shortest paths, and (logsumexp,+) turns it into the forward algorithm and CTC. Differentiating a log-semiring program yields marginals for free (Eisner 2016, 'Inside-Outside and Forward-Backward Are Just Backprop'). An associative `scan` compiles to a work-efficient Blelloch parallel prefix scan, which trains linear-recurrence models (S5/LRU/Mamba-style) in parallel over the sequence.

- **Build:** parser.py/ast.py: `monoid Name: identity = …; fn combine(a, b) = …` and `semiring Name(add=…, mul=…)`, each with `law` clauses. elaborate.py: `reduce[M] expr` binds with the existing LCA rule. ir.Reduction.op is extended from strings to a MonoidDef holding a combine expression over Acc, with tuple accumulators for pairs like (max, sum). kernel.py: the accumulator schedule generalized to any elementwise combine (vec-out and vec-red stay valid). cuda.py/metal.py: two-stage reductions call combine. New anvil/scan.py: up-sweep/down-sweep over the runtime.s thread pool and Metal threadgroup memory, with a fixed tree shape so results stay bit-identical for any thread count. autodiff: gradients of user reductions via jvp of combine (idea 3). Laws are checked QuickCheck-style at compile time by running random inputs through interp.py; a failing law is a compile error with a counterexample. Prelude: LogSumExp, Tropical, Boolean, (max, argmax).
- **Demo:** (1) CTC loss (Graves et al. 2006) in about 10 lines of Anvil, as a log-semiring scan. It retrains bin/draw --text's letter network on whole words, so the network learns segmentation instead of relying on the hand-tuned beam search over stroke divisions. Viterbi decoding is the same code with `Tropical` swapped in. (2) A linear recurrent char model (Martin & Cundy, ICLR 2018) trained with a parallel scan over 1,024 bytes, with tokens/sec shown next to the GRU's sequential steps on the M3 Max.
- **Builds on:** index-notation reductions and the LCA binding rule, ir.Reduction ops, kernel.py accumulator schedules, the runtime.s thread pool, the prelude-in-Anvil philosophy, the examples/draw text reader
- **Risk:** Floating-point non-associativity threatens the bit-identical guarantee unless the scan tree is fixed. Tuple accumulators raise register pressure. Gradients through max-plus are subgradients, which must be documented.

### Anvil PPL: probabilistic programming where inference is a composition of transforms

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

Build a probabilistic programming layer in the spirit of Pyro's handlers and NumPyro, entirely from the pieces above. `sample` and `observe` are effects; trace, condition, replay, log_density and enumerate are handlers; and SVI, HMC/NUTS, SMC and importance sampling are Anvil library code built from grad, vmap, while_loop and scan. Everything compiles ahead of time to fused NEON or Metal code, so small models should run in microseconds per gradient where NumPyro and Stan pay per-operation dispatch or tape costs.

- **Build:** prelude/ppl.anvil: distributions (Normal, Bernoulli, Categorical, Dirichlet, HalfCauchy) as records with sample and log_prob, plus bijectors for constrained parameters (exp, softplus, stick-breaking). Handlers trace, condition(data), log_density, and enumerate, which marginalizes discrete latents exactly through the LogSumExp monoid (idea 6), like Pyro's TraceEnum_ELBO. Inference: svi(model, guide, adam) with a reparameterized ELBO; iterative NUTS (Hoffman & Gelman, JMLR 2014) with a fixed maximum tree depth, leapfrog steps in a while_loop, and chains vmapped so one kernel advances every chain; SMC resampling via gathers. Diagnostics (R-hat, ESS) written in Anvil, and anvil cost estimates per-sample cost.
- **Demo:** A Bayesian neural network on the spirals dataset with 256 NUTS chains vmapped into one compiled program. It prints an ASCII map where the decision boundary is confident near the data and uncertain far from it. Then eight schools (Rubin 1981), timed against Stan and NumPyro in milliseconds per effective sample. The point for students: an inference algorithm is about 30 lines composing grad, vmap and while_loop.
- **Builds on:** ideas 1–8, examples/spirals.anvil, examples/vae.anvil's reparameterized sampling, the DistVal machinery
- **Risk:** NUTS's tree doubling is data-dependent control flow, which fits static shapes badly; use NumPyro-style iterative NUTS with masking. Chains that need different step counts need masking under vmap. The idea depends on nearly every other idea landing.

### Records, ADTs and pattern matching: pytrees as types, and 64 networks trained in one program

*wow 8/10 · months*

Add `record` and `enum` types (`enum Act = Relu | Gelu | Leaky(slope)`), exhaustive `match`, and structural tree_map/tree_zip, so parameters, optimizer state and training state become immutable typed values (Equinox/Flax style) instead of global `param`s. A gradient has the type of its input (`grad(loss)(p)` returns a `Params`), and optimizers become one generic pytree function. Combined with vmap (idea 2), `vmap(train)` over a record of hyperparameters trains a whole population in one compiled program.

- **Build:** parser.py/ast.py: `record`, `enum`, `match`, and functional update `p with {l1.W = …}`. values.py: RecordVal and EnumVal. Compile-time enums (architecture choices) are resolved like compile-time `if`. Run-time sum types are an i32 tag plus the variants' tensors in a static max layout; `match` lowers to ir.If, or to selects under vmap. elaborate.py: `model` desugars to record + methods, keeping `net.l1.W` paths, instantiate and model_params working. autodiff: the cotangent of a record is a record (Swift/Dex TangentVector). Prelude: adam/sgd rewritten once over pytrees, `fn adam_step(p: T, g: T, s: AdamState[T])`. save/load key checkpoints by record path; the runtime routines already write named tensors. ide.py: hover shows record types.
- **Demo:** Population training: one Anvil program trains 64 MNIST MLPs at once, an 8×8 grid of learning rate × init scale, via `vmap(train)(lrs, keys)`. All 64 networks see the same batch, so every layer is one large GEMM on the AMX unit. The aim is to finish the grid in about the time PyTorch takes for a few sequential runs, while an ASCII heatmap of test accuracy fills in each epoch. The program replaces the separate `--set` processes of experiments/run.py's sweep.
- **Builds on:** model/param machinery (elaborate.instantiate, model_params, Scope prefixes), optimizer blocks, save/load runtime routines, experiments/run.py's grid sweep
- **Risk:** For a while there are two worlds: reference-semantics `param`/`minimize` and value-semantics records. Run-time sum types with static layouts waste memory when variants are unbalanced. Wide populations spill out of cache, which may shrink the AMX win.

### Custom derivatives and implicit layers: custom_vjp, fixed_point, root, odeint with the adjoint method

*wow 8/10 · months*

Let derivative rules be written in Anvil (`derivative softplus(x) = sigmoid(x)`, `custom_vjp` for whole functions), moving simplify.deriv's hard-coded table into the prelude where students can read and change it. On top of that, add implicit layers whose gradients come from the implicit function theorem instead of unrolling. Deep Equilibrium Models (Bai, Kolter & Koltun, NeurIPS 2019) use `fixed_point`; Neural ODEs (Chen et al., NeurIPS 2018 best paper) use `odeint` with an adaptive Dormand–Prince while_loop and the continuous adjoint. Memory is constant in the number of solver steps.

- **Build:** parser.py: `derivative f(x) = …` and `custom_vjp f: fwd(…) bwd(…)` declarations. autodiff.py/jvp.py: consult user rules before simplify.deriv; rules are inlined like any Anvil function, so they fuse. Prelude: move the derivative table (exp, log, tanh, sigmoid, max/min routing) into Anvil, with tests that the generated kernels do not change. New prelude/implicit.anvil: `fixed_point(f, x0, tol, max_iter)` with Anderson acceleration forward, and a backward pass that solves (I − ∂f/∂z)ᵀu = g with a while_loop of VJPs; `root` (Newton with jvp); `odeint(f, y0, ts)` (Dormand–Prince 5(4) with step control, adjoint ODE on the augmented state). Depends on while_loop (idea 4) and jvp/vjp (idea 3).
- **Demo:** A Neural ODE learns the two-spirals dataset (examples/spirals.anvil) as a continuous flow, and the terminal animates the points flowing through the learned vector field. `anvil build` shows memory staying flat as the solver tolerance tightens and the step count grows, while the discrete-backprop version's memory grows. Then a single-implicit-layer DEQ on MNIST.
- **Builds on:** simplify.deriv rules, autodiff's symbolic derivatives, the prelude-in-Anvil standard library, examples/spirals.anvil
- **Risk:** Adjoint ODE gradients are inexact (error from reverse integration), so tests must compare them against discrete gradients. Anderson acceleration needs a tiny least-squares solver written in Anvil. Hard dependency on ideas 3 and 4.

### Learning to learn: optimizers as composable values, MAML, hypergradients, learned optimizers

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Once grad composes with loops (ideas 1, 3, 4), the training loop itself becomes differentiable. Make optimizers optax-style first-class gradient transformations (`chain(clip_norm(1.0), scale_by_adam(), scale(-lr))`), then differentiate through them. That gives hypergradient descent learning its own learning rate online (Baydin et al., ICLR 2018), MAML few-shot learning (Finn, Abbeel & Levine, ICML 2017), and a learned LSTM optimizer (Andrychowicz et al., NeurIPS 2016, 'Learning to learn by gradient descent by gradient descent').

- **Build:** prelude/optim.anvil: GradientTransformation records {init, update} built from pytree ops (idea 7). Today's `optimizer` blocks stay as sugar that desugars to a transformation, and `minimize … with chain(...)` works. MAML: `inner = fold(sgd_step, θ, support)` inside an outer grad, using higher-order AD (idea 3) and differentiable fold (idea 4), with a first-order MAML flag. Hypergradient: d loss_t / d lr as one dot product of consecutive gradients, three lines in the prelude. Learned optimizer: a coordinate-wise LSTM vmapped over every parameter element (idea 2), meta-trained with truncated backprop through a scan of 20 inner steps.
- **Demo:** MAML on sinusoid regression. After meta-training, the model fits a new sine wave from 5 points in one gradient step, plotted in ASCII against the true curve: Finn et al.'s classic figure, regenerated live in class. Second beat: a learned optimizer that beats Adam on held-out MLP tasks, with both compiled into the same program.
- **Builds on:** optimizer blocks and minimize in autodiff.py, clip_gradients, warmup_cosine and the adam/sgd/rmsprop definitions in the prelude
- **Risk:** The idea depends entirely on higher-order AD and differentiable loops landing first. Second-order MAML programs are large (inner steps × backward of backward). Learned optimizers are notoriously finicky to meta-train.

### Staging for users: comptime, quote/splice, Futamura projections, and weights-as-code

*wow 8/10 · months*

Anvil's elaborator is already a partial evaluator; expose it as a language feature, with typed quote and splice (as in MetaOCaml and Terra), `comptime` blocks that build architectures programmatically, and reflection over models. The headline feature is `specialize(net.forward)`, which partially evaluates a trained network against its own weights: pruned weights vanish, constants become FMA operands, and small layers unroll into straight-line NEON with the weights in the instruction stream. That is the first Futamura projection, applied to a neural network.

- **Build:** parser.py: `comptime:` blocks, `quote(expr)`, `$x` splice. values.py: CodeVal, an AST plus its captured Scope; hygiene comes from the Scope chain. elaborate.py: splicing re-enters eval at the splice point, compile-time loops can run over model members, and compile-time list comprehensions build `Sequential` stacks. `specialize(f, prune=…)`: start from pyapi.lower/export's existing 'bake what the function reads' path, then a new optimize.py pass folds constant-buffer loads at static offsets into Const, drops zero terms through simplify.mk_binary, and unrolls reductions below a size threshold. kernel.py: literal-pool constants and straight-line FMA emission. export.py: specialized C libraries.
- **Demo:** Magnitude-prune the trained digit reader by 90%, then `specialize` it. `anvil asm` shows the network as straight-line FMAs with literal weights, and the exported library aims to beat today's 0.04 ms per call by several times. Second beat for a PL class: a tiny stack-machine interpreter written in Anvil, specialized against a fixed bytecode program into one fused kernel. That is Futamura's first projection, live.
- **Builds on:** the elaborator's staging design (compile-time values, constant folding, inlining), pyapi/export's baking of everything a function reads, Buffer.init constant buffers, simplify.py folding, kernel.py codegen
- **Risk:** Straight-line code size explodes for big layers, so cap it by layer size and fall back to dense kernels. Quote/splice hygiene and error messages are hard to get right. Unstructured sparsity rarely beats dense NEON/AMX GEMM unless it is very high.

### Pure state and persistent snapshots: functional-but-in-place training, time travel, and hot-swapping a running model

*wow 8/10 · months*

Let training be written as a pure fold, `final = fold(step, init_state, batches)`, and prove with uniqueness analysis that every update can happen in place. The analysis follows Futhark's uniqueness types and Lean 4's functional-but-in-place reuse ('Counting Immutable Beans'; Perceus, Reinking et al., PLDI 2021), so the pure program compiles to the same assembly as today's imperative one. Because state becomes an immutable typed value, the runtime can keep persistent snapshots that share unchanged tensors, step backward through training, and hot-swap new code into a running process Erlang/Elm-style, migrating the state by type.

- **Build:** elaborate.py: `fold` / `for … with state` sugar and `s with {…}` updates over records (idea 7). A uniqueness/last-use pass that extends optimize.reuse_dying_inputs and write-back coalescing; when a copy is unavoidable it warns with the span ('copy inserted because `s` is used later'). arena.py + runtime.s: copy-on-write snapshot ring, plus snapshot/restore routines and a control socket. New `anvil live file.anvil`: watches the file, recompiles with a cached prelude, and moves state into the new binary by matching record paths and shapes (as save/load already matches tensors). A user `migrate(old) -> new` function handles changed shapes, e.g. Net2Net function-preserving widening (Chen, Goodfellow & Shlens, ICLR 2016). `anvil replay --step N` combines time travel with --check NaN tracing.
- **Demo:** Train the char-RNN live in class, then edit the file: change the sampling temperature, widen a layer with Net2Net, swap SGD for Adam. The running program picks up each change within about a second without losing progress. Then set a learning rate that blows up; `anvil replay` steps back 50 snapshots, --check points at the first bad tensor and its source line, and training resumes from before it with a fixed lr. Finally, `anvil asm --diff pure.anvil imperative.anvil` shows identical inner loops.
- **Builds on:** SSA locals and write-back coalescing, optimize.reuse_dying_inputs, arena.py lifetimes, save/load matched by tensor name and size, --check NaN tracing, the REPL
- **Risk:** Users will make shape changes the migrator does not expect, so it needs a fallback: restart with migrated weights. Snapshots of big models need diffs or sparse retention. Compile latency of 0.2–3 s limits the live feel for unrolled programs.

### Proof-carrying compilation: every rewrite validated, and the core proved in Lean

*wow 8/10 · moonshot*

Every Anvil kernel is a pure comprehension. Two kernel programs are therefore equivalent exactly when each output element's symbolic expression matches, up to reassociation of reductions, which is a tractable target for translation validation (Pnueli, Siegel & Singerman, TACAS 1998; Alive2, Lopes et al., PLDI 2021). Validate every fusion, inline, CSE, loop collapse and e-graph rewrite on every compile. Beyond that, formalize the kernel IR semantics, the jvp/transpose AD rules and the algebraic laws in Lean 4, with machine-checked proofs.

- **Build:** anvil/validate.py: symbolic per-element evaluation of a block before and after each optimize.py pass. It normalizes Affine offsets, canonicalizes AC operators and treats reductions as bags. Cases it cannot normalize get polynomial identity testing by random evaluation on interp.py (Schwartz–Zippel). `anvil build --validate` reports each pass's verdict; on failure it gives a minimal counterexample (buffer, element index, both expressions). Every example is validated in CI. Optional proofs/ directory in Lean 4: an inductive kernel IR with denotational semantics over ℝ; proofs that map/fold fusion, banana split and online softmax (idea 5) preserve meaning; and proofs that jvp computes the derivative and transpose the adjoint on the linear fragment, a mechanized version of the 'You Only Linearize Once' argument.
- **Demo:** Re-plant a real historical bug from PLAN.md in optimize.fuse: a fill fused past a loop that assigns into the buffer. `anvil build --validate` rejects the compile and names the exact element where the two programs disagree. Then open the Lean file proving that online softmax equals softmax and walk the class through it.
- **Builds on:** interp.py as the reference semantics, the pure-comprehension kernel IR (ir.Kernel/Affine), the optimize.py passes, the differential tests in tests/test_optimizer.py
- **Risk:** Floating-point reassociation means validation is over the reals (or within a tolerance), so it validates the math, not the bits. Mutation and runtime offsets resist symbolic normalization. The Lean formalization is a large, specialized effort.

### Zero-cost lambdas: first-class functions and function transformations, defunctionalized by the staging elaborator

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Add lambdas (`fn(x) => x * x`), function-typed arguments, partial application, closures returned from functions and models, and make `grad`/`value_and_grad` function transformers, so `grad(f)`, `compose(f, g)` and `iterate(f, n, x)` are values you can pass around. Futhark needs a dedicated defunctionalization pass for this (Hovgaard, Henriksen & Elsman, TFP 2018). Anvil's elaborator already inlines every call at compile time, so every closure disappears before kernel IR exists, and higher-order code compiles to the same NEON as hand-inlined code.

- **Build:** ast.py/parser.py: an `A.Lambda` node in the Pratt parser (`fn(params) => expr`, plus block lambdas). values.py: `ClosureVal` (FnVal already holds decl + captured Scope, so this is mostly sugar) and `TransformVal(kind, fn, argnums)`. elaborate.call(): `grad(f)(x)` allocates fresh 'var' buffers for the differentiated arguments, opens an explicit AD region, inlines call_fn, then runs Backprop on that region. autodiff.py: generalize ad_region from 'since the innermost loop' to an explicit region start marker. builtins.py: value_and_grad, compose, partial, iterate (static). ide.py recorder: hover shows function types like `(f32[n,784]) -> f32[n,10]`. Tests: higher-order and hand-inlined versions must produce identical kernel IR.
- **Demo:** Newton's method as a higher-order function in 3 lines: `newton = fn(f) => fn(x) => x - f(x) / grad(f)(x)`, then `iterate(newton(fn(x) => x**3 - 2), 6, 1.0)` prints the cube root of 2. `anvil asm` shows the whole thing as a dozen straight-line NEON instructions with no calls. The same `newton`, applied to an f32[1_000_000] tensor of targets, runs vectorized and multithreaded. The lesson for the class: in this language, higher-order functions cost nothing at run time.
- **Builds on:** FnVal/Scope closures in values.py, call_fn inlining and fn_stack in elaborate.py, autodiff.grad/Backprop region logic
- **Risk:** Inside a runtime loop, `grad(f)` must not capture kernels from the enclosing iteration's region. The recursion guard (fn_stack depth 8) will bite iterate-style combinators. Lambdas need readable names in error call chains. Users who iterate 1000 times will unroll huge programs, so those loops should go through scan (idea 4).

### Algebraic effects and handlers, compiled away: randomness, logging and accumulation as effects

*wow 7/10 · months*

Make randomness, logging and accumulation algebraic effects with user-definable handlers (as in Koka, Eff and OCaml 5). The staging elaborator resolves them entirely at compile time, in the spirit of capability-passing compilation of handlers (Schuster, Brachthäuser & Ostermann, ICFP 2020), so handlers cost nothing at run time. The same model code can run under different meanings: fresh noise, a splittable key (JAX semantics, so adding one draw no longer shifts every later random number), antithetic pairs, common random numbers, or exact enumeration of a discrete choice.

- **Build:** parser.py: `effect Rand: fn sample(d) -> T`, `with handler:` blocks, and `resume` in handler clauses. elaborate.py: a handler stack like fn_stack. Performing an operation inlines the innermost handler's clause with `resume` bound to a compile-time continuation. Tail-resumptive handlers need no runtime continuation at all; multi-shot handlers like `enumerate` elaborate the continuation once per value, with the cost reported by anvil cost. Built-in effects: Rand (the `~` syntax becomes perform Rand.sample), Log (`emit "loss", loss`, handled by print/csv/sparkline/ignore), and Accum (the existing `+=` scatter, which is Dex's Accum effect). RNG: a `seeded(key)` handler derives the Rand salt from (key, call-path hash) instead of the global stream counter in interp.py and runtime.s. ide.py: inferred effect rows on hover (`fn vae_loss(x) -> f32[] ! {Rand}`).
- **Demo:** One dropout network, four handlers, one screen. `with fresh:` trains it. `with mc(32):` (a vmapped handler) puts MC-dropout uncertainty bars on ambiguous digits drawn in bin/draw. `with antithetic:` shows the VAE's gradient variance dropping live in a sparkline. `with log_to("run.csv")` captures metrics, and `anvil asm --diff` shows that `with ignore:` compiles to exactly the same assembly as no logging at all.
- **Builds on:** the counter-based RNG (Rand salt, stream counter in interp.py and runtime.s), `~` sampling syntax and DistVal, scatter-add `+=` kernels, the inlining elaborator
- **Risk:** Multi-shot handlers cause exponential compile-time branching. Existing programs' bit-identical RNG streams must survive the switch to path-derived keys. Effect-inference errors must stay readable for students.

### Recursion schemes: folds over run-time trees, hylomorphisms and deforestation

*wow 7/10 · months*

Add cata/ana/hylo ('Functional Programming with Bananas, Lenses, Envelopes and Barbed Wire', Meijer, Fokkinga & Paterson, FPCA 1991) over recursive enum types, compiled two ways. A structure known at compile time unrolls, as the checkers `search(depth)` recursion does today. A run-time tree stored as node arrays compiles into a wavefront schedule with one batched kernel per tree level, an ahead-of-time version of TensorFlow Fold's dynamic batching (Looks et al., ICLR 2017). Hylomorphisms are deforested (Wadler 1990), so the intermediate tree is never built.

- **Build:** ast.py/parser.py: recursive `enum Tree = Leaf(x: i32) | Node(l: Tree, r: Tree)` with `cata(alg, t)`, `ana(coalg, seed, depth)` and `hylo`. Run-time representation: a struct-of-arrays node table (kind, child indices, payload), padded to a static maximum like nonzero(size=K). New anvil/recursion.py: a runtime.s routine computes node levels once per batch of trees, then an ir.For over levels gathers each node's children (Gather terms in Affine offsets) and applies the algebra to every node of that level at once; gradients flow through the existing scatter-add backward of gathers. For compile-time hylo with a static depth, a fusion rule skips materializing the per-level arrays.
- **Demo:** (1) Rewrite checkers' look-ahead search as `hylo(minimax, legal_moves, board, depth)` in 3 lines, and show with `anvil ir --diff` that it compiles to the same loops as the hand-written version. (2) A TreeRNN that learns to evaluate random arithmetic expressions such as ((3+4)×2)−5 from their parse trees, batching thousands of differently shaped trees level by level. PyTorch typically handles that with per-node Python loops.
- **Builds on:** compile-time recursion with a counting-down depth (checkers.anvil search), gathers with runtime bounds checks, scatter-add gradients, nonzero(size=K) static-size padding
- **Risk:** Static shapes force padding to a maximum node count and depth. Level-wise batching leaves the machine underused on skinny trees. Recursive types must never unroll forever in the elaborator.

### Lazy streams with stream fusion: data pipelines that compile into the training loop

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Add a `Stream` type with lazy combinators (map, zip, window, shuffle(key), batch, take, iterate, prefetch) that stream-fusion laws eliminate (Coutts, Leshchinskiy & Stewart, ICFP 2007, 'Stream Fusion: From Lists to Streams to Nothing at All'). A pipeline like `emnist |> shuffle |> map(elastic_distort) |> batch(128)` then compiles into the first convolution's im2col gather with no intermediate buffers. Stages that cannot fuse run on a runtime thread one batch ahead, or on the CPU while the Metal GPU trains, and mmap streams handle datasets larger than RAM.

- **Build:** values.py: StreamVal, a compile-time description of a source plus stages in which nothing is materialized. builtins.py: combinators, plus a new mmap_bytes source next to idx/npy/bytes. elaborate.for_batches generalized to `for x, y in stream:`: pure index-map stages lower to TVal view mappings, like today's shuffled-batch gather. Prelude augmentations: random affine and elastic distortion (Simard et al. 2003) as 4-tap interpolating gathers that compose with Conv2d's patches gather. prefetch(k): double-buffered batches filled by a worker from the runtime.s thread pool, or by the CPU while metal.py's command buffer runs. `iterate(env_step, s0)` streams for RL (snake.anvil's environment). anvil cost reports bytes moved per batch before and after fusion.
- **Demo:** Retrain the EMNIST letter network with on-the-fly random affine and elastic distortions fused into the first layer's gather. `anvil cost` shows zero extra memory traffic for augmentation, epoch time barely moves, accuracy climbs past the 86.9% baseline, and bin/draw --text reads messy handwriting better. Second beat: the char model trains on a multi-gigabyte text dump through a 4-line mmap pipeline.
- **Builds on:** batches(...) and the shuffled gather in elaborate.for_batches, TVal zero-copy views, Conv2d's im2col in the prelude, the runtime.s thread pool and loaders, Metal's never-sync host loop
- **Risk:** Arbitrary stages are not index maps and cannot fuse into a gather. Prefetch threads complicate determinism, so random keys must depend on the batch index, not on timing.

## cuda-gpu (16)

### FlashAttention, derived rather than hand-written

*wow 10/10 · months*

Instead of hand-writing FlashAttention, teach the optimizer one algebraic identity, the online normalizer (Milakov & Gimelshein 2018): a max-stabilized exp-sum that feeds a contraction can run as one streaming pass with a running max and rescaling. Applied to the `softmax(s) @ v` users already write in index notation, it yields FlashAttention-2's forward. A rematerialization rule (recomputing P from Q, K and the saved logsumexp is cheaper than storing it) lets autodiff derive the backward. The same rewrite gives fused linear + cross-entropy that never materializes the [B·T, vocab] logits (Cut Cross-Entropy, Wijmans et al. 2024).

- **Build:** A new pass, anvil/online.py, runs in optimize_program. It matches the kernel chain m = max_j s; e = exp(s - m); l = Σ_j e; o = Σ_j e·v / l, where s is itself a contraction, and emits one kernel with loop-carried (m, l, acc) over j-blocks and the correction factor exp(m_old - m_new). The match holds for any score expression, including scale, bias, ALiBi slope·(i - j), tanh softcapping and gathered relative positions, which gives FlexAttention-style score mods with no templates. Mask analysis: `(0.0 if j <= i else -1e9)`, `where j <= i` and sliding windows (`where i - j < W`) are affine predicates, so whole tiles above the diagonal are skipped (block-sparse iteration, about half the FLOPs for causal attention). In autodiff.py, a remat decision driven by cost.kernel_flops vs kernel_bytes keeps only logsumexp. The backward recognizes dS = P∘(dP − rowsum(dO∘O)) and emits FA2's single pass over KV blocks for dQ, dK and dV. Backends: NEON register tiles (fused attention on the CPU), Metal simdgroup matrices, and CUDA via Tile IR and tensor cores, with FA3-style warp specialization (Shah et al. 2024) as a stretch. The same pass rewrites prelude cross_entropy(x @ W_out, labels) into a streaming logsumexp over vocabulary blocks.
- **Demo:** The README's 16×256×64 attention benchmark, where PyTorch SDPA was 2.8× faster, flips. The GPT then trains at a 16k context on the M3 Max with memory linear in T. Classroom moment: write a new attention variant (softcapped + sliding window + ALiBi) in one line of index notation and get a fused forward and backward kernel that no library ships.
- **Builds on:** the prelude's softmax, log_softmax and cross_entropy (written in Anvil, so the pattern is visible in IR), autodiff.py's kernel-level reverse mode, optimize.py fusion, cost.py, examples/transformer.anvil and attention.anvil, the pyapi attention benchmark
- **Risk:** Pattern-matching across kernel chains is brittle once other passes inline or reorder the IR. Run the pass before fusion and keep the matched form canonical. The backward depends on the remat analysis being right. FA3-class Hopper speed also requires the tensor-core idea.

### The NanoGPT speedrun, in Anvil

*wow 10/10 · moonshot*

Enter the public modded-nanogpt speedrun: train GPT-2 small (124M) on FineWeb to 3.28 validation loss on 8×H100 as fast as possible, a leaderboard where world-class engineers trade seconds. A short Anvil program, compiled by a pure-Python compiler with no PyTorch, posting a competitive time would be the strongest benchmark an ML language could have. Everything it forces (bf16 tensor cores, derived FlashAttention with sliding windows, Muon as a user-defined optimizer block, data parallelism, one-launch training) remains a reusable feature.

- **Build:** examples/speedrun/gpt2.anvil writes the record-holding architecture in index notation: rotary embeddings, ReLU², QK-norm, value embeddings, U-net skip connections, tanh logit softcapping, and sliding-window block-sparse causal attention. `optimizer muon` goes in the prelude (Jordan et al. 2024): momentum, then 5 Newton–Schulz iterations X ← aX + (bA + cA²)X with A = XXᵀ under `static for`, in bf16, sharded across ranks with sharding types. A streaming token loader reads memory-mapped .bin shards with prefetch (extending the bytes and npy loaders), in fixed-length windows. It depends on the tensor-core types, derived FlashAttention, sharding types with overlapped reduce-scatter, One Launch graphs and `anvil tune`. `anvil cost` predicts MFU and wall time before launch. Milestones: parity with llm.c's GPT-2 124M reproduction on 1 GPU, then 8 GPUs, then within 2× of the record, then a record attempt.
- **Demo:** A live 8×H100 run streamed in class. Before launch, the compiler's predicted finishing time appears; the validation loss then crosses 3.28 within minutes, from a program students can read in one sitting.
- **Builds on:** examples/transformer.anvil, user-defined optimizer blocks, static for, the prelude, cost.py, and the CUDA ideas above (tensor cores, derived attention, sharding, graphs, tuning)
- **Risk:** The highest risk of any idea here: it depends on five or more other ideas reaching near-state-of-the-art kernel speed. The record moves every few weeks, and each 8×H100 attempt costs real money. A near miss, such as llm.c parity, is still a strong result.

### Tensor cores and precision types: the Adam update inside the wgmma

*wow 9/10 · months*

Add bf16/f16/tf32/fp8 to the type system (`bf16[n, d]`, `fp8e4m3[...]`), with accumulation rules the type checker enforces, and add a native tensor-core GEMM generator. It starts with Ampere's mma.sync, ldmatrix and multistage cp.async, then moves to Hopper's wgmma, TMA and warp-specialized producer and consumer warpgroups. cuBLAS cannot fuse Anvil's epilogues: the optimizer update runs on the accumulator fragments of the weight-gradient GEMM, so dW never reaches HBM.

- **Build:** parser.py, values.py and ir.py: the reserved names f16/bf16 become real dtypes, plus FP8E4M3/E5M2 with per-tensor scales. Promotion rules: bf16 @ bf16 accumulates in f32 and stores as bf16. A `--amp` / precision block keeps f32 master weights in optimizer state (Micikevicius et al. 2018), and FP8 uses delayed amax scaling as in Transformer Engine. NEON gets f16 (fcvt, fmla .8h) and Metal gets half/bfloat, so the types run everywhere and stay testable on the Mac. anvil/backend/tc_gemm.py builds from blas.Gemm plus the Tile IR. The sm_80 path uses mma.sync.aligned.m16n8k16.row.col.f32.bf16.bf16.f32, ldmatrix.x4, 3-4 cp.async stages and swizzled shared memory. The sm_90a path uses wgmma.mma_async m64nNk16 from shared-memory descriptors, TMA cp.async.bulk.tensor with mbarrier completion, a producer warp that lowers its registers with setmaxnreg, and two ping-ponging consumer warpgroups. The epilogue is the fused Expr tree from optimize.py (bias, gelu, Adam with m and v state). Phase-1 shortcut: generate CUTLASS 3.x C++ whose Epilogue Visitor Tree is translated from the Anvil Expr, which gives a fast correct baseline while the native PTX path matures. Blackwell stretch: tcgen05.mma with tensor memory and NVFP4 block scaling.
- **Demo:** On an H100: Anvil's bf16 GEMM at about 80-90% of cuBLAS, from a compiler written in pure Python. Then the 784-1024-1024-10 MLP's training step, where the Adam update is fused into the wgmma epilogue, against PyTorch with cuBLAS and fused Adam (fused=True). Nsight shows the dW tensor never written.
- **Builds on:** blas.prepare/Gemm, optimize.py GEMM epilogue fusion (GEMM_EPILOGUE), the parser's reserved dtype names, arena.py, ptx.py, the Tile IR
- **Risk:** Reaching cuBLAS-class Hopper performance is a long expert grind (swizzles, pipeline depth, register pressure). A large epilogue such as Adam with two state tensors adds register pressure that can stall the tensor cores. Low precision diverges from the f32 interpreter, so tests need tolerance-aware comparisons.

### Sharding types: multi-GPU parallelism checked like shapes

*wow 9/10 · months*

Make the device mesh part of the type: `mesh gpus[dp=4, tp=2]`, `x: f32[B @ dp, T, D]`, `param W1: [D, 4*D @ tp]`. Index notation already says which indices are contracted, so the compiler can derive every collective: a sum over a sharded index becomes an all-reduce or a reduce-scatter. Compile-time autodiff then derives the backward collectives by duality (all-gather ↔ reduce-scatter). Megatron's column- and row-parallel MLP, ZeRO, and 1F1B or zero-bubble pipelines follow from annotations, and a sharding mistake becomes a compile error with a suggested fix.

- **Build:** Types: parser.py, values.py and elaborate.py gain shard annotations on dimensions, propagated through index notation GSPMD-style (Xu et al. 2021). A sharded output index computes locally; a sharded reduction index produces partial sums plus a collective; a mismatch inserts a reshard (all-to-all) or raises an error. ir.py gets a Collective statement (all_reduce, all_gather, reduce_scatter, all_to_all, send/recv), and autodiff.py gets its adjoint rules. Runtime: NCCL loaded with dlopen from anvil_cuda.h, one process per GPU (ncclCommInitRank). Gradients are all-reduced in buckets on a communication stream as soon as each layer's gradient is final; the overlap schedule is computed statically because the order is known. ZeRO-1/2/3 (Rajbhandari et al. 2020) is `adam(shard=dp)`. Pipeline: `stage` annotations on model fields, with a 1F1B or ZB-H1 schedule (Qi et al. 2023) computed at compile time. anvil cost reports bytes over NVLink and InfiniBand and the predicted time per collective. --cuda-emulate gets a fake NCCL over shared memory with N processes, so multi-GPU tests run on the Mac.
- **Demo:** examples/transformer.anvil scaled up on 8 GPUs. Changing one line (dp=8 to dp=4, tp=2) recompiles with Megatron tensor parallelism, and `anvil cost` predicts the communication per step before launch, which the measured timeline then matches. A deliberate mistake (`W2: [4*D @ dp, D]`) gets a red squiggle explaining the all-gather it would imply.
- **Builds on:** shapes-as-types and unification, index notation's LCA binding rule (which indices reduce), compile-time reverse-mode autodiff, cost.py, arena.py, the CUDA backend
- **Risk:** Measuring needs multi-GPU hardware. Propagation through reshapes, views and gathers is subtle (reshaping a sharded dimension). Every rank must issue collectives in the same order; a single static program makes that true by construction, but only if conditionals on computed values are handled carefully.

### WebGPU: Anvil in a browser tab, compiler included

*wow 9/10 · months*

A WGSL backend plus a JavaScript host-code generator makes any Anvil program a web page that trains on the visitor's GPU. The compiler is pure Python with zero dependencies, so it should run unmodified in Pyodide: typing index notation, seeing shapes as you type, reading the generated WGSL and pressing Train all happen at one URL with nothing to install. Students train a GPT on their own laptop.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/wgsl.py derives from cuda.KernelGen as metal.py does: @workgroup_size, workgroup-memory tiles from the Tile IR, subgroupAdd reductions when the subgroups feature is present, and shader-f16 for half precision.
