WebGPU limits are handled through the arena. All tensors live in ONE storage buffer at arena.py offsets, which sidesteps the default limit of 8 storage buffers per shader stage; larger models request a bigger maxStorageBufferBindingSize. WGSL has no float atomics, so float scatter-adds use atomicCompareExchangeWeak loops on bitcast u32, or a deterministic segmented reduction. anvil/backend/js.py lowers ir.For, While, If and Print to async JavaScript, awaiting mapAsync exactly at the sync points metal.gpu_reads identifies, with host scalars as in metal.host_scalars and data from fetch() of idx/npy files. `anvil web file.anvil -o site/` writes one HTML page plus data. `anvil export --web` writes a JS module with the weights baked in. The playground is Pyodide + the compiler + ide.py in a CodeMirror editor, with shape inlay hints, IR and WGSL tabs, and a live loss chart.
- **Demo:** bin/draw with no Python server: the digit and handwriting readers run in WebGPU at 60 fps while you draw, from a static page. Then the playground URL in class: every student edits the MNIST MLP, sees its shapes inline, and trains it in their own tab in seconds.
- **Builds on:** cuda.KernelGen and metal.py codegen, metal.host_scalars and sync-point analysis, arena.py, ide.py, examples/draw, anvil export's baked-weight lowering, the zero-dependency pure-Python compiler
- **Risk:** WebGPU availability and limits vary by browser and device (Safari and Firefox support, mobile GPUs). WGSL lacks float atomics and 64-bit integers. Compiling in Pyodide will be several times slower than native, and async readback forces host code into an async style.

### Fully fused MLPs and an Instant-NGP in a page of Anvil

*wow 9/10 · months*

tiny-cuda-nn showed that small MLPs (width 64-128) should run entirely inside one kernel with activations never leaving on-chip memory, and that multiresolution hash encodings make NeRFs train in seconds (Müller et al. 2021, 2022). Anvil can derive both. One pass detects chains of narrow Linear layers and emits a fully fused forward and backward per row tile. Integer bit operations turn a hash grid into a gather in index notation, whose gradient is the scatter-add Anvil already supports. Flagship: a NeRF trained in seconds from one page of Anvil.

- **Build:** Language: i32 bit operations (^, &, |, <<, >>, wrapping unsigned multiply) through lexer.py, parser.py, ops.py, ir.py and every backend, plus `cumsum`/`cumprod` scan builtins with gradients (for volume-rendering transmittance). A `hash_grid` model in the prelude: for each level, the 8 corner indices via the spatial hash (x·1 ⊕ y·2654435761 ⊕ z·805459861) mod T, trilinear weights, and gathers into a table param. A new "fused MLP" pass in optimize.py turns a chain of Linear+activation layers with width ≤ 128 over a batch dimension into one Tile IR kernel that keeps activation tiles in shared memory or registers. The backward is fused the same way, with dW accumulated per block and then reduced deterministically. Tensor cores (f16) on CUDA, simdgroup matrices on Metal, NEON register tiles on the CPU. `show` gains PNG frame output for turntable renders.
- **Demo:** `bin/anvil run --cuda examples/nerf.anvil` on the NeRF-synthetic Lego scene gives a recognizable novel-view render in about 10 seconds on a 4090, plus a turntable GIF. On the M3 Max with Metal, a neural SDF of the Stanford bunny trains in seconds. In class: the whole renderer and its training fit on one slide of index notation.
- **Builds on:** gathers and differentiable scatter-adds (`E[ids[n], d] += g[n, d]`), atomicAdd in cuda.py, the Embedding model, optimize.py fusion, Tile IR, tensor-core GEMMs
- **Risk:** It needs new language surface (bit ops, scans). Matching tiny-cuda-nn's speed requires careful tuning of the fused-MLP kernel. Ray sampling and occupancy grids are data-dependent work that fits static shapes poorly; start with a fixed number of samples per ray.

### Megakernel: the whole training step in one persistent kernel

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

Go past CUDA graphs and compile an entire forward + backward + optimizer step, and the loop around it, into a single persistent kernel. The dataflow is fully static, so the compiler can split every kernel into tile tasks, assign them to SMs ahead of time, and synchronize with fine-grained dependency counters instead of kernel boundaries, keeping activations on chip between layers. It targets batch-1 latency and small-model training, where today's GPUs sit idle between launches.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/mega.py builds a task graph from the optimized IR: each Tile IR kernel becomes tile tasks, with dependencies between tiles derived from the Affine read and write ranges. Tasks are list-scheduled onto SMs statically using cost.py estimates or tuned times. Per-task counters live in global memory and are synchronized with red.release.gpu and ld.acquire.gpu. Warp specialization: a loader warp prefetches the next task's operands with cp.async/TMA while compute warps run. Consecutive tasks on one SM that share a tile keep it in shared memory, so layer L's output row tile feeds layer L+1 directly. The kernel iterates training steps itself, with device-side counters and prints through One Launch's ring buffer. An inference mode for anvil.function and `anvil export --cuda` decodes the GPT at batch 1 with a fixed-size KV cache. A Metal version would use device-scope atomics with an occupancy-bounded grid.
- **Demo:** The GPT example generating text at batch 1, measured in tokens/s against PyTorch eager and torch.compile(mode="reduce-overhead"), with an Nsight trace showing one kernel running for the whole generation. Then MNIST training at one launch per epoch.
- **Builds on:** the static whole-program IR, optimize.py dependency analysis, the Tile IR, cost.py, One Launch's device counters and ring buffer, arena.py
- **Risk:** Inter-SM synchronization has no forward-progress guarantee and deadlocks if the grid exceeds the blocks that can be resident at once. Static scheduling leaves load imbalance, and debugging is very hard. Prior art (Mirage MPK, Hazy Research's Llama-1B megakernel, both 2025) shows it can be done, but took expert teams.

### One Launch: the whole training program as a single CUDA Graph

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Every kernel, shape and loop in an Anvil program is known at compile time. So the entire program (epochs, batches, `if step % 100`, even `if loss < 0.05: break`) can compile into one CUDA Graph using conditional WHILE/IF nodes (CUDA 12.3+). The CPU launches once and goes idle while the GPU trains. Small models, where PyTorch spends 85-95% of each step on dispatch (logreg: 26 µs of work against 158-374 µs per PyTorch step), become pure GPU time, and PyTorch's own CUDA graphs cannot capture a Python loop with branches.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/cuda_graph.py lowers ir.For, While, If and Break into cudaGraphConditionalHandles. Loop counters become device scalars that a one-thread kernel increments before calling cudaGraphSetConditional (the inverse of Metal's host scalars). A branch on a computed value needs no host round-trip. Dependency edges come from optimize.stmt_reads/stmt_writes instead of stream order, so independent kernels (dW and dx in the backward pass, separate parameter updates) run concurrently with no hand-written streams. Prints inside loops go to a pinned-memory ring buffer of formatted records, which a host thread drains in sequence order. The dataset stays resident on the device when it fits; otherwise batches are double-buffered with cudaMemcpyAsync from pinned memory on a copy stream as memcpy nodes. The shuffle RTCall becomes a device kernel using the shared counter-based RNG. Metal gets the same treatment with an MTLIndirectCommandBuffer that encodes the loop body once. `--timeline` writes a Perfetto/Chrome-trace JSON.
- **Demo:** MNIST logreg and MLP on an H100. The Nsight Systems timeline shows a single cudaGraphLaunch for all 5 epochs, CPU near 0%, and per-step time measured against PyTorch eager and torch.compile(mode="reduce-overhead"). Early stopping (`if loss < 0.05: break`) runs entirely on the GPU.
- **Builds on:** ir.For/While/If/Break structured control flow, metal.host_scalars (inverted), optimize.stmt_reads/stmt_writes dependency analysis, the shared counter-based RNG, cuda.py host codegen
- **Risk:** Conditional body graphs restrict node types (no host-callback nodes, memory fixed at instantiation), so the ring buffer and prefetch workarounds are needed. It needs a recent driver. A hung or mis-specified graph is hard to debug; mitigate with a `--no-graph` mode that runs the same code with ordinary launches.

### Compile-time Nsight: GPU performance bugs found statically

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Every Anvil memory access is an affine function of the thread indices, so the compiler can compute exactly which addresses each warp touches. `anvil gpu-report` proves loads coalesced or counts the sectors per request, finds shared-memory bank conflicts and suggests the padding or XOR swizzle that fixes them, flags divergent branches and predicts occupancy, all before the kernel runs. It does for GPU performance what shapes-as-types did for shape errors.

- **Build:** anvil/gpucheck.py takes the thread→index maps from the Tile IR (or the current one-thread-per-output mapping) and the ir.Affine offsets. It computes 32-byte sectors and 128-byte lines per warp request for each load and store, and the bank-conflict degree for each shared-memory tile access (bank = (address/4) mod 32). Divergence is flagged for Select/where predicates that vary across lanes. Occupancy comes from register counts (ptxas -v or ptx.py's estimate) and shared memory, using per-SM limits for sm_80/89/90 and Apple GPUs (SIMD width 32). `anvil gpu-report --html` draws per-kernel warp heatmaps (32 lanes × addresses) linked to source lines through diagnostics.py spans. ide.py emits VS Code warnings such as "x is read with a stride of 784 floats across the warp: 32 transactions per request; transpose x or swap i and k". When a GPU is present, the predictions are checked against Nsight Compute counters (l1tex__t_sectors_pipe_lsu_mem_global_op_ld).
- **Demo:** In class, write `y[i] = sum W[i, j] * x[j]` and its transposed twin. The report shows one at 32 transactions per warp and the other at 4 before anything runs, and Nsight confirms it. Next, a shared-memory transpose tile with a 32-way bank conflict, fixed by the suggested +1 padding, with the heatmaps before and after.
- **Builds on:** ir.Affine/decompose, diagnostics.py spans, ide.py and the VS Code extension, cost.py, the Tile IR
- **Risk:** The analysis is exact only for affine accesses; gathers are data-dependent and get bounds instead. With today's trivial thread mapping much of the value only arrives once Tile IR exists, although the global-memory coalescing analysis is useful immediately.

### Same bits on every GPU

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Anvil already trains bit-identically for any CPU thread count and shares one counter-based RNG across backends. Go all the way: one program produces the same bits on NEON, Metal, CUDA and WebGPU, meaning the same loss curve and the same final weight hash. That takes its own transcendental functions emitted to every backend, explicit FMA placement, a canonical reduction tree, and atomic-free deterministic scatters. Train on an H100, resume on a MacBook, and get identical numbers, which no mainstream framework does.

- **Build:** anvil/numerics.py holds one definition of exp, log, tanh, sigmoid, sin and cos (the range-reduction and minimax polynomials already in vecmath.py), emitted as NEON, MSL, CUDA/PTX and WGSL with explicit fma calls. Contraction is disabled everywhere else: nvcc --fmad=false, Metal fastMathEnabled=NO, explicit operations in WGSL. Canonical reduction order: every reduction of length n is a fixed blocked tree matching the NEON vec_red and vec_out schedules, implemented identically by GPU split reductions and Tile IR block reductions. Deterministic scatter-add replaces atomicAdd with a sort by destination plus a segmented reduction, or per-block partials summed in a fixed order. `--bitexact` uses Anvil's own GEMMs with a canonical k-order instead of cuBLAS, MPS or Accelerate. `anvil run --hash` prints the SHA-256 of all parameters at exit, and a CI test asserts equal hashes across backends. A determinism report lists which operations break exactness when --bitexact is off.
- **Demo:** Four terminals (CPU, Metal, CUDA on a remote H100, Chrome WebGPU) train MNIST for 1,000 steps and print the same weight hash. Then a checkpoint saved on the GPU box is `load`ed on the Mac and training continues, and the loss curve matches the uninterrupted run to the last bit. This also makes a natural lesson on floating-point non-associativity.
- **Builds on:** the counter-based RNG shared by anvil_host.h and MSL_PRELUDE, deterministic CPU threading, vecmath.py, save/load checkpoints, the interpreter as the oracle
- **Risk:** It costs performance: no vendor GEMMs, no atomics, fixed reduction trees. Denormal flushing and GPU division and sqrt rounding differ by vendor and need software sequences. WebGPU gives the weakest guarantees about float semantics.

### PTX Forge: a pure-Python PTX backend, no nvcc

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Emit PTX straight from the kernel IR, the way aarch64.py emits NEON, and load it through the CUDA driver API via ctypes. No CUDA toolkit and no C++ compiler are needed: `anvil.function` on any machine with an NVIDIA driver compiles in milliseconds, and `anvil ptx` prints readable PTX with each kernel headed by the index notation it came from. It also gives direct access to the instructions nvcc only exposes as intrinsics: mma, ldmatrix, cp.async, wgmma, mbarrier, setmaxnreg.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/ptx.py lowers ir.Kernel to virtual-register PTX. Affine offsets become mad.wide address arithmetic and Gather bounds checks become setp/selp plus the fault word. Loads use ld.global.nc.v4.f32, scatter-adds use red.global.add.f32, and split reductions use warp shuffles (shfl.sync.bfly) instead of the second combine kernel. Transcendentals use ex2.approx/lg2.approx with vecmath.py's range-reduced polynomials where accuracy matters. anvil/backend/cudadrv.py provides ctypes bindings to libcuda (cuInit, cuModuleLoadDataEx with the JIT error log, cuLaunchKernel, cuMemAlloc), so anvil.function's host side stays in Python. `anvil ptx file.anvil` uses the same header and «…» annotation style as `anvil asm`. tests/ptx_sim.py interprets the PTX subset Anvil emits, so the Mac test suite still executes it, cross-checked with ptxas when available. Stretch: write cubin directly for an sm_80 subset from CuAssembler/maxas-style encoding tables, making Anvil an ML compiler that writes NVIDIA machine code itself.
- **Demo:** On a GPU box with only the driver installed (pip install anvil, no toolkit), a `%%anvil --cuda` softmax compiles in about 20 ms. Then `anvil ptx` puts the one-line `out[..., j] = e[..., j] / sum e[..., k]` beside the five shfl.sync instructions its sum became. Class takeaway: "this is what a reduction is on a GPU."
- **Builds on:** aarch64.py/kernel.py per-kernel codegen and readable asm headers, vecmath.py polynomials, pyapi.py (ctypes, ~/.cache/anvil), cuda.py host code and fault reporting
- **Risk:** PTX ISA versions and driver JIT compatibility need care. Tuning moves into PTX-level details that ptxas may override. The cubin stretch relies on undocumented encodings that change with every architecture.

### Tile IR: block-level lowering with a layout algebra

*wow 7/10 · months*

Today every GPU kernel is one thread per output with a serial reduction loop and no shared memory. Add a Tile IR between the kernel IR and the GPU backends: each `out[I] = ⊕_J body` is tiled into thread blocks, warps and per-thread register fragments, and operands are staged through shared memory with coalesced vector loads. Layouts become first-class values in CuTe's shape:stride algebra, with swizzles. This turns Anvil's GPU code from correct into fast on CUDA, Metal and WebGPU at once, and it lets convolutions read the image through Anvil's affine views instead of materializing im2col.

- **Build:** anvil/tile.py defines Layout = (shape, stride) with composition, logical_divide, complement and XOR swizzles (CUTLASS 3 / CuTe algebra), derived from ir.Affine offsets via ir.decompose. A TileKernel is a grid tile × block tile × warp tile × thread fragment, with explicit global→shared→register copy stages. There are three schedule families. GEMM-like kernels (optimize.gemm_like) get 128×128×32 block tiles with double-buffered shared memory. Row reductions get block trees with warp or simdgroup shuffles, replacing split_factor's two-kernel scheme. Elementwise kernels get 128-bit vectorized grid-stride loops. Implicit GEMM: when an operand is a view such as `patches[n,i,j,c,u,v] = xp[n, c, s*i+u, s*j+v]`, the shared-memory tile loader evaluates the affine view directly, so Conv2d's patches tensor is never materialized on the GPU. This needs a per-backend inlining policy, because GEMM_REUSE assumes CPU caches. Split-K and Stream-K (Osama et al. 2023) handle skinny dW products with long batch reductions. cuda.py, metal.py, ptx.py and wgsl.py all generate from TileKernel, and `anvil ir --tiles` prints the tiling.
- **Demo:** The EMNIST CNN, where MPS was 5.4× ahead, runs on Metal and CUDA with implicit-GEMM convolutions. There is no im2col buffer, so memory drops by the size of the patches tensor, and Anvil takes that row of REPORT.md. In class: an animation of one 128×128 tile moving from global memory to shared memory to registers, generated from the Tile IR.
- **Builds on:** ir.Affine/decompose, optimize.gemm_like and collapse_loops, blas.Gemm recognition, cuda.KernelGen and MetalKernelGen, the R×U register-tiling ideas in backend/kernel.py
- **Risk:** A general tiler over arbitrary affine comprehensions is polyhedral-compiler territory. Keep to a few schedule families (GEMM-like, row reduction, elementwise, scatter) and fall back to today's codegen elsewhere.

### `tile fn`: Triton-style block programming with tile types

*wow 7/10 · months*

An expert escape hatch inside the language: `tile fn` kernels written at the block level, where tiles are typed values such as `bf16[128, 64] in smem` and `f32[64, 64] in regs`. Tile shapes, memory spaces, hardware fragment shapes and shared-memory budgets are checked at compile time, the way tensor shapes are today. It is in the spirit of Triton, NVIDIA's cuTile and ThunderKittens, but the compiler can still differentiate around these kernels, fuse epilogues into them and run them on CPU, Metal, CUDA and WebGPU.

- **Build:** parser.py and ast.py gain `tile fn name(A: bf16[M, K], B: bf16[K, N]) -> f32[M, N] grid(M / BM, N / BN):`. The tile operations are load, store, `@` (mma), axis max and sum, `for k in tiles(K, BK)`, `pipeline(stages=3)`, and smem/regs memory-space qualifiers. Type rules in elaborate.py and values.py: tile sizes must divide the tensor shapes, mma operands must match fragment shapes (m16n8k16, simdgroup 8×8), and shared memory must fit the target ("this kernel needs 132 KB of shared memory; sm_86 has 100 KB"). Tile fns lower to the Tile IR and from there to ptx.py, metal.py and wgsl.py. interp.py runs them as nested loops, so they are testable on the Mac. Gradients: a `grad (dO) -> (dA, dB) = ...` clause attaches a custom backward, or autodiff applies automatically when the body is a pure comprehension. A new prelude_gpu.anvil holds tile-fn matmul, fused RMSNorm+matmul and softmax.
- **Demo:** A 25-line `tile fn` matmul, live in class, reaching about 70% of tensor-core peak on an RTX 4090. Every mistake (a non-dividing tile, shared-memory overflow, a wrong fragment shape) shows up as a red squiggle and inlay hint in VS Code through ide.py before anything compiles.
- **Builds on:** shape-polymorphic fn and unification in elaborate.py, ide.py and the VS Code extension, interp.py, the Tile IR, ptx.py
- **Risk:** A second programming model raises language and elaborator complexity, and it overlaps with what the automatic Tile IR should handle. Position it as an escape hatch, not the default.

### Metal Max: win every row of the PyTorch report on the Apple GPU

*wow 7/10 · months*

GPU convolutions are the one place PyTorch clearly beats Anvil (MPS is 5.4× faster on the EMNIST CNN), and Metal has no memory arena. Replace the MPS products with Anvil's own simdgroup_matrix kernels so epilogues fuse, add implicit-GEMM convolutions, a placement-heap arena, indirect command buffers that encode a step once, and f16 throughout. Also fix the 35-second Metal compile for unrolled programs.

- **Build:** metal.py gets MSL GEMMs using simdgroup_float8x8 / simdgroup_half8x8 with double-buffered threadgroup memory (from the Tile IR) and fused bias, gelu and Adam epilogues that MPS cannot do. MPS stays only where `anvil tune` measures it winning. Conv2d forward and backward become implicit GEMM through the affine view of the padded image, with no im2col; the dW sum over images runs as split-K. Memory: one MTLHeap of type placement, with arena.py offsets passed to newBufferWithLength:options:offset:, aliased temporaries, and GPU memory reported by `anvil build --metal`. An MTLIndirectCommandBuffer replays the loop body's dispatches each step, with host scalars in a small argument buffer. half and bfloat (Metal 3.1+) come from the precision types. Metal 4's MTLTensor and Performance Primitives matmul2d are used on M5-class GPUs with neural accelerators, behind a capability check. Compile time: kernels are deduplicated by structural hash (1,376 unrolled char-RNN kernels share a few dozen bodies) and cached in an MTLBinaryArchive.
- **Demo:** benchmarks/vs_pytorch rerun on the M3 Max: the EMNIST CNN on Metal drops from 12.4 s to under MPS's 2.3 s per epoch, the char-RNN's Metal compile falls from 35 s to a few seconds, and REPORT.md gets a table with Anvil fastest in every row.
- **Builds on:** metal.py, anvil_metal.h, blas.prepare and metal.fits, arena.py, metal.host_scalars, benchmarks/vs_pytorch/REPORT.md
- **Risk:** Apple tunes MPS convolutions per GPU generation, and simdgroup-matrix throughput on M3 is modest (no dedicated matrix units before M5), so Anvil's wins come mostly from fusion and avoided memory traffic and may be narrow.

### First Light: CUDA on real NVIDIA silicon

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

The CUDA backend passes every test in emulation but has never run on an NVIDIA GPU. This idea brings it up on rented H100s and 4090s with a one-command remote runner, a real device-memory model and cuBLASLt for every product blas.prepare already finds. Then it publishes the vs_pytorch table for CUDA, which turns the README's "CUDA too" from a promise into a benchmark.

- **Build:** anvil/remote.py plus `anvil run --cuda --remote ssh://host|modal`: ships the self-contained .cu and its data files (ANVIL_DATA), runs nvcc there and streams stdout back. A nightly real-GPU job runs tests/test_cuda.py against the interpreter. In anvil_cuda.h, unified memory gives way to ONE cudaMalloc'd device arena laid out by backend/arena.py, so temporaries share bytes on the GPU too. Pinned host mirrors exist only for buffers the host reads (Print, If on a computed value, RTCall), copied at exactly the sync points metal.gpu_reads already identifies. metal.host_scalars moves into CudaGen, so loop counters and Adam's step are passed by value and a step never syncs. Every blas.Gemm, including strided-batched stacks, goes to cuBLASLt with its native epilogues (BIAS, RELU, GELU, BGRADB) when the fused epilogue matches; otherwise blas.py's existing split applies. `anvil build --cuda --gpu h100` prints the exact device memory and gives a compile-time OOM error ("needs 31.2 GB, an RTX 4090 has 24 GB"). Stretch: under `--memory 8GB` the compiler picks tensors to recompute with a greedy Checkmate-style pass (Jain et al. 2020).
- **Demo:** From the MacBook: `bin/anvil run --cuda --remote h100 examples/transformer.anvil` compiles locally, trains on an H100, and samples text drawn from the same random numbers as the CPU run. Then benchmarks/vs_pytorch re-runs on CUDA against PyTorch eager, torch.compile and CUDA graphs, written up as REPORT_CUDA.md.
- **Builds on:** anvil/backend/cuda.py, anvil_cuda.h, anvil_host.h, arena.py, blas.prepare/Gemm, metal.host_scalars and gpu_reads, tests/test_cuda.py, benchmarks/vs_pytorch
- **Risk:** The first real run will surface bugs that emulation hid: races in split reductions, atomics on managed memory, overflow of the 32-bit index math. The naive one-thread-per-output kernels will lose to PyTorch on large models until Tile IR and tensor cores land. It also needs a rented-GPU budget.

### `anvil tune`, with a cost model trained by Anvil itself

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Every kernel has a space of schedules: GPU tile sizes, pipeline stages, split-K, block size and vector width, and register tiles R×U on NEON. `anvil tune` searches that space on the real device, caches the winners per (kernel hash, device), and learns a cost model that predicts kernel time from static features, so later programs tune in seconds. The cost model is itself written and trained in Anvil.

- **Build:** anvil/tune.py enumerates kernel.Config, TileKernel and tc_gemm parameters and compiles variants in a process pool. Timing uses CUDA events, MTLCommandBuffer GPUStartTime/GPUEndTime, or the CPU clock with repeats and outlier rejection. Variants are pruned by occupancy and shared-memory limits, and results go to a cache at ~/.cache/anvil/tune/<device>.json that every backend consults, replacing the hardcoded BLAS_MIN, MPS_MIN and SPLIT_* thresholds. Features come from cost.py: FLOPs, bytes, arithmetic intensity, extents, strides, and the coalescing score from the static GPU analysis idea. anvil/tuner_model.anvil is an MLP trained with `minimize` on (features, log time) and used as a ranker, as with Ansor's learned model (Zheng et al. OSDI 2020). `anvil roofline file.anvil` uses a table of peak FLOP/s and bandwidth per device to plot every kernel as a dot on an HTML roofline, measured against ideal.
- **Demo:** A live terminal chart of the transformer's kernels tuning on an H100, each climbing toward its roofline ceiling, followed by an instant recompile from the cache. Students get a roofline plot of their own model showing which layers are memory-bound.
- **Builds on:** backend/kernel.Config, blas/metal/cuda size thresholds, cost.py, --profile, anvil.function's compile cache
- **Risk:** Search time, and timings on a shared, busy machine (the PyTorch report already fought background load). The learned model needs thousands of measurements before it beats the heuristics.

## cpp-systems (16)

### TinyML that trains: Anvil on a $5 microcontroller, RAM known to the byte

*wow 9/10 · months*

Anvil already has what on-device-training research had to build specially: a memory plan fixed at compile time, no allocator, and the optimizer update fused into the weight-gradient kernel, so gradients never reach memory. That is the central trick of MCUNetV3, 'On-Device Training Under 256KB Memory' (Lin et al., NeurIPS 2022). Add bare-metal Cortex-M targets and an int8 inference path, and a $5 board can both read and learn, while `anvil build` prints exact flash and RAM use where TFLite Micro makes you guess the arena size.

- **Build:** Targets thumbv7em, thumbv8m and thumbv8.1m in anvil/target.py; M4F/M33 go through the C++/C backend. anvil/backend/mve.py for Cortex-M55/M85 Helium: `vfma.f32 q, q, r` vector-by-scalar FMA tiles over the 8 Q registers, wlstp.32/letp tail-predicated low-overhead loops, vldrw gathers. A freestanding runtime: no libc, printing through a user anvil_putc hook, no threads, the RNG from anvil_host.h, data compiled into const flash arrays (export.py's constant baking already does this). An int8 post-training quantization pass in optimize.py with per-channel scales: a new i8 dtype in ir.py, SMLAD dual-MACs on M4/M33 DSP. `anvil build --target rp2350 --report-memory` from arena.py. Tests on Arm's free Corstone-300 FVP (Cortex-M55 + Ethos-U55) and QEMU mps3-an547.
- **Demo:** A Raspberry Pi Pico 2 (RP2350, 520 KB SRAM) with a cheap touchscreen reads digits you draw with a finger. Correct it ten times on your own handwriting and it fine-tunes its last layer on the chip in a couple of seconds. The compile log said exactly how many bytes of RAM it would use, and it was right. The same program runs on the Arduino Nano 33 BLE Sense used by the Harvard/edX TinyML course.
- **Builds on:** backend/arena.py's static plan, optimize.py's fused optimizer epilogues, export.py's weights baked in as constants, anvil_host.h, the C++ backend for non-Helium cores
- **Risk:** f32 training on a 150 MHz M33 without SIMD is slow, so limit it to last-layer fine-tuning. An int8 dtype has to pass through elaborate, autodiff and every backend. Helium boards are rarer than plain M33/M4. Flash limits model size (Pico 2 has 4 MB).

### constexpr Anvil: the C++ compiler compiles your neural network

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

CTRE (Hana Dusíková's compile-time regular expressions) parses regex string literals inside the C++ compiler; do the same for Anvil. `constexpr auto net = ANVIL(R"( model MLP: ... minimize ... )")` is lexed, parsed, shape-checked, differentiated and fused during C++ compilation, then instantiated as loop nests. There is no Python and no build step, and Anvil's own error messages appear as C++ compile errors.

- **Build:** include/anvil/constexpr/: a constexpr lexer and parser (C++20 allows std::vector and std::string inside constant evaluation); a subset elaborator (index notation, shapes, models, grad, minimize, no I/O); constexpr ports of autodiff.py and optimize.py's inlining and fusion, producing a flat IR serialized into a std::array. That IR is lifted into template parameters, either with the classic constexpr-to-NTTP trick or with C++26 static reflection (P2996, std::define_static_array) on Bloomberg's clang-p2996 fork or GCC's in-progress implementation, and each kernel is instantiated as loops by anvil.hpp's code generator. The Python compiler is the oracle: tests require both to print the same IR text.
- **Demo:** One .cpp file holding the MNIST program as an Anvil raw string. `clang++ -std=c++26` takes about 20 seconds and the binary trains MNIST. Change a 784 to 783 inside the string, and the C++ compiler reports Anvil's shape error, pointing into the string literal.
- **Builds on:** the language definition in parser.py/elaborate.py, autodiff.py, optimize.py, and the anvil.hpp code generator
- **Risk:** Constant-evaluation step limits (-fconstexpr-steps), compile time and compiler memory will blow up on real programs. Reflection support is still experimental. It is a third implementation of the language semantics to keep in sync.

### Index notation to silicon: systolic arrays on an FPGA

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

Anvil kernels are clean affine loop nests, which is exactly the input that polyhedral systolic-array compilers such as AutoSA (Wang, Guo and Cong, FPGA 2021) need. Apply a space-time transformation to each product kernel and emit synthesizable Verilog (or Vitis HLS C++) for an array of processing elements. Simulate it cycle by cycle with Verilator, which compiles the RTL into C++, and run a digit reader on a cheap FPGA with an open-source toolchain.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/hw/: a space-time mapper over ir.Kernel that picks two loop variables as the processing-element grid and one as time, and uses the Affine coefficients to check that dependences are uniform. Fixed-point or int8 datapaths, sharing the quantization pass from the TinyML idea. A small Verilog generator: processing elements, skew FIFOs, weight-stationary buffers in BRAM, and elementwise epilogue units built from the kernel's Let/Store expressions. Host control as a small FSM or soft core. Verilator testbenches compared against interp.py. The open flow: Yosys + nextpnr for Lattice ECP5, or Apicula for Gowin (Tang Nano 20K). GTKWave traces for class.
- **Demo:** The MNIST MLP compiled to a 16×16 weight-stationary systolic array, running on a $30 Tang Nano 20K that drives HDMI with the drawn digit and its prediction. In lecture, a GTKWave trace (or an animation) shows activations moving diagonally through the processing-element grid generated from `y[i] = sum W[i, j] * x[j]`.
- **Builds on:** the affine kernel IR (ir.Kernel, Affine), blas.py's product recognition, optimize.py's epilogue fusion, interp.py as the oracle
- **Risk:** Floating point is expensive on FPGAs, so this is int8/fixed point and inference only at first. Small FPGAs have only a few dozen hardware multipliers, which limits array size. Timing closure and toolchain flakiness. A large amount of new work.

### Triton, but the backward pass writes itself: Anvil ops for PyTorch

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

`anvil export --torch` (and `anvil.torch.op(src)`) turns an Anvil function into a registered PyTorch custom operator. Its backward comes from Anvil's autodiff, fused like any other kernel; its fake/meta implementation comes from the shape signature, so it works under torch.compile without graph breaks; and it runs on CPU (NEON), MPS (Anvil's MSL) and CUDA. Triton kernels and C++ extensions need a hand-written backward; here you write the math once in index notation.

- **Build:** anvil/torch_ext.py generates a C++ extension: a TORCH_LIBRARY(anvil, m) schema built from the Anvil signature, and TORCH_LIBRARY_IMPL(anvil, CPU|MPS|CUDA, m) calling the compiled entry points on data_ptr(), built with torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline. The VJP is produced by elaborating `fn f_vjp(args, dy) = grad(sum(f(args) * dy), args...)` through pyapi.lower, with a residual policy that decides save versus recompute (FlashAttention-style recompute of the softmax). torch.library.register_autograd, plus register_fake that unifies shape variables ([n, d] -> [n, d]) over SymInts. The per-shape compile cache from pyapi.Function. The MPS path hands metal.py's MSL to torch.mps.compile_shader. torch.library.opcheck and gradcheck in the tests.
- **Demo:** In nanoGPT, replace RMSNorm+SwiGLU and the attention block with three-line Anvil index-notation ops. opcheck and gradcheck pass, torch.compile(model) shows zero graph breaks, and the training step gets faster on the M3. Then show the roughly 40-line fused backward kernel that Anvil derived.
- **Builds on:** pyapi.py (lower, the shape-keyed Function cache, zero-copy pointer slots), autodiff.py, metal.py MSL generation, cuda.py
- **Risk:** PyTorch's C++ ABI changes between releases, and extension builds are slow. Anvil compiles per concrete shape, so dynamic shapes cause recompiles and need warmup. Encoding into PyTorch's MPS command stream needs care. f32 only until Anvil has bf16/f16.

### An x86-64 backend: AVX2, AVX-512 with masks and embedded broadcast, and Intel AMX tiles

*wow 8/10 · months*

A second native backend, hand-scheduled to the same level as NEON: BLIS-shaped register tiles, AVX-512 `vfmadd231ps zmm, zmm, [mem]{1to16}` embedded broadcasts (the x86 equivalent of NEON's by-element fmla), and mask registers that keep awkward shapes like 10 classes fully vectorized with no scalar tail. Large products go to Intel AMX `tdpbf16ps` tiles, mirroring the Apple-AMX path. It also tests Anvil where PyTorch is strongest, Linux x86 with oneDNN, which makes for a more honest benchmark.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/x86/kernel.py: an x86 Lowering that shares kernel.py's schedule logic. Configs use 8 or 16 lanes; vec_out tiles are 6×16 on AVX2 (12 ymm accumulators, the BLIS Haswell shape) and up to 12×32 on AVX-512 (24 zmm); vec_red uses vextractf128/vpermilps horizontal trees; tails use {k1}{z} masking. mir.py generalized: register pools of 16 ymm or 32 zmm plus k1-k7, Intel-syntax Ins formats. x86 vecmath with the same Cody-Waite and polynomial algorithms as vecmath.py, so results agree. runtime_x86.s: SysV ABI, a futex pool on Linux and pthreads on macOS. amx.py reuses blas.py's `match` and emits ldtilecfg, tileloadd and tdpbf16ps with bf16 packing, after Linux's arch_prctl(ARCH_REQ_XCOMP_PERM, XFEATURE_XTILEDATA). `anvil asm --target x86-64-v3|v4`. Testing: I checked today that AVX2+FMA x86 binaries built with `clang -arch x86_64` run under Rosetta on this Mac, while AVX-512 dies with SIGILL there, so AVX2 is testable locally and AVX-512/AMX need Intel SDE or a cloud Sapphire Rapids machine.
- **Demo:** One slide, three panes: the same MNIST k86 micro-kernel as NEON (`fmla v5.4s, v22.4s, v21.s[0]`), as AVX2, and as AVX-512 (`vfmadd231ps zmm4, zmm0, dword ptr [rsi]{1to16}`). Then the nine REPORT.md projects on an AWS c7i (Sapphire Rapids) against PyTorch with oneDNN, with the wide MLP's products on AMX tiles.
- **Builds on:** kernel.py schedules, mir.py linear-scan allocator, vecmath.py, runtime.s, blas.py product matching, interp.py as the oracle
- **Risk:** Two hand-written backends double the maintenance, which pushes toward separating schedules from instruction encodings. oneDNN is a strong baseline and may win large GEMMs. AMX computes in bf16, not f32, which changes the numerics.

### WebAssembly SIMD: Anvil writes .wasm bytes itself and trains in a browser tab

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Wasm SIMD is 128-bit f32x4, the same 4-lane shape as NEON, and Anvil's static memory plan maps directly onto wasm linear memory. A Python module can therefore encode the binary itself (LEB128, sections, v128 opcodes) with no Emscripten and no LLVM. The drawing app no longer needs a server, and models train in a browser tab, on a phone too, using several threads through SharedArrayBuffer and Atomics.wait.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/wasm.py: a binary encoder (about 400 lines) plus kernel lowering that reuses the 4-lane vec_out/vec_red logic: v128.load, f32x4.mul/add, f32x4.splat for by-element operands, f32x4.relaxed_madd where relaxed SIMD is available. Locals replace the register allocator; vecmath.py's polynomials are reused; arena.py's offsets become constant addresses. anvil/backend/anvil_wasm.js: imports for print, show, clock and data fetches; a Web Worker pool driven by memory.atomic.wait32/notify; the counter-based RNG. `anvil build --target wasm` writes .wasm + .js. Tests in Node 20 (installed; SIMD on by default, relaxed SIMD behind a flag) against interp.py. Optional WebGPU: WGSL from cuda.py's KernelGen, the way metal.py produces MSL.
- **Demo:** bin/draw as a static GitHub Pages site. Draw a digit or write a word on your phone, and the EMNIST reader with its beam search runs in the tab in milliseconds. Press 'train' and watch the MNIST MLP learn in the browser in a few seconds. The dream demo morphs digits on the client.
- **Builds on:** kernel.py's 4-lane NEON schedules (the same width as wasm), arena.py, vecmath.py, examples/draw and the digit/letter models
- **Risk:** Wasm SIMD has no by-element FMA, and relaxed-SIMD FMA can differ between engines. Threads require COOP/COEP headers (GitHub Pages needs a service-worker shim). Memory tops out at 4 GB without memory64. The drawing app's Python stroke preprocessing has to be rewritten in JS.

### Anvil Explorer: the whole compiler in the browser, Compiler Explorer style

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

The compiler is pure Python with no dependencies, so it should run unchanged in Pyodide. The result is a static web page where students type Anvil and see, live: shapes (inlay hints from ide.py), the kernel IR before and after autodiff and fusion, and the generated NEON, AVX-512, RVV, wasm, CUDA, MSL and C++ side by side, with source lines colored the way godbolt does. Run executes it through the wasm backend. Nothing to install, and it works on a school Chromebook.

- **Build:** web/explorer/: Monaco editor plus a Pyodide worker that loads an anvil wheel. ide.py's JSON API extended with per-stage IR dumps (after elaborate, autodiff and optimize) and per-target assembly with span-to-line maps; aarch64.py already heads each kernel with its source span. A diff view of -O0 against optimized IR. An `anvil cost` panel. Run through the wasm backend. Shareable links with the source in the URL fragment. Optionally, an `anvil asm` language entry on godbolt.org.
- **Demo:** The first lecture of the course: everyone opens one URL and types `y[i] = sum W[i, j] * x[j]`. Change 128 to 120 and the shape error appears. Add `minimize`, and the backward kernels and the fused SGD update appear in the IR pane while the 4×16 FMA tile appears in the NEON pane.
- **Builds on:** the dependency-free Python compiler, ide.py / `anvil check --json`, the VS Code extension's hint logic, `anvil ir` and `anvil asm`, and every backend's text emitter
- **Risk:** Pyodide takes 5-10 s to start, and CPython in wasm runs 2-3x slower, so large unrolled programs compile slowly. Assembly for targets the browser can't run is display-only.

### CUTLASS/CuTe backend: Anvil's affine offsets are CuTe layouts, its fused epilogues are EVTs

*wow 8/10 · months*

Two structural matches make this fit cleanly. Anvil's Affine offsets (Σ cᵢ·varᵢ over loop variables) are flat CuTe layouts (shape:stride). And what Anvil fuses after a product (bias, GELU, even the Adam update) is exactly what CUTLASS 3's Epilogue Visitor Trees express (Chen et al., 'EVT', ASPLOS 2024). Emit CUTLASS C++ templates for every product blas.py recognizes, and Anvil gets Ampere/Hopper tensor-core GEMMs with the optimizer update fused into the epilogue, plus implicit-GEMM convolutions, which closes the GPU-convolution gap.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/cutlass.py starts from blas.py's Product/Gemm description (batch loops, summed loops, row- or column-major A and B) and emits CollectiveBuilder kernels (SM80/SM90, TF32 or BF16). The kernel's epilogue Let/Store expressions become Sm90EVT trees: Binary/Unary/Select nodes become Sm90Compute ops, loads of bias or optimizer state become Sm90RowBroadcast/Sm90AuxLoad, extra stores become Sm90AuxStore. Conv2d's index-notation window offsets become CuTe composed layouts for CUTLASS implicit-GEMM convolution. Non-product kernels stay on cuda.py. Layouts appear in `anvil ir` as (64,784):(784,1). Option: emit the CuTe Python DSL instead of C++ to cut compile times. Type-checked on the Mac with clang's CUDA front end, as cuda.py is today; real runs on a cloud A100/H100 or a Jetson Orin (sm_87).
- **Demo:** The 784-2048-2048-10 MLP's backward step on an H100, where the weight-gradient GEMM's epilogue is the Adam update: m, v and w are updated in registers and the gradient is never written to memory. Nsight shows one kernel where PyTorch runs a cuBLAS GEMM plus a separate fused-Adam kernel.
- **Builds on:** blas.py's product matching and epilogue splitting, optimize.py's epilogue fusion, cuda.py's host code and runtime
- **Risk:** Anvil has never run on NVIDIA hardware, so this needs a cloud GPU budget. CUTLASS templates take minutes to compile. EVT can't express some epilogues (selects over loads, gathers). TF32/BF16 numerics differ from the f32 CPU results.

### Bit-identical training on every chip

*wow 8/10 · months*

Anvil is already bit-identical across thread counts; extend that across instruction sets. The ingredients: a canonical reduction order that doesn't depend on vector width (8 virtual lanes and a fixed pairwise combine tree), FMA contraction decided in the IR, Anvil's own exp/log/tanh polynomials everywhere instead of libm or GPU fast-math, and fixed denormal modes. Then the same program trained on NEON, AVX2, AVX-512, RVV, wasm, CUDA and Metal ends with the same SHA-256 of its weights. PyTorch's determinism settings only promise repeatable runs on the same hardware.

- **Build:** A --repro mode in optimize.py and kernel.py. Reductions split into 8 strided partial sums combined in a fixed tree, realized as 2×4 NEON registers, one ymm, half a zmm, `vsetivli 8` on RVV, 2×f32x4 in wasm, and a fixed per-thread order on GPUs. An explicit fma Expr in ir.py, so the compiler decides contraction rather than -ffp-contract or a shader compiler. vecmath.py's polynomials ported into anvil_host.h, MSL and WGSL, with MTLCompileOptions safe math and __fmaf_rn plus -fmad=false on CUDA. FPCR/MXCSR flush-to-zero set identically at startup. BLAS, MPS and AMX switched off in repro mode, using Anvil's own GEMMs in a canonical k order. `anvil run --repro --hash` prints a digest of the weights. CI compares digests across Docker platforms and QEMU. Background: Demmel and Nguyen's reproducible summation (ReproBLAS).
- **Demo:** The class runs `anvil run --repro --hash examples/transformer.anvil` on a MacBook, an x86 Linux laptop, a Raspberry Pi, a RISC-V board and a browser tab, and the room reads off the same 64-hex-digit hash. Then the same training in PyTorch on two machines diverges within a few dozen steps. It opens a lecture on floating-point non-associativity.
- **Builds on:** thread-count determinism, the counter-based RNG shared by all backends, vecmath.py (already libm-free), and the multi-ISA backends
- **Risk:** Repro mode gives up speed (no AMX or MPS, a fixed reduction tree). Metal and NVCC may still reassociate or contract unless forced off, so every target needs verifying. Every future backend must keep the contract.

### Anvil on bare metal: a Raspberry Pi that boots straight into training

*wow 8/10 · moonshot*

The AArch64 backend already writes self-contained NEON assembly with its own thread pool, RNG, loaders and printing; take macOS away and it can run with no OS at all. A Pi boots in a couple of seconds into an Anvil program: four Cortex-A cores train MNIST, output goes to HDMI and UART, and step times come from the PMU cycle counter with no OS jitter. That is ML from the boot vector up, for a course that spans OS, architecture and ML.

- **Build:** A target aarch64-none-elf in target.py. A boot stub: drop from EL2 to EL1, turn on the MMU and caches with an identity map (NEON speed depends on the caches), enable FP/SIMD in CPACR_EL1. Secondary cores started through PSCI or the spin table, replacing runtime.s's pthreads with ldxr/stxr plus wfe/sev wakeups. UART and framebuffer drivers behind print and show. Data baked into kernel8.img (MNIST is 47 MB), or read through a FAT reader; alternatively build the host side on Circle, the C++ bare-metal framework for Raspberry Pi, for USB mouse and SD card support. pmccntr_el0 for clock(). CI under QEMU `-M raspi4b` or `virt`.
- **Demo:** Power on a Raspberry Pi with no operating system on the SD card. Within seconds the screen shows 'anvil' and an epoch counter while MNIST trains on four cores. Then draw a digit with a USB mouse and it reads it. A side panel compares step-time variance, near zero here, with Linux's jitter.
- **Builds on:** aarch64.py's NEON codegen, runtime.s (thread pool, RNG, printing, loaders), the static arena, `show`
- **Risk:** The Pi 5's RP1 peripherals are poorly documented for bare metal (start on a Pi 4). MMU and cache bugs are very hard to debug. With no Accelerate, products use Anvil's own GEMM only. The payoff is mostly teaching.

### Shapes cross the language boundary: a reentrant C ABI with typed C++, Rust and Swift bindings

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Today `anvil export` makes a single global instance behind a mutex. Make the generated code reentrant, with every buffer addressed from a context register, define a stable C ABI that can export training steps as well as inference, and generate bindings where Anvil's static shapes become host-language types: `std::mdspan<const float, extents<28,28>>` in C++, `&[[f32; 28]; 28]` in Rust, fixed-size types in Swift. A wrong shape is then a compile error in clang++, rustc or swiftc, not a crash.

- **Build:** aarch64.py + kernel.py: base-register addressing. Reserve x28 out of mir.GPR_POOL and address buffers as [x28, #arena_off] using arena.py offsets; the extern pointer slots (anvil_input0 and so on) move into the context. Runtime faults return error codes instead of anvil_oob calling exit(1). anvil/abi.py + anvil_rt.h: anvil_ctx_new/free/clone, anvil_call, parameter views, save/load to memory, and an ABI version plus shape-signature hash. `anvil export --train`: a `minimize` step exported as a function, e.g. critic_learn(ctx, board, reward). anvil/bindings/{cpp,rust,swift}.py: a header-only C++ class per model (operator() and learn()), a Cargo crate whose build.rs links the .a, and a SwiftPM package. An .xcframework with ios-arm64 and simulator slices (the same NEON code). Tests build with clang++, cargo (rustc 1.81 is installed) and swiftc. Swift 6.0.3 is installed; InlineArray<784, Float> needs 6.2, so until then use buffer pointers with preconditions.
- **Demo:** Export the checkers critic once as libcheckers.a and use it from four languages: a C++ raylib board that keeps learning from every game you play (on-device TD updates through critic.learn()), a Rust CLI, a SwiftUI iPhone app, and Python. A deliberate [27, 28] argument fails to compile in all three typed languages.
- **Builds on:** export.py (the C wrapper, pointer slots, weights baked in), pyapi.lower, backend/arena.py, the anvil_host.h runtime
- **Risk:** Base-register addressing changes every load and store the backend emits, and it gives up one GPR in GEMM tiles. The worker thread pool is process-global and has to be shared safely between contexts. Swift's fixed-size generics are behind Rust's const generics.

### anvil.hpp: index notation, shape inference and autodiff as C++20 types

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

A header-only C++20/26 library that mirrors Anvil inside C++. `Tensor<f32, 128, 784>` carries its shape in its type. `y(i) = sum(j, W(i, j) * x(j))` is an expression template whose index ranges are inferred by template unification, which is Anvil's binding rule written as metaprogramming. `grad(loss, W)` differentiates the expression type at compile time into a fused backward loop nest, and a shape error is a clang error carrying Anvil's own message, through C++26 user-generated static_assert messages (P2741).

- **Build:** include/anvil/anvil.hpp (2-3k lines): Index<"i"> via fixed_string NTTPs; expression node types (Load, Binary, Unary, Select, Sum/Max) mirroring ir.py's Expr classes; a consteval range-inference pass over an expression type's index set; loop nests generated with fold expressions, reduction innermost; compile-time reverse mode mirroring autodiff.py's rule dX[f(I,J)] += dY[I]·∂body/∂X (an injective offset becomes a reduction, otherwise a scatter); Model/param as aggregates and minimize(loss, net, adam{...}) fusing the update into the gradient loop as optimize.py does. Ports of prelude.anvil (Linear, softmax, layer_norm, cross_entropy, adam) written in the DSL. The counter-based RNG copied bit-for-bit from anvil_host.h, so tests can require results equal to interp.py on the same seeds. A negative-compilation test suite for shape errors.
- **Demo:** A 45-line mnist.cpp that trains to about 97% after `clang++ -O3 -std=c++2c`. Change Linear(784, 128) to Linear(784, 120) and clang prints: "index `j` ranges over 128 in one place but 120 in another". Benchmark it against Eigen and Fastor, the closest prior art (compile-time einsum, but no range inference and no autodiff).
- **Builds on:** ir.py's expression model, autodiff.py's derivative rules, prelude.anvil, the anvil_host.h RNG
- **Risk:** Template error floods and compile times. Speed depends on clang's auto-vectorizer. It is a second implementation of the semantics that could drift, so keep it a teaching mirror and test it against interp.py.

### ONNX in, readable Anvil out: pretrained models you can read and fine-tune

*wow 7/10 · months*

A zero-dependency ONNX importer and exporter, built on a hand-written 250-line protobuf codec. Import does not produce an opaque graph: it writes a readable .anvil file, with convolutions in index notation and weights loaded by npy(), which then trains, reports its cost and compiles to NEON, Metal or CUDA like any other Anvil program. Export goes the other way: index-notation kernels map to ONNX Einsum plus elementwise nodes, so Anvil-trained models run in onnxruntime, onnxruntime-web and on phones.

- **Build:** anvil/onnx/proto.py: a varint/length-delimited codec with field tables for ModelProto, GraphProto, NodeProto, TensorProto and AttributeProto, plus a safetensors reader. anvil/onnx/importer.py: a topological walk that writes Anvil source. The op table covers Conv, Gemm, MatMul, Relu, Add, Mul, GlobalAveragePool, MaxPool, Flatten, Reshape, Transpose, Concat, Gather, Softmax, LayerNormalization, Erf and more. BatchNormalization is folded into conv weights at import; dynamic dims are fixed with --dim batch=1. anvil/onnx/exporter.py works from the elaborated, pre-autodiff kernel IR: matmul-shaped kernels become MatMul/Gemm, other sums of products become Einsum (opset 12+), gathers become Gather, reductions become Reduce*, and Rand is refused with a warning. Prelude additions it forces: BatchNorm2d (still to-do in IDEAS), average pooling, padding modes. onnxruntime as a dev-only test oracle.
- **Demo:** `anvil import resnet18.onnx` writes resnet18.anvil, about 150 lines a student can read top to bottom. It classifies photos on the M3, matching onnxruntime to 1e-5, and then `minimize` fine-tunes the last layer on 10 classes from the students' own phone photos. In the other direction, the EMNIST letter reader is exported and runs in onnxruntime-web.
- **Builds on:** the npy()/save_npy loaders, Conv2d (im2col) and the prelude, the elaborator, blas.py for the products
- **Risk:** ONNX has a long tail of ops and attribute variants. Its symbolic dims clash with Anvil's static shapes. Large imported graphs will stress compile time and expose the GPU-convolution gap.

### Vector-length-agnostic codegen: RISC-V RVV 1.0, ARM SVE2 and SME from one schedule

*wow 7/10 · months*

RVV and SVE don't know their vector width at compile time: vsetvli or predicates pick it at run time. Give Anvil a vector-length-agnostic scheduling layer where a tile is R rows × LMUL vector groups and tails disappear into the vector length. The by-element FMA pattern maps to RVV's `vfmacc.vf` and SVE's indexed fmla, and the same layer covers SME's outer-product `fmopa` into ZA tiles on M4, which absorbs the SME item already on the IDEAS list. One IR then gives NEON, AVX-512, RVV and SVE side by side.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/vla.py: strip-mined loops with `vsetvli t0, a0, e32, m4, ta, ma`; LMUL chosen by register pressure (32 registers divided into LMUL groups); vfmacc.vf tiles; vfredusum, or the ordered vfredosum, for reductions; strided and segment loads; RVV exp/log ported from vecmath.py. An rv64 Linux runtime (syscalls, futex pool) and ELF assembly. An SVE2 variant: whilelt predicates, ld1w, indexed `fmla z.s[]`. An SME streaming-mode product path for M4: smstart, `fmopa za0.s`. Tests in Docker `--platform linux/riscv64` under qemu-user with `-cpu rv64,v=true,vlen=128..1024`, and qemu-aarch64 with `-cpu max,sve512=on`.
- **Demo:** MNIST trains on a roughly $100 Banana Pi BPI-F3 (SpacemiT K1, 8 cores, RVV 1.0, VLEN 256). In class, the same binary runs under QEMU at VLEN 128, 256, 512 and 1024 and prints identical results: one program that adapts to vector widths no current chip has.
- **Builds on:** kernel.py's vec_out/vec_red schedules, the mir.py allocator, aarch64.py (SVE shares its encoding and ABI), the ELF/Linux work
- **Risk:** Today's RVV boards have slow in-order cores, so absolute speed won't impress. LMUL × tile size is a large search space. QEMU timings mean nothing, so speed needs real boards. SME needs an M4; this machine is an M3.

### Anvil runs everywhere: a readable C++20 backend plus native Linux

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Add a `--target cpp` backend that writes any Anvil program as one readable, dependency-free C++20 file. Each kernel becomes a named loop nest headed by its index-notation math, register tiles become small local arrays, and the static arena becomes one aligned array. Alongside it, port the NEON backend to ELF so it runs natively on Linux AArch64. Anvil goes from Apple-only to Raspberry Pi, Graviton, Jetson, x86 Linux and Windows, and the generated C++ makes a good class handout on what a network actually computes.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/cpp.py, grown from cuda.py's KernelGen/CudaGen, which already emit CPU C++ under ANVIL_EMULATE: drop the launch macros; write the vec_out/vec_red tiles chosen by kernel.choose_configs as `float acc[4][16]` locals with `#pragma omp simd`; add a std::jthread chunking pool that mirrors runtime.s; take buffer offsets from backend/arena.py; keep source-span comments. anvil/target.py: target triples (aarch64-apple-darwin, aarch64-linux-gnu, x86_64-linux-gnu, cpp). An ELF mode in aarch64.py and runtime.s: `adrp x0, sym` + `:lo12:` instead of @PAGE/@PAGEOFF, no leading underscore, .type/.size, a futex or pthread pool (CLOCK_MONOTONIC is already in anvil_host.h). A GEMM fallback when Accelerate is missing, either Anvil's own vec_out kernels or OpenBLAS/ArmPL through blas.match. tests/test_cpp.py on the util.check_cuda pattern: every example's output must equal interp.py's. A Docker CI matrix: linux/arm64 runs natively on the M3, linux/amd64 through Rosetta.
- **Demo:** `anvil build --target aarch64-linux examples/transformer.anvil`, copy it to an $80 Raspberry Pi 5, and train the char-GPT there. The same day, on a $249 Jetson Orin Nano, the CUDA backend runs on a real NVIDIA GPU for the first time. In class, open mnist.cpp: about 300 lines of plain loops, each labeled with the Anvil line it came from.
- **Builds on:** cuda.py's ANVIL_EMULATE C++ path and anvil_host.h, aarch64.py + runtime.s, backend/arena.py, and the interp.py test oracle
- **Risk:** Auto-vectorized C++ will run 2-4x slower than the hand-scheduled NEON, so position it as the portable baseline, not the fast path. Linux has no Accelerate, so large matmuls lose the AMX path: about 90 GFLOP/s per core from NEON alone.

### An LLVM IR backend: every CPU, plus PTX and AMD GPU code without nvcc

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Emit textual LLVM IR, keeping Anvil's zero Python dependencies. Anvil knows exactly which buffers can alias (every buffer is its own static allocation), and that becomes noalias and alias-scope metadata that a C compiler could never prove; register tiles become explicit vector types. One backend then reaches x86, RISC-V, POWER and wasm, and through LLVM's NVPTX and AMDGPU targets it produces PTX and AMD GCN kernels with no CUDA toolkit. It also makes a good compiler-class experiment: Anvil's hand schedules against LLVM's auto-vectorizer on the same kernels.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/llvm.py: kernel IR becomes LLVM functions, either with `!llvm.loop` vectorize/unroll metadata or with explicit `<8 x float>` / `<vscale x 4 x float>` code following kernel.Config tiles. It uses llvm.fma, ports vecmath.py's polynomials into IR so there is no libm, and writes host control flow as IR calling anvil_host.h compiled by clang. GPU mode: kernels as NVPTX/AMDGPU kernel functions using the thread-id intrinsics (llvm.nvvm.read.ptx.sreg.tid.x, llvm.amdgcn.workitem.id.x); cuda.py's launch and split-reduction logic is reused on the host, which loads PTX with cuModuleLoadData or HIP. `anvil asm --via llvm` for side-by-side views. Needs Homebrew LLVM, because Apple clang has no NVPTX, AMDGPU or wasm targets.
- **Demo:** `anvil asm --via llvm examples/mnist.anvil` next to Anvil's own NEON for kernel k86. Expected result: LLVM vectorizes the j loop but does not build the 4×16 by-element-FMA register tile, and it measures several times slower, which makes a live lesson in register tiling. Then `--target nvptx64` writes a .ptx file that ptxas accepts for sm_90 without nvcc ever running.
- **Builds on:** ir.py, kernel.py's schedule configs, vecmath.py, cuda.py's host generation and reduction splitting
- **Risk:** Needs a full LLVM install (about 1 GB). LLVM's textual IR changes between versions. The PTX and AMDGPU output can only be checked for syntax here, not run, without hardware.

## compiler-research (16)

### MOONSHOT: Anvil-Mirage, a multi-level superoptimizer that rediscovers FlashAttention from three lines

*wow 10/10 · moonshot*

Mirage (OSDI 2025) showed that searching over kernel-, block- and thread-level graphs, with equivalence verified by random testing over finite fields, discovers fused GPU kernels that people otherwise write by hand. Build that for Anvil. Given naive attention in index notation, the search over fusions, tilings, on-chip placements and the e-graph's algebraic rewrites (including the online-softmax monoid) should land on FlashAttention-2 for Metal and CUDA. It should then find new fused kernels for gated MLPs, RMSNorm+matmul and LoRA without anyone writing a pattern.

- **Build:** anvil/mirage/: a µGraph representation at three levels (kernel graph over device memory, block graph over threadgroup memory with tile loops, thread graph over registers) built from the Tile IR operator set. Enumeration is pruned by abstract expressions, using the e-graph to reject candidates that can't be a subexpression of the target. Equivalence verification uses pure-Python modular arithmetic over Z_p, Mirage's second-field trick for exp, and float sampling for max. Candidates are ranked by measured time from the autotuner harness, and winners are emitted into metal.py and cuda.py.
- **Demo:** Start the search on `softmax(q @ k.T / sqrt(d) + mask) @ v`, watch a live search log, and after some minutes it prints a kernel with FlashAttention's structure (loop over K/V tiles, running max and sum in registers, rescaled accumulator). Then run it at a 32k context on the Apple GPU against PyTorch SDPA.
- **Builds on:** the e-graph, online-reduction fusion, the Tile IR, the autotuner's measurement harness, interp.py for float sampling
- **Risk:** Search-space explosion (Mirage needed heavy pruning and GPU-hours). max and select are not field homomorphisms, so verification there is only probabilistic. It depends on three or four other ideas landing first.

### Equality saturation over index notation: an e-graph optimizer for forward and backward passes

*wow 9/10 · months*

simplify.py is a set of greedy smart constructors, so it keeps whichever form it sees first. Anvil kernels are literal sum-product expressions over affine indices, which is exactly what SPORES-style relational equality saturation was designed for. An e-graph would hold every equivalent form of a training step at once (reordered matrix chains, sums pushed through products, cancelled transposes, factored intermediates) and extract the cheapest one using the cost model Anvil already has. It runs before and after autodiff, where the generated backward passes are full of redundancy.

- **Build:** anvil/egraph.py: union-find over hash-consed e-nodes with egg-style deferred rebuilding, an e-matching VM, a rule scheduler (egg's BackoffScheduler) to contain AC blowup, and DAG-aware extraction priced by cost.kernel_flops and cost.kernel_bytes. anvil/tensor_terms.py: a tensor-level term language (Σ_j, ×, +, map, broadcast) with α-normalized index binders so equivalent sums unify. It lifts kernels out of the IR between autodiff.py and optimize.py and lowers them back, and it treats views and gathers as opaque leaves. A `rewrite lhs => rhs if cond` declaration in parser.py and elaborate.py lets the prelude and users add rules (`rewrite log(softmax(x)) => log_softmax(x)`). Every rule is tagged `exact` (IEEE-preserving, e.g. commutativity) or `real` (associativity, distributivity), so -O0 equivalence tests and reproducible builds can restrict to the exact rules. `anvil ir --egraph` renders e-classes to HTML or Graphviz for teaching.
- **Demo:** Low-rank model: `loss = sum((U @ V.T) ** 2)` with U and V of shape 50000×32. Naively that is a 10 GB intermediate and about 160 GFLOP. The e-graph rewrites it to `sum((U.T @ U) * (V.T @ V))`, which is 32×32 and takes microseconds, and the gradient gets the same treatment. `anvil cost` shows the before and after numbers, and `anvil ir --egraph` shows the e-class where the two forms met. A second demo reassociates `A @ B @ x` to `A @ (B @ x)` automatically.
- **Builds on:** simplify.py smart constructors and deriv, optimize.py CSE and kernel_signature, cost.py FLOP and byte model, the Kernel/Reduction/Affine IR in ir.py
- **Risk:** AC rules on 1,376-kernel unrolled programs can explode, so saturation needs per-kernel neighborhoods and rule budgets. Converting views, gathers and scatter-adds to and from terms is lossy, so they stay opaque. `real` rewrites change rounding and conflict with today's bit-exact -O0 vs -O tests. Choosing the extraction cost so it doesn't fight fusion decisions made later is subtle.

### Online-reduction fusion: the compiler derives FlashAttention, Welford and one-pass logsumexp

*wow 9/10 · months*

Anvil's largest kernel gap against PyTorch (2.8× slower than SDPA) comes from attention materializing the t×t score matrix. Instead of hand-writing FlashAttention, teach the optimizer one algebraic fact. When a reduction over j depends on an earlier reduction over the same j only through a factor that is a monoid homomorphism (exp(x−m) = exp(x)·exp(−m), or (x−μ)² = x²−2μx+μ²), the two can be fused into one streaming pass whose state is a monoid with a rescaling combine. From the user's three lines of index-notation attention, the pass derives online softmax, FlashAttention's (m, ℓ, O) recurrence, Chan/Welford variance for layer_norm, and one-pass logsumexp for cross_entropy.

- **Build:** A new pass, anvil/online.py, in optimize.py's pipeline. It matches chains of Reduction kernels over the same reduction Vars where a later body loads an earlier result. A small factorizer (syntactic cases for exp/sub/mul/pow 2, or the e-graph) splits f(x_j, r) into g(x_j) ⊗ h(r), then derives combine, identity and finalize. ir.Reduction gets a `monoid` op with a tuple state. kernel.py lowers multi-accumulator reductions with a rescale once per j-block (the R×U tile holds O's accumulators plus m and ℓ per row). Metal and CUDA keep the K/V j-block in threadgroup or shared memory. Autodiff saves only (m, ℓ) as residuals and recomputes P blockwise in the backward pass (FlashAttention-2's backward), via a rule in autodiff.py for monoid reductions, or by running the same fusion on the backward chain dS = P ⊙ (dP − rowsum(dO ⊙ O)).
- **Demo:** examples/attention.anvil at a 16k context. Before, the 16×16k×16k scores need 16 GB and won't fit. After, memory is O(t·d) and it runs on the CPU and the Apple GPU. `anvil ir` prints the derived combine rule (m,ℓ,o)⊕(m',ℓ',o') = (M, ℓe^{m−M}+ℓ'e^{m'−M}, …), so the FlashAttention algorithm appears in the IR dump without anyone having written it. Benchmark against PyTorch SDPA on MPS.
- **Builds on:** Reduction kernels in ir.py, prelude softmax and layer_norm (already in the stable detach(max) form), vec_red row tiling in kernel.py, Metal and CUDA split reductions, autodiff.py's per-kernel backward
- **Risk:** The factorization only covers exp-shift and polynomial-centering dependencies, not arbitrary ones. Online rescaling changes rounding compared with two passes, so it must be a `real` rewrite. Register pressure at d=64 accumulators per row on NEON is a concern. Fusing the backward pass is the harder half, and doing it badly loses the memory win during training.

### Same bits everywhere: one training run, one SHA-256 on an M3 CPU, an Apple GPU and an NVIDIA GPU

*wow 9/10 · months*

Anvil is already bit-identical across thread counts, and every backend shares one counter-based RNG. Push that to the limit: write down the exact floating-point semantics of every kernel (reduction tree, FMA contraction, transcendental implementations, denormal mode), make all four backends implement it, and prove it by matching checkpoint hashes across hardware. No mainstream framework does this. PyTorch's deterministic mode is per device, and atomics, cuBLAS split-K and vendor libm all differ.

- **Build:** A `--reproducible` mode specified in docs/NUMERICS.md. Sums are sequential FMA over blocks of 32 along k, followed by a fixed pairwise tree whose lane decomposition is part of the spec, so NEON 4-lane accumulators, Metal SIMD-groups and CUDA warps can all realize it exactly. ir.Reduction gets an `order` attribute, and interp.py becomes the executable reference. kernel.py's register tiles assign lanes to match the spec, and cuda.py and metal.py split reductions conform to it. The atomicAdd scatter-adds in the GPU backends are replaced: autodiff.py emits the pull/gather form when the access is invertible, and sort-by-destination plus segmented reduction otherwise. One shared transcendental implementation (the vecmath.py polynomials, emitted as MSL and CUDA with explicit fma()) replaces libm, with fast-math off and FTZ pinned everywhere (FPCR.FZ on NEON). Anvil's own GEMM replaces Accelerate, MPS and cuBLAS in this mode. `anvil diff-bits a.ckpt b.ckpt` bisects per-kernel hashes to the first divergent kernel. CI trains MNIST on CPU, Metal and CUDA-emulate and compares the checkpoints byte for byte.
- **Demo:** On stage: `anvil run --cpu`, `--metal`, and `--cuda` on a rented H100 each print the same SHA-256 of the trained MNIST weights after 5 epochs. Then flip `--fast` and the hashes diverge, and `anvil diff-bits` names the first kernel where they split (softmax's row sum on the GPU).
- **Builds on:** the shared counter-based RNG (runtime.s, anvil_cuda.h, MSL prelude), thread-count determinism, interp.py as the semantics, the CUDA emulation path, vecmath.py
- **Risk:** A fixed reduction order with no split-K and no atomics may cost 1.2–2× on some GPU kernels. Metal's compiler may contract or reorder despite the flags, which needs adversarial tests. GPU denormal behavior has to be pinned and verified. Dropping AMX and MPS GEMMs in this mode loses Anvil's fastest matmuls. The author has no NVIDIA GPU to validate the final hop.

### Derivatives of derivatives: grad∘grad, jvp, Hessian-vector products and Taylor mode, all compiled and fused

*wow 8/10 · months*

Today autodiff.py stops with "differentiating through a gradient (higher-order derivatives) is not supported yet". Anvil's backward pass is just more kernels, so closing that loop turns autodiff into a true function transformation on kernel IR. That covers reverse-over-reverse, forward mode, forward-over-reverse HVPs, and Taylor-mode jets for k-th derivatives in one pass, all fused into the same vectorized loops. It unlocks physics-informed networks, MAML, WGAN-GP gradient penalties, Newton-CG and hypergradient learning rates.

- **Build:** autodiff.py: make gradient buffers first-class SSA values by lifting the accumulate-into-grad restriction. Differentiating a scatter-add G[f(i)] += v[i] gives the gather dv[i] = dG[f(i)], and mutation-hazard checks extend to grad buffers and reuse_dying_inputs aliases. A new anvil/forward.py emits one tangent kernel per primal kernel over the same domain, so optimize.py fuses it horizontally for free. New builtins in builtins.py: `jvp(f, x, v)`, `hvp(loss, w, v)`, `laplacian(u, x)`, `jet(f, x, order=k)`. Taylor-mode coefficient recurrences per Unary op go in simplify.py (exp: c_k = (1/k)Σ j·a_j·c_{k−j}; log, tanh and sigmoid via the same ODE trick). CSE or the e-graph controls expression swell. `minimize loss over lr` gives hypergradient descent by differentiating through the optimizer step that minimize inlines.
- **Demo:** examples/pinn.anvil: a physics-informed network for the 2-D heat equation (or Burgers'). The loss is `mean((u_t - nu * laplacian(u, xy)) ** 2)`, a second derivative of the network with respect to its inputs. It trains in seconds on the CPU, and `show` animates the solution field in ASCII. A side-by-side with JAX's nested jacfwd(jacrev) shows Anvil's 2nd-order kernels fused into the forward GEMM epilogues. Second demo: MAML on sinusoid regression.
- **Builds on:** autodiff.py Backprop and the injective/covers rules, simplify.deriv, the grad builtin (input gradients in the dream example), minimize/optimizer inlining
- **Risk:** Grad-of-grad through scatter kernels and in-place-reused buffers is where subtle wrong-gradient bugs hide, so it needs gradcheck at second order. Expression swell will hurt compile time for unrolled programs, which are already 3.3 s for the char-RNN. Taylor mode through max, argmax and select is non-smooth, so the semantics need defining. JAX already does higher-order AD, so the novelty is the compilation and fusion, not the math.

### `under memory 64 MB`: automatic rematerialization plus provably optimal memory planning

*wow 8/10 · months*

Before anything runs, Anvil knows every tensor's size, every kernel's FLOPs and the order of the whole training step. Those are exactly the inputs Checkmate needed a MILP for. Make memory a constraint in the language and let the compiler solve, jointly, which activations to keep and which to recompute, the kernel order, and the arena placement. It then prints a certificate (arena = max-live lower bound) or the optimality gap. For loops with autodiff, Griewank's Revolve gives provably optimal binomial checkpointing: backprop through T steps in O(log T) memory.

- **Build:** Syntax: `minimize loss with adam() under memory 64 MB` in parser.py and elaborate.py. anvil/remat.py runs after autodiff on the graph of forward kernels whose outputs backward kernels read, and decides per tensor: keep, recompute from its inputs, or recompute a segment. Recomputation clones forward kernels into the backward region, and optimize.py then often fuses them into a GEMM epilogue at almost no cost. The RNG is counter-based, so recomputed dropout and samples replay identical numbers, provided the replay reuses the original stream counter. Solvers: Revolve or Chen √n DP for chains; a dependency-free anvil/solve/ilp.py (dual simplex plus branch-and-bound over fractions) for graphs with a few hundred binaries; and a DTR/MONeT-style greedy fallback by FLOPs-per-byte-freed. arena.py's first-fit-largest-first becomes an exact DSA/strip-packing search with max-live pruning (TelaMalloc-style), with Serenity-style DP over kernel orderings to minimize peak. The same planner produces a Metal MTLHeap arena and a single cudaMalloc arena, which closes the 'no GPU memory arena' gap. `anvil cost --memory` prints the time/memory Pareto frontier.
- **Demo:** The small GPT: sweep `--set MEM=…` and the compiler draws the memory-vs-step-time Pareto curve statically, then each point is confirmed by running it. The GPT trains with a 4× longer context in the same memory at about +25% time. Also, `anvil build` on every example prints "arena 47 KB: optimal (= lower bound)".
- **Builds on:** arena.py lifetimes and placement, cost.py per-kernel FLOPs and bytes, autodiff.py's region model, the counter-based RNG, Metal and CUDA host code
- **Risk:** A pure-Python ILP may be too slow on unrolled programs, so decompose by loop structure. Recomputation interacts with in-place reuse (reuse_dying_inputs) and write-back coalescing. Revolve inside run-time loops depends on the separate 'autodiff through run-time loops' tape work. Exact placement with GPU alignment rules is fiddly.

### `anvil tune`: an autotuner whose learned cost model is an Anvil program, compiled by Anvil

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Today choose_configs() in kernel.py picks schedules with hand-set heuristics (`prefer_red`, `big_u = 4 if size <= 40`), and blas.py uses a fixed size threshold. Replace guesses with measurement and learning, in the style of TVM and Ansor, except that the cost model is a neural network written in Anvil, compiled by Anvil and called from the compiler through anvil.function. The compiler trains a network to compile better, and that network's own kernels are tuned by it: a self-hosting fixed point.

- **Build:** anvil/tune/space.py: per-kernel choice points, namely Config(mode, U, R, hoist, parallel grain), thread count, BLAS vs NEON, loop order, inline/fuse decisions from optimize.try_inline and try_fuse, and the GPU threadgroup and split_factor. anvil/tune/measure.py: builds each kernel alone into a benchmark dylib, reusing aarch64.py's --profile instrumentation, and times it with warmup, interleaving and medians to survive a loaded laptop. anvil/tune/features.py: extents, per-access strides, the FLOP/byte ratio from cost.py, register pressure from mir.py live ranges, and an op histogram. anvil/tune/costmodel.anvil: an MLP or a small attention-over-accesses model trained with a pairwise ranking loss (as in TLP and Ansor) via `minimize`. Search is evolutionary and model-guided, and measurements land in ~/.cache/anvil/tune.db keyed by optimize.kernel_signature. PGO: `anvil run --pgo` records per-kernel times, while-loop trip counts and branch frequencies, and `anvil build --schedule tuned.json` consumes them.
- **Demo:** Tuned against heuristic speedups across the nine vs-PyTorch projects, with a live terminal scatter of predicted vs measured time tightening as the model trains. The tuner independently rediscovers the README's hand-found row-tiled `vec_red 4×4` win for `dz @ Wᵀ`. Punchline: the cost model's own training kernels were picked by the cost model.
- **Builds on:** kernel.py choose_configs and Config, aarch64.py --profile, cost.py, anvil.function, blas.py threshold, optimize.kernel_signature, --set sweeps
- **Risk:** Timing noise under background load (the report saw load averages of 8–14). Kernels timed in isolation miss cross-kernel cache effects. Measurement data is small, so the model can overfit. Gains on GEMM-dominated programs may be modest (10–30%) because the AMX does the heavy lifting.

### A tile-level IR between kernels and GPUs: tensor cores, simdgroup matrices, async copies, software pipelining

*wow 8/10 · months*

Both GPU backends map one thread to one output element. That is why MPS beats Anvil 5× on convolutions, and it is why the CUDA backend would leave an H100's tensor cores idle. Add a Triton-like middle level where a kernel is a grid of tiles that stage blocks in shared or threadgroup memory, multiply them with matrix instructions, and pipeline the next load behind the current math. One tile IR would have three lowerings, and fused epilogues (bias, activation, even the Adam update) stay fused, which cuBLAS and MPS cannot do.

- **Build:** anvil/tile/ir.py: blocked layouts, TileLoad and TileStore (async plus barriers), Dot (MMA), elementwise and reduce on tiles. anvil/tile/lower.py lowers from kernel IR, reusing blas.product's GEMM matcher (it already finds stacks and sums of products), and does implicit-GEMM convolution that gathers patch tiles straight from x, so there is no im2col buffer. anvil/tile/pipeline.py: modulo-scheduled software pipelining (2–4 stages) and swizzled shared-memory layouts against bank conflicts. Lowerings: cuda.py to mma.sync (Ampere), WGMMA plus TMA (Hopper) and cp.async; metal.py to simdgroup_matrix 8×8 MMA plus threadgroup memory; kernel.py's NEON register tiles as the CPU instance, with SME outer-product ZA tiles on M4. Tile shapes and stage counts come from the autotuner.
- **Demo:** The EMNIST CNN on Metal goes from 5× slower than MPS to parity or better, because the bias, ReLU and optimizer-update epilogues ride inside the GEMM tiles. A lecture slide shows the same tile program printed three ways side by side: MSL simdgroup_matrix, CUDA mma.sync, and NEON fmla, illustrating one algorithm across three memory hierarchies.
- **Builds on:** cuda.py KernelGen/CudaGen, metal.py (MPS products, argument buffers), blas.product matcher, kernel.py vec_out register tiling
