- **Risk:** The CUDA path still can't be performance-validated without hardware. Tensor-core details (WGMMA descriptors, TMA tensor maps) are deep and fast-moving. simdgroup_matrix performance is poorly documented. Scope creep is a real danger.

### Sparse tensors as a type: TACO-style formats with sparse autodiff

*wow 8/10 · months*

Anvil's index notation is the same input language TACO compiles. Add per-dimension storage formats to tensor types (`f32[n, m] @ csr`, `@ coo`, `@ bsr(4,4)`, `@ 2:4`) and generate co-iteration code from merge lattices, so `y[i] = sum A[i, j] * x[j]` becomes SpMV when A is CSR. The twist no sparse compiler offers is autodiff over formats. The gradient of SpMM with respect to A's values automatically becomes an SDDMM evaluated only at A's nonzeros.

- **Build:** ast, parser, elaborate and values.TVal gain a format tuple of level types (dense, compressed, singleton), and a sparse tensor becomes pos/crd/vals buffers. nnz stays static, because it comes from the .mtx header at compile time, the same way npy headers are parsed today, so the arena and cost.py keep working. anvil/sparse/lattice.py builds merge lattices (Kjolstad et al. OOPSLA 2017) for sum/product co-iteration. Compressed levels lower to loops with data-dependent bounds from pos[], using the existing Gather/ScalarRef machinery in ir.Affine. There is a workspace transformation for sparse outputs (CGO 2019). autodiff.py gets a rule that a load of vals[p] is differentiable only at p, so the backward kernel iterates the same pattern. Builtins: `prune(W, 0.9)` and `csr(...)`, plus a Matrix Market loader in the runtime. kernel.py adds row-panel SpMM with the dense operand in NEON registers.
- **Demo:** A two-layer graph convolutional network on Cora (2,708 papers) in about 15 lines: `h[i, f] = relu(sum A[i, j] * (X @ W)[j, f])` with A in CSR. It trains to about 81% test accuracy in under a second. Second demo: prune the MNIST MLP to 95% sparsity and keep training with `W: f32[784, 128] @ csr`, while `anvil cost` shows FLOPs scaling with nnz and the SDDMM gradient touching only nonzeros.
- **Builds on:** index notation with `where`, Gather and ScalarRef in ir.Affine, scatter-add autodiff, compile-time header parsing for npy, the data loaders
- **Risk:** Data-dependent loop bounds cut against the static-extent assumptions in tiling, threading by weighted work, and cost estimates. Sparse NEON code is hard to make beat dense AMX below about 90% sparsity. Patterns that change during training (dynamic pruning) break static nnz unless capacity bounds go into the type.

### The compiler that fixes your NaN: Herbie-style numerical-stability rewriting

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

`--check` already stops at the first NaN and names the line. Go one step further: take the inputs that blew up, re-evaluate the expression at 60 digits with Python's dependency-free `decimal` module, localize which subexpression loses the bits, and search for an equivalent expression with lower error. The compiler then proposes the fix as a source diff (or applies it under `--stable`), for example log(1+exp(x)) → softplus, log Σ exp → max-shifted logsumexp, or 1−cos x → 2 sin²(x/2).

- **Build:** anvil/accuracy.py: a per-expression ulp-error estimator that evaluates kernel bodies on sampled f32 inputs (bit patterns via struct) and with a decimal oracle, then applies Herbie's local-error heuristic to pick rewrite sites. A stability rule set (rules/stability.anvil, about 60 Herbie-derived identities plus regime splits via select). New primitives log1p, expm1 and erf in ir.py, with deriv rules in simplify.py and NEON code in vecmath.py (an expm1 path already exists for tanh). Extraction minimizes (error, then FLOPs), on the e-graph if it exists or on a small local rewriter if not. diagnostics.py renders the suggestion, and ide.py exposes it as a code action (lightbulb) in the VS Code extension. Reductions are sampled row-wise so sum-of-exp overflow is caught.
- **Demo:** Students write the textbook sigmoid cross-entropy `-(y*log(sigmoid(z)) + (1-y)*log(1-sigmoid(z)))` and the VAE KL term, and training hits NaN at step 840. `anvil run --check` reports: "loses 31 bits for z > 17; stable equivalent: `max(z,0) - z*y + log1p(exp(-abs(z)))`, apply? [y]". With `--stable` the run trains to completion, matching the original wherever the original was finite. The same suggestion pops up live in the editor.
- **Builds on:** --check NaN tracing to source line, simplify.py, vecmath.py expm1 path, diagnostics.py, ide.py and the VS Code extension
- **Risk:** Error that matters often comes from across reductions and many training steps, not one expression. Regime splits can slow hot loops. 'Improvements' that only hold on sampled inputs need wording that admits it. It is best built after the e-graph, though a standalone version is possible.

### MOONSHOT: Anvil in Lean 4, a mechanized semantics and a machine-checked proof that its autodiff is right

*wow 8/10 · moonshot*

Give the kernel IR a formal semantics in Lean 4 (tensors as functions on bounded index tuples, kernels as sums over boxes with affine accesses) and prove the core theorems. The two rules autodiff.py implements (reduction when the access is injective, scatter otherwise) compute the adjoint of the forward linearization. Fusion and loop collapsing preserve meaning, and every rule in the rewrite library is sound over ℝ. Then connect the proofs to the Python compiler through translation validation, so a specific compiled MNIST step comes with a theorem. This is where the user's type-systems and FP interests meet the ML compiler, and it could carry a course on its own.

- **Build:** A formal/ Lean 4 project on Mathlib (Finset.sum, HasFDerivAt) with definitions mirroring ir.Affine, Kernel, Reduction and Store. Theorems: backward_kernel_correct (both the injective/covers case and the scatter case), fuse_correct, collapse_correct, and auto-generated rule_sound statements, most closed by ring or simp. `anvil ir --lean` exports a program's IR as a Lean term. formal/Validate.lean checks a compilation's before and after IR with a proven-sound normalizer for the affine fragment. Floating point is explicitly out of scope and handled by the reproducibility and exactness-tag work.
- **Demo:** `anvil build --lean-certify examples/mnist.anvil` emits a Lean file containing `theorem mnist_step_gradient_correct`, which type-checks: the gradient Anvil compiled for that exact training step is the true derivative over ℝ. Students then break sigmoid's derivative in simplify.py and Lean rejects the certificate, showing the failing goal.
- **Builds on:** the kernel IR's small uniform form, autodiff.py's two backward rules and their side conditions, simplify.deriv's rule table, the translation-validation work
- **Risk:** Real-number proofs say nothing about floating point, and the framing must make that clear. Mathlib calculus is heavy going. Keeping the Lean model in sync with an evolving Python IR is costly. It is a very large time investment for a single author.

### Mixed precision chosen by the compiler from its own gradients (f16, bf16, fp8 per tensor)

*wow 7/10 · months*

Half precision doubles NEON throughput (fmla .8h) and halves memory traffic, but where is it safe? The ADAPT result from algorithmic differentiation says rounding a tensor t perturbs the loss by about Σ|∂loss/∂t|·ulp(t)/2, and Anvil can compute ∂loss/∂t for every intermediate with its own `grad`. Combine that with a profiling run of dynamic ranges, and the compiler assigns each tensor the cheapest format that fits a user error budget, inserts loss scaling where gradients underflow, and explains every choice per source line.

- **Build:** F16, BF16 and FP8 (E4M3/E5M2) dtypes in ir.py, with a split between storage and compute precision and casts in simplify.cast. Buffer.nbytes (hard-coded 4*numel) and the arena become dtype-aware. anvil/precision.py has three parts: (1) an instrumented build reusing --check's per-kernel tensor scan in the runtime to record ranges, percentiles and zero fractions; (2) a sensitivity pass that adds an adjoint for every intermediate (one full grad run) and accumulates |adjoint|·ulp; (3) a knapsack that minimizes bytes and cycles under the budget. kernel.py adds 8-lane f16 vec_out schedules (fcvtl/fcvtn at boundaries) and bfdot/bfmmla on M2 and later. vecmath gets f16 polynomials, Metal gets `half`, and CUDA gets bf16/fp8 types. Dynamic loss scaling is a prelude optimizer wrapper. `anvil precision file.anvil --budget 1e-3` prints the map in `anvil cost` style.
- **Demo:** `anvil precision examples/transformer.anvil` colors each source line by chosen format, with reasons ("layer_norm variance stays f32: sensitivity 3e-2"). The transformer then trains about 1.7× faster to the same loss within seed noise. Turning the budget knob live degrades the loss curve predictably, which makes a floating-point lecture you can watch.
- **Builds on:** --check runtime tensor scanning, grad of any intermediate (dream example), cost.py per-line reports, kernel.py vec_out schedules, Metal and CUDA generators
- **Risk:** First-order error estimates underestimate error compounded over thousands of steps, so validate by training. Matmul gains may not materialize on the CPU, since Accelerate/AMX f16 paths aren't exposed the same way. The 4-bytes-per-element assumption is woven through the whole codebase.

### Polyhedral scheduling across kernels: overlapped tiling, fusion and wavefronts

*wow 7/10 · months*

Anvil kernels are already polyhedral objects (box domains, affine accesses in ir.Affine, static extents), but fusion is pairwise and only when domains line up. A Pluto-style affine scheduler over whole blocks would find cross-kernel tilings with overlapping halos (conv→relu→pool→conv computed tile by tile in L1 or threadgroup memory), interchange for locality, and loop skewing for wavefront parallelism. For example, layer 2 of a GRU at step t can run while layer 1 does step t+1. Every dependence is proven exactly.

- **Build:** anvil/poly/: dependence polyhedra from Store/Load pairs between kernels, decided by a pure-Python Fourier–Motzkin and Omega test (tractable, since the constraints come from boxes and small coefficients). Pluto's lexicographic ILP is linearized with Farkas' lemma and solved by the in-house ILP solver. A schedule-tree representation (isl-like) sits between optimize.py and the backends. ir.Kernel extends to a nested form (one tiled loop nest containing a sequence of sub-kernels), lowered by kernel.py and by the GPU generators, which map outer tiles to threadgroups and keep intermediates in threadgroup memory. Gathers and runtime offsets are fenced off as non-affine. Tile sizes come from cost.py or the autotuner. `anvil poly --svg` draws iteration spaces, dependences and the chosen tiling.
- **Demo:** The EMNIST CNN on Metal: conv→relu→pool fused into one tiled kernel whose intermediate never reaches device memory, with `anvil cost` showing DRAM traffic dropping several-fold. `anvil poly --svg` generates the classic polyhedral lecture figure from the student's own program.
- **Builds on:** ir.Affine, Var and Kernel, optimize.py fuse/merge and collapse_loops, arena.py lifetimes, cost.py bytes model
- **Risk:** Pluto's objective can choose poor tilings for GEMM-like kernels, which are better left to AMX and MPS. Multi-statement fused kernels are a large refactor of kernel.py's register-tiled lowering. CPU gains may be small. It overlaps the Tile IR idea, so choose an order.

### Superoptimized, correctly rounded math on NEON, Metal and CUDA, verified on all 4 billion floats

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

vecmath.py's exp is a degree-7 Taylor polynomial: accurate, but not minimal, not correctly rounded, and different from Metal's and CUDA's libm. A search over range reductions, polynomial degree and f32 coefficients can find the cheapest NEON sequence. RLIBM's LP formulation turns each input's rounding interval into a constraint, which makes the result correctly rounded. Exhaustive testing over all 2^32 inputs, which takes seconds using Anvil's own threaded kernels, then turns 'tested' into 'proved' for unary functions. STOKE-style MCMC extends the search to tiny fused epilogues such as gelu, silu and sigmoid'.

- **Build:** anvil/superopt/remez.py: Remez exchange plus fpminimax-style coefficient rounding against a 100-digit `decimal` oracle. anvil/superopt/rlibm.py: per-input rounding intervals, reduced polynomial LPs via an exact simplex with Clarkson sampling. anvil/superopt/stoke.py: MCMC over mir.py instruction sequences (fmla, fmul, frintn, fcvtzs, shl…) with an M3 latency/throughput cost and a test-vector correctness term. verify.anvil: enumerates every f32 bit pattern with i32 index math and compares bit-exactly. Emitters for MSL and CUDA use explicit fma(), so all backends share each function bit for bit (this feeds the reproducibility idea). New fused primitives: expm1, log1p, erf (exact gelu). `--math=fast|cr` selects between the 1-ulp and correctly rounded versions.
- **Demo:** Live: "searching… exp: 9 instructions (was 14), correctly rounded, verified on 4,294,967,296 inputs in 6 s on 12 cores". Softmax and gelu benchmarks speed up further, and a table shows libm and PyTorch's exp disagreeing with the correctly rounded value on N inputs.
- **Builds on:** vecmath.py inline NEON math, mir.py instructions and register allocator, kernel.py inline lowering, the threaded runtime
- **Risk:** Correctly rounded variants are often slower than 1-ulp ones, which is why both are offered. Low degrees may be infeasible for the LP. MCMC over long sequences explodes. Exhaustive proof only covers unary functions, so pow and atan2 need sampling or interval arguments.

### anvil.jit: millisecond compiles into executable memory, with tiered and value specialization

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Every new anvil.function signature shells out to `cc` to assemble and link a dylib, which takes hundreds of milliseconds, and batch-1 calls pay that latency. Write an encoder for the roughly 120 AArch64/NEON instructions the backend emits and place code directly into MAP_JIT memory, using pthread_jit_write_protect_np and sys_icache_invalidate (Apple silicon's W^X dance). Then go further: tiered compilation, like HotSpot or V8, and value specialization, a concrete instance of the first Futamura projection, where a trained network specialized to its weights becomes straight-line code.

- **Build:** anvil/jit/encode.py: instruction encodings plus label and literal-pool resolution for what mir.render produces, tested byte for byte against the system assembler on every example's .s. anvil/jit/loader.py: ctypes mmap(MAP_JIT), a separate data segment for buffers, runtime.s prebuilt once as a dylib and bound by dlsym, and Accelerate symbols resolved at load. anvil/jit/tier.py: call counters, interpreter → quick native → autotuned native, and guard-based specialization on a symbolic batch size (compile for n, guard, recompile on miss). anvil/specialize.py extends export.py's baked weights: zero terms vanish, immediates via kernel.py's _fmov_encodable, and fully unrolled straight-line code for tiny models. repl.py runs each input natively instead of re-running the session on the interpreter.
- **Demo:** The drawing app's reader: first-call compile drops from about 300 ms to about 5 ms, and steady-state calls get faster. The 'weights as code' moment: the checkers critic, pruned and quantized, compiles into a few-KB straight-line function with no weight loads at all, and `anvil asm --specialized` shows the network literally as instructions.
- **Builds on:** mir.py render, aarch64.py, runtime.s, pyapi.py signature cache, export.py baked constants, repl.py
- **Risk:** Encoder bugs are silent miscompiles, hence the byte-for-byte diff tests. runtime.s must be made loadable separately. MAP_JIT needs the allow-jit entitlement under a hardened runtime (fine for typical dev Pythons). Value specialization only pays off for tiny or pruned models.

### Proof-carrying optimizations: verified rules, translation validation and adjoint certificates

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Today the optimizer is checked by differential testing: -O0 and -O must print identical float64 output under the interpreter. That is strong, but only covers the test programs. Make every compilation check itself. Each rewrite rule is verified once, Alive-style. Each pass run emits a certificate that a small independent checker validates. Each generated backward kernel passes the inverse-problems community's dot-product (adjoint) test. The compiler can then state exactly what it guarantees, and the trusted base shrinks to about 1,000 lines.

- **Build:** anvil/verify/rules.py: real-number soundness by polynomial identity testing over a large prime field (Schwartz–Zippel) for the polynomial fragment, decimal-oracle sampling for transcendental rules, and exhaustive f32/f16 checks for rules tagged `exact`. anvil/verify/tv.py: per-pass translation validation, comparing each output buffer's symbolic per-element expression before and after fusion, inlining, CSE and store forwarding (hash-consed DAGs, affine offsets normalized with ir.decompose). It also checks collapse_loops' affine bijection and that arena.py never overlaps simultaneously live buffers. anvil/verify/adjoint.py: for each forward kernel and its backward kernels, ⟨Jv, w⟩ = ⟨v, Jᵀw⟩ in float64 via interp.py on random vectors. `anvil build --certify` prints a summary. A mutation-testing harness breaks optimize.py rules on purpose.
- **Demo:** In class, re-introduce a historical bug from PLAN.md's Fixed list (fusing a fill with a later read when a loop in between assigns into the buffer) and `--certify` refuses: "fusion of k41 into k57 changes out[i]; counterexample i=3". Then show it also catches a deliberately wrong derivative rule through the adjoint test.
- **Builds on:** interp.py float64 reference semantics, the -O0 vs -O equivalence tests, simplify.py rules, autodiff.py's injective/covers conditions, ir.decompose
- **Risk:** Translation validation across loops, control flow and in-place reuse can raise false alarms or be incomplete. Symbolic per-element comparison may blow up on unrolled programs. The adjoint and finite-field tests are probabilistic, so state the confidence.

## ml-capabilities (16)

### anvil chat: real pretrained LLMs (GPT-2, then Llama/Qwen GGUF) at llama.cpp speed

*wow 10/10 · months*

Load GPT-2, Llama-3.2 or Qwen2.5 straight from Hugging Face safetensors or llama.cpp GGUF files, and generate text from a model written as about 120 lines of index notation. Today the compiler beats PyTorch 6.5x on small training jobs. This would make it a local LLM runtime that competes with llama.cpp on Apple silicon, llama.cpp's home turf, with the whole architecture readable on one slide.

- **Build:** (a) builtins.py: `safetensors(path)` and `gguf(path)` parse the file header at compile time, the way `npy` already does. They return a compile-time record (a new RecordVal in values.py) of typed tensors plus config constants (n_layer, n_head, rope_theta), so shapes and loop counts come from the file. (b) runtime.s / anvil_host.h: a new `anvil_rt_mmap` routine maps the weights read-only into the existing `ir.indirect` pointer slots: no copy, instant start. Where the file places `blk.{l}.attn_q` at an offset affine in l (GGUF usually does), the loader proves that at compile time. `W[l]` then becomes an ordinary view with a ScalarRef offset, so a run-time `for l in range(L)` compiles ONE layer's kernels instead of unrolling 28 layers. (c) ir.Var gains bounded run-time extents (`pos+1 <= 4096`), so decode attention loops only over the live KV cache. kernel.py, metal.py and cuda.py emit these as run-time trip counts. (d) optimize.py: an online-softmax fusion that rewrites `softmax(s) @ v` into one FlashAttention-2-style kernel (a new ir.Reduction op carrying the running max, sum and accumulator), plus split-K flash-decoding for batch 1. (e) prelude: rms_norm, rope, swiglu, grouped-query attention, top-p/min-p sampling; examples/llm/{gpt2,llama,qwen}.anvil. (f) BPE: the prompt is tokenized at compile time inside the compiler, and output is decoded through the run-time-indexed string tables Anvil already has. A run-time BPE routine handles interactive chat.
- **Demo:** Weeks milestone: `bin/anvil run examples/llm/gpt2.anvil --set PROMPT="..."` generates text with OpenAI's real 124M weights. This works with today's language plus the loader, using a masked static KV cache. Flagship: `bin/anvil chat Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct-Q8_0.gguf` streams tokens in the terminal next to `llama-bench`. Before the run, `anvil cost` predicts tokens/s (bytes per token over 400 GB/s), with a target of landing within 15%.
- **Builds on:** npy's compile-time header parsing, ir.indirect pointer slots, ScalarRef run-time offsets in ir.Affine, run-time-indexed string tables, examples/transformer.anvil, the Metal backend + MPS, anvil cost
- **Risk:** Decode is bound by memory bandwidth, and llama.cpp's NEON and Metal kernels are hand-tuned. Matching them needs quantized matvecs (next idea) and simdgroup reductions. Tokenizer pre-tokenization regexes (Llama 3, Qwen) are fiddly. f32 GPT-2 is easy; the months go into quantized Llama.

### Text-to-image on the laptop: SD-Turbo in Anvil, one step, under a second

*wow 10/10 · months*

Load Stable Diffusion Turbo (Adversarial Diffusion Distillation, Sauer et al. 2023) from safetensors and generate 512x512 images in a single denoising step on the Apple GPU, with the text encoder, U-Net and VAE all written in Anvil. Along the way it closes Anvil's biggest benchmark gap, because it forces fast GPU convolutions.

- **Build:** metal.py: implicit-GEMM convolution kernels using simdgroup_matrix<half>, with no materialized im2col, fixing the 5x gap to MPS on the EMNIST CNN. f16 storage for the 865M-parameter U-Net (from the precision idea). Prelude additions: GroupNorm, nearest/bilinear upsampling in index notation, cross-attention, timestep embeddings, ResBlock and Transformer2D. The OpenCLIP ViT-H text encoder reuses the transformer from anvil chat with CLIP BPE, the prompt tokenized at compile time. A VAE decoder. Samplers written in Anvil: DDIM, DPM-Solver++ 2M, Euler-ancestral, and 1-step ADD. A `save_png` runtime routine (zlib is already linked for idx.gz). Compile-time kernel deduplication by structural hash, so hundreds of identical blocks don't blow up MSL compile time (35 s for 1,376 kernels today). Training side: a rectified-flow (Lipman et al. 2022) DiT on CIFAR-10 or CelebA-64 trained overnight, scored with FID.
- **Demo:** `bin/imagine "a watercolor blacksmith's anvil on a mountain"` writes a 512x512 PNG in under a second while the class shouts prompts. A slide shows that the whole U-Net is about 150 lines of Anvil.
- **Builds on:** examples/diffusion.anvil (DDPM, classifier-free guidance), prelude Conv2d, the Metal backend + MPS, safetensors loading from anvil chat, f16 from the precision idea
- **Risk:** Metal convolution performance and f16 numerics (VAE decoders are known to overflow in f16). Compile time and memory for a roughly 1B-parameter graph. OpenCLIP tokenizer details.

### Anvil writes Anvil: QLoRA fine-tuning plus type-checked decoding

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

Fine-tune a small code LLM inside Anvil, with QLoRA, on Anvil's own examples and prelude. Then sample from it with the Anvil compiler in the decoding loop, so every suggestion parses, shape-checks and compiles by construction. The language teaches a model to write it, and the type system makes sure the model cannot produce a shape error.

- **Build:** (a) `lora(net, rank=16, targets=["q", "v"])`: a compile-time model transformer in elaborate.py that rewrites ModelInstVal forwards to `x @ W + s * (x @ A) @ B`, with the base weights frozen in nf4/q4 (previous idea), trained by `minimize loss over adapters(net)`. (b) autodiff.py: `checkpoint` regions that recompute a block's forward inside the backward, and backward GEMMs that dequantize frozen weights in registers, so no full-precision copy is ever stored. arena.py then reports exact peak training memory at compile time. (c) Data: instruction pairs synthesized from examples/*.anvil, prelude.anvil, the tests and README snippets, plus compiler-verified augmentations (mutated programs whose error messages become targets). (d) Constrained decoding: a grammar mask derived from parser.py's state at every token (as in llama.cpp GBNF or XGrammar). At every newline, ide.py's checker elaborates the partial program; a line that fails is masked and resampled (beam search with rollback). This is the 'type-constrained decoding' of Muendler et al. (PLDI 2025), applied to a tensor language. (e) editors/vscode/extension.js gets completions from the local model.
- **Demo:** In VS Code or Cursor, type `# 3x3 conv, stride 2, then max-pool`, and a local Qwen2.5-Coder-0.5B with a LoRA adapter, running in Anvil, completes the index notation with shape inlay hints already showing. A live counter reads 'compiles: 97% (base model: 12%)' (target numbers, not measured). Then ask anvil chat to explain a compile error.
- **Builds on:** anvil chat and quantized types (ideas above), ModelInstVal and `minimize ... over`, the DCE that already drops gradients of untrained params, arena.py static memory, ide.py `check --json`, the VS Code extension
- **Risk:** Elaborating once per line is affordable; once per token is not. A 0.5B model may still write programs that check but are wrong. About 30 example programs is very little data, so the quality of the synthetic data decides everything.

### Differentiable rendering: TinyNeRF, then Instant-NGP, then 3D Gaussian Splatting

*wow 9/10 · months*

A renderer whose gradients come for free: volume rendering and Gaussian-splat rasterization written in index notation, trained on the Apple GPU and viewed in a browser. 3D Gaussian Splatting (Kerbl et al., SIGGRAPH 2023) is one of the most visible graphics techniques of recent years, and showing a class that the whole renderer, backward pass included, is about 200 lines makes a strong demo.

- **Build:** Weeks milestone: TinyNeRF with positional encoding, an 8-layer MLP, stratified `~ uniform` samples, and alpha compositing via an exclusive cumulative product (the scan idea, or `prod ... where s < t` today). Months: i32 bitwise ops in ops.py/lexer.py (xor, shifts, and), which Anvil lacks today, for Instant-NGP's spatial hash (Mueller et al. 2022), plus a hashed-grid gather whose gradients scatter-add into the table. 3DGS: projection with the EWA Jacobian and degree-3 spherical harmonics in index notation. A `sort_by_key` primitive (radix sort in runtime.s, onesweep on Metal) for (tile, depth) keys. Per-16x16-tile front-to-back compositing with early termination as a new metal.py schedule, and a backward that recomputes transmittance, as gsplat does. Densify, split and prune on a static-capacity Gaussian pool with an `alive` mask, which keeps shapes static. `save_ply` for viewers, and a bin/splat page with a WebGL splat viewer, built like bin/draw.
- **Demo:** `bin/splat data/nerf_synthetic/lego` trains for a few minutes on the M3 Max GPU to 30+ dB PSNR, then you orbit the Lego bulldozer in the browser at 60 fps. Earlier in the lecture, TinyNeRF renders a turntable in the terminal using truecolor half-block characters.
- **Builds on:** index notation with gathers and differentiable scatter-add, `~ uniform` sampling, the Metal backend, bin/draw's browser server, anvil.function
- **Risk:** Sorting and uneven per-tile work don't fit the one-thread-per-output kernel model. Backward atomics contend heavily. Matching gsplat's CUDA kernels on Metal is hard. Real captures need COLMAP; the synthetic NeRF data avoids it.

### Neural Snake: a world model you can play

*wow 9/10 · weeks*

The compiled Snake environment generates millions of frames, and a network learns the game's dynamics. Then students play Snake where there is no game code at all: every frame is dreamed by the model, as in GameNGen and DIAMOND but at classroom scale. It shows vividly what generative models learn, and it is built from pieces Anvil already has.

- **Build:** Data is generated in-process by examples/snake.anvil's environment and its DQN agent with epsilon-exploration: (frames, action, next frame) tuples into a ring buffer. World model: an autoregressive transformer over the 16x16 grid's cell tokens, conditioned on the last k frames and the action, or a DIAMOND-style conditional diffusion reusing examples/diffusion.anvil. GameNGen's noise augmentation on context frames fights drift, and rare events (eating, dying) are reweighted. A raw-keyboard runtime routine (termios non-canonical mode in runtime.s) reads the arrow keys. The real engine and the dream are shown side by side with `show`. Stretch: a Wolfenstein-style raycaster environment written in index notation, plus its dreamed version.
- **Demo:** Two terminals, real Snake and dreamed Snake, both played with the arrow keys. The class hunts for glitches: does the dreamed snake grow when it eats? Does it die at the walls? What happens after idling for 200 frames?
- **Builds on:** examples/snake.anvil (environment and DQN in one binary), examples/diffusion.anvil, examples/transformer.anvil, `show`, `~` sampling, `input`
- **Risk:** Autoregressive drift ruins long play sessions. Rare events are underrepresented in the data. Each frame must stay under about 50 ms to feel interactive.

### Whisper in Anvil: live captions and a voice assistant with no Python at run time

*wow 9/10 · months*

Run OpenAI's Whisper-tiny or Whisper-base from safetensors on microphone input. The log-mel spectrogram is index notation, the encoder-decoder reuses the transformer machinery from anvil chat, and captions stream into the terminal. Pipe the captions into anvil chat and the whole voice assistant runs in one compiled language.

- **Build:** runtime.s / anvil_host.h: a `wav(path)` loader and a `mic()` streaming source (CoreAudio AudioQueue in the ObjC++ host that metal.py already generates). Log-mel in index notation: Hann-windowed frames gathered from the waveform (`fr[f, n] = wav[160 * f + n] * hann[n]`), the 400-point DFT as two matmuls against cos/sin tables (about 0.5 GFLOP for 30 s of audio, under a millisecond on AMX, no FFT needed), power, an 80-band mel matmul, log10 and clamping, checked against OpenAI's implementation to 1e-5. The Whisper encoder (2 conv1d layers + 4 blocks, d=384) and decoder with cross-attention and a KV cache. Greedy and beam search with Whisper's timestamp and suppression rules, written in Anvil. Token decoding through run-time string tables (51,865 tokens). Sliding 30 s windows with a simple energy-based voice-activity detector.
- **Demo:** Speak to the MacBook and captions appear in the terminal in real time. Then say 'explain index notation': the transcript feeds anvil chat, whose answer streams back. It is a voice assistant built entirely in Anvil, benchmarked against whisper.cpp's real-time factor.
- **Builds on:** im2col convolutions, the transformer example, safetensors and KV-cache work from anvil chat, run-time-indexed string tables, metal.py's ObjC++ host, AMX matmuls via blas.py
- **Risk:** Streaming audio and windowing are fiddly. Whisper's decoding heuristics (timestamps, repetition, temperature fallback) matter for quality. It adds a CoreAudio dependency.

### Quantized types: q8_0, q4_k, nf4, fp8 as real dtypes, with the quantizers written in Anvil

*wow 8/10 · months*

Make block-quantized formats first-class element types that the shape checker, cost model, editor and every backend understand. `W: q4_k[4096, 11008]` is dequantized in registers inside the fused kernel, and the quantization algorithms themselves (GPTQ, AWQ, k-quants) are Anvil programs. This shrinks a model about 7x and makes bandwidth-bound decode about 4x faster.

- **Build:** ir.py: Buffer gets an element format (bits, block size, scale layout) in place of the hardcoded `nbytes = 4 * numel`. A Load from a quantized buffer elaborates to an ordinary f32 expression (`d[blk] * (nib(i) - 8)`; k-quant super-blocks of 256 with 6-bit sub-scales), so fusion, autodiff (for QAT/QLoRA) and interp.py need no special cases. parser.py already reserves f16/bf16/u8/i64; extend it with i8, i4, f8e4m3, f8e5m2, q8_0, q4_k, q6_k and nf4. kernel.py: new GEMV/GEMM schedules that quantize activations to q8 on the fly and use SDOT (FEAT_DotProd) and SMMLA (FEAT_I8MM, M2 and later), with nibble unpacking via ushr/and/tbl; BFMMLA for bf16. metal.py: dequantize into threadgroup memory, then simdgroup_matrix<half,8,8>. cuda.py: dp4a and mma.sync int8. anvil/quant.anvil: absmax, the k-quant scale search, AWQ (activation-aware per-channel scaling), and GPTQ (column-by-column OBS updates using a Cholesky factor of the Hessian, in a run-time loop). `anvil quantize in.safetensors --to q4_k_m -o out.gguf` writes files llama.cpp can load. ide.py hover shows `q4_k[4096, 11008] · 4.5 bits/weight · 24.8 MB`, and cost.py counts quantized bytes.
- **Demo:** A perplexity-vs-bits chart on WikiText-2 for Llama-3.2-1B, where Anvil-written GPTQ, AWQ and Q4_K_M land on llama.cpp's curve within noise. A GGUF written by Anvil runs in llama.cpp and the other way round. Changing one type annotation quadruples decode tokens/s.
- **Builds on:** Buffer/Load/Const dtype plumbing (f32/i32), the parser's reserved dtype names, kernel.py register-tiled schedules, interp.py as the bit-exact reference, anvil cost, ide.py hovers
- **Risk:** k-quant bit layouts are intricate and must match llama.cpp byte for byte for interop. Sub-byte tensors break the 4-bytes-per-element assumption in arena.py, aarch64.py and the runtime loaders. GPTQ numerics need damping and f64 accumulation.

### The laptop speedrun: GPT-2 124M from scratch to 3.28 FineWeb loss on one MacBook

*wow 8/10 · moonshot*

Reproduce the modded-nanogpt speedrun target (validation loss 3.28 on FineWeb; a few minutes on 8xH100, roughly 5x10^17 FLOPs) on a single M3 Max in about a day, with every kernel generated by a dependency-free Python compiler. The whole training run is one binary, as in llm.c, but the model is 80 lines of math.

- **Build:** metal.py: generated bf16/f16 GEMMs using simdgroup_matrix with double-buffered threadgroup memory and fused epilogues. These are Anvil's own kernels rather than MPS, so bias, ReLU-squared and residual adds fuse. FlashAttention-2 forward AND backward on Metal: the online-softmax reduction from anvil chat plus its derivative rule in autodiff.py, recomputing P in the backward. A GPU memory arena (arena.py ported to Metal, fixing a listed weakness). Gradient accumulation. A `bin(path, u16)` loader with mmap'd, double-buffered streaming of FineWeb10B GPT-2 token shards. Muon in prelude.anvil as a ~15-line `optimizer` block: 5 Newton-Schulz iterations with coefficients (3.4445, -4.7750, 2.0315), and a compile-time branch on the parameter's rank that falls back to Adam. modded-nanogpt's architecture tricks (ReLU-squared, QK-norm, RoPE, logit softcap, value embeddings) as prelude code. Checkpoint/resume through save/load. Stretch: the same .anvil file on an H100 through cuda.py with mma.sync/WGMMA, compared against llm.c.
- **Demo:** A terminal dashboard with sparklines shows FineWeb validation loss crossing 3.28 on a MacBook, with MFU and tokens/s, next to a projection of several days for nanoGPT on PyTorch MPS on the same machine. Over the day, the class watches the model's samples turn from noise into English.
- **Builds on:** examples/transformer.anvil, the Metal backend's host-side loop counters (training loops never sync), `minimize ... with adam`, user-definable optimizer blocks, save/load checkpoints
- **Risk:** The Apple GPU peaks at about 14 TFLOP/s, so a ~24 h run needs at least 50% MFU. MPS reaches that; hand-generated simdgroup kernels may not. Thermal throttling and day-long runs need robust resume, and any convergence bug costs a day per experiment.

### `scan`: linear recurrences as a parallel primitive, for Mamba, minGRU and linear attention

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Let index notation refer to its own previous element (`h[t, d] = a[t, d] * h[t-1, d] + b[t, d]`) and compile it to a parallel associative scan, whose gradient is another scan running backward. This gives Mamba/S6, minGRU/minLSTM, RetNet and linear attention at any sequence length with no unrolling. For the most important class of recurrences, it answers the 'no autodiff through run-time loops' limitation.

- **Build:** elaborate.py: detect a comprehension that loads its own output at t-1 with a body affine in that load, and emit a new ir.Scan kernel kind (ir.py) with combine (a1,b1) o (a2,b2) = (a1*a2, a2*b1 + b2). autodiff.py rule: the reverse scan hbar[t] = ybar[t] + a[t+1]*hbar[t+1], with abar[t] = hbar[t]*h[t-1] and bbar = hbar. No tape; memory is O(T*d) for h only. kernel.py: vectorize across d in NEON lanes, with a three-phase chunked scan across threads over a FIXED chunk tree, so results stay bit-identical for any thread count. metal.py and cuda.py: a chunked scan with simd_shuffle_up (Hillis-Steele within a simdgroup) plus decoupled look-back. An optional log-space variant (Heinsen 2023) for stability. prelude: `selective_scan` (Mamba's Abar = exp(Delta*A), Bbar = Delta*B, with input-dependent Delta, B and C), minGRU/minLSTM (Feng et al. 2024), EMA, discounted returns/GAE (reused by the compiled-gym idea), cumsum and cumprod.
- **Demo:** charrnn today unrolls 32 GRU steps into 1,376 kernels (3.3 s to compile for the CPU, 35 s for Metal). The same model as a minGRU over T=1,024 should compile in about 0.3 s and train on 32x longer context. Then load state-spaces/mamba-130m-hf from safetensors and generate text with constant memory per token, side by side with the transformer's growing KV cache.
- **Builds on:** index notation with affine offsets (t-1 is already an affine load), `where`, the autodiff rule framework in autodiff.py, static for (as the baseline to beat), safetensors loading from anvil chat
- **Risk:** Recognizing scans robustly, including multiple states and Mamba's [d, n] state per channel, without making index notation ambiguous. Products of a[t] underflow over long T. Mamba's speed depends on keeping the expanded state on chip; a naive scan materializes [T, d, n].

### Mixture of experts: choosing an expert is just a gathered offset

*wow 8/10 · months*

Anvil's IR already expresses `W[route[t], i, j]` as a zero-copy view with a gather term, and its backward is already a scatter-add into only the experts that were used. Add top-k routing and capacity-factor dispatch and MoE follows naturally. Then run Qwen3-30B-A3B (128 experts, 8 active, about 3B active parameters) on a 48 GB laptop.

- **Build:** builtins.py: `top_k(x, k)` returning (values, i32 indices), lowered in kernel.py as a register-resident partial sort per row and in metal.py as a simdgroup bitonic select. Capacity-bounded dispatch with static shapes ([E, C, d] buffers; Switch Transformer capacity factor with token dropping) through the existing scatter-add. Switch load-balancing loss and router z-loss in the prelude. A grouped-GEMM schedule (one dispatch, per-expert offsets) in metal.py and kernel.py, because gathered weights don't fit cblas_sgemm's contiguous operands. For inference, gathered expert matvecs fused with q4 dequantization (quantized-types idea), and experts processed in order of use during prefill.
- **Demo:** Weeks milestone: an 8-expert char-level MoE transformer trained on the README, where `show` draws a heatmap of which expert handles digits, punctuation, code fences and Unicode; specialization appears after a few minutes of training. Flagship: `anvil chat Qwen3-30B-A3B-Q4_K_M.gguf` at interactive speed on the M3 Max.
- **Builds on:** Gather terms in ir.Affine, differentiable scatter-add (`E[ids[n], d] += g[n, d]`), argmax, nonzero(size=K)'s static-capacity pattern, the char transformer, anvil chat and quantized types
- **Risk:** Routing imbalance wastes static capacity. Gathered GEMMs can't use AMX. Random expert access across 18 GB of mmap'd q4 weights stresses the page cache. Top-k on GPUs needs care to stay deterministic.

### AlphaZero in one file: batched Gumbel MCTS compiled together with the network

*wow 8/10 · months*

The checkers player already learns by TD and searches by compile-time recursion. Upgrade it to the real thing: Monte-Carlo tree search with a policy/value ResNet, batched across hundreds of self-play games so that leaf evaluations run as one GPU call. Gumbel MuZero's root search (Danihelka et al. 2022) plays strongly with 16-32 simulations, which makes laptop-scale AlphaZero realistic.

- **Build:** MCTS in Anvil on static-capacity tree tensors [games, nodes, actions] holding child, N, W and P. PUCT/Gumbel selection in index notation, descent in a `while` loop, batched leaf evaluation, and backup via scatter-add along the path. A `top_k`/Gumbel-Top-k builtin, shared with MoE. A policy/value ResNet (Conv2d plus BatchNorm, still a to-do in IDEAS.md). The replay buffer as a ring tensor. The 8 dihedral symmetries as gather tables for augmentation. Rule files for Connect Four, Othello, checkers (reusing examples/checkers.anvil) and Go 9x9 (captures by iterative flood-fill dilation; superko via Zobrist hashes, which need 64-bit or paired i32 integers). A bin/play browser board built on anvil.function.
- **Demo:** Connect Four AlphaZero trained in about 30 minutes on the laptop and scored against Pascal Pons' perfect solver (% optimal moves on his benchmark positions). Students play it in the browser and lose. Then AlphaZero-checkers plays the existing TD critic.
- **Builds on:** examples/checkers.anvil (rules in index notation, nonzero(size=K), the self-play loop, save/load), prelude Conv2d, Adam, the Metal backend
- **Risk:** Tree code compiles into many tiny kernels, so per-simulation overhead (especially Metal dispatches) may dominate. Static tree capacity limits the number of simulations. Go needs bitwise ops and hashing.

### Compiled gym: environment, agent and physics in one binary

*wow 8/10 · months*

Write environments in index notation, vectorized over thousands of copies, and compile them together with PPO into one native program: the 1000x speedups PureJaxRL reports, on a laptop CPU, without JAX. Because the physics is Anvil code, it is also differentiable, so policies can learn by backpropagating through the simulator (SHAC, analytic policy gradients), the way DiffTaichi's soft robots learn to walk.

- **Build:** Prelude/examples: vectorized CartPole, Acrobot and Pendulum, a batched version of examples/snake.anvil, 2048, a 2D mass-spring soft body with actuated springs and ground friction (DiffTaichi's mass_spring), and a 2D rigid walker. Auto-reset via `where(done, reset, state)`. PPO with GAE (a reverse scan, from the scan idea) and minibatch epochs via `batches`. The deep compiler work: autodiff through run-time loops (an open IDEAS.md item) using a checkpointed tape in autodiff.py and elaborate.py. Loop-carried buffers are saved every k steps into a static [T/k, ...] buffer and segments are recomputed in the backward (sqrt(T) memory, Griewank-style), so `minimize` works on a 1,000-step rollout without unrolling.
- **Demo:** `bin/gym walker`: a jelly creature learns to run to the right in about 20 seconds by gradient descent through 1,000 physics steps, drawn live in the terminal. Then PPO on 4,096 CartPoles runs at a target of tens of millions of environment steps per second, against Gymnasium + Stable-Baselines3's thousands, with a live steps/s counter in class.
- **Builds on:** examples/snake.anvil (environment and DQN in one program), batch dimensions in index notation, `~` sampling, `batches`, while/for loops, the multithreaded runtime
- **Risk:** Run-time loop autodiff is a deep change to how autodiff regions and loop-carried SSA work. Contact dynamics give exploding or noisy gradients (SHAC's short-horizon critic is the fix). Some environment logic is branchy and vectorizes poorly.

### Probabilistic programming: `observe`, NUTS and ADVI, with constraint types

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

`~` already does reparameterized sampling; make it a full probabilistic programming language. Write a hierarchical model in Stan-like syntax, and the compiler builds the log density, its gradient (compile-time AD), the constraining bijectors and a NUTS sampler into a native binary. Chains run on separate cores, results are bit-reproducible, and there is no 30-60 s Stan C++ compile.

- **Build:** parser.py and elaborate.py: `observe y ~ normal(mu, sigma)` accumulates into an implicit `target` log-density buffer. Latent `param`s with priors (`param tau: f32 ~ half_cauchy(5)`) get support-typed constraints inferred from the distribution (`positive`, `unit`, `simplex[k]`, `cholesky_corr[k]`), with automatic bijectors (softplus/exp, logit, stick-breaking) and log-Jacobian terms. Prelude log_prob for normal, student_t, cauchy, gamma, beta, dirichlet, poisson, bernoulli_logit, categorical and LKJ. `infer m with nuts(chains=8, warmup=1000, draws=1000)`: an iterative NUTS (NumPyro-style, no recursion, O(depth) memory) with dual-averaging step size and diagonal mass-matrix adaptation, one chain per thread. `with advi()` (mean-field, reparameterized) and `with svgd()`. R-hat, bulk/tail ESS and divergent transitions reported with source lines, in the style of --check.
- **Demo:** Eight schools (non-centered) and the radon hierarchical model in about 15 lines each: 8 chains x 2,000 draws finish before Stan would have finished compiling its C++, and the posterior summaries match Stan's. Then a Bayesian neural net on the spirals example draws its uncertainty map in ASCII.
- **Builds on:** `~` with reparameterized gradients, `grad` of anything, the counter-based RNG (one stream per chain), the bit-identical multithreaded runtime, `maximize elbo` (examples/vae.anvil)
- **Risk:** NUTS's dynamic trajectory length means heavy control flow in a static-shape language. The constraint type system is new design work. Stan and NumPyro cover far more models, so the case rests on speed and teaching value.

### Custom gradient rules: neural ODEs with adjoints, implicit layers, checkpointing

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Let a function declare its own backward pass in Anvil. One mechanism unlocks the adjoint method from Neural ODEs (Chen et al., NeurIPS 2018 best paper) with memory independent of the number of solver steps, Deep Equilibrium Models via the implicit function theorem, gradient checkpointing, straight-through estimators and gradient reversal. All of them become prelude code instead of compiler features.

- **Build:** parser.py/ast.py: a `backward` clause on `fn` that receives the output cotangent and returns input cotangents. elaborate.py marks the call's kernels as an opaque region, and autodiff.py's Backprop emits the user's backward block instead of differentiating the region. VJPs inside a backward come from nested `grad(sum(f(z, theta) * detach(a)), z, theta)`, which already works. prelude.anvil: `odeint(f, z0, t0, t1)` with adaptive Dormand-Prince in a run-time `while` loop, and an adjoint backward that integrates [z, a, integral of a^T df/dtheta] in reverse. `fixed_point(f, x0)` with Anderson acceleration forward and an implicit solve backward. `checkpoint(f)`, `straight_through(round)`. FFJORD (Grathwohl et al. 2019) with the Hutchinson trace estimator using `eps ~ normal`.
- **Demo:** A continuous normalizing flow morphs a Gaussian blob into the word ANVIL as a point cloud, animated with `show` in the terminal. `anvil build` reports the same memory for 10 or 10,000 solver steps, while the static-for unrolled version grows 1,000x. Gradient checks match finite differences.
- **Builds on:** grad inside run-time loop bodies (compile-time AD per region), `~ normal` reparameterization, while loops, `detach`, the finite-difference gradcheck tests
- **Risk:** Adjoint gradients are approximate: the reverse solve can drift for stiff dynamics. Custom rules must compose with fusion and the shape checker. A nested `grad` inside a backward sits close to autodiff.py's 'higher-order derivatives not supported' limit.

### Precision inference: the compiler chooses bf16/f16/f32 per tensor and proves it won't overflow

*wow 7/10 · months*

Instead of torch.autocast's hand-written allow-lists, a static numerical analysis over the kernel IR computes value ranges and rounding-error growth for every tensor in both the forward and backward pass. It picks the cheapest safe precision and inserts f32 accumulators and loss scaling where needed. Mixed precision becomes a type that the editor shows and the compiler checks.

- **Build:** anvil/precision.py, a pass between autodiff.py and optimize.py: interval plus affine arithmetic over Kernel expressions (exp, log, sums of squares, division). It is seeded by declared input ranges and either a calibration run on interp.py or the run-time statistics the --check scanner already gathers. First-order rounding-error propagation in the style of FPTaylor/Daisy, scaled by reduction length. Decisions are stored on ir.Buffer as a storage dtype and a compute dtype. For f16 gradients, `minimize` gets dynamic loss scaling inserted automatically (skip the step on inf, using the --check scan routine in runtime.s). Backends: f16 NEON (FMLA .8h, FCVTN/FCVTL), bf16 (BFDOT/BFMMLA), Metal half/bfloat, MPS f16 products. CLI: `anvil precision file.anvil` prints a per-line report with reasons, and `--precision auto|bf16|f32` applies it. ide.py inlay hints show e.g. `bf16 (|x| <= 12.4, rel.err 2^-8)`.
- **Demo:** `--precision auto` on examples/transformer.anvil and examples/diffusion.anvil runs about 2x faster on Metal (target), with the loss curves overlaid on the f32 runs. Then the classroom trap: delete the max-subtraction in softmax, and before anything runs the compiler reports 'exp at line 12 can overflow f16: logits reach 31.2 at step 0 (calibration)'.
- **Builds on:** the kernel IR's uniform expression trees, the --check nan/inf scanner, interp.py, cost.py, editor inlay hints
- **Risk:** Static bounds are pessimistic through long reductions and training dynamics (ranges drift as the weights train), so decisions need profile data and run-time guards. f16 gains on the CPU are modest without f16 access to AMX.

### Graph neural networks: message passing in one line, deterministic on the GPU

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

`agg[dst[e], d] += h[src[e], d]` is already valid, differentiable Anvil, so GCN, GraphSAGE, GIN, GAT and E(n)-equivariant networks are a few lines each. Add scatter-max and sorted segmented reductions, and GPU GNN training in Anvil becomes bit-reproducible, which PyG and DGL do not guarantee because they rely on atomics.

- **Build:** elaborate.py/ops.py: `max=` and `min=` scatter-reductions, with gradients routed to the argmax (autodiff.py already routes max/min). `segment_softmax` in the prelude for GAT. A run-time sort (radix sort in runtime.s, onesweep on Metal), used once at load time to put edges in CSR order. A segmented-reduction schedule in kernel.py and metal.py: one thread or simdgroup per destination node, no atomics. Loaders for Planetoid (Cora/CiteSeer/PubMed), OGB arxiv and QM9 via npy/csv. Prelude layers: GCNConv, SAGEConv, GATConv, GINConv, and an EGNN layer (Satorras et al. 2021).
- **Demo:** GCN on Cora reaches Kipf & Welling's ~81% in tens of milliseconds, and GAT ~83%. Then a learned particle simulator ('Learning to Simulate', Sanchez-Gonzalez et al. 2020), a GNN over neighbor graphs trained on the compiled gym's mass-spring simulation, rolls out 1,000 steps in the terminal next to the true physics. EGNN on QM9: rotate the molecule and the prediction does not change.
- **Builds on:** differentiable scatter-add in index notation, gathers with run-time bounds checks, max/min gradient routing, csv/npy loaders, the thread-determinism tests
- **Risk:** Gathers are memory-bound and pay per-element bounds checks. Dynamic graphs whose neighbor lists change every step need static-capacity padding. Beating PyG on speed depends on a good segmented schedule.

## apple-silicon (16)

### Moonshot: training on the Neural Engine

*wow 10/10 · moonshot*

Apple's ANE is officially inference-only. But Anvil's autodiff turns a whole training step (forward, backward and optimizer) into an ordinary forward-only dataflow program, which is exactly what the ANE executes. This compiles whole training steps to the Neural Engine and trains at a fraction of a watt while the CPU and GPU sit idle. As far as is publicly known, no compiler trains on the ANE from source.

- **Build:** This builds on idea 8. The full step (the kernels after autodiff.py and optimize.py) is emitted as one MIL function. Parameters and Adam moments become Core ML state (stateful models on macOS 15/iOS 18: read_state / coreml_update_state), or they are passed as IOSurface inputs and outputBackings so the work can be split: the ANE runs the fp16 forward and backward, and the CPU applies the fp32 master-weight update in place in the same unified memory, then writes the fp16 weights back. Dynamic loss scaling is inserted by autodiff (idea 11). Backward patterns are rewritten for the ANE: scatter-add gradients of gathers become one-hot matmuls, and max-pool masks become selects. MLComputePlan checks that every op is on the ANE, and the build fails otherwise. If Core ML blocks this, the lower path is the private AppleNeuralEngine.framework (_ANEClient/_ANEInMemoryModel), as reverse-engineering efforts such as tinygrad's ANE experiments used, to pass IOSurfaces directly and skip recompilation.
- **Demo:** The MNIST CNN trains while `anvil top` shows the ANE power rail lit and the CPU and GPU near zero. Its accuracy curve is overlaid on the CPU fp32 run. A joules-per-epoch table compares ANE, CPU, GPU and PyTorch, and the ANE run should be the most energy-efficient training run in the table. Then the same on an iPhone.
- **Builds on:** compile-time reverse-mode autodiff that produces plain kernels, minimize's inlined optimizer, ideas 8 (MIL backend), 9 (unified arena) and 11 (loss scaling)
- **Risk:** Very high. The ANE compiler may reject training-specific ops or state updates and silently fall back to the CPU. fp16 gradients can underflow. Per-call overhead may dominate small steps. Private APIs break between OS releases and are not allowed on the App Store. Weights passed as inputs may run much slower on the ANE than constant weights.

### Whole-chip execution: one training step scheduled across P-cores, E-cores, AMX, GPU and ANE with zero copies

*wow 9/10 · months*

With unified memory a tensor never has to move, yet every framework picks one device. Anvil knows the whole program, its shapes and its costs at compile time, so it can solve the scheduling problem statically. It places each kernel on the engine where it finishes earliest (HEFT), splits large products between AMX and the GPU in proportion to their speed, pipelines micro-batches across engines, and synchronizes with GPU events instead of command-buffer waits, all on one arena that every engine addresses.

- **Build:** Unified arena: arena.py's plan lives in a single 16 KB-aligned region (`.p2align 14` in the assembly's __bss, or vm_allocate), wrapped once as an MTLBuffer with newBufferWithBytesNoCopy and exposed to the ANE as IOSurfaces, so CPU, GPU and ANE kernels share addresses. New anvil/backend/hetero.py: builds each block's kernel DAG, costs every (kernel, engine) pair from `anvil calibrate` (dispatch latency, throughput, bandwidth) and cost.py, and list-schedules with HEFT (Topcuoglu et al. 2002). It splits Gemms along M by the measured AMX:GPU ratio and can add a GPipe-style micro-batch pipeline across engines. It emits a hybrid ObjC++ host, derived from metal.py's MetalGen, that links the NEON leaf kernels from aarch64.py through C-ABI shims (those kernels use an internal convention that clobbers callee-saved registers). GPU work is encoded with MTLSharedEvent wait/signal values on the dependency edges, and CPU work goes to the runtime pool. `anvil schedule file.anvil --svg` draws the static schedule as a Gantt chart and overlays the measured times.
- **Demo:** The transformer's training step as an animated Gantt chart with four lanes busy at once: embedding gathers and layer norms on P-cores, attention on the GPU, MLP products split between AMX and the GPU, the next batch prepared on E-cores. It beats both `--metal` (1.6x the CPU today) and the CPU. The small MLP stops losing on the GPU (0.6x today) because the scheduler keeps its tiny kernels on the CPU automatically.
- **Builds on:** arena.py's static plan, metal.py's host-side scalars and never-wait encoding, blas.py Gemm records, cost.py, the CPU thread pool, idea 1's calibration
- **Risk:** CPU-GPU event latency (tens of µs) can eat the gains on small kernels. The engines share DRAM bandwidth and the system-level cache, so their throughputs don't simply add. A wrong cost model gives bad schedules. In fp32, AMX plus GPU realistically adds only 1.1-1.3x over the GPU alone on large products; the big wins are mid-size models and hidden latency.

### Moonshot: write in the air with Anvil on Apple Vision Pro (plus an iPhone 'magic wand')

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

This puts the handwriting reader into spatial computing. Pinch to put your pen down, write a word in the air with a fingertip, and floating text appears in the room, read by a network written in Anvil. Then teach the headset a new gesture by performing it ten times: a tiny sequence model trains on-device in under a second and the gesture works immediately.

- **Build:** apps/vision-airwrite, using the visionOS 2.2 SDK installed here: an ImmersiveSpace with ARKit's HandTrackingProvider (27 joints per hand, about 90 Hz). A pinch marks pen-down. The fingertip path is projected onto a best-fit plane, redrawn the existing MNIST/EMNIST way, and read by the letters model through `anvil export --target visionos --swift` (idea 12). Because air strokes are wobblier, idea 13's personalization fine-tunes on air-written samples. The few-shot gesture learner feeds joint sequences (2 hands x 27 joints x 3 coordinates x 64 frames) into a 1D conv or GRU written in Anvil (using static for over time, as charrnn.anvil does) and trains it with the exported step. Recognized text and gestures drive RealityKit text and particle effects. iPhone fallback: a CoreMotion 100 Hz accelerometer/gyro 'magic wand' (the classic TinyML demo) trained on-device by the same exported step.
- **Demo:** In class, a student wearing Vision Pro writes 'hello' in the air and it appears floating in front of them. They record a 'snap' gesture 10 times, and seconds later it triggers confetti. Without a headset, students wave an iPhone to draw letters in the air.
- **Builds on:** examples/letters.anvil and the draw app's stroke normalization, charrnn's static-for sequence model, ideas 12 and 13
- **Risk:** It needs a Vision Pro (about $3.5k), and hand tracking isn't available in the visionOS simulator, so iteration requires the device. Air strokes differ from MNIST-style pen strokes (a domain gap). Hand-tracking data requires an immersive space and user permission.

### Moonshot: the classroom is the datacenter, with federated learning across students' iPads

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

Turn a room of iPhones and iPads into a training cluster. Each device trains an exported Anvil step on its owner's handwriting, FedAvg rounds average the models over a peer-to-peer mesh, and the projector shows the global model getting better at everyone's handwriting while no drawing ever leaves a device. It is a live lecture on distributed optimization, privacy and energy, with the students as the nodes.

- **Build:** apps/classroom: a Swift package around the exported trainer (idea 12). Networking uses MultipeerConnectivity sessions (8 peers each, arranged hierarchically for about 30 devices) or Network.framework with Bonjour. The teacher's Mac coordinates FedAvg rounds (McMahan et al. 2017), and its aggregation and evaluation are themselves an Anvil program. A DP-SGD option (Abadi et al. 2016) uses per-example gradients, which in Anvil are just a weight-gradient kernel that doesn't sum over the batch index, written in index notation and then clipped and noised. Secure aggregation with pairwise masks (Bonawitz et al. 2017) is an advanced module. --exact (idea 4) makes every round replayable and verifiable, and idea 1 puts each device's joules on the dashboard.
- **Demo:** 25 students write digits and letters for three minutes. The dashboard shows rounds ticking by, each device's update norm and joules, and global accuracy on everyone's held-out writing climbing past what any single device's model reaches. A slider turns on differential-privacy noise, and the class watches the privacy/accuracy trade-off happen live.
- **Builds on:** anvil export --train (idea 12), the deterministic counter-based RNG and bit-exact mode (idea 4), index-notation gradients, the handwriting models
- **Risk:** Logistics: installing on 25 devices through TestFlight or ad hoc distribution. MultipeerConnectivity is flaky at classroom scale. With one writer per device the data is non-IID, which slows FedAvg. School-managed devices may block the app.

### One bitstream, every engine: bit-exact training across CPU, GPU and CUDA, with live migration

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Anvil is already bit-identical across thread counts; this extends the guarantee across engines. In --exact mode the NEON, Metal and CUDA backends compute every reduction in one canonical order, with the same fused multiply-adds and the same polynomial exp/log/tanh, so a training run produces the same SHA-256 of its weights on any engine. All program state lives in one static arena, so a run can hop between engines mid-epoch and the result does not depend on where it ran. No framework offers this, and PyTorch is not even deterministic on a single GPU.

- **Build:** cli.py: `--exact`. kernel.py's choose_configs: per output, accumulate sequentially over k with fused multiply-adds, which is already vec-out's natural order and the order of Metal's and CUDA's thread-per-output loops. The vec-red horizontal sums and cuda.py's split_factor two-pass reductions are replaced by one canonical tree (a fixed 4-lane interleave, then a pairwise combine), emitted identically by kernel.py and cuda.py's KernelGen, which metal.py reuses. vecmath.py's coefficients (Cody-Waite exp, atanh-series log, expm1-based tanh) generate explicit fma() sequences for MSL and CUDA; Metal already uses MTLMathModeSafe. In exact mode blas.py and MPS are disabled, unless the AMX/SME emitters (ideas 5-6) are shown to accumulate in k order with matching rounding. Migration: the static arena, loop counters and RNG stream counters together are the complete program state. An `anvil_snapshot` runtime call writes them, and `anvil run --hop N --engines cpu,metal,cpu-e` resumes the same program on another backend. Tests: every example's weight hashes after N steps match across 1 and 8 threads, Metal and CUDA emulation.
- **Demo:** Train the small GPT while a slider moves it between the GPU, the P-cores and the E-cores every few hundred steps. Three terminals print the SHA-256 of all weights at every step, and the hashes are identical. Then 'proof of training': someone replays a published checkpoint's training on a different Mac (or, later, an NVIDIA box) and gets the same bits.
- **Builds on:** the existing bit-identical multithreading, the counter-based RNG shared by every backend, the static arena (arena.py), vecmath.py, the CUDA/Metal shared KernelGen
- **Risk:** Exact mode gives up AMX/MPS speed on large products, possibly 2-4x on the wide MLP, and that cost needs measuring. Metal compilers might still contract or reorder operations under safe math, so each OS release needs re-checking. Real CUDA hardware is still unverified. The NumPy interpreter (pairwise sums) is left out of the bitwise check.

### Raw AMX codegen: Anvil emits Apple's matrix-coprocessor instructions itself (M1-M3)

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Anvil reaches the AMX unit only through cblas_sgemm, so it has to split off its signature fused epilogues and pay a library call for every product. Emitting the undocumented AMX instructions directly, as reverse-engineered in corsix/amx, puts the SGD update back inside the weight-gradient GEMM's tile drain, now at AMX speed. It also makes tiny products nearly free: in the 26 µs softmax-regression step, 22 µs is matrix products.

- **Build:** New anvil/backend/amx.py. It takes the Gemm records blas.prepare already finds (every transpose, stacks and sums of products) and emits AMX encodings as `.word`: AMX_SET/CLR; LDX/LDY of 64-byte rows; FMA32 in matrix mode, a 16x16 fp32 outer product per instruction, using all four Z subsets as a 32x32 accumulator tile; EXTRH/STZ to drain. NEON code packs panels whose strides are not unit. The drain writes each 16-float row to an L1-resident scratch line, and kernel.py's Lowering runs the existing epilogue (bias, activation, SGD/Adam update) from there, with no round trip of the whole result through memory. Threading: M-panels are split across the two P-clusters (one AMX unit each) through the existing pool. Startup chooses AMX or Accelerate from hw.cpufamily. `anvil asm` annotates «fma32: 16x16 outer product, 512 FLOPs». The suite that checks blas.py against the interpreter is reused unchanged.
- **Demo:** `bin/anvil asm examples/mnist.anvil` shows a readable AMX inner loop. The vs_pytorch logreg and mlp step times drop sharply; the target is logreg going from 26 µs to under 10 µs. A 256x256x256 GEMM with bias and ReLU beats Accelerate followed by a separate epilogue.
- **Builds on:** anvil/backend/blas.py's Gemm detection and its tests, kernel.py's epilogue lowering, the existing thread pool
- **Risk:** AMX is undocumented and unsupported. Encodings and behaviour differ between M1, M2 and M3, and Apple can change them. App Store apps must not use it, so it stays a macOS research and benchmark path. On M4 it is officially replaced by SME (idea 6). A coprocessor fault or hang is hard to debug.

### An SME2 backend for M4, with ZA tiles as a register class

*wow 8/10 · months*

M4 exposes the matrix unit through Arm's official Scalable Matrix Extension. Anvil could be the first ML language to emit SME2 from source: outer products into ZA tiles, predicated edges with no scalar cleanup loops, widening f16/bf16 and int8 outer products, and fused epilogues that Accelerate can't provide, all in readable assembly that explains each tile.

- **Build:** New anvil/backend/sme.py: smstart/smstop around each kernel and a FMOPA schedule for blas.prepare's Gemms. mir.py gains a ZA tile register class (za0.s-za3.s: four 16x16 fp32 accumulators at the 512-bit SVL, a 32x32 tile per kernel) and allocation of z0-z31/p0-p15. It uses SME2 multi-vector loads (ld1w {z0.s-z3.s}) and `whilelt` predicates for edges. Widening FMOPA (f16/bf16 to f32, 2x) and SMOPA (i8 to i32, 4x) come once the dtypes exist (idea 11). Epilogues use MOVA ZA->Z, or store the tile and finish it with NEON after smstop: Remke & Breuer's 'Hello SME!' (2024) found streaming-mode vector ops slow on M4. Feature detection via sysctl hw.optional.arm.FEAT_SME, which is 0 on this M3 Max. Testing without an M4: clang -march=armv9-a+sme2 assembles the kernels, a Linux harness runs the leaf kernels under qemu-aarch64's SME emulation against interp.py, and timing is done on M4 hardware.
- **Demo:** Run the unchanged examples/mnist.anvil on an M4 Mac mini or iPad Pro. `anvil asm` prints `fmopa za0.s, p0/m, p1/m, z0.s, z4.s` under a ZA-tile occupancy diagram in the kernel header. A GEMM+bias+GELU approaches the ~2 TFLOPS fp32 per cluster that 'Hello SME!' reports for M4 and beats Accelerate followed by a separate epilogue, from about 1,000 lines of Python.
- **Builds on:** blas.prepare's product detection, mir.py's linear-scan allocator, the R x U tiling logic in kernel.py, the 'SME on M4' item in IDEAS.md and PLAN.md's next steps
- **Risk:** This Mac (M3 Max) has no SME, so timing needs M4 hardware. One SME unit per cluster is shared by its cores. Entering and leaving streaming mode costs time on small kernels. QEMU checks correctness only, not performance.

### Own the GPU: simdgroup-matrix GEMMs, implicit-GEMM convolutions and FlashAttention derived by the compiler, on Metal

*wow 8/10 · months*

Two of Anvil's known weaknesses share one fix: GPU convolutions (MPS is 5.4x faster) and attention that materializes the TxT score matrix. The fix is for Anvil to write its own tiled matrix kernels for the GPU. A compiler pass rewrites softmax(QKᵀ)V into an online-softmax streaming kernel, so FlashAttention is derived from index notation rather than written by hand. Convolutions index their windows directly instead of materializing im2col, and every epilogue fuses because there is no MPS boundary to split at.

- **Build:** New anvil/backend/msl_tiles.py: GEMM templates on simdgroup_float8x8 / simdgroup_multiply_accumulate with double buffering in threadgroup memory, measured against MLX's 'steel' GEMM and Philip Turner's metal-flash-attention. Epilogues come from cuda.py's KernelGen expression emitter. Implicit-GEMM convolution: on Metal, optimize.py stops materializing the window expansion, and the tile loader computes `xp[n, c, s*i+u, s*j+v]` addresses itself. New optimize.py pass `online_softmax`: it recognizes max-reduce -> exp(x-m) -> sum -> divide -> contraction chains, including the prelude's softmax with causal masks, and rewrites them into one kernel with a running max and rescaled accumulators (Milakov & Gimelshein 2018; FlashAttention-2). An autodiff.py rule gives the fused node a backward that recomputes the scores. GPU memory arena: arena.py's plan placed in one MTLHeap with aliasable buffers, closing the 'no GPU memory arena' gap. Optional Metal 4 path on macOS 26: MTLTensor plus Metal Performance Primitives matmul2d cooperative tensors, which use the M5 GPU's per-core neural accelerators.
- **Demo:** examples/transformer.anvil at context length 4096: `anvil build --metal` reports 0 bytes for the score matrix, at a context where materializing it would be impractical. The EMNIST CNN on Metal goes from 5.4x slower than MPS to faster. The same Anvil source runs on an M5 GPU's neural accelerators through the Metal 4 path.
- **Builds on:** metal.py/anvil_metal.h, cuda.py's KernelGen, optimize.py's fusion passes, arena.py, the vs_pytorch CNN numbers as the target
- **Risk:** Matching years of MPS/MLX GEMM tuning across GPU generations is a long grind. The online-softmax rewrite needs robust matching of masking (`where`, causal `if`). Metal 4 needs macOS 26, and this Mac runs macOS 15. Tiled reductions must keep a fixed order to preserve determinism.

### A Neural Engine backend: Anvil -> Core ML MIL, with per-line placement reports

*wow 8/10 · months*

The 16-core ANE (about 18 TOPS on M3 Max, more on recent iPhones) sits idle in every Anvil run. This backend compiles Anvil to Core ML's ML Program format (MIL) directly, through a protobuf writer in plain Python with no dependencies, so models run on the ANE at a fraction of a watt. MLComputePlan is then used to report, per source line, which engine each operation landed on and why (for example, 'line 31: this gather runs on the CPU').

- **Build:** New anvil/backend/mil_proto.py: a minimal protobuf wire-format encoder (about 300 lines) for Model.proto/MIL.proto, plus the mlpackage layout (Manifest.json, weights/weight.bin with 64-byte-aligned blobs). New anvil/backend/coreml.py lowers kernel IR to MIL: blas.prepare's Gemms become matmul/linear, elementwise bodies become MIL elementwise ops, reductions become reduce_sum/reduce_max/argmax, affine views become reshape/transpose/slice_by_index, gathers become gather_along_axis, and im2col patterns become conv. It applies the ANE rules from Apple's 'Deploying Transformers on the Apple Neural Engine' (2022): (B, C, 1, S) layout, Linear as a 1x1 conv, softmax split per head. Index notation makes layout a compile-time choice the compiler can search. fp16 casts are inserted with range checks. Toolchain: `xcrun coremlcompiler compile`, which is present in this Xcode. Host: ObjC++ loads the .mlmodelc with computeUnits = cpuAndNeuralEngine, passes inputs as IOSurface-backed MLMultiArrays and receives outputs through MLPredictionOptions.outputBackings, with no copies. `anvil coreml --plan` uses MLComputePlan (macOS 14.4+) to get each op's device and estimated cost and maps them back to source spans. ide.py shows them in the VS Code extension as ANE/GPU/CPU inlay hints.
- **Demo:** `bin/draw --text --ane`: the handwriting reader runs on the Neural Engine, and every line of examples/letters_model.anvil carries an 'ANE' badge in the editor. `anvil run --energy` (idea 1) shows the reader drawing about 0.1 W on the ANE against several watts on the CPU, at sub-millisecond latency.
- **Builds on:** blas.prepare's product detection, export.py's bake-the-weights lowering, ide.py inlay hints, the installed coremlcompiler
- **Risk:** The MIL protobuf schema and opset versions must be exactly right, and Apple's compiler is opaque. The ANE silently falls back to CPU/GPU for unsupported ops or shapes, which is why the plan report is essential. The ANE computes only in fp16. Per-prediction dispatch overhead (hundreds of µs) hurts tiny models. Training is not covered here (idea 14).

### Teach your iPad: on-device handwriting personalization with Apple Pencil

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Port the handwriting reader (bin/draw --text) to an iPad app where the network learns your own handwriting. Every correction becomes a training example, and an exported Anvil training step fine-tunes the EMNIST CNN on the iPad in about 100 ms, so after a minute of writing it reads you far better than it reads anyone else. No data leaves the device, and the app shows how many joules learning cost.

- **Build:** apps/ipad-handwriting (SwiftUI + PencilKit): stroke capture, then a Swift port of text_reader.py (line/piece/link grouping, MNIST-style redraw, beam search with the dictionary). The letters model comes through `anvil export --swift --train` (idea 12) with a fine-tuning step for the last layers or a low-rank adapter on the classifier, plus a replay buffer of EMNIST samples against catastrophic forgetting. It adds a tap-to-correct UI, per-user checkpoints through save/load, an energy readout from task_power_info_v2, and optional ANE inference through idea 8.
- **Demo:** A student writes a sentence and the app misreads their 'a' as 'o'. They correct five letters, a progress ring trains for about a second, and the sentence now reads correctly. Hand the iPad to a classmate and accuracy drops. A 'who wrote this?' head, trained on everyone's corrections, then names the writer.
- **Builds on:** examples/letters.anvil and letters_model.anvil, examples/draw/text_reader.py, save/load checkpoints, idea 12's trainable Swift export
- **Risk:** Porting the Python reader to Swift is substantial work. Small correction sets overfit, so the app needs replay and a low learning rate. Pencil strokes differ from the mouse strokes the reader was tuned on. Distributing the app to a class requires TestFlight.

### Joules as a first-class metric: energy(), anvil run --energy, anvil cost --energy

*wow 7/10 · days*

Every Anvil run reports joules next to seconds, split by P-cores, E-cores, GPU, ANE and DRAM, per epoch and per source line. Because shapes are static, the compiler can also predict a program's energy before it runs and then check that prediction. That makes 'accuracy per joule' a number students optimize, and Anvil should win it by an even wider margin than it wins on speed, since it has no Python spinning and no allocation.

- **Build:** (1) runtime.s: a builtin `energy()` next to `clock()` that calls proc_pid_rusage(RUSAGE_INFO_V6) and reads ri_energy_nj (whole-process CPU) and ri_penergy_nj (P-cores; the E-core share is the difference), plus ri_instructions/ri_cycles. It is a plain libc call like the existing sysctlbyname call and needs no sudo. (2) anvil_host.h / anvil_metal.h: an IOReport sampler for GPU, ANE and DRAM energy. IOReport is a private library in the dyld shared cache, and its 'Energy Model' group can be read without sudo, as macmon does. On iOS: task_info(TASK_POWER_INFO_V2).task_energy. (3) anvil/builtins.py + elaborate.py: `energy()` as an f32 builtin; `anvil run --energy` prints a per-engine summary; `--profile` gains a joules column by sampling at kernel boundaries. (4) New anvil/energy.py + `anvil calibrate`: microbenchmarks fit pJ/FLOP for each engine and pJ/byte for DRAM, written to ~/.cache/anvil/device.json. anvil/cost.py gets an `--energy` column (mJ per step per line) built from its existing FLOP and traffic counts, Horowitz-style. (5) benchmarks/vs_pytorch/run.py measures PyTorch's child-process energy the same way and adds a joules and images-per-joule column to REPORT.md.
- **Demo:** 'MNIST to 97% for N joules'. A live joule counter runs during training. Next, `anvil cost --energy examples/mnist.anvil` predicts about 2.5 J/epoch before running and the run measures something close. Last, a table compares joules per epoch for Anvil CPU, Anvil Metal, PyTorch eager, torch.compile and MPS, in physical terms: carrying a 1 kg bottle up a flight of stairs takes about 30 J, and the table shows how many MNIST trainings one iPhone battery would pay for.
- **Builds on:** the clock() builtin and the assembly runtime's libc calls, --profile per-kernel timing, anvil/cost.py static FLOP/traffic counts, the benchmarks/vs_pytorch harness
- **Risk:** IOReport is private and its channel names change across chips and OS versions. The counters update only every few milliseconds, so per-kernel attribution needs repeated runs. Background load (the README reports load averages of 8-16) adds noise. The static energy model will be off by about 30% until calibrated.

### Multi-engine roofline, `anvil top`, and Anvil source lines inside Instruments

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

This turns the M3 Max into a lecture. Every kernel of a training step is plotted on the roofline of every engine (NEON P-core, E-core, AMX, GPU, ANE, 400 GB/s DRAM), so students see why a kernel is slow and which engine it belongs on. A live `anvil top` shows each engine's utilization, watts and temperature, plus the source line running on it right now, and Xcode Instruments' timelines show Anvil source lines instead of anonymous kernels.

- **Build:** New anvil/roofline.py: arithmetic intensity from cost.py's kernel_flops/kernel_bytes, combined with measured time from --profile, rendered as a self-contained HTML/SVG roofline with no dependencies. The ceilings are measured by `anvil calibrate`: a STREAM-triad bandwidth test, NEON FMA peak per core type, Accelerate/AMX sgemm, MPS, and ANE through Core ML. Each dot links to its `anvil ir` and `anvil asm` text. Runtime: a 4 KB shm_open page where the thread pool in runtime.s and the Metal host publish the current kernel id and per-kernel counters. New anvil/top.py: an ANSI TUI with no dependencies that reads that page, IOReport power, SMC temperatures and NSProcessInfo.thermalState. Instruments: the C/ObjC++ hosts emit os_signpost intervals named like 'k86 matmul+add mnist.anvil:27'. The assembly runtime calls these through a small C shim. Metal encoders get labels and pushDebugGroup with the source line, so Metal System Trace and the GPU debugger show Anvil lines.
- **Demo:** Run the LeNet CNN with --metal while the roofline is projected.
