The im2col copies sit on the DRAM roof, the products sit under the AMX roof, and the Metal convolution sits far below the GPU roof. That one picture explains the PyTorch report's 5.4x MPS convolution gap. Then `anvil top` during the transformer shows the P-cores pegged, the E-cores idle and the GPU half-used, with the source line that owns each.
- **Builds on:** anvil/cost.py, --profile, kernel headers that carry source spans, the Metal host (anvil_metal.h), the existing per-kernel naming in `anvil asm`
- **Risk:** AMX and ANE peaks can only be reached through Apple's libraries, so their ceilings are achievable rates, not theoretical ones. The signposts need a C shim next to the assembly runtime. This is mainly an insight and teaching tool, not a speedup.

### A thermal- and power-aware governor: train under a power budget and never throttle

*wow 7/10 · months*

Phones and fanless MacBook Airs throttle hard under sustained load, and no training framework notices. This compiles several schedules into one binary (GPU plus P-cores at full speed, P-cores only, E-cores plus ANE), all sharing one arena, and a runtime governor switches between them at step boundaries based on live temperature and power. `--watts 5` or `--quiet` becomes a promise, and sustained throughput beats a naive run that gets throttled.

- **Build:** aarch64.py/metal.py: emit several versions of each loop body (the schedule variants from ideas 3 and 9) with an identical arena layout, so switching is a pointer swap. anvil_host.h: anvil_governor. On macOS it is a PI controller on measured package power (IOReport) and SMC temperature; on both macOS and iOS it listens for NSProcessInfo.thermalState notifications, and on iOS it reads task_power_info_v2. Policies: `--watts`, `--temp`, `--quiet`, and `--battery` (reacts to isLowPowerModeEnabled). With --exact (idea 4) the result is bit-identical whichever schedules ran. In the iOS export (idea 12): BGProcessingTaskRequest(requiresExternalPower = true) and iOS 26's BGContinuedProcessingTask with GPU resources. The governor logs a CSV of temperature, watts, schedule and steps/s.
- **Demo:** 'Your iPhone teaches itself checkers overnight': the checkers self-play TD learner (bin/checkers) trains on an iPhone while it charges. A 30-minute chart compares two runs. The ungoverned run hits thermal state 'serious' after a few minutes and its speed collapses; the governed run stays at 'fair' and plays more games per minute overall. In the morning, you play it. On a Mac: a fanless MacBook Air trains the letters CNN with `--quiet`.
- **Builds on:** the bin/checkers self-play program and save/load checkpoints, the static arena, ideas 1, 3, 4 and 12
- **Risk:** Apple exposes no fine-grained DVFS or core pinning, so control is coarse (which schedule, how many threads, duty cycle). Thermal time constants are minutes long, so tuning the controller takes long experiments. iOS background-execution limits and App Review apply.

### Anvil inside Xcode: shape-typed Swift APIs, trainable exports, iOS/visionOS XCFrameworks

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

This makes Anvil a first-class part of Apple app development. Drop a .anvil file into an Xcode project and get a native Swift API whose types carry the tensor shapes: f32[28, 28] becomes InlineArray<28, InlineArray<28, Float>> with the same memory layout. A shape mismatch between the Swift code and the model is then a Swift compile error, and Anvil's own errors appear inline in Xcode. Exports include the training step, not just inference, so apps can learn on device.

- **Build:** export.py gains three options. `--swift` generates a Swift wrapper that uses Swift 6.2's integer generic parameters (SE-0452) and InlineArray (SE-0453) for small fixed shapes and Span/MutableSpan for large ones, with zero copies. `--train` exports `step(x:y:) -> Float`, `predict`, `save`, `load` and `reset`, with parameters and optimizer state in the static arena, and prefixes symbols per model so several models can link into one app. `--target ios|ios-simulator|visionos` makes toolchain.py pass -target arm64-apple-ios17.0 and so on, makes the runtime's file and print paths sandbox-safe, and builds an XCFramework with `xcodebuild -create-xcframework`. New editors/xcode/AnvilPlugin: a SwiftPM build-tool plugin that runs the compiler on .anvil sources and prints `file:line:col: error:` lines so Xcode underlines them. A sample app is built and run in the iOS Simulator (the iOS 18.2 and visionOS 2.2 SDKs are installed).
- **Demo:** In Xcode, the digits app calls `DigitsReader.read(canvas)`. Change the model to take 32x32 input and the Swift call site turns red with a type error before anything runs. Then tap 'Train' in the simulator and the exported training step fine-tunes the model on the device.
- **Builds on:** anvil export (C library with baked weights), anvil.function's pointer-slot ABI, the AArch64 backend (iOS uses the same Apple arm64 ABI), ide.py's diagnostics
- **Risk:** This Mac has Swift 6.0.3 / Xcode 16.2, and integer generics need Xcode 26; the fallback is generated fixed-size wrapper structs. A symbolic batch dimension needs several precompiled sizes until dynamic shapes exist. The hand-written assembly runtime must be audited for iOS (sandboxed files, no external tools, zlib data paths).

### A big.LITTLE-aware runtime: all 16 cores, an E-core data pipeline, and an energy/time Pareto tuner

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Today the thread pool uses min(P-cores, 8), so on an M3 Max 4 of the 12 performance cores and all 4 efficiency cores sit idle. The runtime should know about heterogeneity: separate P and E pools steered by QoS, the next batch's shuffle, gather and augmentation prepared on the E-cores one step ahead, and `anvil tune --objective time|energy|edp` choosing per-kernel pools and thread counts from measured joules. The output is a Pareto frontier that runs from 'fastest' to 'the fan never spins'.

- **Build:** runtime.s: two worker pools set up with pthread_set_qos_class_self_np: QOS_CLASS_USER_INTERACTIVE for P-cores, and QOS_CLASS_BACKGROUND, which macOS confines to the E cluster. The compare-and-swap chunk dispatcher gets per-pool chunk sizes (E-core chunks about a third of the size). Every output element is still computed by the same code, so results stay bit-identical. The 8-thread cap is lifted once 12 threads are measured. aarch64.py: a software-pipelined `batches()`. The per-step contiguous batch gather (a documented deviation in PLAN section 10) for batch t+1 runs on the E pool into a double buffer while the P-cores train on batch t, and arena.py plans that double buffer. New anvil/tune.py (an IDEAS.md item not yet done): compiles variants (pool and threads per kernel class, tile size), measures seconds and joules (idea 1), writes a schedule file that aarch64.py reads, and plots the frontier.
- **Demo:** On battery, `anvil run --eco examples/letters.anvil` trains the EMNIST CNN on the 4 E-cores for a fraction of the joules per epoch, and `--max` runs on 12 P-cores with E-core prefetch. One chart shows the Pareto curve of about 10 measured runs. The per-step gather plus random-shift augmentation disappears from the profile because the E-cores did it.
- **Builds on:** the hand-written assembly thread pool (CAS chunk dispatch, wfe sleeping), parallel_grain/kernel_work in aarch64.py, batches(), arena.py
- **Risk:** macOS offers no hard core pinning: QoS is a hint and threads can migrate. E-cores are 3-4x slower, so chunking must avoid stragglers. Models dominated by AMX gain little, because there is one AMX unit per cluster.

### Apple-native precision: f16, bf16 and int8 with compiler-managed scaling

*wow 6/10 · months*

Every Apple engine runs faster in reduced precision. On this M3 Max, NEON's FEAT_FP16 does 8 lanes per FMA, FEAT_BF16 adds BFMMLA and FEAT_I8MM adds SMMLA (sysctl reports all three). The GPU has half and bfloat, and the ANE works only in fp16. This adds those dtypes to Anvil's types and lets the compiler decide where reduced precision is safe using static range analysis, with dynamic loss scaling inserted into the generated backward pass.

- **Build:** ir.py: F16, BF16 and I8 dtypes, with per-channel scales for I8. elaborate.py: `f16[n, 784]`, `.f16()`, promotion rules. New anvil/precision.py: interval analysis over kernel IR (ranges propagated through exp/log/softmax/layer_norm), plus an optional calibration run on interp.py, producing per-tensor dtype decisions for `--precision auto`. autodiff.py: dynamic loss scaling as in Micikevicius et al. 2018 (scale, check for non-finite values, skip the step and halve), with f32 master weights. kernel.py: `fmla v.8h` fp16 schedules, `bfmmla` 2x4 by 4x2 tiles, `smmla`/`sdot` int8 GEMM micro-kernels. vecmath.py: fp16 polynomials. cuda.py/metal.py: half types. export.py: int8 post-training quantization of exported functions.
- **Demo:** `anvil run --precision auto examples/transformer.anvil` runs the NEON forward pass about 2x faster, with the f32 and auto loss curves overlaid and matching. `anvil ir` explains each decision (for example, 'logits stay f32: range reaches 1e4'). The exported int8 digits reader is 4x smaller and still reads all 300 synthetic test drawings correctly.
- **Builds on:** ir.py dtypes, autodiff.py, kernel.py schedules, export.py, --check's non-finite detection, the 'half precision' item in IDEAS.md
- **Risk:** Large fp32 GEMMs already run on AMX/Accelerate, so CPU gains concentrate in NEON and elementwise kernels. Interval analysis is conservative and may refuse f16 often. fp16 training can be unstable. This overlaps with the type-system work.

## tooling-dx (16)

### Rewind: a time-travel debugger for training runs (anvil debug)

*wow 9/10 · months*

Anvil training is bit-deterministic and every byte of state sits at a fixed address, so the whole state at step N fits in a memcpy and any step can be replayed exactly. Rewind records cheap keyframes, then lets you step backwards through training, jump to any step, break on source lines or on tensor conditions, and inspect any tensor at any moment, including temporaries that were fused away or reused. PyTorch can't do this: it is neither deterministic nor statically allocated, so rr/gdb-style replay of a training run is not on the table there.

- **Build:** (1) Keyframe set from liveness: reuse arena.flatten()/lifetime() in anvil/backend/arena.py to find the buffers live across the training loop's back edge (params, optimizer state, loop-carried vars, loop counters, RNG stream counter). That set is the minimal checkpoint. (2) A debug codegen mode in anvil/backend/aarch64.py: `_anvil_rt_trap(id)` in runtime.s at kernel boundaries, which publishes state into an mmap'd file and blocks on a pipe, plus resume points (a jump table at each loop header, coroutine-style) so execution can re-enter a loop nest midway with restored counters. (3) Compiled watchpoints: `break when any(isnan(net.l2.W))` or `when loss > 3 * loss_prev` are parsed as Anvil and elaborated into a fused kernel inserted after the writer, so they run at native speed, unlike gdb's software watchpoints. (4) anvil/debug/dap.py: a Debug Adapter Protocol server, so VS Code's gutter breakpoints, call stack (Anvil inline call chains from elaborate.py), Variables pane (tensors with shapes and stats) and a webview tensor viewer (heatmap, histogram, image) work out of the box. A gdb-like TUI comes too. (5) reverse-continue: binary search over keyframes plus deterministic replay. Arena temporaries that were overwritten are rebuilt by replaying from the step's keyframe. Values fused away are recomputed by running that line's unfused kernel on interp.py.
- **Demo:** transformer.anvil with lr=3e-2 diverges around step 1,800. `reverse-continue until isfinite(loss)` lands on the last finite step. Step into the attention block: the tensor viewer shows one head's score matrix with logits near 1e4. Drag a timeline slider from step 0 and watch that head's attention collapse onto a single token. Fix the lr and replay from step 1,500 instead of starting over.
- **Builds on:** bit-identical determinism (tested across thread counts), static memory and arena.py liveness, the --check runtime (_anvil_rt_check, check_description), counter-based RNG, interp.py, editors/vscode
- **Risk:** Resuming in the middle of a loop nest is the hardest codegen piece. Accelerate/AMX sgemm and Metal/MPS may not be bit-reproducible, so replay mode should pin Anvil's own GEMMs. Programs driven by input()/stdin can't be replayed. Keyframe interval trades memory against replay latency for large models.

### Anvil in the browser: the whole compiler in Pyodide plus a WebAssembly SIMD backend, and the Anvil Book

*wow 9/10 · months*

The compiler is dependency-free Python, so it runs unmodified in Pyodide. Add a backend that emits WebAssembly binary directly from the kernel IR, and the whole toolchain (shapes on hover, autodiff, fusion, SIMD machine code) runs offline in a browser tab. A playground URL becomes the class's install step, and the docs become a book where every example runs.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/wasm.py: a direct .wasm binary encoder (LEB128, type/import/memory/code sections, no LLVM or emscripten) that lowers the existing vec_out/vec_red schedules. NEON 4×f32 maps to v128 f32x4, `fmla` to Relaxed SIMD `f32x4.relaxed_madd`, and register tiles to v128 locals, with R×U chosen per target (x86 has 16 xmm registers, so 4×8 instead of 4×16). The static arena becomes fixed offsets in one linear memory; static memory is exactly wasm's model. Threads use wasm threads with SharedArrayBuffer/Atomics.wait, and coi-serviceworker supplies COOP/COEP on static hosting. The runtime is in JS: print/show into a <pre>, clock, idx via fetch + DecompressionStream('gzip'), save/load to IndexedDB. The counter-based RNG is compiled into the module. The playground uses CodeMirror 6, with anvil/ide.py running in Pyodide for diagnostics and inlay shapes, and programs shared in the URL hash. 'The Anvil Book' sits on top: a language reference and course chapters with a live editor in every code block, doc-tested in CI like rustdoc, plus stdlib pages generated from prelude.anvil with symbolic per-call FLOP formulas. Stretch: WGSL generated from the MSL/CUDA generator, for WebGPU training.
- **Demo:** Students open a link on Chromebooks and train the MNIST MLP to about 96% in the tab in seconds. Then they draw digits on a canvas and the network they just trained reads them: bin/draw with no server, no Python and no install.
- **Builds on:** the dependency-free compiler, backend/kernel.py schedules, arena.py, cuda.py/metal.py generators, ide.py, examples/draw/index.html
- **Risk:** Pyodide cold start (3–5 s) and slower elaboration. Hand-encoding wasm is meticulous (validate with wasm-tools in tests). MNIST's size means shipping a subset. Threads need cross-origin isolation. Relaxed SIMD needs a fallback path for older browsers.

### MOONSHOT: Hot-swap training: edit a model's code while it trains

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

Save the file and the running training process picks up the new code at the next step: a new loss, an added regularizer, a changed schedule, even a new layer. Every weight and optimizer moment is kept, and the state migration is type-checked from static shapes. Erlang/Smalltalk-style live upgrade, in the spirit of Bret Victor's 'Inventing on Principle', applied to neural networks.

- **Build:** A 'live' build mode moves all persistent state (params, optimizer state, loop-carried vars, counters, RNG stream) into an mmap'd state region with a symbol table (name, dtype, shape, offset): the state ABI. Code reaches it through pointer slots, as anvil.function already does for inputs and outputs, and is compiled as a dylib. A small C host built on anvil_host.h runs the step loop and dlopens the new version at a step boundary. anvil/live/migrate.py diffs the two state tables: same name and shape are kept; new params initialize from their declarations; a changed shape is a compile error with a suggested `reinit`, an Elm-style type-checked migration. Execution resumes at the training loop header with counters kept (sharing Rewind's resume points). The Live dashboard marks each swap on the loss curve. On Metal, the MTLBuffers stay alive across swaps.
- **Demo:** On the projector, the VAE trains. The instructor adds a KL-warmup term and saves; the loss curve kinks at the swap and recovers. Then a residual block is added mid-run: its new weights start at zero while the old ones keep training.
- **Builds on:** anvil.function shared libraries with pointer slots, static memory, save/load's name-and-shape matching, the Live dashboard, Rewind's resume points
- **Risk:** Deciding which loop and which state count as the same across edits (a rename loses state). The arena and fused update kernels get recompiled wholesale. GPU state lifetime. It is a big systems project.

### MOONSHOT: Why did it say that? Training-data attribution by deterministic replay

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

Draw a 4 that the network reads as a 9, click 'why?', and see the training images that pushed it toward 9 and those that argued for 4. They are found by TracIn (Pruthi et al., NeurIPS 2020): the sum over checkpoints of the dot product of your example's gradient with each training example's gradient. Anvil has three ingredients that rarely come together: exact checkpoints from deterministic replay, gradients of anything, and per-example gradients written in index notation that never need to be materialized.

- **Build:** anvil/attribute.py and `anvil why`. Checkpoints come from Rewind keyframes, so attribution follows the real trajectory exactly. Per-example gradient dot products use the ghost factorization: for a Linear layer, ⟨g_n, g_test⟩ = (x_n·x_t)(dy_n·dy_t), so a pass over 60k MNIST images costs about one training epoch per checkpoint (im2col turns Conv2d into the same Linear form). A contraction-order rewrite, shared with the tensor-network optimizer, lets the compiler find that factorization from the naive index-notation formula. Results appear in bin/draw as a grid of proponents and opponents; for text models, the training passages most responsible for a generated token. Then a self-influence view surfaces likely mislabeled training examples, a known TracIn use. TRAK-style random projections (Park et al. 2023) are the path to GPT scale.
- **Demo:** In bin/draw, a misread digit brings up the 8 most responsible training images within seconds, often including a sloppy or mislabeled 9. Then the self-influence view ranks the MNIST training images most likely to be mislabeled, and the class goes looking for real label errors in MNIST.
- **Builds on:** Rewind keyframes and determinism, grad(loss, x) of anything, index-notation per-example math, bin/draw, cost/fusion machinery
- **Risk:** TracIn is a first-order approximation over sampled checkpoints and can mislead. Ghost dot products for convolutions and attention take more work. Scaling past small models needs random projections, which adds approximation error.

### Anvil Studio: watch the compiler think

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

A local web app with linked panes: source, kernel IR, assembly/MSL/CUDA, the dataflow graph and the memory arena. Click a line and every kernel it became lights up, forward and backward. A slider scrubs through the optimizer passes, so you can watch a thousand kernels fuse into a few hundred. The compiler's invisible decisions (fusion, inlining, in-place reuse, tiling, arena packing) become something a class can watch, like Compiler Explorer and the Halide visualizer combined for an entire training step.

- **Build:** anvil/studio/: server.py on http.server (the pattern of examples/draw/server.py) plus one static index.html in vanilla JS/SVG with no build step. `anvil studio file.anvil`, and `--html out.html` exports a self-contained page for slides. A trace hook in optimize.Optimizer.run snapshots ir.fmt_program after each pass and logs every decision with its reason: 'fused k86→k87 as GEMM epilogue', 'refused to inline relu into a matmul operand: recomputed 16× per column', 't4 reuses dying input t3'. Kernel origin sets are kept through fusion. aarch64.py, cuda.py and metal.py emit per-kernel source ranges. arena.plan() exports (lo, hi, offset, size) for an 'arena Tetris' chart (statement position × byte offset), with a 'no sharing' toggle that shows checkers' 47 KB growing to 332 KB. cost.py FLOPs/bytes plus --profile times give a per-kernel roofline. The dataflow graph is laid out with ELK.js (layered), colored by forward/backward/optimizer.
- **Demo:** Open transformer.anvil and drag the pass slider from 0 to 8: the dataflow graph collapses, the arena chart compacts, and the kernel count ticks down live. Click `minimize loss with adam()` to see the Adam update fused into the weight-gradient GEMM, then its 4×16 register-tiled inner loop in the asm pane.
- **Builds on:** ir.fmt_program, Kernel.span/label/name, Config.describe() in backend/kernel.py, arena.Plan, cost.py, --profile, per-kernel asm headers
- **Risk:** Unrolled `static for` programs have 1,000+ kernels, so the graph needs grouping by source line and loop. The trace hook must cost nothing outside studio mode.

### Live training dashboard with knobs (anvil run --live)

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

A `log` statement streams scalars, histograms and images from native code to a browser over a lock-free shared-memory ring. Loss curves, weight distributions and generated samples update as the model trains, with no stall in the hot loop. Constants declared as `knob` become sliders, so you can change the learning rate, the guidance scale or dropout while training runs.

- **Build:** Language: `log loss, acc`, `log image("samples", x)`, `log hist(net.l1.W)` (parser.py, elaborate.py → ir.RTCall "log"). runtime.s gets `_anvil_rt_log`, which writes records into an mmap'd single-producer ring with plain stores and one release store of the head index. It never blocks and drops records when the ring is full. Metal and CUDA hosts get the same routine through anvil_host.h. `knob const LR = 0.1 in [1e-4, 1.0]` is elaborated as a run-time scalar in the shared region instead of a folded immediate, read once per step (the Metal backend's host-side scalars already work this way). anvil/live/server.py tails the ring and pushes Server-Sent Events to a single-page dashboard: canvas charts, TensorBoard-style ridgeline histograms over time, image grids, and live --profile counters. `--live=term` falls back to Unicode sparklines (the open IDEAS item). Runs persist to .anvil/runs/<program-hash>/ as .npy, so sweeps from experiments/run.py and --set overlay each other.
- **Demo:** diffusion.anvil on the classroom projector: digits condense out of noise in the browser every epoch. Students vote on the classifier-free guidance scale and the instructor drags the knob; samples sharpen within seconds. Then snake.anvil, with the game rendered live in the dashboard while the Q-value histogram drifts.
- **Builds on:** runtime.s RTCalls (print/show/clock), Metal host-side scalars, examples/draw/server.py, --profile tables, experiments/run.py and --set
- **Risk:** A knob blocks constant folding wherever its value was fused into kernels as an immediate, so knobs must be explicit. Needs an overflow policy for the ring. Logging images of large tensors needs subsampling.

### anvil add gpt2: packages with shape-typed semver and real pretrained weights

*wow 8/10 · months*

A package manager whose packages are Anvil source plus content-addressed weights, where each exported function's shape signature is its API. `anvil publish` refuses a minor version that changes `-> [n, 768]` to `-> [n, 1024]`, the way Elm enforces semver with types. The first package is GPT-2 124M imported from Hugging Face safetensors, so `anvil add gpt2 && anvil run chat.anvil` generates text from real weights in native NEON/AMX code.

- **Build:** anvil/pkg/: an anvil.toml manifest (tomllib), a lockfile of SHA-256 hashes, a resolver, and a cache in ~/.anvil/pkgs. `use "gpt2"` resolution goes into elaborate.py's `use` handling. The registry is a git index repo of TOML entries (crates.io-index style), with weights on GitHub Releases or the HF Hub. A `safetensors(path, name)` builtin in builtins.py parses the 8-byte length and JSON header at compile time, as npy() already does. API extraction from ide.declaration_line plus elaborator signatures with shape variables gives `anvil diff-api` and enforced version bumps. `anvil add` checks your call sites against the package's typed interface before downloading 500 MB. Packages: gpt2 (an index-notation GPT with a static 1,024-position KV cache masked by a run-time position), mnist-cnn, emnist-reader, vae. A byte-level BPE tokenizer ships as a package helper.
- **Demo:** Fresh checkout, `anvil add gpt2`, `anvil run chat.anvil`: GPT-2 text streams out, with tokens/s measured head-to-head against llama.cpp/ggml f32 and PyTorch eager on the same M3 Max. Then `anvil add mnist-cnn` and fine-tune it in ten lines.
- **Builds on:** `use "file.anvil"`, compile-time npy header parsing, save/load, anvil export, examples/transformer.anvil, the benchmarks/vs_pytorch harness
- **Risk:** Static shapes force fixed-length KV caches with masking, wasting work at short contexts. Batch-1 decoding is memory-bound GEMV, where GEMM-tuned schedules may lose to ggml. Weight hosting and licensing. Registry trust and supply-chain security.

### Shape-typed holes and program synthesis from shapes

*wow 8/10 · months*

Write `scores: [b, h, t, t] = ?` and the editor shows the hole's type, everything in scope that fits, and synthesized index-notation expressions with that shape: Agda/Idris holes, for tensors. Give a couple of input/output examples and it searches for the matching contraction (programming by example), so the shape types act as a pair programmer that can't invent a wrong shape.

- **Build:** parser.py: `?` and `?name` hole expressions. elaborate.py: a HoleVal (a cousin of PoisonVal) carrying a fresh shape variable that unifies with every later use (annotations, the parameters it is passed to, the operator it feeds) and is reported at the end as `hole ?h : f32[64, 128]` with candidates. anvil/synth.py: type-directed bottom-up enumeration (Myth/Synquid/λ²-style) over in-scope tensors, prelude functions and index-notation contractions. Einsum candidates are enumerated by matching output legs to operand dimensions of equal extent, pruned by observational equivalence on random inputs through interp.py, and ranked by size, name agreement and 'uses every input'. A PBE mode checks candidates against example tensors the user gives. LSP code actions fill the hole.
- **Demo:** In class, type `scores: [b, h, t, t] = ?` with q and k of shape [b, t, h, d] in scope. The top suggestion is `scores[b, h, i, j] = sum q[b, i, h, d] * k[b, j, h, d] / sqrt(D)`. Then `y: [n, c_out, 26, 26] = ?` with an image and a 3×3 kernel in scope rediscovers the convolution comprehension.
- **Builds on:** shape unification in fn signatures, ide.py Recorder, PoisonVal cascade suppression, interp.py as a fast evaluator
- **Risk:** Combinatorial blowup and ambiguity when dimensions share a size (t = d = 64), so ranking decides whether results feel smart or random. PBE beyond 2–3 operands gets slow.

### Penrose diagrams: index notation as live tensor-network pictures

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Every index-notation line renders as a tensor-network diagram as you type. Tensors are nodes, contracted indices are edges, output indices are dangling legs, and `where` shifts become convolution connectors. Autodiff becomes diagram surgery: ∂/∂W deletes W's node and its open legs become the gradient. A contraction-order optimizer shows, and applies, the cheapest evaluation order for multi-tensor sums, so the picture doubles as a performance tool.

- **Build:** anvil/tn.py builds a hypergraph from the elaborated comprehension (CompCtx index bindings, the LCA rule, inferred ranges) and lays it out to SVG with a force/spectral layout. It shows up as a webview in editors/vscode and as a Studio pane. A 'differentiate' view is driven by autodiff.Backprop's per-load rules. Contraction order comes from dynamic programming over operand subsets (opt_einsum's 'optimal', Pfeifer et al. 2014 netcon) with FLOP and memory cost per order. The result is offered as a rewrite and as an optimizer pass for sums of 3+ operands, which the elaborator currently evaluates in the order written.
- **Demo:** Type the attention formula and watch the diagram form. Click 'grad wrt q': the q node vanishes and the legs reconnect into the backward formula. Then `y[i] = sum A[i,j]*B[j,k]*C[k,l]*x[l]` gets reordered right-to-left, and anvil cost shows the FLOP drop live.
- **Builds on:** index-notation elaboration (range inference, where, ...), autodiff rules, cost.py, the VS Code extension
- **Risk:** Large or batched (`...`) expressions clutter the layout. Gathers and affine indices have no standard diagram glyph, so one has to be invented and kept clear.

### Explain this kernel: provenance and narrated code generation

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

`anvil explain mnist.anvil:27` (also an LSP hover and a Studio pane) writes a plain-English, math-typeset account of any kernel. It covers which source lines and which derivative rule produced it, how it is scheduled, which register holds which slice of which tensor, and what limits its speed, with an animation of the register tile sweeping across the matrices. In effect, a compilers textbook chapter generated from your own program.

- **Build:** Provenance in ir.Kernel: `origin: [(span, op, role)]`, with role forward, ∂ wrt X by rule R, or optimizer update. elaborate.py sets it; autodiff.Backprop records the rule (∂(x@W)/∂W = xᵀ·dy, max routing, scatter); optimize.fuse merges it. A symbolic register map in backend/kernel.py Lowering: each VReg carries the tensor slice it holds (v5 = t4[i+0, j+0:4]), so the asm gets complete «…» annotations automatically instead of the hand-written ones in the README. anvil/explain.py composes the account: math (KaTeX in HTML, Unicode in the terminal) → loops → Config (R×U tile, vec_out/vec_red, parallel split, LICM-hoisted terms) → cost (arithmetic intensity against the M3 Max roofline) → what's left on the table (e.g. scalar `ldr s` A-panel loads, PLAN next-step #3). An SVG/Canvas animation generator is driven by the real loop extents and strides, including the CUDA thread→element map and Metal threadgroups.
- **Demo:** Hover k208 in the asm: 'Gradient of layer 1's weights (rule dW = xᵀ·dz) fused with the SGD update; the gradient never touches memory. 784×128 outputs reduced over 64 examples, 4×16 tile in v5–v20, 8 threads by rows, ~70% of NEON peak.' Next to it, the tile marches across W.
- **Builds on:** Config.describe(), check_description(), autodiff's ∂-labels, cost.py, the asm's per-kernel headers
- **Risk:** Provenance must stay correct through 8 rounds of fusion/CSE, and a wrong explanation is worse than none. Narration should be template-based and golden-tested.

### Errors that fix themselves: type-guided repair search

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Every diagnostic carries machine-applicable edits. For shape errors, the compiler searches for the smallest edit that makes the whole program type-check: a transpose, swapped operands, permuted indices, a corrected Linear fan-in, the flattened size after a conv stack. It shows the fix as a diff and applies it with --fix. Seminal (Lerner et al., PLDI 2007) did this for OCaml type errors; this does it for tensor shapes, aimed at what is probably the most common shape bug in deep-learning code.

- **Build:** diagnostics.py: `Fix(edits=[(span, replacement)], applicability)` on AnvilError, rendered as rustc-style diffs. `anvil check --fix` and LSP codeAction. anvil/repair.py: on a shape error, mutate the AST near the blamed spans (operand swap, `.T`, index permutation in comprehensions, a `Linear(a, b)` argument taken from the observed dimension, an inserted `.reshape(n, -1)`, `mean(...)` around a non-scalar loss, `argmax`, i32/f32 casts). Re-elaborate the candidates in a process pool (about 50 ms each, forked after parse and prelude), keep those where the whole program checks, and rank by edit distance and downstream changes. examples/errors/ grows to 50 classic bugs, each a case in tests/test_errors.py.
- **Demo:** A LeNet with `Linear(400, 120)` after convs that produce 16×4×4: 'error: Linear expects 400 inputs, got 256 (= 16·4·4 from max_pool2d at line 12)' with 'fix: Linear(256, 120)'. Press enter and it trains.
- **Builds on:** diagnostics.py rendering, the 'did you mean' helper, call-chain labels, examples/errors, tests/test_errors.py
- **Risk:** A repair can type-check and still be wrong (W.T versus swapping operands). Auto-apply only when exactly one minimal repair exists; otherwise list them ranked.

### Training receipts: verifiable, replayable training runs

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Because Anvil training is bit-deterministic, a run can emit a receipt: a Merkle chain of state hashes every N steps, bound to the program, the data and the seed. Anyone can then verify a trained model by replaying a few random segments in seconds. Proof-of-Learning (Jia et al., IEEE S&P 2021) struggles because GPU training isn't reproducible; Anvil makes it exact, which is ideal for an autograder.

- **Build:** `anvil run --receipt` hashes the keyframe set (the same liveness analysis as Rewind) every N steps with SHA-256 using the ARMv8 SHA extensions (sha256h/sha256su0) in runtime.s, plus a program hash (source, compiler version, flags), data file hashes and the seed. `anvil verify model.weights receipt.json --spot 5` restores segment-start keyframes, replays N steps each and compares hashes. A reproducible mode pins Anvil's own GEMMs (ANVIL_BLAS=0) and fixed reduction trees so results match across Apple chips and OS releases. CI compares runs on M1 and M3 runners.
- **Demo:** The class autograder: a student submits digits.weights and a receipt. `anvil verify` replays 5 random 100-step segments in a few seconds and confirms the weights came from that program on MNIST, or points to the segment where they didn't.
- **Builds on:** thread-count-independent determinism (already tested), save/load checkpoints, counter-based RNG, Rewind's keyframe liveness
- **Risk:** Accelerate/AMX and Metal/MPS may not be bit-reproducible across chips or OS versions, so the verifiable mode is CPU-only with Anvil's own kernels. The prover must store or regenerate keyframes.

### Precision lab: shadow execution in f64/bf16/f16, plus anvil gradcheck

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Run a program with a float64 shadow and get a ranked list of the kernels that lose precision: the error each one adds versus the error it inherits, with Herbie-style rewrites. Switch the shadow to bf16/f16 emulation to find out whether the model will train in half precision, which kernel breaks first, and which must keep f32 accumulators. That derives a mixed-precision policy before anyone writes an f16 backend.

- **Build:** anvil/numerics.py: a native run with --check-style dumps of each kernel's f32 inputs and outputs at sampled steps. Each kernel is re-run on interp.py (it already takes float_dtype) in f64 from the same f32 inputs for local error in ULPs, and end-to-end for global error. A rounding-emulation mode in interp.py rounds every store (or only accumulators) to bf16/f16 with round-to-nearest-even, optionally stochastic rounding. A catalog of rewrites (log(1+x)→log1p, 1−sigmoid(x)→sigmoid(−x), Welford variance, softplus forms) is offered as fixes. `anvil gradcheck` (an open IDEAS item): central differences in f64 per parameter, reported as relative-error tables.
- **Demo:** transformer.anvil under bf16 store emulation diverges at a few hundred steps. The lab ranks the long reductions (the sum of exps in log_softmax, the means in layer_norm) as the culprits, proposes f32 accumulation for exactly those kernels, and the bf16 curve tracks f32 again.
- **Builds on:** interp.py's float_dtype, --check per-kernel hooks, the interpreter as oracle, prelude.anvil
- **Risk:** Native and interpreter reduction orders differ, so local-error attribution must feed both the same inputs per kernel. Dumping every kernel's outputs is heavy, so steps must be sampled.

### anvil lsp: a persistent, incremental language server for every editor

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Replace spawning a compiler on every keystroke with a long-lived LSP server that caches the elaborated prelude and re-elaborates only what changed. Then add what a real IDE has: go-to-definition into prelude.anvil, find references, rename, completion ranked by shape fit, signature help showing bound shape variables, semantic coloring of output versus reduced indices, and code lenses with each line's FLOPs and estimated time. Neovim, Helix, Zed and Emacs get it for free.

- **Build:** anvil/lsp.py (JSON-RPC over stdio, no dependencies; `anvil lsp`): a document store and debounced analysis with cancellation. Elaborator snapshots after the prelude and after each top-level declaration, keyed by content hash; this also delivers the IDEAS item 'cache the elaborated prelude'. Recorder in ide.py gains binding identities (for references and rename) and per-statement scopes (for completion). Completion is ranked by shape fit: after `x @` it offers tensors whose leading dimension equals x's last. signatureHelp shows the unification substitution at the call site ('n = 64, k = 10'). semanticTokens come from the LCA binding rule (output index, reduced index, param, data, temp). codeLens comes from cost.py, and documentSymbol gives model trees with parameter counts. editors/vscode/extension.js becomes a thin LSP client.
- **Demo:** The same session in Neovim and VS Code: completion inside `y[i] = sum W[i, j] * x[` lists only tensors with a 784-long dimension, the reduced `j` is a different color from the output `i`, rename `l1` → `encoder` works across three files, and the code lens above `minimize` shows FLOPs per step and an estimated time.
- **Builds on:** ide.py Recorder/hovers/inlays, `anvil check --json --stdin`, editors/vscode/extension.js, cost.py
- **Risk:** The elaborator is a staging interpreter that runs the whole program, compile-time loops included, so true incrementality is limited. Snapshot-per-declaration plus the existing ~50 ms full run is the practical floor; the char-RNN's 3.3 s compile shows the worst case.

### Flame graphs by Anvil call stack, Perfetto timelines, measured roofline

*wow 6/10 · days*

`anvil run --profile=trace` writes a Perfetto timeline with every kernel on every worker thread and every AMX/MPS call. It also writes a flame graph keyed by the Anvil call stack (train → net.forward → l1 → Linear.forward), even though everything was inlined and fused. A calibrated roofline puts each kernel against the M3 Max's measured ceilings, so 'why is this slow' gets a picture.

- **Build:** aarch64.py and the runtime.s thread pool: per-worker chunk timestamps (cntvct_el0) into per-thread buffers, written out as Chrome Trace Event JSON. elaborate.py records each kernel's inline call chain (already built for the 'in this call to forward' error labels). Fused kernels are split across their origins by FLOP share from cost.py. Collapsed-stack output for speedscope/flamegraph.pl, and Brendan Gregg-style differential flame graphs between two runs or --set configs. `anvil roofline --calibrate` runs microbenchmarks: STREAM-style bandwidth, NEON FMA peak, AMX sgemm peak. Later: per-dispatch Metal timestamps via MTLCounterSampleBuffer, and CUDA via cudaEvent and NVTX ranges for nsys.
- **Demo:** transformer.anvil in Perfetto: forward and backward per layer and per head, with idle threads during a serial kernel showing up as visible gaps. A diff flame graph between ANVIL_BLAS=0 and ANVIL_BLAS=1 shows exactly where AMX wins.
- **Builds on:** --profile (cntvct tables, _anvil_rt_profile_report), the runtime.s thread pool, call-chain labels, cost.py
- **Risk:** Timestamping every chunk perturbs tiny kernels. Attribution inside fused kernels is an estimate. Metal and CUDA timing take longer than the CPU path.

### anvil fmt + anvil lint: a formatter checked by the compiler and lints for ML bugs

*wow 5/10 · weeks*

A canonical formatter that aligns index-notation equations like math, where every rewrite is checked by compiling before and after and comparing the kernel IR. Alongside it, a linter for bugs that silently ruin training: double softmax, log(softmax(x)), sum-not-mean losses that scale with batch size, unshuffled training batches, dropout left on at evaluation, labels outside the class count, exp of an unbounded value. Every lint comes with a machine-applicable fix.

- **Build:** lexer.py/parser.py keep trivia (comments and blank lines are currently dropped) to give a lossless CST. anvil/fmt.py is a pretty-printer on Wadler's 'prettier printer' document algebra, with idempotence and IR-equivalence property tests over every example and fuzzed programs. anvil/lint.py runs over the AST and kernel IR: pattern lints, plus interval abstract interpretation (ranges from loaders: idx/255 ∈ [0,1]; softmax ∈ [0,1]; exp(x − max x) ≤ 1) to flag possible exp overflow, log of a possibly-zero value, division by a possibly-zero sum, and sqrt at 0 in a backward pass. It also covers the IDEAS items for labels out of range and a loss that cannot go down. `--fix`, LSP code actions, and fmt as LSP document formatting.
- **Demo:** A 25-line classifier with three classic bugs trains to about 11%. `anvil lint --fix` rewrites three lines and it trains to 97%.
- **Builds on:** parser.py, ir, optimize.py read/write sets, the existing 'parameter never trained' warnings, diagnostics.py
- **Risk:** Preserving comments means refactoring the lexer and parser. Interval analysis returns ⊤ for anything that touches params, so the numeric lints must stay pattern-driven to avoid noise.

## teaching-demos (16)

### Classroom Cluster: the lecture hall trains one GPT together

*wow 10/10 · moonshot*

Every phone in the room becomes a worker in one data-parallel training job, and the projector shows the cluster: throughput per phone, slow phones, the loss curve. Because Anvil is deterministic, synchronous mode produces weights whose hash matches the instructor's laptop run of the same sharded program. The instructor then switches to async SGD, local SGD and DiLoCo, and the class sees what each trade-off costs.

- **Build:** anvil/dist/coordinator.py: a stdlib-only asyncio websocket server (hand-written framing). It assigns shards by batch index and does a fixed-order f32 all-reduce so results are bit-reproducible. Modes: synchronous SGD; Hogwild-style async with staleness tracking; local SGD and DiLoCo (Douillard et al. 2023: inner AdamW steps, then an outer Nesterov step every H steps); top-k and int8 gradient compression. Compiler side: export `fn loss_and_grad` (pyapi already returns gradients) and keep the optimizer on the coordinator, or add outer-optimizer blocks to prelude.anvil. web/cluster/: the phone worker (wasm SIMD or WebGPU from idea 1) and a projector dashboard. `anvil run --shards N` on one machine produces the reference hash. Milestones: MNIST in synchronous mode (weeks after idea 1), then a char-transformer on the syllabus, then a GPT of about 10M parameters with DiLoCo.
- **Demo:** 'Open the QR code.' 60 phones train the transformer on the course notes for 10 minutes, and the final weight hash matches the one on the slide that the laptop produced the night before. Then one row of students switches to airplane mode: synchronous training stalls on the dashboard while DiLoCo keeps going.
- **Builds on:** bit-identical results, the counter-based RNG (any shard can regenerate its noise), anvil.function returning gradients, transformer.anvil, the wasm/WebGPU backends from idea 1
- **Risk:** Lecture-hall WiFi: 10M parameters is 40 MB per sync, so this needs DiLoCo-style rare syncs plus compression. Phones throttle when hot and pause background tabs. WebGPU and wasm round differently, so synchronous mode must use one backend without relaxed SIMD to stay bit-exact. Phone speeds vary widely.

### Whiteboard to model: handwritten math that compiles and trains

*wow 10/10 · moonshot*

Write `L = mean_i ( log Σ_j exp(z_ij) − z_{i,y_i} )` with an Apple Pencil, and Anvil reads the formula, turns it into index notation and trains softmax regression on MNIST: the 10-714 homework, written by hand. The twist is that the type checker acts as the language model, rejecting every misreading whose indices or shapes don't fit together.

- **Build:** Extend examples/draw/text_reader.py's stroke grouping and beam search to 2-D layout (subscripts, superscripts, Σ with limits, fraction bars) using a symbol-layout tree, as in the CROHME competition. Train a symbol CNN in Anvil (examples/math_symbols.anvil) on HASYv2 and CROHME symbols, the way letters.anvil was trained. anvil/mathparse.py turns layout trees into Anvil AST: free indices on the left become outputs and all others are reduced, which is exactly Anvil's rule. The beam is rescored by running elaborate.py on each candidate and dropping parses with bad shapes or indices. examples/draw/math.html is a canvas page showing the recognized LaTeX, the generated Anvil and the training run.
- **Demo:** The instructor writes the softmax loss on an iPad mirrored to the projector. The page shows the LaTeX, the one-line Anvil program, and then MNIST test error falling to 8% within about a second: the handwritten formula has trained a model.
- **Builds on:** the bin/draw --text pipeline (stroke grouping, beam search over segmentations, dictionary rescoring), letters.anvil, index notation being literally the math, examples/softmax_regression.anvil, the elaborator as a fast checker
- **Risk:** Recognizing handwritten 2-D math is hard: strong CROHME systems read roughly 60-70% of expressions fully correctly. The bet is that restricting the grammar to index-notation math and filtering with the type checker closes the gap. Notation is ambiguous (implicit sums, subscripts that are indices).

### Anvil Everywhere: the whole compiler in a browser tab, with a WebAssembly SIMD backend

*wow 9/10 · weeks*

The compiler is dependency-free Python, so it runs unchanged in Pyodide. Add a backend that writes WebAssembly bytes directly, with no LLVM or Emscripten, and every student's phone can compile and train Anvil programs. Scan a QR code, edit the MNIST program and train it in the tab in seconds. Every other teaching demo here builds on this, because it removes all installation.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/wasm.py: a direct binary encoder (LEB128; type, func, memory, export and code sections). It lowers kernel IR with the same schedules as anvil/backend/kernel.py (vec-out, vec-red, R×U register tiles) onto SIMD128 f32x4. NEON's .4s maps onto it lane for lane. FMA uses Relaxed SIMD f32x4.relaxed_madd where the browser has it, and mul+add in deterministic mode. The exp/log/tanh polynomials are ported from vecmath.py. anvil/backend/runtime_wasm.js provides the runtime.s services: print, show, idx/gzip through DecompressionStream, the counter RNG from anvil_host.h, and checkpoints in IndexedDB. It also runs a Web Worker thread pool over SharedArrayBuffer with Atomics.wait. arena.py lays out one linear memory. web/playground/: a CodeMirror 6 editor wired to ide.py's `check --json` for live errors and shape inlay hints, with examples/ as a gallery. Phase 2: a WGSL dialect next to the CUDA and MSL generators (cuda.py, metal.py) for WebGPU, which Safari 26 and Chrome on Android support.
- **Demo:** The first slide shows a QR code. 80 phones open the playground, compile examples/mnist.anvil in the tab and pass 96% in a few seconds with no server. Then bin/draw runs as a static web page with no Python behind it.
- **Builds on:** the pure-Python compiler (parse/elaborate/autodiff/optimize need nothing native), kernel.py schedules, vecmath.py, arena.py, the anvil_host.h RNG, ide.py JSON diagnostics, examples/draw
- **Risk:** Pyodide is about a 10 MB download, and CPython on wasm compiles 2-4x slower (unrolled static for programs suffer most). On x86 laptops wasm engines have only 16 SIMD registers, so the 4x16 tile spills: ship two tile variants and pick one at load time. Threads need COOP/COEP headers. Relaxed SIMD breaks bit-identical results across devices. MNIST is about 11 MB to download over classroom WiFi: cache it in a service worker or use a subset.

### Compiler Theater: watch every pass animate, from source line to register

*wow 9/10 · weeks*

`anvil explain --trace` records every decision the compiler makes, and a web viewer replays it like a film. Kernels appear under the source line that made them, autodiff grows the backward pass in red, fusion merges kernels, the memory arena packs buffer lifetimes like Tetris, and the 4x16 register tile lights up. Compiler Explorer (godbolt) shows the output; this shows why the compiler produced it.

- **Build:** anvil/trace.py, a Tracer threaded through driver.compile_source, collects these events. From elaborate.py: each kernel with its span and inlined call chain. From autodiff.Backprop.run: each backward kernel tagged with its forward kernel and the rule used (injective reduction or scatter-accumulate). From optimize.Optimizer.run: wrappers around coalesce, inline, simplify, cse, fuse, dce, reuse_dying_inputs and collapse_loops. Every rewrite already passes through the `changed` setter, so that is the hook, plus a new Kernel.origin field holding parent kernel ids and the pass name. From kernel.choose_configs: the candidate schedules and why each was rejected (feasible_vec_out, spills). From mir.allocate_with_spills: live intervals. From arena.py: buffer lifetimes and their offsets. web/theater/ has a timeline scrubber; source, kernel-DAG, IR (fmt_kernel) and asm panes cross-highlighted by span; an arena packing animation; and a view of the 32 NEON registers. There is also a terminal mode: `anvil explain --pass fuse` prints before/after diffs.
- **Demo:** Type `loss = cross_entropy(x @ W + b, y); minimize loss with sgd(lr=0.1)` and press play. Three kernels drop out of the source line, autodiff adds about eight red backward kernels, and fusion merges them until the SGD update sits inside the weight-gradient matmul, so the gradient is never stored. The arena then packs checkers' scratch memory from 332 KB into 47 KB, and the micro-kernel's 16 accumulators light up in the register file.
- **Builds on:** Optimizer's single `changed` funnel, fmt_kernel/fmt_program, the per-kernel asm headers in aarch64.py, arena.py, the ide.py recorder and spans, the inlined call-chain labels in diagnostics
- **Risk:** Kernel identity must stay stable through CSE, fusion and in-place reuse. Large programs such as the 1,252-kernel char-RNN need grouping by source line and zooming. The trace format becomes something every pass has to keep up to date.

### Glass Box GPT: an interpretability lab for a model you trained ten seconds ago

*wow 9/10 · months*

Train a 2-layer attention-only transformer on repeated sequences in seconds. Then find its induction heads, run the logit lens, patch activations and train a sparse autoencoder on its MLP, each as a short Anvil program, because grad differentiates anything and every intermediate is a named tensor. Students reproduce the headline results of mechanistic interpretability in one lab session.

- **Build:** A `tap(x, "name")` builtin (builtins.py, elaborate.py) exposes an intermediate as an output buffer (EXTERN_KINDS, like anvil.function results), and `patch(x, "name")` reads a replacement from an optional input. pyapi.py and the playground use them to capture and intervene. examples/interp/: induction.anvil (prefix-matching and copying scores per head, Olsson et al. 2022), logit_lens.anvil (nostalgebraist 2020), patching.anvil (causal tracing, Meng et al. 2022), sae.anvil (an L1 sparse autoencoder, Bricken et al. 2023), attribution.anvil (integrated gradients via grad). A web/glassbox/ viewer shows attention patterns and feature activations over text.
- **Demo:** Train the toy transformer live (about 10 s) and click 'find induction heads': the head-score grid lights up one head in layer 2. Ablate that head, and the loss on the repeated second half rises to the first half's level, while the loss on the first half doesn't move.
- **Builds on:** examples/transformer.anvil, grad with respect to anything (as used in dream.anvil), anvil.function returning several results, index-notation attention
- **Risk:** Models trained in seconds may not form clean circuits for every seed; choose seeds and show the phase change in the loss. Sparse autoencoders on tiny models are noisy. tap and patch must not disturb fusion in normal builds.

### Anvil Arcade: in-browser games where the class trains and fights AIs

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Three multiplayer games built on existing examples. In 'Fool the Net', each player gets a real digit and an L-infinity ink budget and paints a perturbation by hand, while the live gradient from dream.anvil shows which pixels matter; the class leaderboard ranks the smallest change that flips the network's reading, and then PGD shows the machine's answer. In 'Teach the Snake', snake.anvil's DQN trains on each phone with rewards and features the student picks, and every snake races on the projector. In 'Draw Together', the class invents categories, draws examples, and a model retrains on everyone's sketches every 30 seconds.

- **Build:** web/arcade/ runs on the wasm playground (idea 1). Games call compiled Anvil functions through a JS port of pyapi.py's calling convention: a grad(loss, x) saliency map, and cross_entropy on the 99% digits_model.anvil. examples/arcade/fool.anvil: FGSM and PGD from dream.anvil, rewritten as functions. snake_agent.anvil: snake.anvil split into step, act and learn functions, with the board as a tensor. draw_together.anvil: fine-tunes the digits CNN's last layer on new categories. examples/arcade/hub.py: a small stdlib-only websocket hub for scores and the shared dataset, reusing examples/draw/server.py's MNIST-style stroke rendering.
- **Demo:** 'Fool the Net' on 60 phones: within 3 minutes the class finds that a 7 reads as a 1 after changing eight barely visible pixels. The instructor then shows that the same perturbations also fool a second network trained with a different seed, and asks the class why.
- **Builds on:** examples/dream.anvil (input gradients, adversarial digits), examples/snake.anvil, the bin/draw stroke pipeline and digits_model.anvil, grad of anything
- **Risk:** Depends on idea 1. DQN training speed on phones is uncertain. Classroom WiFi has to carry the hub traffic. Drawn content may need moderation.

### Silicon Microscope: roofline, pipeline and cache views of the generated kernels

*wow 8/10 · months*

A lab bench for a systems class. Drag a slider to change a kernel's register tile and watch the assembly regenerate, the register file fill, the kernel's point climb the roofline toward the FMA limit, and llvm-mca's bottleneck move from the load port to the FMA units. Students finally see why the 4x16 tile runs at about 90 GFLOP/s and a bigger one falls off a cliff.

- **Build:** anvil/perfmodel.py: per-kernel arithmetic intensity from cost.py, plus machine limits (FMA throughput, stream bandwidth, AMX sgemm) measured once by generated microbenchmarks and cached. Static throughput comes from piping each kernel's inner loop (the Lk*_loop labels in aarch64.py's output) through llvm-mca with LLVM's Apple scheduling model. A set-associative L1/L2 simulator (M3: 128 KB 8-way L1D, 16 MB L2) works directly from the IR's Affine offsets, so access traces come from the math without running anything. `anvil tune` (listed in IDEAS, not done) enumerates kernel.choose_configs candidates (R×U, vectorized axis, row-tiled vec-red, thread grain) and times them. A `--config k86=8x16` override. web/microscope/ shows the roofline, a register map (from mir.allocate_with_spills) and a cache heat map. Stretch: real cache-miss counts from Apple's kperf PMU interface.
- **Demo:** Start the MNIST layer-1 matmul with a 1x4 tile: its point sits on the memory-bound slope. Drag to 4x16 and it reaches about 90 GFLOP/s at the FMA roof. Drag to 8x16 and the register map turns red with spills as the point drops. Switch to the AMX path and it leaves the NEON roofline entirely.
- **Builds on:** kernel.py's Config/choose_configs and register tiling, mir.py register allocation, cost.py, --profile, the Affine access form in ir.py, blas.py (AMX)
- **Risk:** llvm-mca's Apple models are approximate. kperf is private and can break between macOS versions. The Python cache simulator must sample tiles to stay fast.

### Build Your Own Anvil: a course carved from the real compiler

*wow 8/10 · months*

Assignments are generated from the production compiler by stripping out marked solution regions. Students write the real autodiff pass, the real fusion pass and the real NEON micro-kernel, and the repo's own test suite grades them. The big moment: a student's 150-line autodiff, dropped into Anvil, trains the GPT in examples/transformer.anvil at full speed.

- **Build:** Solution markers (`# <<student>> … # <</student>>`) in autodiff.py (Backprop.run), optimize.py (fuse, cse, dce), elaborate.py (index-range inference), backend/kernel.py (the vec-out schedule) and arena.py. tools/make_course.py, in the manner of otter-grader and nbgrader, generates skeleton repos and maps the existing tests (test_gradcheck.py, test_optimizer.py, test_arena.py, and test_examples.py's match against the interpreter) to assignments, with hidden variants. A micro-anvil/ reference of about 2,000 lines (lexer → index notation → kernel IR → autodiff → C) covers the early weeks. Assignments: A1 shapes and index notation; A2 parser; A3 elaboration; A4 autodiff; A5 fusion; A6 a C or wasm backend; A7 a GEMM micro-kernel with a GFLOP/s target; A8 a game AI under a FLOP budget.
- **Demo:** Week 6 demo day. Each student's fusion pass is plugged into the real compiler, and the projector shows the CNN's seconds per epoch for every student's pass, between 2.55 s (-O0) and the reference's 0.85 s. One student beats the reference.
- **Builds on:** the modular pipeline (driver → elaborate → autodiff → optimize → backend), 289 tests including gradcheck and interpreter-equivalence checks, the experiments ablation (-O0 vs. optimized). Compare CMU 10-714's Needle, Cornell's minitorch and micrograd; none goes from index notation all the way to assembly.
- **Risk:** The production code is dense and was not written for students; it needs refactoring around the marked regions. Solutions leak once the repo is public, so keep hidden test variants and a private solution branch. The markers must be kept in sync as the compiler changes.

### The Living Book: an interactive textbook from math to metal

*wow 8/10 · months*

A book where every code cell runs in the browser and every `const` automatically becomes a slider, using the compiler's existing --set NAME=VALUE. Readers drag the learning rate and watch the loss explode before the next paragraph explains why. It goes from index notation through MNIST, CNNs, GPT, diffusion and RL, then explains how the compiler built each of them, with the Compiler Theater and Scope embedded.

- **Build:** book/ holds chapters in Markdown with anvil cells. tools/book.py builds a static site (no framework) on the wasm playground. `const` declarations collected by ide.py's recorder become sliders, and string consts (like `OPT = "adam"`, which already compare at compile time) become dropdowns. A canvas renderer for `show` and a `plot` builtin draw live curves. Chapters embed the Compiler Theater (idea 2), Scope (idea 3) and exercises graded by anvil grade (idea 9). 'Break it' sections contain planted bugs. Every figure is generated by an Anvil program, and CI rebuilds the book, so it cannot drift from the code.
- **Demo:** Chapter 1 opens on the README's MNIST program with sliders for width and learning rate. Dragging the learning rate from 0.1 to 3.0 sends the loss curve to nan in real time, and Scope's red marker points at the exploding layer.
- **Builds on:** `const` and --set, the ide.py recorder, show, the example gallery, and ideas 1, 2, 3 and 9
- **Risk:** Writing a good textbook takes longer than building the tools. Browser compute limits the later chapters (diffusion, letters) to pretrained weights plus fine-tuning.

### anvil live: edit the model while it keeps training

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Save the file and the running program picks up the new code without losing a step. Parameters and optimizer state carry over by name and shape, a widened layer grows by Net2Net's function-preserving initialization, and a new layer starts as the identity. It is TensorFlow Playground with real code, and a live-coding performance for lecture.

- **Build:** anvil/live.py: a file watcher that recompiles (caching the elaborated prelude, an IDEAS item) and swaps the running program. A full-state snapshot routine in runtime.s saves parameters, optimizer state, loop counters and the RNG stream counter (save only covers parameters today); a signal triggers it at the next loop head. State carries over by model path and shape, using Net2WiderNet and Net2DeeperNet (Chen, Goodfellow, Shlens 2016) for changed widths and inserted layers. Live sparklines in the terminal, and a browser panel with decision boundaries for 2-D data (spirals) and loss curves.
- **Demo:** spirals.anvil trains on the projector with its decision boundary animating. The instructor changes tanh to relu and saves: the boundary snaps into polygons without a restart. Adding a layer barely moves the loss. Swapping adam for sgd(lr=0.5) visibly bends the loss curve.
- **Builds on:** save/load checkpoints, the counter-based RNG (the stream position is one integer), named model parameters, compile times of 0.3 s, the REPL, the `anvil watch` and sparkline items in IDEAS
- **Risk:** Carrying state across arbitrary edits (renames, reshapes) is ambiguous. The snapshot must capture every loop-carried variable the compiler created. Recompiling unrolled programs is too slow to feel live.

### Anvil in your hand: iPhone and microcontroller targets with TinyML demos

*wow 8/10 · months*

The AArch64 runtime only calls libc, zlib and pthreads, so the same assembly should run on an iPhone with only a different link step. A portable C backend with the static arena takes Anvil down to a $30 microcontroller with no heap at all. Students train a gesture model on the laptop in two seconds and flash it onto a board they wave in the air.

- **Build:** `anvil build/export --target ios` in backend/toolchain.py (`xcrun -sdk iphoneos clang -target arm64-apple-ios17`, with data paths from the app bundle), plus a generated Swift package wrapping export.py's header. A sample SwiftUI app has the digit reader and fine-tunes the last layer on 20 samples of the user's own handwriting, on the phone. anvil/backend/c.py: a portable C backend derived from cuda.py's emulation path, with kernels as loops, arena.py's plan as one static array, and weights as const arrays in flash. `--target cortex-m4` uses CMSIS-DSP for the GEMM tile. anvil/quantize.py: int8 post-training quantization, calibrated on the interpreter, for chips without an FPU. An IMU capture sketch writes CSV for csv().
- **Demo:** 'Magic wand': students wave an Arduino Nano 33 BLE Sense in letter shapes, recording 30 seconds per gesture. Anvil trains a 1-D CNN in 2 s and exports a 20 KB C file, and the flashed board lights up on the right letter. Then the same idea on an iPhone that learns your handwriting in under a second, offline.
- **Builds on:** runtime.s (libc-only), anvil export with weights baked in, arena.py's static memory plan (exactly what TFLite Micro's arena wants), the --cuda-emulate C++ path, csv(). Compare TensorFlow Lite Micro and Edge Impulse.
- **Risk:** Getting hardware to a whole class is a logistics problem. iOS needs code signing and provisioning. int8 accuracy and the C backend's speed on M-class cores are open questions. The Cortex-M4's 256 KB of RAM limits model size.

### Anvil Arena: speedruns and bot tournaments judged by the compiler

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Competitions where the compiler is the referee. `anvil cost` counts training budgets in FLOPs statically, so a student on a 2019 laptop competes fairly with one on an M3 Max, and determinism means every leaderboard entry reproduces exactly from its source and seed. Tracks include MNIST under 10k parameters, a char-GPT bits-per-byte speedrun under a fixed FLOP budget (in the spirit of modded-nanogpt and airbench), and a checkers bot ladder.

- **Build:** anvil/arena/: `anvil submit` runs a program in a sandbox with a read-only data path, has the scorer supply held-out test labels, and records the weight hash. FLOPs are counted by cost.py from static loop counts, plus a runtime counter in the profiler build for while loops and early stopping. A leaderboard page shows accuracy-vs-FLOPs Pareto fronts. Bot interfaces are type signatures checked by the elaborator, for example `fn evaluate(planes: [n, 4, 8, 4]) -> [n]` for checkers critics and an `act` function for Snake. A tournament runner reuses checkers.anvil's move generator and search for round-robins with Elo or TrueSkill ratings. Replays are rendered in the browser from the existing draw routine.
- **Demo:** Friday 'Checkers Cup': 40 student critics, each trained by self-play under the same FLOP budget, play a 780-game round robin on the projector in about a minute. The bracket and Elo table animate, and the winner's games replay move by move.
- **Builds on:** cost.py static FLOP analysis, bit-identical runs, checkers.anvil (rules verified against an independent implementation, search, TD learning), snake.anvil, save/load
- **Risk:** Static FLOP counts miss data-dependent loops and runtime gathers, so runtime counters are needed. Untrusted native programs must run in containers on the grading server. Submissions could load precomputed weights from files, so restrict load and npy.

### Anvil Scope: a time-travel tensor debugger that steps through backprop

*wow 7/10 · months*

Anvil runs are bit-identical (counter-based RNG) and an MNIST epoch takes 0.086 s, so going back to step 1,234 just means running again; nothing has to be recorded. Scope lets students scrub through training like a video and click any tensor to see its values and gradient. They can step through the backward pass one kernel at a time, with each chain-rule step written as math next to the real numbers.

- **Build:** Generalize the --check runtime routine, which already scans every f32 tensor a kernel writes in an unfused build, into `--dump-at STEP[:KERNEL]` (backend/aarch64.py, runtime.s) that writes those tensors to a file. anvil/scope.py drives the reruns and bisects to the first non-finite element. This extends --check to report the last good step and the chain of kernels that broke it. It also diffs two runs (a seed or a code change) tensor by tensor. Backward kernels from autodiff.py are shown in KaTeX index notation beside the forward kernel each came from. `anvil gradcheck` (listed in IDEAS, not done) shows per-element finite-difference heat maps. The UI in web/scope/ reuses the Theater's graph view. Small programs take their values from an interp.py hook in exec_kernel.
- **Demo:** Train the VAE, drag the step slider to 2,000, click the encoder's sigma and watch the reparameterized gradient flow back through `z ~ normal(mu, sigma)`. Then remove the max-subtraction from softmax. Scope jumps to the step and element where the first inf appears and highlights the source line and the backward kernel that spread it.
- **Builds on:** bit-identical execution, the counter-based RNG, the --check scanning routine and its unfused build, interp.py, autodiff.py's kernel structure, save/load
- **Risk:** Unfused dumps round differently from the fused production run; show both and label them. Big programs produce gigabytes of tensors, so dumps must be lazy, per kernel. Rerunning from step zero is only cheap for classroom-sized models.

### Typed holes and shape-directed synthesis (Hazel for tensors)

*wow 7/10 · months*

Put `?` anywhere in an Anvil program and the compiler reports the hole's type, such as `? : [n, 128] -> [n, 10]`, and offers well-typed fillers from scope. Agda, GHC's valid hole fits and Hazel (Omar et al., POPL 2019) do this for functional programs. It turns the shape system into a teaching tool and a real type-theory lesson: unification, constraint solving and synthesis on actual ML code.

- **Build:** parser.py: `?` and `?name` hole expressions. elaborate.py: holes become fresh shape/type variables, solved by the same unification as shape-polymorphic fn signatures. Add symbolic compile-time integers so `Linear(784, ?)` can be solved from later uses; this is a first step toward symbolic shapes. anvil/synth.py: an enumerative, type-directed search over prelude functions, model fields, @, .T, reshape and pipes, ranked by size and by cost.py FLOPs, each candidate checked by re-elaborating. `anvil check --json` reports holes and candidates. editors/vscode/extension.js shows hole types as inlay hints and adds a 'fill hole' code action. `--explain-shapes` prints the unification derivation (n := 64 from x at line 3, ...). Content: 'Shape Golf', 30 puzzles graded in the playground.
- **Demo:** In VS Code a student writes `l1 = Linear(784, 128)` and `fn forward(x: [n, 784]) -> [n, 10] = x |> l1 |> relu |> ?`. The inlay hint reads `? : [n, 128] -> [n, 10]`, the code action fills in `Linear(128, 10)`, and `--explain-shapes` prints the derivation one step at a time.
- **Builds on:** shape polymorphism by unification in elaborate.py, the ide.py recorder, the VS Code extension's inlay hints, diagnostics.py
- **Risk:** The elaborator is a staging interpreter that runs compile-time code, so a hole inside a compile-time value needs lazy or symbolic evaluation. The search blows up beyond 3-4 combinators. Many fillers type-check but make no sense, so ranking matters.

### GPU Lab: a SIMT visualizer, a coalescing checker, and free real NVIDIA GPUs

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

The CUDA backend has never run on an NVIDIA GPU. A Colab/Kaggle notebook fixes that for free and makes it a course lab. A warp-level visualizer shows 32 lanes executing the generated kernels, with branch divergence and memory transactions drawn as cache lines, so students learn GPU performance by watching the compiler's thread mapping work or fail.

- **Build:** notebooks/cuda_colab.ipynb: pip-installs the repo (pure Python), runs `anvil cuda` on every example, compiles with nvcc on the free T4, and diffs the outputs against the interpreter. These are tests/test_cuda.py's emulation checks, now on hardware. `anvil cuda --explain` in backend/cuda.py reports, for each __global__ kernel: which IR variable maps to threadIdx.x; the per-warp stride of every load from its Affine coefficients, giving 32-byte sectors per request; uncoalesced and atomic-heavy kernels; an occupancy estimate. web/simt/: a JS simulator that runs one warp over the kernel IR (lanes, active masks from Select and bounds checks, sectors touched). Assignment: add shared-memory tiling to cuda.py (an open item in PLAN.md) and climb a T4 GFLOP/s leaderboard against cuBLAS.
- **Demo:** Take `y[j, i] = x[i, j]`. In the visualizer each warp touches 32 cache lines (red); students switch the thread axis and it drops to 4 sectors (green). On the Colab T4 the measured time drops by up to about 8x, and the same notebook runs examples/mnist.anvil on a real NVIDIA GPU for the first time.
- **Builds on:** backend/cuda.py (one thread per output, grid-stride loops, split reductions, atomics), the --cuda-emulate path, tests/test_cuda.py, Affine offsets
- **Risk:** Free Colab GPUs are not always available and have quotas. The first hardware runs may expose bugs emulation hid, which is useful but costs class time. The simulator teaches the ideas but is not cycle-accurate.

### anvil grade: autograding that knows shapes, gradients, costs and algebra

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Exercises are Anvil functions with typed specs, and the grader checks what a TA would. It tests the signature at random shapes, compares values to the reference, checks gradients against finite differences, and enforces FLOP and memory budgets read statically from `anvil cost`. For index-notation answers it proves algebraic equivalence by putting the contraction in a normal form, instead of only sampling, and feedback points at the student's own line using the compiler's diagnostics.

- **Build:** anvil/grade.py and exercises/*.anvil specs (`exercise softmax: fn(x: [n, k]) -> [n, k]`, with properties such as rows summing to 1 and shift invariance). Random shapes that satisfy the signature's unification constraints give property-based tests in the style of Hypothesis. References run on interp.py. --check produces feedback like 'overflows at x = 89; here is the input and the kernel that made inf'. Gradients use `anvil gradcheck`, budgets come from cost.py and arena.py, and AST rules can require index notation instead of `for`. anvil/equiv.py: a normal form for pure contraction kernels (the sum of products as a tensor-network hypergraph with canonical index labels), plus Schwartz–Zippel random testing for polynomial bodies. Output in Gradescope results.json and PrairieLearn external-grader formats; the same checks run instantly in the browser playground.
- **Demo:** A student submits softmax without the max-subtraction and gets back 'correct on small inputs; overflows at x = 89 (input attached, line 3, kernel exp)'. Another writes `sum_j A[j, i] * B[k, j]` and is told it is provably equal to the reference `(B @ A).T`.
- **Builds on:** static shapes and unification, interp.py, --check, cost.py, test_gradcheck.py's finite differences, diagnostics.py rendering
- **Risk:** Equivalence beyond multilinear expressions cannot be decided in general, so the grader falls back to random testing and says so. Student programs need a sandbox on the grading server: the loaders read files, so the data path must be restricted.

## language-design (16)

### Papers compile: math-notation syntax and `anvil tex`

*wow 9/10 · weeks*

Make Anvil source look like the equations in the paper, and make the compiler print a program back out as typeset math, including the backward pass it derived. Students paste `softmax(Q·Kᵀ/√dₖ)·V` from Vaswani et al. and it runs. `anvil tex --backward` then shows them the gradient equations of attention that the compiler derived, each linked to its source line.

- **Build:** lexer.py: Unicode subscript index letters (`Wᵢⱼ xⱼ` lexes as `W[i, j] * x[j]`), binder subscripts on reductions (`Σⱼ`, ASCII `sum_j`), postfix `ᵀ ² ⁻¹`, `⊙ ⊗ ∘ ‖x‖ ⟨a,b⟩ 𝔼[…] 𝟙[…] ∈`, type sugar `x: ℝ[n, d]`, `∂L/∂W` as grad. parser.py/ast.py: binder-annotated Reduce, plus norm and inner-product nodes. New anvil/tex.py with two printers: AST to LaTeX, and kernel IR to LaTeX (ir.fmt_expr already prints Σ). The IR printer runs after autodiff.py and simplify.py, so the derived dX equations come out simplified. New CLI command `anvil tex file.anvil [--backward] -o page.html` (KaTeX, every equation linked to its line and kernel id). `anvil fmt --unicode/--ascii` converts between the two spellings both ways. editors/vscode/extension.js: a Lean/Agda-style input method (`\sum`+Tab gives Σ, `\_i` gives ᵢ) and LaTeX hover previews.
- **Demo:** Type eq. 1 of 'Attention Is All You Need' and the LayerNorm formula exactly as printed, inside transformer.anvil, and train it. Then `anvil tex examples/transformer.anvil --backward` opens a page with every forward equation of the GPT and the backward equations the compiler derived for causal attention, softmax and layer norm, each one a link back to its line and kernel. That's backprop through attention, derived and typeset by the compiler, ready for lecture slides.
- **Builds on:** lexer ALIASES (Σ ∏ ∇ √ → ≤ ≠ ·), Greek identifiers, ir.fmt_program's math notation, compile-time autodiff that already emits backward kernels
- **Risk:** Unicode has subscripts for only some letters (ₐₑₕᵢⱼₖₗₘₙₒₚᵣₛₜᵤᵥₓ; there is no subscript b, c, d or q), so a `W_{ij}` fallback is needed. `yᵢ` currently lexes as one identifier, so this breaks the lexer for existing code. Typesetting fused IR can get ugly (print the IR before fusion instead). Two spellings fragment style unless `anvil fmt` picks one canonical form.

### Records, pytrees and type providers: real GPT-2 with typed weights

*wow 9/10 · weeks*

Add structs, enums and tree-shaped values, so whole models can be added, scaled, copied, saved and differentiated as values: `ema = 0.999*ema + 0.001*net`, and `grad(loss, net)` returns a record shaped like net. Then add F#-style type providers, where a data file's header becomes a typed record at compile time. A Hugging Face safetensors file becomes `w.h[3].attn.c_attn.weight : f32[768, 2304]`, with autocomplete and hovers before anything runs.

- **Build:** ast/parser: `struct Name: field: type ...`, `enum Act = relu | gelu | silu`, compile-time `match`. values.py: RecordVal and EnumVal. ops.py: arithmetic mapped over records of the same structure. autodiff.grad: a record argument gives a record of gradients. builtins: `zeros_like`, `copy(net)`, and `save`/`load` of any record, optimizer state included (PLAN's v0.2 item). pyapi.py: dicts of arrays in and out. New anvil/providers.py, reusing the compile-time header readers that idx/npy/csv already have: `safetensors(path)` reads the JSON header at compile time and turns dotted keys into nested records, with numeric keys becoming tuples so `static for` can iterate them; `json(path)` gives a compile-time record of consts; `csv(path)` with a header gives named columns; `tokenizer(vocab, merges)` BPE-encodes a const prompt at compile time and provides a string table for decoding through PickVal. Large tensors load at run time through the existing path for tensors over 64 MB (pointer slots). ide.py: completion of field names.
- **Demo:** Real GPT-2 124M from Hugging Face's model.safetensors in about 80 lines of Anvil (`static for blk in w.h:`), generating text natively with AMX matmuls and on Metal. Every weight's shape shows on hover before the first run. Then LoRA-fine-tune it on the README with `minimize loss over lora with adam()`. Small extras: snake.anvil gets a real DQN target network (`target = copy(q)`), and diffusion.anvil gets EMA weights in one line.
- **Builds on:** TupleVal, `static for` over a tuple of models (I tested this and it works), compile-time header reading in idx()/npy()/csv(), PickVal string tables, heap/indirect buffers for big tensors
- **Risk:** There is no KV cache, so each new token re-runs the whole window: fine at 124M, slow beyond. 500 MB of f32 weights to load at startup. BPE has edge cases (the byte-level mapping). Arithmetic between records of different structure needs defined rules. A ~50k-entry decode table in the generated assembly.

### Recurrence equations: differentiable run-time loops written as math, auto-parallelized

*wow 9/10 · months*

Let a tensor be defined from its own earlier elements: `h[t,b,j] = h0[b,j] if t == 0 else tanh(sum h[t-1,b,k] * U[k,j] + xw[t,b,j])`. The result h is a whole tensor that stores every state, so the backward pass needs no tape; it is just the same recurrence run in reverse. This replaces `static for` unrolling (capped at 1,000 steps, and 1,252 kernels for the char-RNN) with real loops.
