When the recurrence is linear in its past (`h[t] = a[t]*h[t-1] + b[t]`), the compiler turns it into a Blelloch parallel scan, the trick behind Mamba and S5. 2-D recurrences such as edit distance get a Lamport wavefront schedule.

- **Build:** elaborate.st_IndexAssign: detect the LHS name on the RHS at lexicographically negative constant offsets, i.e. dependence vectors of a uniform recurrence in the Karp–Miller–Winograd sense. Emit a new ir.Recur statement: one ordered outer dimension with parallel inner kernels (for CUDA/Metal, the loop over t runs on the host). New anvil/recur.py: dependence analysis, a legality check (all distances positive), and a hyperplane schedule for 2-D cases. autodiff.py: the adjoint of a Recur is a Recur in reverse time that reads the stored h. Linearity test: simplify.deriv shows the body is affine in h[t-1] when ∂body/∂h[t-1] does not involve h. Such recurrences are rewritten with the associative combine (a1,b1)∘(a2,b2) = (a1·a2, a2·b1 + b2). Chunked scan schedules in backend/kernel.py (NEON), cuda.py and metal.py (up/down sweep in threadgroup memory). Reference semantics in interp.py.
- **Demo:** charrnn.anvil with its GRU as a 3-line recurrence and no `static for`. It compiles much faster than the 1.5 s it takes today, and trains on sequences of 512 bytes instead of 32 (the unroll cap makes that impossible today). Next to it, a Mamba-style selective SSM in about 12 lines of index notation trains on the README through an automatically derived parallel scan, compared on bits per byte with the GRU and the GPT. Encore: Levenshtein distance written as its textbook recurrence runs as an anti-diagonal wavefront.
- **Builds on:** affine offsets and range inference in index notation, loop-carried SSA, structured For in the kernel IR, autodiff over kernels, host-side loop counters in CUDA/Metal
- **Risk:** The linearity test must be robust: gated recurrences are linear in h but with data-dependent coefficients. Scans over full matrices (non-diagonal A) are expensive. Storing all T states can take a lot of memory, so the compiler will need to checkpoint every k steps. The initial condition and `where` bounds need careful handling. A parallel scan rounds differently from a sequential loop, which breaks the bit-identical-results promise unless the combine order is fixed.

### Named dimensions: catch the axis bug shape checking can't

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Shapes catch size mismatches, but not two axes that happen to have the same size getting swapped. I checked: in transformer.anvil T = D = 64, and writing `P[d, t]` for `P[t, d]` passes `anvil check` today (215 kernels, no error) and trains silently worse. Nominal dimensions (`dim T = 64`, `dim D = 64`) make axes into types, like Dex index sets and Haliax. Index notation already names axes locally, so Anvil can enforce those names globally almost for free.

- **Build:** parser/ast: `dim T = 64` declarations, `x: [B, T, D]` annotations, and `as` naming for loaded data (`idx(...): [N, 28 as H, 28 as W]`). values.py: TVal gains a `dims` tuple holding a Dim identity, or None for unnamed axes in legacy code. ops.py: views carry dims through transpose, slice and gather. Broadcasting aligns by name (xarray rules) and falls back to position for unnamed dims. `@` contracts the shared name. `split(D -> (HEADS, DH))` and `merge` replace untyped reshape. elaborate.py solve_ranges/scan_indices: an index variable takes the dim of its first plain use, and a later use at a different dim with the same size is an error. builtins: reductions by name (`x.sum(T)`, `softmax(s, over=j)`). ide.py: hovers and inlays show `f32[B=16, T=64, D=64]`. Programs without names behave exactly as today.
- **Demo:** The swapped `P[d, t]` in transformer.anvil, which compiles and trains today, becomes `error: index t ranges over positions (T) in e[b, t, d] but features (D) in P[d, t]`, with both spans underlined in VS Code as you type. Then the GPT's `.reshape(-1, T, HEADS, DH)` lines become `split(D -> (HEADS, DH))`, and the hover shows the head axis by name.
- **Builds on:** index-notation range inference (where every index use is already unified), the TVal view machinery, ide.py's recorder and inlay hints
- **Risk:** Annotation burden: names have to come from loaders and model arguments, and dim arithmetic (`Linear(D, 4*D)`) needs a rule: is 4·D a new dim? Dim variables interact with shape variables in polymorphic fns. Name-aligned broadcasting will surprise NumPy users. Checking must stay gradual so existing examples are untouched.

### Functions as values: lambdas, rank polymorphism, and grad/jvp/vmap to any order

*wow 8/10 · months*

Anvil already passes functions to functions at compile time (I checked: `twice(sq, x)` and `Stack(Linear, 2)` both work), but it has no lambdas and no transformations that return functions. Make it a real functional language: `x => x * 2`, `f ∘ g`, Remora-style rank polymorphism (a function written for one example automatically applies over extra leading axes), and `grad(f)`, `jvp`, `vjp`, `vmap` and `hessian`, composable to any order. Every call is inlined, so closures and higher-order code compile down to plain fused NEON loops and cost nothing at run time.

- **Build:** parser/ast: a Lambda node (`x => e`, with `λx. e` as an alias), placeholder partial application `f(_, 2)`, and `∘`/`>>` composition. values.py: FnVal closures (FnVal already keeps its scope). elaborate.call_fn: rank polymorphism. When an argument has more leading axes than the declared cell shape `[d]`, the extra axes go on a frame stack, and ops.py make_kernel/emit_map/emit_reduce prepend those frame Vars to every kernel emitted in the call. This is the batching transform, done during elaboration. New anvil/transforms.py: `grad(f)` returns a FnVal that traces f and runs autodiff.Backprop on that region. `jvp` is a new forward-mode pass over kernel IR (tangent kernels from simplify.deriv; a sum's tangent is a sum; max routes through a select). `hvp = jvp(grad(f))`, `jacobian = vmap(jvp)`. Reverse-over-reverse needs autodiff to accept the scatter-accumulate and select kernels it emits itself (PLAN lists higher-order gradients as not done), with finite-difference checks of each order in tests/test_gradcheck.py.
- **Demo:** A physics-informed network solves the heat equation with no data. `u = (x, t) => net(stack([x, t]))`, and the loss is the PDE residual `∂t(u) - α * ∂x(∂x(u))` plus boundary terms, trained natively. Its solution is drawn with `show` as an ASCII heat map next to the analytic solution. Coda: per-example gradients `vmap(grad(loss))` give DP-SGD in 6 lines.
- **Builds on:** passing FnVal/BuiltinVal values, inlining elaboration, `...` leading-dim index notation, reverse-mode autodiff over kernels, simplify.deriv
- **Risk:** Second derivatives through scatter kernels and relu masks need care, and many are zero almost everywhere. The frame-stack batching touches every op in ops.py/builtins.py (gathers, scatters, nonzero, runtime calls inside a vmapped fn). Compile time grows with nested inlining.

### User-defined monoids and semirings: FlashAttention from a 6-line declaration

*wow 8/10 · months*

Today a reduction is one of sum, max, min, prod or argmax. Let users declare their own associative reductions (`monoid`: an identity and a combine), and swap the arithmetic of a whole computation (`with semiring tropical:`). Online softmax is a monoid over (max, normalizer, weighted sum), so attention written with it fuses into a FlashAttention-style tiled kernel that never stores the [T, T] score matrix, which fixes a listed weakness. The same mechanism runs the HMM forward algorithm as likelihood (log semiring) or as Viterbi (tropical), and its gradient is forward–backward (Eisner 2016).

- **Build:** ast/parser: `monoid name(fields): identity ...; combine(a, b) = ...; finish(a) = ...`, reductions by monoid name, and `with semiring S:` blocks that rebind sum and `*` inside index notation (Goodman's semiring parsing). ir.py: Reduction.op extended to an accumulator of several components (a tuple of Acc slots) plus a combine Expr. optimize.py: a reduction producer may fuse into a reduction consumer when the outer reduction is a monoid (q·k computed inside the j loop: the flash fusion), and associativity allows splitting reductions into tiles and threads (the roadmap's parallel reductions). backend/kernel.py and aarch64.py: tiled schedules that keep tuple accumulators in registers, with the existing LICM hoisting components that don't vary with the output dimension (m and l). cuda.py/metal.py: tree combines across warps and threadgroups. autodiff.py: VJPs of monoid reductions by recomputation, as FlashAttention's backward does. Prelude: logsumexp, online_softmax, welford (mean and variance in one pass, for layer_norm), topk.
- **Demo:** transformer.anvil's two attention lines become `out[b,a,i,e] = softmax_sum j: (s[b,a,i,j], v[b,j,a,e])`. `anvil ir` shows one fused kernel and no s buffer, `anvil cost` shows peak memory falling from O(T²) to O(T), and the context grows from 64 to 4,096 bytes on the M3 Max. Coda for class: write the HMM forward algorithm once, decode with `with semiring viterbi:`, and get posteriors from `grad`.
- **Builds on:** the kernel IR's single reduction form, register-tiled GEMM schedules, epilogue fusion, `...` index notation, max routing in autodiff
- **Risk:** Tuple accumulators put pressure on the register allocator and the 4×16 tile. Fusing one reduction into another is a new optimizer capability with subtle legality rules. Float combine is not exactly associative, so results stop being bit-identical across tile sizes and thread counts unless the order is fixed. The backward pass of fused attention is a second hard kernel.

### Traits and operator overloading: run your model on intervals, get a robustness proof

*wow 8/10 · months*

Let user types overload `+ * @ max exp ...` through `impl` blocks, with traits for clear errors, and make functions generic over number types. Running mnist.anvil's unchanged `forward` on `Interval(x - ε, x + ε)` then computes certified output bounds: Interval Bound Propagation (Gowal et al. 2018) as a 30-line library rather than a separate framework like auto_LiRPA. The same mechanism gives dual numbers (forward-mode AD as a teaching exercise), complex tensors (an FFT written in Anvil), and later generics over f16/bf16/f64.

- **Build:** ast/parser: `trait Num: fn add(a, b); ...`, `impl Interval: ...`, generic parameters `fn gemm[T: Float](a: T[n,k], b: T[k,m])`. values.py: RecordVal (shared with the records idea) tagged with its impl. ops.py binary_op/unary_op/matmul/select_op and builtins max/min/exp/sum: when an operand is a record with an impl, dispatch to the user's function. Dispatch is resolved and inlined at compile time, so there is none at run time. Errors name the missing method and the chain of calls: `Interval has no exp, needed by gelu (prelude.anvil:12), called from your line`. New std/interval.anvil (center–radius matmul `c@W ± r@|W|`, monotone activations, the IBP loss), std/dual.anvil, std/complex.anvil. The ir.py dtype set widens later for f16/bf16/f64, behind trait bounds.
- **Demo:** 'Prove your digit reader can't be fooled.' Run the network from dream.anvil (which makes adversarial digits) on intervals of radius ε, and Anvil prints the fraction of test digits certified robust: digits no attack can flip. Train 5 epochs with the IBP loss: certified accuracy at ε = 0.1 jumps, and dream.anvil's attack stops working on the certified digits. Bonus slide: forward-mode AD via `Dual` in 15 lines, matching `grad` to within 1e-6.
- **Builds on:** inlining elaboration (dispatch disappears at compile time), a prelude written in Anvil, elementwise ops like `max(x, 0)`, differentiation through any computation (so IBP training differentiates through the interval arithmetic)
- **Risk:** Unclear semantics for records inside broadcasting and index notation (does `x[i] * W[i, j]` dispatch per element?). Errors have to travel through generic inlining. Interval bounds on a network not trained for them are loose, so the demo needs IBP training. Dtype generics need f16 NEON work in the backend, which belongs to another track.

### Algebraic effects and handlers: a probabilistic programming language compiled to NEON

*wow 8/10 · months*

Make `~` an effect: sampling, `observe` and `param` become operations whose meaning is supplied by handlers (trace, condition, replay, scale, do), as in Pyro's poutine and NumPyro. Anvil inlines everything at compile time, so handlers are resolved statically and the output is plain fused kernels: Pyro-style composable inference at native-code speed. The same machinery handles errors: a `nan` effect raised by the `--check` scans lets a training step roll back and retry.

- **Build:** ast/parser: `observe y ~ dist`, named sample sites, `effect`/`handler` declarations, and `handle e with h:` blocks. New anvil/effects.py: a handler stack in the Elaborator, consulted at compile time by builtins.sample_into/bind_dist. Built-in handlers: trace (a record of site values and log-probs), condition(data), replay(trace), block, scale(k), do(site=value) (Pearl interventions). Prelude distributions gain `log_prob` (normal, bernoulli, categorical, and beta via a reparameterized Kumaraswamy). std/infer.anvil: `elbo(model, guide, data)` replays the model on the guide's trace and sums log p − log q, which gives reparameterized gradients automatically; discrete sites get score-function terms (as in Pyro's TraceGraph_ELBO). The `nan` effect: minimize keeps a second copy of the params in the arena, and a handler such as `on nan: restore; lr /= 2` retries the step.
- **Demo:** examples/vae.anvil rewritten as a generative story (`z ~ normal(0, 1)`, `observe x ~ bernoulli(decoder(z))`) plus a guide, trained with `maximize elbo(model, guide, x) over vae with adam()`. It reads like Kingma and Welling's equations, and `anvil ir` shows the same kernels as the hand-derived VAE, so the training curve matches bit for bit. Then a Bayesian MLP on spirals shows its uncertainty as an ASCII map with `show`.
- **Builds on:** reparameterized `z ~ normal(mu, sigma)`, the counter-based RNG (deterministic replay is trivial), `maximize`, inlining elaboration, the per-kernel NaN scan in --check
- **Risk:** Discrete latent variables need enumeration or high-variance score-function estimators. Handler semantics across `static for`, run-time loops and model bodies need a precise spec. Good errors may need effect typing (which functions may sample). Rollback doubles parameter memory.

### Numerical refinement types: prove your loss can't NaN before it runs

*wow 8/10 · moonshot*

`--check` finds a NaN after it happens; prove at compile time that it can't. Value-range refinements (`x: f32[n] in [0, 1]`, `positive`, `finite`) start at the data loaders and flow through every kernel by abstract interpretation. The compiler can then follow the argument: x − max(x) ≤ 0, so exp ≤ 1, so sum(exp) ≥ 1, so log is safe. Conversely, it flags `log(sigmoid(z))` underflow or bce with eps=0 as errors, with a counterexample and a suggested rewrite. No ML framework can do this today.

- **Build:** New anvil/ranges.py: an abstract interpreter over the kernel IR. Domains: intervals, plus sign, '≥ 1' and finiteness facts, and a few relational patterns (x − max_j x, sums of non-negative terms, Box–Muller outputs). Widening across loop iterations; params are assumed finite or bounded (`|w| ≤ B`), stated as refinements. parser/ast: `in [a, b]`, `positive` and `finite` on types and params. Ranges come from the data (idx/255, csv statistics, npy scanned at compile time). diagnostics.py: an 'unsafe log/div/sqrt/pow' error that shows the derivation chain line by line. It runs on forward kernels and on the backward kernels autodiff generates, so it also catches infinite gradients (sqrt at 0 in a layer_norm without eps). Kernels proven safe skip the --check scans. Optional Z3 for relational cases.
- **Demo:** `anvil check --prove-finite` over every example: softmax, log_softmax, cross_entropy, adam and layer_norm are proven NaN-free. A student's `loss = -mean(log(sigmoid(z)) * y)` gets an error before a single step runs: `log can receive 0: sigmoid(z) underflows for z < -88, and z is unbounded (it comes from net.l2); use log_sigmoid(z)`.
- **Builds on:** the kernel IR's small pure expression language, --check's per-operation provenance messages, compile-time reading of data headers, the symbolic simplifier
- **Risk:** Training makes params unbounded, so most real proofs only hold under the assumption 'params stay finite', and the tool must say so. Imprecise intervals and widening cause false alarms, which train users to ignore the checker. Float details (subnormals, the inline NEON exp/log approximations) must be modeled exactly, or the proofs are about the wrong functions.

### Sparse, ragged and graph tensors as types: TACO inside Anvil

*wow 8/10 · moonshot*

Put storage formats in the type (`A: f32[n, n] @ csr`, `tokens: i32[b, ?len]`) and compile the same index notation to sparse loops that iterate the operands together, as Kjolstad's TACO does. A graph neural network layer becomes one line, `h2[v, d] = sum A[v, u] * hw[u, d]`. Mixture-of-experts routing becomes a ragged gather, and variable-length sentences train without padding. Autodiff derives the SpMM and SDDMM gradients.

- **Build:** ast/parser: per-dimension level formats (dense, compressed, singleton) and ragged `?len` dimensions. ir.py: compressed levels iterate through position arrays. This fits the existing Gather terms in ir.Affine (each coordinate is a gathered load), plus run-time loop bounds read from position arrays (the same machinery as run-time sizes). New anvil/sparse.py: merge lattices to co-iterate `*` (intersection) and `+` (union), and workspaces for sparse outputs (Kjolstad 2019). autodiff.py: the gradient with respect to a dense operand is a product with the transposed pattern; with respect to sparse values it is SDDMM restricted to the pattern. Segment reductions for ragged dimensions. backend/kernel.py: scalar and vec-red schedules over compressed rows. cuda.py/metal.py: row-split CSR SpMM. Loaders for npz, Matrix Market and edge lists.
- **Demo:** A 2-layer GCN on Cora (and GraphSAGE on ogbn-arxiv) in 15 lines of index notation, trained natively and compared with PyTorch Geometric on accuracy and time per epoch. Then a GPT block with a top-2 MoE whose tokens are routed in ragged groups, with `anvil cost` showing no FLOPs spent on padding.
- **Builds on:** index notation, Gather terms in affine offsets, differentiable scatter-add `+=`, nonzero(), run-time offsets
- **Risk:** This is a second compiler inside the first (merge lattices, sparse outputs, format conversions). The NEON tiling schedules assume dense affine strides. Sparse GPU kernels need load balancing. Autodiff through sparse patterns must keep 'stored zero' and 'absent' apart.

### Verified Anvil: a formal report, a Csmith-style fuzzer, and Lean proofs

*wow 8/10 · moonshot*

Write Anvil's definition the way the Scheme Report and The Definition of Standard ML were written. That means typing rules for index notation (the LCA binding rule, range inference, broadcasting), a denotational semantics in which the counter-based RNG is a pure function, and AD as a source transformation with a stated theorem. From those rules, derive Ansmith, a generator that produces only well-typed random programs and cross-checks all four backends, both optimization levels and every thread count. Then mechanize the core in Lean 4: well-typed index notation never reads out of bounds, and the per-kernel reverse-mode rule is the adjoint of the kernel's linearization.

- **Build:** docs/REPORT.md: grammar, static semantics as inference rules, dynamic semantics. interp.py refactored into the executable reference (the spec). spec/conformance/*.anvil with expected outputs that every backend must reproduce. tests/fuzz/ansmith.py: type-directed generation (affine windows, gathers, scatters, reductions, loops, minimize), a delta-debugging shrinker, and oracles: the interpreter vs AArch64 at -O0/-O2 with 1 and 8 threads vs Metal vs CUDA-emulate, plus finite-difference gradient checks and anvil cost's FLOP counts against measured ones. lean/Anvil/: a core calculus of kernels, the affine-offset model, a bounds-safety theorem for range inference, an adjoint theorem for reduction and scatter kernels, and a proof that vertical fusion preserves semantics.
- **Demo:** Live in class: start Ansmith and watch it shrink a fresh miscompile to a 6-line program. The bugs it hunts are the kind PLAN.md lists as already fixed (a fill fused past a loop that writes into the same buffer; autodiff with a buffer written more than once). Then `lake build` checks the bounds-safety theorem, and the slide reads 'every Anvil program that type-checks is proven never to index out of bounds'.
- **Builds on:** the NumPy interpreter that already defines the semantics (RNG and shuffle included), the differential test harness, the gradcheck tests, PLAN.md's precise design text
- **Risk:** The Lean core calculus is a model, not the Python implementation, so the proofs are about the model. Floating point makes 'fusion preserves semantics' true only up to reassociation. GPU atomics are nondeterministic, so the fuzzer needs tolerant oracles. Large effort that adds no user-facing features.

### Comptime and hygienic macros: einops and einsum as a 40-line Anvil library

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

The elaborator is already a staging interpreter. Give users that power, Zig-style: `comptime` code with lists, dicts and string operations, `quote`/`$splice` to build index-notation code, and hygienic `macro`s. Then einops' `rearrange(x, "b t (h e) -> b h t e", h=4)` is an ordinary Anvil library function: it parses its pattern at compile time and emits a zero-copy affine view, and a mistake in the pattern is underlined inside the string literal. `test` blocks give the language its own test runner.

- **Build:** values.py: compile-time ListVal/DictVal and string methods (split, strip, chars, format, int()). parser/ast: `comptime:` blocks, `quote:` with `$x` splices, `macro name(args):`, `@decorator` on fns and models, and `test "name":` blocks. elaborate.py: macros expand during elaboration. Hygiene comes from gensym'd index names (the existing fresh_index_names), and spans can point into string literals for diagnostics.py. New std/einops.anvil (rearrange/reduce/repeat over ops.view/transpose/reshape, which are free affine views) and std/einsum.anvil (emits index notation through quote). Decorators: `@checkpoint` (recompute in the backward pass) and `@summary` (a parameter table at compile time). `anvil test` runs test blocks on the interpreter and the native backends. Compile-time `json()`/`toml()` configs.
- **Demo:** Replace every `.reshape(-1, T, HEADS, DH)` in transformer.anvil with `rearrange(h @ Wq, "b t (h e) -> b t h e", h=HEADS)`. A typo in the pattern gets a caret under the bad letter inside the string, and `anvil ir` shows identical kernels. Then a ResNet-18/34 generator reads its stage depths from a JSON file at compile time and emits the model.
- **Builds on:** CVal/SVal/TupleVal compile-time values, compile-time `if` and string comparison, views as affine offsets, the multi-error diagnostics renderer
- **Risk:** Macro hygiene for index names, whose LCA binding depends on scope. Error spans inside generated code. Two meta-levels confuse students unless comptime and runtime code look clearly different. Heavy comptime loops run in the Python-hosted interpreter and slow compiles.

### Size-dependent types: check library code once, and allow run-time sizes

*wow 7/10 · months*

Today a shape-polymorphic function is checked only when it is called and inlined, so a bug in prelude.anvil surfaces at the call site of whoever uses it. Check function bodies once, for all sizes, with symbolic dimensions and linear-arithmetic constraints, as Futhark's size types and Dex do. Then extend the same symbols to sizes only known at run time (`[?n ≤ 64, 784]`): a typed dynamic batch (the partial last batch, variable-length nonzero) that keeps static memory planning.

- **Build:** New anvil/sizes.py: a symbolic size language over ir.Affine (which already proves `(s + 64) - s = 64` with run-time scalars), with normalization of `//`, `%` and products by constants, and a small Presburger/Omega-test solver for constraints like `h - k + 1 ≥ 1`. A generic checking pre-pass over FnDecl/ModelDecl bodies that infers missing signatures, so hovers show `fn attention(x: [b, t, dm], ...) -> [b, t, dh]`. `match x.shape: case [n, d]: ... case [n, c, h, w]: ...` for dispatch on rank, and `let [b, t, d] = x.shape`. Run-time sizes: ir.Var extents may be a run-time ScalarRef with a static capacity, arena.py plans memory at that capacity, and backend/kernel.py loops to run-time bounds using its existing tail handling. `batches(drop_last=false)`.
- **Demo:** `anvil check anvil/prelude.anvil`, with no program, verifies the whole standard library for all sizes and prints each function's inferred generic type. A deliberately broken Conv2d padding formula is caught at its definition with a counterexample (h = 1, k = 3). Then mnist.anvil also trains on its final partial batch of 32 and matches PyTorch's per-epoch numbers exactly.
- **Builds on:** shape variables and unification (elaborate.unify_shape), ir.Affine with run-time scalars, slices with run-time offsets and static lengths, the static arena
- **Risk:** Nonlinear size arithmetic (a product of two symbols, like h*w in a reshape) is undecidable in general, so those cases must fall back to checking at the call site. A generic pre-pass and the elaborator are two type checkers that could disagree. Run-time extents switch off register-tile schedules that rely on static trip counts.

### Type bridges and FFI: Anvil shapes become C++ compile errors, C++ code becomes an Anvil op

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Anvil knows every shape at compile time; hand that knowledge to the host language's type system. `anvil export --cpp` emits `std::mdspan<const float, std::extents<size_t, 1, 28, 28>>` signatures, so calling with the wrong shape fails to compile in clang. Python gets `.pyi` stubs with PEP 646 shapes and jaxtyping annotations, and Rust gets const-generic arrays. In the other direction, `extern "C" fn` lets Anvil call C/C++ code (a game's physics step) with declared shapes and an optional VJP, and `kernel cuda` / `kernel metal` blocks drop hand-written kernels into the generated files.

- **Build:** export.py: `--cpp` (a header-only RAII class owning the arena; std::mdspan, or the Kokkos reference implementation on C++17), `--rust` (a crate with `&[[f32; 784]; N]` const generics), `--swift`. New anvil/stubs.py for anvil.function and %%anvil: jaxtyping annotations like `Float32[np.ndarray, "b 784"]` using Anvil's dim names, checked by pyapi at call time. parser/ast: `extern "C" fn name(x: [n, 4], a: i32[n]) -> [n, 4] [grad = name_vjp]`. ir.RTCall gains external symbols called with buffer pointers. aarch64.py emits an Apple-ABI call from main; cuda/metal host code synchronizes first; interp.py calls through ctypes; autodiff uses the declared VJP, written in C or in Anvil. `kernel cuda fn ...: """..."""` is spliced into cuda.py's output with its declared shapes, and Anvil checks the launch.
- **Demo:** A small C++ cart-pole or racing game. The Anvil training program calls the game's real C++ physics_step through extern "C" (no reimplementation of the physics) and learns a policy. Then `anvil export --cpp` produces policy.hpp, which the game includes. Passing a 5-float observation where the policy expects 4 is a clang error that quotes Anvil's shape [1, 4]. Python side: jaxtyping flags a mis-shaped NumPy call to anvil.function with Anvil's dim names.
- **Builds on:** anvil export (a C library with weights baked in), the RTCall mechanism the runtime routines use, pyapi's zero-copy pointer slots, the single-file CUDA/Metal generators
- **Risk:** Python can't check shapes statically in practice: pyright only sees literal shapes, and jaxtyping checks at run time. An extern call inside threaded kernel regions forces a sync point and blocks fusion. A non-differentiable extern used in a loss needs a clear error. C++23 mdspan availability in Apple clang.

### Generators with stream fusion: zero-cost data pipelines

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

`gen` functions with `yield` let users build data pipelines from composable pieces: `emnist() |> transpose |> random_shift(2) |> mixup(0.2) |> batch(128)`. As with Haskell stream fusion or Rust iterators, the compiler inlines the consumer's loop body at each `yield`. The pipeline compiles into exactly the hand-fused gather kernels people write today: no queues, no copies, no allocation. `prefetch(g, 2)` runs the producer on a runtime worker thread with double buffering.

- **Build:** ast/parser: `gen` declarations and `yield` statements. elaborate.st_For: looping over a generator call inlines the generator's body, and each `yield` elaborates the loop body as a continuation, with break and continue mapped onto labeled exits from the generator's loops. The generator's loop-carried state uses the existing SSA write-backs. std/data.anvil: map, filter (mask-based, so sizes stay static), zip, take, window, shuffle_buffer, batch(n) (a static ring buffer that yields when full), interleave, plus augmentations (random crop, flip, cutout, mixup, random erasing). runtime.s and anvil_host.h: a `prefetch` producer thread with a two-slot ring, scheduled by the existing thread pool. cost.py: per-stage pipeline cost.
- **Demo:** letters.anvil's hand-rolled 'pad, transpose and shift in one gather' becomes a 4-stage pipeline, and a `diff` of `anvil ir` before and after shows identical kernels: proof in class that it costs nothing. Then a CIFAR-10 augmentation pipeline (crop, flip, cutout, mixup) races torch DataLoader with 8 workers on images per second.
- **Builds on:** batches() (BatchesVal, for_batches), inlining elaboration, the counter-based RNG streams, the assembly thread pool, the Metal host loop
- **Risk:** Inlining the consumer at every yield duplicates its code. Generators whose number of yields depends on the data don't fit static shapes. `prefetch` breaks determinism unless the producer's random streams are keyed by item index. It overlaps with batches(), which should be reimplemented on top of it.

### Namespaces, packages and a torch.nn-sized standard library written in Anvil

*wow 6/10 · months*

`use` currently copies another file's declarations into the global scope. Give Anvil real modules (`import std.nn as nn`, `pub`, qualified names in errors), a package manifest and lockfile, and a model hub with content-addressed weights. Then grow the standard library to torch.nn breadth, entirely in readable, gradient-checked Anvil, including what is missing today: BatchNorm needs model state that isn't trained, which the language can't express yet.

- **Build:** parser/ast: `import a.b as c`, `from a import x`, `pub`. values.py: ModuleVal. elaborate.st_Use becomes namespaced modules with a per-module elaboration cache (IDEAS' 'cache the elaborated prelude'). New anvil/pkg.py: anvil.toml, a lockfile, `anvil add github.com/u/pkg@v1`, and weight files addressed by sha256 and fetched on demand. A model-level `buffer running_mean: [d]` (kept by save/load, skipped by minimize) and a `training` mode. The std tree (anvil/std/*.anvil): nn (BatchNorm, GroupNorm, RMSNorm, MultiHeadAttention with RoPE/GQA, SwiGLU, TransformerBlock, Conv1d, ConvTranspose2d, depthwise conv, Upsample, LSTM/GRU cells, top-k MoE), optim (Lion, Muon, Adafactor, LAMB, schedule-free AdamW), linalg (Cholesky, Householder QR, triangular solve, Jacobi eigh, all differentiable), distributions with log_prob, losses (focal, InfoNCE, Huber, KL), init (orthogonal, trunc_normal). tests/test_stdlib.py checks each against finite differences and PyTorch.
- **Demo:** Muon (Newton–Schulz orthogonalized momentum, the optimizer behind the NanoGPT speedrun records) in about 10 lines of Anvil: `optimizer muon` with a `static for` of 5 quintic iterations and `if rank(w) == 2`. It trains the README GPT to lower bits per byte than Adam in the same number of steps, and `anvil ir` shows it fused into the update kernels.
- **Builds on:** use (loads each file once and detects cycles), a prelude written in Anvil, optimizer blocks with state and compile-time if, static for
- **Risk:** Library breadth is a long tail: every layer needs gradient checks on three backends. Package security, and hosting for weights. Namespaces change parameter paths (`net.l1.W`), which existing .weights checkpoints depend on, so compatibility must be kept.

## performance (16)

### Anvil runs Llama: the llama.cpp / MLX-LM showdown

*wow 10/10 · months*

Load a real GGUF model (Llama-3.2-1B, Qwen2.5-0.5B/1.5B, SmolLM2) into a ~120-line Anvil program and generate text at batch 1 faster than llama.cpp and MLX-LM on the same Mac. Static shapes work in Anvil's favour here: the whole decode step (RMSNorm, quantized matvecs, RoPE, GQA attention over the KV cache, SwiGLU, sampling) becomes one fused program with its memory planned at compile time, with no graph building and no allocation per token.

- **Build:** (a) A `gguf("model.gguf")` builtin in anvil/builtins.py next to bi_npy. It parses the header at compile time, so every weight gets a static type (`q4_0[2048, 8192]`) and the metadata becomes consts. (b) Block-quantized Q8_0/Q4_0/Q4_K formats in ir.py, with dequantize-on-load in anvil/backend/kernel.py: unpack nibbles with ushr/and, `sdot` for GEMV, and `smmla` on blocks repacked 4x8-interleaved (ggml's aarch64 Q4_0_4_8 path, llamafile's tinyBLAS), with f16 block scales via fcvtl. (c) examples/llama.anvil: the KV cache is a static ring buffer f32[L, T_MAX, KVH, DH], and the position is a run-time scalar in Affine offsets, which already work. Attention runs over `where j <= pos`. (d) Fusion targets in optimize.py: RMSNorm as a matvec prologue, RoPE in the Q/K projection epilogue, `silu(g) * u` fused into the down-projection. (e) Metal GEMV kernels with simdgroup reductions in metal.py. (f) A BPE tokenizer built from the GGUF metadata (Python side via anvil.function, or a routine in anvil_host.h), plus bin/chat.
- **Demo:** `bin/chat Llama-3.2-1B-Q4_0.gguf` streams tokens with a live tok/s counter beside `llama-bench` and `mlx_lm.generate` run on the same M3 Max. Then `anvil cost` predicts the bandwidth roofline before running (weight bytes per token / 400 GB/s), and `anvil ir` shows the whole decode step as a few dozen fused kernels.
- **Builds on:** npy loader (compile-time header parsing into static types), run-time scalars in Affine offsets, `where`, examples/transformer.anvil, anvil.function, Metal backend, anvil cost
- **Risk:** Beating llama.cpp's hand-tuned NEON and Metal kernels is hard. Decode is memory-bound, so any win must come from fusion and bandwidth use, not FLOPs. Prefill needs fast quantized GEMM (I8MM or AMX) to avoid losing badly. Tokenizer and chat-template details are fiddly. Depends on the low-precision dtype work.

### The megakernel: a whole training loop (or LLM decode step) in one persistent kernel

*wow 10/10 · moonshot*

Anvil knows the whole program at compile time: every shape, buffer address, loop and kernel. That lets it do automatically what Hazy Research's 'No Bubbles' Llama-1B megakernel and CMU's Mirage Persistent Kernel (2025) did by hand: compile everything into one persistent kernel with tile-level dataflow dependencies, with no launches and no barriers between operators. On the CPU the same idea replaces the per-kernel fork-join thread pool with a tile graph scheduled statically per core.

- **Build:** (a) anvil/schedule.py: split each kernel into tiles and compute tile-to-tile dependencies from Affine read/write footprints, which are exact because offsets are affine. (b) CPU: runtime.s gains per-tile completion counters, and aarch64.py emits a static instruction list per core (tile id plus wait conditions) instead of calling _anvil_rt_parallel for each kernel. (c) CUDA: one cooperative persistent kernel (cudaLaunchCooperativeKernel guarantees forward progress) whose blocks execute a compiled instruction stream with global-memory flags. Loop counters live on the device. (d) Metal: the same design, never launching more threadgroups than are resident, since Metal has no cooperative launch guarantee. (e) The next layer's weight tiles are prefetched while the current layer computes.
- **Demo:** logreg and spirals run thousands of training steps in a single GPU dispatch, taking per-step time on Metal from about 50-135 us today down to single digits. Llama-1B batch-1 decode runs as one kernel per token, against llama.cpp and MLX. A CPU --profile timeline shows no idle gaps between kernels.
- **Builds on:** whole-program static shapes and memory plan, read/write sets in optimize.py, Metal host-scalar analysis, the runtime.s CAS job-word thread pool
- **Risk:** Forward-progress and deadlock hazards on Metal. Register pressure in a giant kernel forces an interpreter-style instruction design like MPK and Hazy used. Very hard to debug. Pays off only where launch and barrier overhead dominate.

### FlashAttention falls out of the compiler (online-softmax monoids)

*wow 9/10 · months*

Users keep writing attention as three lines of index notation. A fusion pass recognizes max -> exp -> sum -> divide -> contraction over the same index and rewrites the chain into one tiled kernel with an online-softmax monoid (Milakov & Gimelshein 2018; FlashAttention, Dao 2022). Autodiff then derives the FlashAttention-2 backward by recomputing P from the saved logsumexp. The `detach(max x)` in the prelude's softmax is literally the compiler's license: it marks the max as a stabilizer that cancels exactly, so a running max is just as valid.

- **Build:** (a) ir.py: generalize `Reduction` from a single op to a monoid with tuple state and a combine/rescale step. sum, max and argmax become instances, and online softmax (m, l, o[e]), logsumexp and Welford mean/variance are added (layer_norm in one pass). interp.py gets the reference semantics. (b) optimize.py: a `flash_fuse` pass that matches the prelude softmax chain plus the `p @ v` contraction. Causal masks, whether `(0.0 if j <= i else -1e9)` or `where j <= i`, become a triangular domain so fully masked tiles are skipped. (c) autodiff.py: a rematerialization rule that stores L[i] = m + log l and recomputes exp(s - L) in the dQ/dK/dV kernels, with D_i = rowsum(dO*O). (d) kernel.py: a blocked Br x Bc schedule that keeps the QK^T tile in NEON registers (or AMX), rescales in registers, accumulates PV, and threads over (batch, head, query block). (e) Metal/CUDA versions using threadgroup or shared-memory tiles, via the GPU tile codegen idea.
- **Demo:** examples/attention.anvil and transformer.anvil stay unchanged, but `anvil ir` now prints one kernel tagged <flash attention: online softmax>. A context sweep from 256 to 16k on the M3 Max: the materialized version's memory grows as T^2 and falls off a cliff, while the fused one stays O(T). It beats PyTorch CPU `scaled_dot_product_attention` (0.43 ms vs Anvil's current 1.23 ms at 16x256x64) and MLX's fast SDPA on Metal. Classroom slide: 'FlashAttention is a monoid.'
- **Builds on:** Kernel IR reductions, prelude softmax/log_softmax with detach(max), reverse-mode autodiff over kernels, epilogue fusion in optimize.py, register-tiled vec_out/vec_red schedules
- **Risk:** The pattern match has to survive inlining and CSE variants, so it must match on the IR, not on source text. Recompute versus storing P must be decided by cost for short contexts. Multi-accumulator reductions touch every backend and raise register pressure (o[e] accumulators per row).

### Bare-metal matrix units: Anvil emits AMX (M1-M3) and SME2 (M4) instructions itself

*wow 9/10 · months*

Every large matmul currently goes through Accelerate's cblas_sgemm, so epilogues are split off (blas.py `split`) and on that path dW really is written to memory. Anvil already writes its own assembly, so it can drive Apple's matrix coprocessor directly: the undocumented AMX instructions reverse-engineered in corsix/amx on M1-M3, and the architected SME/SME2 FMOPA on M4, which is the same unit made official. That brings back true fusion (bias + GELU + Adam straight out of the Z/ZA tiles while they are in L1), removes per-call overhead on small and batched products (attention heads, conv stacks), and opens fp16/bf16/int8 matrix throughput.

- **Build:** (a) anvil/backend/amx.py: an AMX micro-kernel generator. ldx/ldy/fma32/fma16/extrx/stz are emitted as `.word` encodings bracketed by set/clr, with packed panels and threading across P-clusters (one AMX unit per cluster). (b) anvil/backend/sme.py: streaming-mode SME2 kernels (smstart/smstop, fmopa za.s, multi-vector ld1w), following 'Hello SME!' (Remke & Breuer 2024) and Arm KleidiAI. (c) blas.py `prepare` becomes a choice between a NEON tile, an AMX/SME tile and Accelerate, with the epilogue kept inside the kernel. (d) Chip selection with `--target m3|m4`, plus a runtime.s sysctl check of FEAT_SME. (e) The existing BLAS tests (every transpose, stack, sum and epilogue) rerun bit-exact against the interpreter.
- **Demo:** `anvil asm examples/mnist.anvil` shows a kernel full of amxfma32 with the SGD/Adam update fused into the tile extraction. A 1024^3 product runs at about 2.3 TFLOP/s (matching Accelerate), and a 64x64x64 head product runs several times faster than a cblas call. The wide-MLP epoch drops because dW never touches DRAM. On an M4 the same program emits fmopa.
- **Builds on:** blas.py product matching, kernel.py register tiling, mir.py register allocator, the raw-assembly toolchain; the dev machine is an M3 Max (FEAT_SME=0, FEAT_I8MM/BF16=1)
- **Risk:** AMX is undocumented and may differ subtly on M3 from corsix's M1/M2 notes. No M4 is in hand for SME (needs a borrowed machine or an EC2 M4 Mac). Feeding epilogues from the AMX tiles without stalling is the hard part. A future macOS could restrict AMX use (unlikely but possible).

### llm.c from 60 lines: Hopper/Blackwell tensor cores and FP8 with scales in the type system

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

Karpathy's llm.c trains GPT-2 124M with thousands of lines of hand-written CUDA. The moonshot is the same model as a ~60-line Anvil program that compiles to one .cu file using Hopper's TMA, wgmma.mma_async and warp-specialized producer/consumer warpgroups (the FlashAttention-3 recipe). It also trains in FP8 (e4m3/e5m2) with the scaling factor in the tensor type, so the compiler proves every FP8 tensor is scaled before use.

- **Build:** (a) An sm_90 path in cuda.py/gpu_tile.py: TMA descriptors (cuTensorMapEncodeTiled), mbarrier pipelines, wgmma with register-resident accumulators, and setmaxnreg warp specialization. Optionally it emits CuTe C++ layouts rather than raw PTX. An sm_100 path adds tcgen05.mma with tensor memory and block-scaled MXFP8. (b) ir.py/elaborate.py: `f8e4m3[n, d] @ scale` types, with delayed or per-block scaling (amax history kept as optimizer-like state in the prelude) and compile errors for unscaled FP8. (c) Flash attention on the same path. (d) examples/gpt2.anvil reading the tokenized FineWeb shards llm.c uses. (e) The whole-step CUDA Graph from the first-light idea.
- **Demo:** GPT-2 124M trains on one H100 from examples/gpt2.anvil, with tokens/s and MFU side by side against llm.c and PyTorch + torch.compile, and matching loss curves. Then FP8 is switched on by changing one type annotation.
- **Builds on:** the CUDA backend, examples/transformer.anvil, the precision-type, flash-attention, GPU tile codegen and first-light ideas
- **Risk:** Needs H100/B200 access and deep PTX expertise. Matching llm.c and cuDNN attention is months of tuning. FP8 training stability depends on careful scaling recipes.

### Tile-level GPU codegen: Triton inside Anvil (Metal simdgroup_matrix and CUDA tensor cores)

*wow 8/10 · months*

Both GPU backends run one thread per output element, use no threadgroup or shared memory, and hand products to MPS. That is why MPS is 5x faster on convolutions and why the GPU can't fuse epilogues. This idea gives every GPU kernel a real tiled schedule: tiles staged through threadgroup memory, simdgroup_matrix 8x8 MMA on Apple GPUs, ldmatrix + mma.sync + cp.async multistage pipelines on NVIDIA, and prologues and epilogues fused in registers. It's the same kernel IR with a GPU schedule like MLX's steel GEMM or Triton's.

- **Build:** (a) anvil/backend/gpu_tile.py: a schedule IR shared by Metal and CUDA (block tile BMxBNxBK, simdgroup/warp tile, stages, swizzled shared layout, vector width), derived from each kernel's Affine accesses. (b) metal.py: threadgroup arrays with simdgroup_load / simdgroup_multiply_accumulate / simdgroup_store, and simd_sum/simd_max for row reductions (softmax, layer_norm, cross_entropy) in place of serial loops. (c) cuda.py: the same with mma.sync (tf32 for f32; f16/bf16 via the precision idea), ldmatrix, cp.async.cg, and commit/wait_group pipelining; optionally emit CuTe/CUTLASS C++ atoms instead of raw PTX. (d) Fused epilogues (bias, gelu, Adam update) and prologues (layer_norm applied as tiles load). (e) `anvil tune --metal` picks tile configs, with MPS kept behind a flag for comparison.
- **Demo:** `anvil run --metal` trains the wide MLP and the GPT with zero MPS calls and beats both MPS and MLX on the same models. The output of `anvil metal` shows a readable simdgroup GEMM with the Adam update in its store loop. Targets: a 1024^3 product at or above MPS's 9.7 TFLOP/s from REPORT.md, and, with the conv idea, the EMNIST CNN on Metal under MPS's 2.30 s per epoch.
- **Builds on:** metal.py/cuda.py shared KernelGen, argument buffers, host-side scalars, Affine offsets, the two-pass reduction split
- **Risk:** Reaching 80-90% of MPS/MLX GEMM takes per-generation tuning (M1, M2 and M3 differ). M5's GPU neural accelerators need Metal 4 tensor ops and macOS 26, while the machine runs macOS 15. The CUDA half can't be tested without real hardware.

### Swarm training: compile 1,000 models into one program

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

The experiments sweep 27 MNIST models by compiling each one separately. An IR-level vmap would add a 'member' dimension to every parameter, optimizer state and per-model temporary, while the data stays shared. Then 1,000 small models with different learning rates, seeds or inits train as one set of batched products that finally fill the AMX unit and the GPU. With every member in one binary, population-based training (Jaderberg et al. 2017) is just a gather along the member axis.

- **Build:** (a) anvil/swarm.py: a kernel-IR transform that prepends a Var to the domain of every kernel touching member state and gives those buffers a leading dimension. This is mechanical with Affine offsets, and the transform checks that control flow is uniform across members. (b) cli.py: `--swarm N --vary lr=logspace(1e-4,1e-1)`, turning compile-time consts into per-member tensors. (c) blas.py/metal.py: the extra dimension maps onto existing strided-batched products. (d) A prelude `pbt_exploit(scores)` that copies the best members' weights over the worst. (e) A live terminal leaderboard using sparklines from the IDEAS.md backlog.
- **Demo:** `anvil run --swarm 1024 --vary lr=... experiments/mnist_sweep.anvil` trains 1,024 MLPs with a live leaderboard and prints a learning-rate vs accuracy curve at the end. It runs against torch.func.vmap + stack_module_state and JAX vmap on the same sweep.
- **Builds on:** experiments/mnist_sweep.anvil and --set, kernel IR Affine offsets, blas.py batched products, Metal backend, show/print output
- **Risk:** Per-member data-dependent control flow (early stopping, different epoch counts) breaks uniformity. Memory grows N-fold, which leans on the arena. Varying architecture needs masking rather than different shapes.

### Bit-identical everywhere, at full speed: deterministic CPU, Metal and CUDA

*wow 8/10 · months*

Anvil is already bit-identical for any thread count on the CPU, and its backends agree to four digits. This goes all the way: the same bits on NEON, Metal and CUDA, for any thread count and any batch size, without the slowdowns PyTorch's use_deterministic_algorithms imposes. Batch-invariant kernels follow Thinking Machines' 2025 'Defeating Nondeterminism in LLM Inference'. You could train on a Mac, resume on an H100, and get the same checkpoint hash.

- **Build:** (a) ir.py: one fixed reduction-tree spec (fixed chunk sizes, pairwise order) implemented identically in kernel.py, metal.py and cuda.py, covering split-K and two-pass reductions. (b) Deterministic GPU scatter-add: sort by index plus a segmented reduction instead of atomicAdd. (c) One shared polynomial exp/log/tanh (vecmath.py's Cody-Waite + polynomial) emitted for MSL and CUDA instead of platform libm, with FMA contraction made explicit. (d) A fixed-order variant of each tile schedule, so accumulation order is pinned. (e) `anvil run --hash`, printing a running SHA of the parameters, and CI that compares hashes across backends.
- **Demo:** The GPT is trained for 1,000 steps on CPU, on Metal and on CUDA, and prints three identical SHA-256 hashes side by side, along with the throughput cost (target under 10%). Then a checkpoint moves mid-run from the Mac to an H100 and training continues bit for bit.
- **Builds on:** the counter-based RNG shared by all backends, the thread-count determinism tests, vecmath.py, the CUDA/Metal two-pass reductions, the PYTHONHASHSEED determinism tests
- **Risk:** simdgroup_matrix and tensor cores fix their own accumulation order and rounding in hardware, so some paths may need fixed-order f32 at a speed cost. MPS, cuBLAS and Accelerate are not reproducible across platforms, so deterministic mode must use Anvil's own kernels.

### Every engine at once: CPU, AMX, GPU and Neural Engine sharing one unified-memory program

*wow 8/10 · moonshot*

Apple silicon puts NEON cores, AMX/SME units, the GPU and the Neural Engine on one pool of memory, and no framework uses them together. Anvil's static graph and cost model make a compile-time partitioner possible. Small kernels stay on the CPU, which already beats the GPU on the small MLP. Big products go to the GPU, and fp16 inference subgraphs go to the ANE. The devices are pipelined across micro-batches with zero copies.

- **Build:** (a) anvil/partition.py: assign each kernel to a device by cost (anvil cost plus Arena/tune measurements) to minimize makespan, with sync costs (MTLSharedEvent signal/wait, tens of microseconds) as edge weights. (b) A hybrid executable: the aarch64.py main program with Metal command buffers encoded inline; the host code is already shared through anvil_host.h. (c) Micro-batch pipelining: the GPU runs step k's forward while the CPU runs step k-1's optimizer, with a one-step-stale variant flagged explicitly. (d) The ANE via fp16 subgraphs exported as Core ML ML Programs (MIL) and called on MLMultiArray views of the shared buffers. Direct ANE programming, following public reverse-engineering work, is a stretch goal.
- **Demo:** The diffusion example samples with its denoiser split across GPU and ANE while the CPU does the sampler math, beating any single device. A device-timeline ASCII chart from --profile shows all four engines busy at once.
- **Builds on:** anvil_host.h shared host code, Metal shared-storage buffers, the CPU backend, anvil cost, the static memory plan
- **Risk:** Sync latency can erase the gains for small kernels. The ANE is inference-only, fp16, and reachable only through Core ML (compile latency, opaque scheduling) unless reverse-engineered. Building and debugging a two-backend executable is complex.

### Precision is a type: f16, bf16, int8 and block-quantized tensors

*wow 7/10 · months*

Make the numeric format part of the tensor type (`W: bf16[784, 128]`, `x: q8_0[n, 4096]`), with promotion and accumulation rules checked at compile time. Then teach every backend the fast instructions the M3 already has and Anvil never uses: FMLA .8h (2x fp32 throughput), FMLAL/FMLAL2 (f16 x f16 -> f32), BFMMLA, SDOT and SMMLA (I8MM). Mixed-precision training is nearly free in Anvil because the optimizer update is already fused into the weight-gradient GEMM: f32 master weights live only in that epilogue, with deterministic stochastic rounding from the counter-based RNG.

- **Build:** (a) ir.py: F16/BF16/I8/U8 dtypes, plus block-quantized formats whose block size and scale dtype are type parameters (Buffer.nbytes stops being 4*numel). (b) elaborate.py/ops.py: dtype unification, `as bf16` casts, f32 accumulators by default for reductions, and errors on lossy implicit casts. (c) kernel.py: vector width per dtype (.8h lanes), fcvtl/fcvtn/bfcvt, sdot GEMV, and bfmmla/smmla tiles with 2x4 / 2x8 interleaved packing. (d) metal.py/cuda.py: half/bfloat in MSL, __half/__nv_bfloat16 in CUDA. (e) interp.py: numpy float16, with bf16 emulated by uint16 rounding. (f) f16/bf16 npy and safetensors loaders. (g) `anvil quantize` (post-training quantization calibrated on data) and a prelude `fake_quant` for quantization-aware training with a straight-through estimator.
- **Demo:** The GPT example trained in bf16 matches f32 loss within seed noise at about 1.7x the speed, and `anvil cost` shows memory traffic halving line by line. The digit reader quantized to int8 with SMMLA runs about 3x faster and still reads 300 of 300 synthetic drawings. A compile error appears when someone sums 4096 f16 values without an f32 accumulator.
- **Builds on:** Buffer.dtype and the f32/i32 split, vecmath.py, anvil cost, the counter-based RNG, fused optimizer epilogues, the npy loader
- **Risk:** The dtype plumbing touches every module: the elaborator, the interpreter, three code generators, runtime printing and loaders. BFMMLA/SMMLA throughput on Apple cores must be measured; it is not guaranteed to beat FMLA .8h. Validating mixed-precision training numerically needs care.

### First light on NVIDIA: real-GPU CI, cuBLASLt epilogues, the whole step as one CUDA Graph

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

The CUDA backend has never touched an NVIDIA GPU. A few weeks gets it running on a rented RTX 4090 or H100 under CI, then makes it fast where Anvil has a structural edge. Control flow and shapes are static, so a whole training step can be captured once as a CUDA Graph and replayed every step, which removes per-kernel launch cost. torch.compile's reduce-overhead mode can only approximate this at run time.

- **Build:** (a) tools/gpu_ci.py: runs `pytest -m cuda` and the vs_pytorch suite on a rented GPU (Modal, RunPod or Lambda, on the user's own account) and records results under benchmarks/. (b) cuda.py: cudaMallocManaged is replaced by one cudaMalloc'd static arena planned by backend/arena.py, with data in pinned host memory and async copies for batches. (c) anvil/backend/cublaslt.py: the CUDA counterpart of blas.py, with fused CUBLASLT_EPILOGUE_BIAS, GELU_BIAS and DGELU epilogues and strided-batched products. (d) CUDA Graph capture of each loop body. Host-computed loop counters (the analysis metal.host_scalars already does) go into a small device params block, so one graph replays every step. (e) `if step % 100` handled with conditional graph nodes (CUDA 12.4+) or by splitting the graph.
- **Demo:** The nine vs_pytorch projects on an H100, in a table against PyTorch eager, torch.compile (default and reduce-overhead) and MPS-equivalent baselines. spirals and logreg reach single-digit microseconds per step because each step is one cudaGraphLaunch. An Nsight Systems timeline shows PyTorch's sea of launches next to Anvil's single graph.
- **Builds on:** cuda.py and anvil_cuda.h, --cuda-emulate and the clang CUDA type-check tests, Metal's host-scalar analysis, arena.py, blas.py matching
- **Risk:** Needs paid GPU time and a CUDA toolchain. First contact will surface bugs emulation hid (alignment, atomics races, 32-bit index overflow). One-thread-per-output kernels will look weak until the tile codegen lands.

### Convolution algorithm zoo: implicit GEMM, Winograd and direct convs, chosen per layer

*wow 7/10 · months*

Anvil's only convolution is im2col plus a product. That means a big copy, and it leaves Metal 5x behind MPS on the EMNIST CNN. This adds the three algorithms real libraries use and lets the compiler pick one per layer from static shapes. Implicit GEMM never materializes the patches: the window offset `stride*i + u` is folded into operand packing. Winograd F(2x2,3x3)/F(4x4,3x3) (Lavin & Gray 2016) handles 3x3 stride-1. Blocked direct convolution (NCHWc, as in oneDNN/XNNPACK) handles depthwise and tiny channel counts. Each covers forward and both gradients.

- **Build:** (a) optimize.py: a recognizer for the existing patches -> contraction pattern from prelude Conv2d, which turns the rule that window expansions stay materialized into an algorithm choice. (b) kernel.py: implicit-GEMM packing that reads xp through the window Affine, with padding as predicated loads so the zero-padded copy disappears. (c) Winograd as a rewrite into three small index-notation kernels (input transform B^T d B, filter transform G g G^T cached across steps, output transform A^T m A) around a batched product, so it runs on every backend and reads clearly in `anvil ir`. (d) A layout pass choosing NCHW, NHWC or NCHWc per tensor. (e) Metal/CUDA implicit GEMM via gpu_tile. (f) Algorithm selection by anvil cost plus anvil tune.
- **Demo:** EMNIST CNN: Metal goes from 12.4 s per epoch to under MPS's 2.30 s, and CPU from 8.1 s to about 3 s. Then examples/resnet18_cifar.anvil runs head to head with PyTorch MPS and MLX. Classroom: Winograd as three lines of index notation with the transform matrices, and the multiply count dropping 2.25x.
- **Builds on:** prelude Conv2d (im2col in index notation), window-expansion handling and loop collapsing in optimize.py, blas.py stacked and summed products, anvil cost
- **Risk:** Winograd F(4,3) loses f32 precision and must be validated for training. A Winograd backward is complex, so implicit GEMM is likely the backward default. There are many shape regimes to cover.

### GPU memory planning and optimal rematerialization under a memory budget

*wow 7/10 · months*

On the CPU, Anvil's static arena keeps programs tiny (charrnn uses 17 MB). On Metal every tensor gets its own MTLBuffer, so the same program needs 2,003 MB and the CNN 2,373 MB. Port the arena to the GPU, then go past what any framework does: the compiler knows every tensor's size and lifetime, so it can choose exactly which activations to keep and which to recompute to fit a memory budget, solving the Checkmate problem (Jain et al., MLSys 2020) at compile time.

- **Build:** (a) arena.py reused by metal.py and cuda.py: one MTLHeap/MTLBuffer (or one cudaMalloc) with static offsets. MPS descriptors and argument buffers already take offsets for views. (b) anvil/remat.py: a pass between autodiff and optimize that duplicates forward kernels into the backward region. It minimizes extra FLOPs (from anvil cost) subject to peak memory (from the arena planner), using a small dependency-free branch-and-bound/LP solver, with the Chen et al. 2016 sqrt(n) heuristic and the Gruslys et al. chain DP as fallbacks. (c) `anvil build --memory 512MB`, plus `anvil cost --pareto` printing the time-memory curve. (d) FlashAttention's recompute becomes a special case of this pass.
- **Demo:** charrnn on Metal drops from 2,003 MB to roughly 20 MB. A GPT whose activations need 8 GB trains in 2 GB, with the compiler printing its plan ('recompute gelu in block 3: +4% FLOPs') and an ASCII Pareto curve. It doubles as a lecture on ILPs inside compilers.
- **Builds on:** backend/arena.py lifetime planner, anvil cost FLOP and memory accounting, autodiff over kernels, Metal argument buffers
- **Risk:** Asynchronous GPU execution: aliasing is safe within one in-order queue, but MPS and multiple command buffers need careful ordering. An exact solver must be written without dependencies, or the pass falls back to heuristics. Rematerialization interacts with fusion decisions.

### BLIS-grade CPU GEMMs with prologue fusion, back-to-back GEMM fusion, and `anvil tune`

*wow 6/10 · months*

The NEON GEMM is register-tiled but has no cache blocking, packing, prefetch or k-unrolling. It runs at about 600 GFLOP/s on 8 cores, against a theoretical ~1.5 TFLOP/s on 12 P-cores. Rebuild it on the Goto/BLIS five-loop structure, then do what libraries can't. Packing is a full pass over the operand anyway, so prologues (layer_norm, relu, dropout masks, transposes) can be fused into it. GEMM -> elementwise -> GEMM chains can be fused so an MLP block's hidden activation never leaves L2 (Chimera, HPCA'23; CUTLASS back-to-back GEMM). `anvil tune` then searches tile sizes per kernel and per chip.

- **Build:** (a) kernel.py: a `blocked` schedule with mc/kc/nc sized from sysctl's 128 KB L1d and 16 MB L2, prfm pldl1keep/pldl2keep prefetch, k-unroll by 4, ldp q-pair loads and 8x12 tiles. (b) Generated packing kernels with fused prologues. A new optimize.py rule lets an elementwise producer sink into a GEMM's pack, which structurally removes the PLAN section 10 bug class where relu was recomputed per 16 columns. (c) optimize.py `b2b_fuse`: row-block tiling across two contractions with an L2-resident intermediate, forward and backward. (d) anvil/tune.py and an `anvil tune` command: time the candidates (R, U, mc, kc, nc, threads, NEON vs AMX vs Accelerate) and cache the winners in ~/.cache/anvil/tune.json, keyed by chip and kernel hash. (e) A TVM-style learned cost model (Chen et al. 2018) that is itself an Anvil MLP trained on the tuning logs, used to prune the search.
- **Demo:** `anvil tune examples/transformer.anvil` shows live per-kernel speed-up bars. NEON fp32 GEMM reaches 1.2 TFLOP/s or more on 12 cores. The fused LN -> W1 -> gelu -> W2 block beats Accelerate plus separate kernels at batch 64 and below. Classroom punchline: the model that tunes Anvil was trained by Anvil.
- **Builds on:** kernel.py vec_out/vec_red register tiles, mir.py allocator with tile retry, work-weighted threading, the optimize.py epilogue-fusion limits (GEMM_EPILOGUE), --profile
- **Risk:** Large fp32 products will still go to AMX, so NEON gains concentrate on mid-size, fused and non-GEMM kernels. Fused prologues add register pressure. Tuning takes time, and cached results go stale across compiler versions.

### Sub-microsecond inference calls: winning the batch-1 latency war

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Batch-1 latency is the one row in the kernel table where PyTorch clearly wins: an MLP call takes 5.7 us there against Anvil's 14.8 us, because Anvil pays for ctypes and a lock. The arithmetic itself is about 2 us. The fix: a tiny CPython extension per compiled function, baked weights prepacked into micro-kernel layout at compile time, tiny calls run entirely on the calling thread, and the whole forward collapsed into one leaf function.

- **Build:** (a) pyapi.py: emit a C stub per signature (METH_FASTCALL/vectorcall, buffer-protocol pointer extraction, shape and dtype checks as integer compares), built by cc next to the .s. It replaces the ctypes slot writes, and per-thread scratch arenas replace the threading.Lock. (b) export.py: the same zero-overhead entry point for C callers. (c) kernel.py: GEMV-specialized schedules (blas.py already refuses M < 2), with weights prepacked into the 4-row vec_red layout at compile time, since anvil.function already bakes them in as constants. (d) aarch64.py: a fused-function mode that emits a small program's kernels as one function with no thread-pool calls when total work is under a threshold. (e) p50/p99 latency rows in benchmarks/vs_pytorch/kernels.py.
- **Demo:** The MNIST MLP at batch 1 from Python in about 1.5 us, against PyTorch 5.7 us, torch.compile 16 us and MPS 42 us. The drawing app re-reads the digit on every mouse-move at 1 kHz. bin/checkers' critic evaluates a million positions per second, enough for a deeper search.
- **Builds on:** pyapi.py pointer slots, export.py baked constants, the row-tiled vec_red dot-product schedule, the runtime.s thread pool
- **Risk:** A C extension needs Python headers, which adds a toolchain step. Per-thread arenas multiply scratch memory. The gains only matter for tiny models.

### Anvil Arena: a public benchmark suite, roofline dashboard and leaderboard

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Turn the vs_pytorch study into a living public leaderboard. The tasks: the nine training projects, single kernels, attention vs context length, LLM prefill and decode tok/s, batch-1 latency, compile time and peak memory. The competitors: PyTorch (eager, compile, MPS, CUDA), MLX, JAX, llama.cpp and tinygrad, on every Apple chip and NVIDIA card people submit. Each Anvil kernel is plotted on its chip's roofline, so the board shows how far everyone is from the hardware limit, not just who wins.

- **Build:** (a) benchmarks/arena/: task specs and adapters (anvil, torch, mlx, jax, llama-bench, tinygrad) writing one JSON schema with machine info, including the load average the REPORT already records. (b) `anvil bench --json` and `anvil cost --roofline`: per-chip peak FLOP/s and GB/s from a microbenchmark, plus each kernel's achieved fraction from --profile. This also delivers IDEAS.md's unfinished item, an estimated time per step from the cost model. (c) A static site (GitHub Pages) generated from results/*.json, with per-chip roofline plots and history per commit. (d) Perf-regression CI on Apple-silicon runners that fails any PR slowing a task by more than 5%.
- **Demo:** A public page, 'Anvil vs MLX vs llama.cpp vs PyTorch on M3 Max', with per-kernel roofline scatter plots. As a class assignment, students submit their own laptop's results and an optimized kernel to climb the board.
- **Builds on:** benchmarks/vs_pytorch (run.py, summary.py, REPORT.md, kernels.py), anvil cost, --profile
- **Risk:** Fair benchmarking is contentious: each framework's best settings, warm caches, thermal state. Adapters need upkeep as MLX and PyTorch change. Little novelty unless the numbers are strong.

## moonshots (15)

### anvilc: Anvil compiles itself, on the GPU

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

Rewrite the Anvil front end in Anvil as data-parallel tensor programs, the way Aaron Hsu's Co-dfns hosts an APL compiler on the GPU. Lexing becomes a parallel scan over finite-state transition functions (Hillis & Steele 1986), the AST becomes a parent vector, and shape checking becomes unification done one tree level at a time. The project then bootstraps to a fixed point: the Python compiler builds anvilc, anvilc builds itself, and stage 2 and stage 3 come out byte-identical. A compiler for an ML language that runs as ML kernels on the GPU is the kind of self-reference that reaches the front page of Hacker News.

- **Build:** Language prerequisites first, in anvil/ir.py, elaborate.py, backend/kernel.py, cuda.py and metal.py: (1) a `scan` primitive (a new ir.Scan next to ir.Reduction), with a Hillis-Steele schedule on NEON and the single-pass decoupled-lookback scan of Merrill & Garland 2016 on Metal and CUDA; (2) i32 bit operations (xor, shl, shr) for hashing; (3) `sort`/`argsort`, as an LSD radix sort routine in runtime.s plus a Metal kernel; (4) capacity+count tensors that generalize `nonzero(mask, size=K)`, for token counts unknown at compile time. Then the compiler itself in anvilc/*.anvil. lex.anvil: bytes() → character classes by a gather on a 256-entry table → each character becomes an i32[S] transition vector → scan by composition (a gather of gathers) → token starts via nonzero → INDENT/DEDENT from prefix sums of leading spaces. parse.anvil uses Hsu's representation: parent p[n], kind k[n], depth d[n]. Bracket depth comes from a scan, parents from all-nearest-smaller-values with pointer jumping, and one pass per precedence level replaces precedence climbing. check.anvil: shape inference sweeps depth levels bottom-up, with a union-find for shape variables done by pointer jumping. emit.anvil writes 'Anvil Core' (index-notation kernels, inlined fns, static shapes, no autodiff at first) as C or MSL through string tables and decode(). bin/bootstrap runs stage0 (Python) → stage1 → stage2 → stage3 and diffs the results, then runs David Wheeler's diverse double-compiling check, with the Python compiler as the second, trusted compiler.
- **Demo:** `anvilc --metal anvilc/*.anvil` lexes, parses and shape-checks the compiler's own source on the M3 Max GPU in milliseconds. The AST is printed as a parent-vector table with `show`, and `diff stage2.c stage3.c` prints nothing. Stress test: a generated 1,000,000-line program, with lines per second compared against the Python front end (target 100×).
- **Builds on:** bytes()/decode() and the string tables of the char-RNN and checkers examples, nonzero(mask, size=K), Gather.rest offsets, the Metal backend's training loops that never wait on the GPU, and interp.py as the oracle for every bootstrap stage
- **Risk:** Full self-hosting needs the elaborator (staging interpreter, inlining, models, autodiff) in data-parallel form, which is research-hard: Hsu's APL work took years. Mitigation: self-host a defined 'Anvil Core' subset and keep the Python compiler for the rest. The GPU front end alone is already a headline. Static shapes make dynamic token counts awkward (capacity bounds everywhere), and a scan primitive touches all four backends.

### Anvil Silicon: the training loop as a circuit (FPGA, then a real chip)

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

A Verilog backend turns the kernel IR (forward pass, backward pass and SGD update) into a fixed-point hardware datapath, so the network learns in the fabric itself with no CPU. Anvil's static shapes and static memory map almost one-to-one onto block RAMs and address counters. First milestone: a $30–100 open-toolchain FPGA trains MNIST on a desk. Finale: a tiny network on a TinyTapeout shared-shuttle ASIC, a chip that learns XOR, compiled from 20 lines.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/verilog.py turns each ir.Kernel into an FSM plus datapath module. Affine offsets become counter-based address generators (the strides are compile-time constants in ir.Affine). Reductions become a pipelined MAC on a DSP slice. Elementwise Lets become pipeline stages. Stores become BRAM write ports. The program's Block/For/If become a top-level sequencer FSM. anvil/quantize.py converts f32 to Qm.n fixed point (int8 weights, int16/int32 accumulators), with stochastic rounding on weight updates from the existing counter RNG (rand_bits). A fixed-point mode in interp.py makes the hardware bit-exact against the reference. arena.py's lifetime-based layout becomes BRAM bank assignment, with read/write ports per cycle as a new constraint. Nonlinearities use lookup tables (exp, sigmoid); relu and max are free. `anvil cost` gains a cycle estimate (MACs per cycle, port conflicts). tests/test_verilog.py simulates through Verilator (Verilog → a C++ model) and compares every element with the fixed-point interpreter. Synthesis uses the open Yosys + nextpnr flow (ECP5 via Project Trellis, Gowin via Apicula). Stretch: the same netlist through a Yosys-to-redstone flow, for a neural network in Minecraft.
- **Demo:** A ULX3S (ECP5) or Tang Nano 20K board connected only to power and a UART prints 'epoch 1  test accuracy 91.8%' while a 784-32-10 int8 MLP trains on MNIST from on-board memory. The whole design comes from `anvil verilog --fixed q8.8 examples/mnist.anvil`. Classroom finale: the same compiler's output for a 2-4-1 network, fabricated on a TinyTapeout tile, learns XOR on the chip as students flip its input switches.
- **Builds on:** the kernel IR (affine offsets, static extents, one kernel form), arena.py memory planning, interp.py as a bit-exact oracle, the counter-based RNG, anvil cost, determinism
- **Risk:** Fixed-point training needs stochastic rounding and careful scaling to converge. MNIST does not fit in BRAM, so it must stream from SPI flash or SDRAM, or use a 14×14 subset. A TinyTapeout tile holds only a few hundred flip-flops of weights and one time-multiplexed multiplier. Shuttle schedules (months until silicon arrives) are outside your control.

### Swarm: the whole classroom trains one model on its phones

*wow 9/10 · months*

Students open a URL on whatever they brought (phones, Chromebooks, a gaming PC), and during one lecture the room trains one transformer together. Anvil compiles the training step to WebAssembly SIMD and WebGPU. A coordinator on the teacher's laptop runs DiLoCo-style low-communication training, which survives devices joining, sleeping and leaving. Anvil is bit-deterministic with a counter-based RNG, so each device regenerates its own data shard and dropout masks from (seed, step, rank). The same property lets the teacher re-execute any student's update and catch a cheater: distributed training that can be verified.

- **Build:** (1) anvil/backend/wgsl.py, a third sibling of CudaGen/MetalGen. KernelGen.ex already emits C-like expressions; WGSL adds explicit types and storage-buffer bindings, and metal.host_scalars' host-side loop counters carry over. (2) A wasm path: the --cuda-emulate C++ (already a portable C backend) built with clang --target=wasm32 -msimd128. The assembly runtime's routines (printing, clock, data loading) are replaced by a small JS shim. (3) anvil/swarm/, a stdlib-only Python coordinator: global weights, the DiLoCo outer loop (Douillard et al. 2023: H local AdamW steps, then an outer Nesterov step on averaged deltas, about 500× less communication than per-step all-reduce), int8-quantized delta uploads, elastic membership. (4) Verification: vecmath.py's exp/log/tanh polynomials ported to wasm, so the CPU path is bit-identical on every device. The coordinator recomputes a random 2% of submitted deltas and rejects mismatches. (5) A dashboard for the projector: devices as nodes, tokens/s each, the global loss curve, live samples. An NVIDIA gaming PC can join through the CUDA backend, which would be its first run on real hardware.
- **Demo:** Thirty students scan a QR code, and within 40 minutes their phones train a character transformer on the course's own lecture notes. The teacher unplugs a phone mid-run and nothing breaks. One tab, modified to send garbage gradients, turns red on the dashboard within a minute. The session ends with the swarm's model writing a fake lecture in the professor's style.
- **Builds on:** the CudaGen/MetalGen generators, anvil_host.h, the C++ emulation path, the counter-based RNG and bit-identical determinism, transformer.anvil
- **Risk:** WebGPU on older phones is missing (fall back to wasm). Bit-exactness across different GPUs is unachievable (FMA contraction, hardware transcendentals), so verification must use the wasm CPU path or tolerances. School Wi-Fi often isolates clients (use a hotspot). Phones throttle thermally, so throughput is uneven.

### No OS: Anvil boots straight into a training loop

*wow 9/10 · months*

Anvil's output is already one assembly file with a hand-written runtime, static memory and no allocation while training. All it still borrows from the operating system is printf, fopen, pthreads and the clock. Replace those and the program becomes a kernel image: a Raspberry Pi with no operating system powers on and trains MNIST on four NEON cores, printing to a serial port. The same approach scales down to a $5 microcontroller that learns on-device (its memory use proven at compile time) and up to an M1/M2 Mac booted bare-metal through Asahi's m1n1.

- **Build:** (1) An ELF/Linux AArch64 dialect for aarch64.py and runtime.s: `:lo12:` relocations instead of @PAGE/@PAGEOFF, no leading underscores. This also unlocks Linux AArch64. (2) anvil/backend/baremetal/. boot.s drops to EL1, sets CPACR_EL1 to enable FP/SIMD, and builds an identity-mapped page table with Normal write-back memory (with the MMU off, unaligned NEON loads fault and nothing is cached). uart.s provides a PL011 mini-printf that replaces _printf/_putchar/_fwrite. CNTVCT_EL0/CNTFRQ_EL0 replace clock_gettime. PSCI CPU_ON or spin tables wake secondary cores, replacing pthread_create in _anvil_rt_parallel. .incbin data, decompressed at build time, replaces fopen/gzread. A linker script places arena.py's layout. (3) Targets: QEMU `virt` (CI), Raspberry Pi 4/5 (kernel8.img), and a portable-C tier through the --cuda-emulate C++ for an RP2350 Pico 2, where `anvil build` proves the arena fits in 520 KB. (4) Stretch goals: chainload via m1n1 on M1/M2, emitting raw AMX opcodes (corsix/amx) because Accelerate isn't available; a sizecoding target, a static Linux ELF using raw syscalls that trains MNIST in under 8 KB of code.
- **Demo:** A Raspberry Pi 5, a UART cable, and an SD card holding one ~150 KB image plus the data. At power-on the terminal prints 'anvil bare-metal, 4 cores', then MNIST epochs climb to 97%, with no Linux anywhere. Second act: a Pico 2 with an accelerometer learns three of a student's hand gestures in 15 seconds with no computer attached.
- **Builds on:** runtime.s, aarch64.py, arena.py's static memory layout, ir.indirect heap tensors, the C++ emulation as a portable C backend, the deterministic counter RNG
- **Risk:** Pi multicore bring-up (spin tables vs PSCI) and MMU/cache setup are fiddly and poorly documented. Data loading must be redone without a filesystem. m1n1 runs on M1/M2 but not M3, and the AMX opcodes are unofficial. Microcontroller code goes through C, not the hand-tuned NEON schedules.

### Training in a spreadsheet: every gradient is a cell, every F9 is a step

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

`anvil export --xlsx` compiles a whole training program (forward pass, backward pass, SGD update) into an Excel / Google Sheets / LibreOffice workbook of ordinary formulas. Press F9 and the network takes one training step. Click any weight's gradient cell to see its exact derivative formula. GPT-2 inference in Excel went viral; a compiler that turns source code into trainable spreadsheets, with autodiff visible cell by cell, would be a very strong teaching tool.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/xlsx.py, writing .xlsx with only zipfile and XML (keeping zero dependencies). Each Buffer becomes a named range on a sheet, with 3-D/4-D tensors laid out as 2-D tiles. Each Kernel's stores become per-element formulas: reductions as SUMPRODUCT over row/column ranges for matmuls, or SUM/MAX of computed expressions; Select as IF; exp/log/tanh as built-ins. The counter-based random numbers are precomputed as constant cells. Params are self-referential cells under iterative calculation, with a step-counter cell and double-buffered weights (even steps write W_a from W_b, odd steps the reverse), so one recalculation is exactly one SGD step whatever the app's evaluation order. Conditional-formatting color scales draw heatmaps (a decision-boundary grid, weight images), and a chart sheet plots the loss. tests/test_xlsx.py recalculates the workbook N times in headless LibreOffice and compares every cell with interp.py.
- **Demo:** spirals.xlsx (the 2-16-16-2 MLP from examples/spirals.anvil) on the projector. Hold F9: a 40×40 grid of colored cells morphs from noise into two interlocking spirals while the loss chart falls. A student clicks the gradient cell for W1[3,7] and reads `=SUMPRODUCT(dZ1!C2:C201, X!B2:B201)*(Z1!D4>0)`. A second workbook: softmax regression on 1,000 MNIST digits, whose ten learned weight images appear as colored cell grids.
