- **Builds on:** the kernel IR (backward kernels are just more kernels), autodiff.py, interp.py as the oracle, the export path, examples/spirals.anvil and softmax_regression.anvil
- **Risk:** Iterative-calculation order is implementation-defined (hence double buffering, which must be validated in each app). Function support differs between Excel, Sheets and LibreOffice. An MNIST-sized MLP (100k weight cells) recalculates slowly. Summation order inside SUMPRODUCT can differ slightly from interp.py.

### The compiler that learns its own schedules by gradient descent

*wow 8/10 · months*

Replace the hand-ordered schedule list in choose_configs and the fusion heuristics in optimize.py with a cost model trained in Anvil on measured kernel timings. Then search for schedules by gradient descent through that model over a relaxed (Gumbel-softmax) schedule space. This is the input-gradient trick dream.anvil uses to dream digits, applied to dreaming fast code. To close the loop, the cost model is itself an Anvil program, compiled with the schedules it chose. DiffTune (Renda et al. 2020) used a differentiable surrogate this way to tune llvm-mca's parameters, and Halide's learned autoscheduler and TVM's Ansor rely on search. Doing it with gradients, inside the language being compiled, is new.

- **Build:** (1) anvil/fuzz.py generates random well-typed index-notation kernels (matmul-, conv-, reduction-, gather- and elementwise-shaped, with random extents and strides) and checks them against interp.py. It doubles as a Csmith-style fuzzer for the compiler. (2) `anvil tune` (anvil/tune.py) compiles each kernel under every candidate Config (mode, U, R, hoist), every parallel grain (aarch64.parallel_grain), and new knobs: loop order, k-unroll, and the fuse/inline decisions of Optimizer.try_fuse/try_inline. It times them with the existing --profile counters and stores the dataset as .npy. (3) A featurizer over the IR: per-access stride vectors from ir.Affine coefficients, extents, op counts by kind, reuse distances, bytes/FLOPs from cost.py. (4) examples/compiler/costmodel.anvil, a small transformer over per-access feature tokens trained to predict log run time. (5) Schedule search: categorical knobs as logits, Gumbel-softmax samples, `minimize predicted_time over logits` through the frozen model (dream.anvil's pattern), then round, measure, and add the result to the dataset (active learning). (6) choose_configs calls the model through anvil.function at compile time, and the tests require every chosen schedule to match the interpreter.
- **Demo:** A projected scatter plot of predicted against measured time for 5,000 held-out fuzzed kernels. Then `anvil tune examples/` re-schedules every example, and a table shows per-example speedups over the hand-written heuristic. Final slide: the cost model was compiled with its own schedules and now predicts faster.
- **Builds on:** backend/kernel.py choose_configs and Config, aarch64.parallel_grain, optimize.py's fusion heuristics, --profile, cost.py, anvil.function, dream.anvil's input gradients
- **Risk:** Today's schedule space is about a dozen Configs, so the headroom may be only 10–30% until the space grows (loop orders, tile shapes, fusion choices). Timing noise on a busy machine (the README measured at load average 16) needs repeated runs. A Gumbel relaxation can land on infeasible schedules (register spills), so the result must always be rounded and measured.

### Proved, not tested: Lean 4 certificates for shapes, bounds, fusion and gradients

*wow 8/10 · months*

Each time Anvil compiles a program, it also writes a Lean 4 file proving three things: every index access is in bounds, every fusion the optimizer did preserves meaning, and every backward kernel is the true adjoint of its forward kernel. `lake build` checks the file. This is translation validation, as Alive2 does for LLVM: the 18,000 lines of Python stay untrusted while every output is certified. The shape checker also gets a formal soundness theorem.

- **Build:** (1) lean/AnvilCore/: a Lean 4 formalization of the kernel IR, mirroring anvil/ir.py (a Kernel is a domain, an optional reduction, and stores over affine offsets). It includes a semantics over ℝ and a typing judgment for static shapes, with progress/preservation: well-shaped programs never index out of bounds. (2) anvil/certify.py emits Lean per program. Bounds obligations come from the elaborator's compile-time bounds proofs and ir.Affine.bounds(), as linear integer goals closed by `omega`. Each Optimizer.merge, try_inline and collapse_loops step gets an equivalence lemma between the kernels before and after, closed by `simp` + `omega` over the affine substitutions. Each autodiff.Backprop.backward_kernel gets an adjoint lemma, proved once generically for Y[I] = Σ_J f(X[g(I,J)]) with Mathlib's HasFDerivAt and the chain rule, then instantiated per kernel. (3) `anvil check --prove`, plus a CI job. Stretch: the shape checker reimplemented in Lean, compiled by Lean to C, and called by editors/vscode, so the checker in the editor is the proven one.
- **Demo:** `anvil check --prove examples/transformer.anvil` writes about 1,400 Lean theorems (one per access, fusion and gradient), and Lean checks them all in under a minute. Then a deliberately planted optimizer bug (an off-by-one in loop collapsing) passes the existing test suite on small shapes, but Lean rejects its certificate and names the kernel and the failing index.
- **Builds on:** the single kernel form out[I] = ⊕_J body(I, J), ir.Affine, the compile-time bounds proofs in elaborate.py, autodiff.py's rules, test_gradcheck.py and the optimizer-equivalence tests
- **Risk:** Fusion and reassociation are equal only over the reals, so the certificates prove real-valued equivalence, and bit-level reduction order needs a separate argument. The generic adjoint theorem is substantial Mathlib proof engineering. Checking time may be long for heavily unrolled programs (the char-RNN has 1,376 kernels). ATL (Coq, POPL 2022) is prior art, so the novelty is integrating this end to end into an ML language.

### Qubits are axes of size 2: a quantum backend

*wow 8/10 · months*

An n-qubit state is a tensor c64[2, 2, ..., 2], and a gate is index notation over one or two of its axes. So Anvil's shape checker becomes a quantum-circuit checker, and its reverse-mode autodiff becomes adjoint differentiation for variational circuits. The same program can then emit OpenQASM 3 and run on real IBM quantum hardware. There the compiler generates parameter-shift circuits as the backward pass, because hardware cannot run backprop.

- **Build:** (1) A c64 dtype through ir.py (a complex Expr type, or pairs of f32), simplify.deriv (Wirtinger derivatives for real-valued losses), interp.py (numpy complex64), kernel.py (NEON FCMLA/FCADD complex multiply-accumulate; FEAT_FCMA is present on Apple M1 and later), and cuda.py/metal.py (float2). (2) A contraction-order planner in optimize.py. Today `sum A[i,j]*B[j,k]*C[k,l]` is one kernel that loops over every index; a greedy opt_einsum/cotengra-style pairwise planner fixes that, and it also speeds up classical programs. (3) anvil/quantum.anvil: qubits(n), H/X/CNOT/RX/RY/RZ(θ) as functions over reshaped axes (`out[l, a, r] = sum U[a, b] * psi[l, b, r]`), Pauli-string expectations, and shot sampling with the counter RNG. (4) anvil/backend/qasm.py lowers circuit programs to OpenQASM 3. A hardware rule in autodiff.py implements d⟨H⟩/dθ = (⟨H⟩(θ+π/2) − ⟨H⟩(θ−π/2))/2 (the parameter-shift rule of Mitarai 2018 and Schuld 2019) by emitting the shifted circuits. A small runner submits jobs to IBM Quantum's REST API or a local simulator.
- **Demo:** VQE for the H2 molecule: 40 lines of Anvil sweep the bond length and draw the dissociation curve, reaching the exact ground state (−1.137 Ha at 0.74 Å) in simulation. Then `anvil run --qasm` runs the same file on a real IBM processor, and the class sees the noisy hardware curve beside the exact one. Bonus: a 30-qubit state vector (8 GB) simulated on the M3 Max GPU, where 'gate on qubit 31 of a 30-qubit register' is a compile-time error.
- **Builds on:** index notation and range inference, reverse-mode autodiff on the kernel IR, minimize/adam, the counter-based RNG for shots, the Metal backend for large state vectors
- **Risk:** Complex numbers touch every layer: the IR, autodiff and all four backends. State-vector simulation is well-trodden (Qiskit Aer, cuQuantum), so the novelty is the language integration, not speed. IBM's free tier gives minutes per month and its queues can be long. Static rank means every register size is a separate compile.

### Anvil writes Anvil: a code model that runs, fine-tunes and decodes inside Anvil

*wow 8/10 · months*

Load a real pretrained code LLM (Qwen2.5-Coder-0.5B) from safetensors into an Anvil program, LoRA-fine-tune it in Anvil on Anvil code, and decode with Anvil's shape checker in the loop so it cannot emit a shape error. The model that writes Anvil models is itself trained by Anvil. A fuzzer of well-typed programs grows the corpus, and generated programs are compiled and run, and kept only if they learn. Along the way: 'llama2.c in 120 lines of Anvil'.

- **Build:** (1) A `safetensors()` loader in builtins.py: the JSON header is parsed at compile time into shapes, as npy() does, and bf16 is widened to f32 on load. (2) examples/llm/qwen.anvil: RMSNorm, RoPE, grouped-query attention and SwiGLU in index notation. The KV cache is static [L, T_max, H, D] buffers written at a run-time position (runtime-offset slices already exist), with top-p sampling from the counter RNG. Benchmarked against llama.cpp and MLX. (3) LoRA adapters as `param`s with frozen base weights and `minimize loss over lora with adam`. The corpus is examples/, the prelude, test snippets and docs, plus anvil/fuzz.py programs and mutations filtered through `anvil check`. (4) Constrained decoding: at each newline, beams go through `anvil check --json --stdin` (ide.py); beams with shape errors are pruned, or their diagnostic is fed back into the prompt. This needs the cached elaborated prelude from IDEAS §4. (5) An `anvil suggest "..."` CLI and an editor command in editors/vscode/extension.js.
- **Demo:** In VS Code the teacher types '# a VAE for MNIST with a 16-d latent and KL warmup' and presses a key. The Anvil-hosted model writes 40 lines, shape inlay hints appear, and the program compiles on the first try, trains and prints its ELBO. The status bar notes that this model was fine-tuned by Anvil on this laptop 20 minutes earlier.
- **Builds on:** transformer.anvil, npy()'s compile-time header parsing, runtime-offset slices, anvil check --json (ide.py), anvil.function, the VS Code extension
- **Risk:** The f32 weights alone take about 2 GB, and fine-tuning is slow without f16 and fused attention (attention currently materializes the score matrix). The real Anvil corpus is about 25 programs, so quality depends on the fuzzer and mutations. Checker-in-the-loop decoding is only as fast as elaboration, which can take seconds per check on big programs today.

### Differentiate the whole training run: distill MNIST into ten images

*wow 8/10 · months*

Make `grad` work through run-time loops, then point it at an entire training run: the gradient of the final validation loss with respect to the training data, the learning-rate schedule, or the initialization. Anvil's determinism makes this cheap. The counter-based RNG and the shuffles replay exactly, so the reverse pass can recompute forward states from a few checkpoints (Griewank's revolve) instead of storing them. Flagship: dataset distillation, ten synthetic images that train a fresh network in a few steps.

- **Build:** (1) autodiff.py: a reverse rule for ir.For with a fixed trip count. The SSA loop-carried write-backs (already explicit in elaborate.py's LoopCtx.carried) become a tape, a reverse loop runs the backward kernels in reverse iteration order, and binomial checkpointing (revolve, Griewank & Walther 2000) chooses which iterations to store and which to recompute. Random draws and shuffles are recomputed from (seed, stream, step), never stored. (2) Second order: differentiating through `minimize` inside the loop means differentiating the optimizer updates in prelude.anvil (Adam state included), so second derivatives of max/relu/sqrt go into simplify.deriv. (3) API: `g = grad(val_loss, [lr_schedule, synthetic_x])` over an ordinary `for step in range(...)` with minimize inside. (4) examples/distill.anvil and examples/hypergrad.anvil. charrnn.anvil moves off `static for` (compile time 3.3 s → ~0.3 s).
- **Demo:** `bin/anvil run examples/distill.anvil` uses `show` to draw ten ghostly synthetic digits learned by gradient descent through 3 steps of SGD. A fresh network trained on only those ten images for 3 steps scores about 90% on the MNIST test set (Wang et al. 2018 reported about 94% with a fixed initialization). Second panel: a learned per-step learning-rate schedule that comes out as warmup followed by decay, discovered rather than designed.
- **Builds on:** autodiff.py's Backprop, the SSA loop-carried values in elaborate.py, the counter-based RNG and shuffle, static for, dream.anvil, show
- **Risk:** Recomputation cost multiplies with checkpointing on long inner loops. Second derivatives through Adam's sqrt and max are numerically touchy. Distillation works far better with a fixed initialization. The loop tape must coexist with the optimizer's in-place reuse and write-back coalescing.

### Trains inside a game: Anvil as a game-engine compute shader

*wow 8/10 · months*

Compile an Anvil program to GLSL compute shaders plus a C++ GDExtension, so a Godot game trains its own agents on the GPU in the spare milliseconds of each frame. Physics written in Anvil index notation is differentiable, so agents can learn by backpropagating through the simulation (as DiffTaichi and Brax do). The alternative is evolution strategies, whose perturbations the counter RNG regenerates from seeds instead of storing them (Salimans et al. 2017). Players watch the AI learn while they play.

- **Build:** (1) anvil/backend/glsl.py: a GLSL 450 compute generator derived from CudaGen/MetalGen, with the same kernels, explicit binding layouts and workgroup sizes, and a tiled shared-memory matmul replacing MPS. (2) anvil/backend/gdextension.py emits a C++ GDExtension node (AnvilProgram) that owns RenderingDevice storage buffers, records each step's compute list once and replays it every frame. Host-side loop counters, as in metal.py, keep the game from ever stalling on a readback. GDScript gets `step(n)` and `tensor(name)`, and tensors can be bound directly as instance buffers so rendering reads positions with no copy. (3) examples/game/physics.anvil: a batched 2D car / spring-mass simulator in index notation; policies are trained either by BPTT with `static for` over a 32-step horizon or by ES. (4) A Godot demo project under examples/game/ with 1,024 instance-rendered agents.
- **Demo:** In a Godot scene of 1,024 little cars, at second 0 they spin in circles. With the game running at 60 fps, the HUD reads 'training: 3 ms/frame on GPU', and within two minutes the cars are lapping the track. The player drives one car and gets overtaken by the swarm. The teacher edits the reward in the .anvil file, hot-reloads, and the cars learn a new behavior live.
- **Builds on:** CudaGen/MetalGen kernel generation and host_scalars, static for (differentiable unrolled time), the counter RNG, minimize/adam, the Snake DQN example
- **Risk:** Godot's RenderingDevice has binding limits and no MPS-quality matmuls, so it will be slower than the Metal backend. Gradients through contact physics are poor (needs smoothing, or ES instead). Long unrolled horizons make compile times explode (the char-RNN's 1,376 kernels take 3.3 s).

### Private by math: the server reads your digit without seeing it (FHE backend)

*wow 8/10 · months*

Compile an Anvil function to a homomorphic-encryption circuit under CKKS, so inference runs on encrypted inputs and the server sees neither the data nor the answer. Static shapes and static control flow are exactly what FHE compilers need. Anvil can compute the multiplicative depth and choose the ring size, modulus chain and slot packing at compile time, as Microsoft's EVA and Google's HEIR do, but starting from a 20-line ML program.

- **Build:** (1) anvil/backend/fhe.py lowers an exported function's kernel IR to CKKS operations. Matmuls use the Halevi-Shoup diagonal method with rotations, and convolutions use packed rotations, both chosen from the ir.Affine access patterns. A pass next to cost.py inserts rescale/relinearize operations, counts depth and selects parameters (N, log Q, scale). (2) An `fhe` mode in prelude.anvil swaps relu for trained low-degree polynomials (x², as in CryptoNets 2016, or a degree-3 fit), and the model is retrained that way. (3) C++ generated against OpenFHE and packaged through export.py, plus a small client (keygen, encrypt, decrypt). (4) A plaintext CKKS simulator in interp.py with modeled noise, to check accuracy before paying for real encryption. (5) A bin/draw --private mode.
- **Demo:** Two laptops. A student draws a 7 in bin/draw --private, and it is encrypted locally and sent to the teacher's laptop. That screen shows only ciphertext hex while the Anvil-compiled CNN runs on it for a second or two and returns another ciphertext. The student's laptop decrypts it and prints '7 (98%)'. `anvil cost --fhe` reports depth, ring size and seconds per reading before anything runs.
- **Builds on:** anvil export (function → C library with weights baked in), cost.py, affine access analysis in the kernel IR, digits_model.anvil, bin/draw
- **Risk:** CKKS latency (seconds per small CNN) and the error from polynomial activations require retraining the model. The backend depends on OpenFHE, so it must stay optional to keep the zero-dependency rule. Packing and rotation optimization is a research area. Security parameters must come from the library's 128-bit defaults, never be hand-picked.

### Every transistor at once: one training step across NEON, AMX, the GPU and the Neural Engine

*wow 8/10 · moonshot*

The M3 Max has four kinds of compute sharing one pool of unified memory; PyTorch uses one at a time. Anvil already targets three of them (NEON, AMX through Accelerate, and Metal). Add the Apple Neural Engine, driven directly through its reverse-engineered private interface as tinygrad did, plus raw AMX instructions as documented by corsix. Then let the compiler's cost model split each step across all four at once, with zero copies. 'The ML compiler that lights up every compute unit on the chip' is a headline.

- **Build:** (1) anvil/backend/amx.py emits raw AMX opcodes (the .word encodings from corsix/amx: ldx/ldy/fma32/stz) inside kernel.py's GEMM schedules. Epilogues (bias, activation, the fused optimizer update) then run on the AMX result tiles instead of being split off around cblas_sgemm as blas.py does today; on M4, the official SME2 ZA path instead. (2) anvil/backend/ane.py lowers eligible fp16 matmul/conv chains with elementwise epilogues to the Neural Engine through the private AppleNeuralEngine framework, with an f16 dtype added to ir.py. (3) anvil/partition.py, a heterogeneous scheduler: per-kernel FLOP/byte counts from cost.py plus a measured throughput table per device decide how to split the batch dimension within a step or pipeline layers across devices. It inserts fences (MTLSharedEvent, the runtime.s thread-pool join) instead of copies, because every buffer lives in the same unified-memory arena. (4) `anvil run --all`, plus a Chrome-trace timeline with four lanes.
- **Demo:** A live timeline of one wide-MLP training step with the P-cores, AMX, GPU and Neural Engine lanes all busy at once. Then a scoreboard: PyTorch MPS, Anvil --metal, and Anvil --all on the 784-1024-1024-10 MLP, where --all aims for 1.6–2× over the best single device.
- **Builds on:** blas.py (AMX through Accelerate), metal.py (host-side loop counters, training that never waits), the runtime.s thread pool, cost.py, arena.py
- **Risk:** The Neural Engine interface is private, undocumented and fp16-only, changes between macOS releases, and rejects arbitrary graphs; any update could break it. AMX opcodes are unofficial on M1–M3 and give way to SME on M4. Synchronization can eat the gain on small models. Mixing fp16 ANE results in breaks Anvil's bit-exact determinism, so the ANE share must be opt-in.

### ∂Forth: gradient descent fills in the missing line of a program

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

A differentiable interpreter for a tiny stack language, written in Anvil: the program counter, the stack and memory are probability-weighted tensors. Leave a hole in a program and `minimize` learns what belongs there from input/output examples (Bošnjak et al. 2017, 'Programming with a Differentiable Forth Interpreter'). It runs on today's Anvil, because `static for` already differentiates through an unrolled interpreter loop. A program synthesized by backprop in the language that compiles it is mind-bending in a PL or ML class.

- **Build:** examples/forth/dforth.anvil keeps the machine state as tensors: the data stack D[depth, width] with a soft stack pointer p[depth] (a distribution), a return stack the same way, and a program counter pc[n_instr] as a distribution. Each instruction (DUP, SWAP, OVER, DROP, +, <, IF/JMP) is an index-notation update that moves probability mass: push shifts the pointer distribution, and a read is `sum p[i] * D[i, :]`. A sketch with holes has `param slot: [n_choices]` logits, and each hole executes a softmax mixture of instructions. Training unrolls `static for` over T interpreter steps and runs `minimize loss over slots with adam`, with a curriculum from short lists to longer ones. Then the program is discretized by argmax and re-run on an exact, hard interpreter (also in Anvil) to verify it. The only compiler work needed is cached prelude elaboration, to keep unrolled compile times sane.
- **Demo:** The screen shows a bubble-sort program in Forth with one blank where the compare-and-swap decision goes. Anvil trains for about 10 seconds on 64 random lists while an ASCII heatmap (`show`) of the soft program counter sharpens. The blank resolves to `> IF SWAP THEN`, and the hard interpreter then sorts unseen lists of length 8 correctly.
- **Builds on:** static for (gradients through time), index notation for soft reads and writes, softmax/minimize/adam, show for heatmaps, the integer-interpreter style of the checkers example
- **Risk:** Differentiable interpreters are notoriously hard to train: TerpreT (Gaunt et al. 2016) showed gradient descent often fails where discrete search succeeds. Holes must stay small, and curricula help. T steps × stack depth unrolls into many kernels and slow compiles.

### A neural instrument: Anvil models as real-time audio plugins

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Audio threads forbid allocation, locks and unbounded work: everything PyTorch does at run time and nothing Anvil does. Anvil's memory is static, its cost is known at compile time and its results are deterministic. `anvil export --plugin` wraps an Anvil function as an AU/VST3/CLAP plugin in C++, and `anvil cost` checks the per-buffer cycle budget before the plugin goes into a DAW. Train a neural amp model or a DDSP synth in Anvil, then play it live in FL Studio.

- **Build:** (1) anvil/export_audio.py: thin C++ glue (CLAP's C API is the simplest; AU/VST3 for wider host support) around the export.py library. process(in, out, nframes) calls the Anvil function per block, with streaming state (causal-conv history, GRU state) held in static buffers. (2) A realtime check in cost.py: FLOPs and bytes per sample × sample rate against measured core throughput, refusing to export when the worst case misses the buffer deadline. The arena already guarantees no allocation on the call path. (3) wav()/save_wav() in builtins.py. (4) examples/audio/: a WaveNet-style dilated causal-conv amp model (as in Neural Amp Modeler) trained on a dry/wet recording pair, and a DDSP harmonic-plus-noise synth (Engel et al. 2020) driven by MIDI pitch and loudness.
- **Demo:** In FL Studio, a guitar DI track runs through 'AnvilAmp', trained five minutes earlier on the laptop from a 3-minute recording of a real amp, at about 2% CPU with 64-sample buffers. Then a DDSP violin plays from a MIDI keyboard. `nm` on the plugin shows no malloc anywhere on the audio path.
- **Builds on:** anvil export (C library with weights baked in, about 0.04 ms per call), the static arena, cost.py, the conv and GRU layers, bytes()
- **Risk:** Neural amp modeling and DDSP already exist (NAM, RTNeural), so the novelty is the compile-time real-time guarantee, not the sound. Plugin SDK glue and host support (CLAP vs AU/VST3 in a given DAW) are fiddly. Low-latency streaming convolutions need ring-buffer state that the language doesn't express yet.

## scientific-computing (16)

### Differentiable smoke that spells your word, with checkpointing planned at compile time

*wow 10/10 · months*

In the drawing app you write a word. Anvil runs Stable Fluids (Stam 1999) forward 100 steps, and gradient descent adjusts the initial velocity field until a puff of smoke rolls into your letters. This is the result of McNamara, Treuille, Popović and Stam (SIGGRAPH 2004), live on the GPU. The compiler work behind it is reverse mode through run-time time loops with binomial (revolve) checkpointing. Because every state size is static, the snapshot schedule and its exact memory are decided at compile time: `anvil cost` would print something like "backward: 11 snapshots × 3.1 MB, 2.6× recompute".

- **Build:** (1) examples/physics/smoke.anvil: semi-Lagrangian advection (backtrace, i32(floor) gathers, bilinear weights; gradients reach field values through the scatter path and velocities through the weights), pressure projection by multigrid-preconditioned CG with the implicit adjoint from the solvers idea, vorticity confinement, and boundaries from the boundary-types idea. (2) autodiff.py: a loop-reversal mode for `for t in range(T)` with loop-carried state. The forward pass stores snapshots on the Griewank-Walther revolve schedule, and the backward pass replays segments in reverse. (3) backend/arena.py places the snapshot buffers. (4) A memory-budget clause (`minimize loss over v0 with adam() memory 1GB`) picks the snapshot count, and cost.py reports the recompute factor. (5) examples/draw/server.py streams frames during optimization. (6) gradcheck in f64 from the precision idea.
- **Demo:** Write "ANVIL" in the browser. Each optimization iteration replays the simulation, and the smoke forms the letters more sharply. A slider scrubs through time. VALIDATION: gradients against f64 finite differences. The forward simulation against PhiFlow (Holl et al. 2020) on an identical setup. Divergence after projection checked by an assert every step. Revolve recompute counts equal Griewank's binomial formula. Speed against DiffTaichi's smoke example (Hu et al., ICLR 2020) and PhiFlow on JAX.
- **Builds on:** Loop-carried SSA buffers, the static arena planner, gathers and scatters with their adjoints, the Metal backend, examples/draw, cost.py
- **Risk:** Autodiff through run-time loops is the biggest compiler change in this list. Gradients through long chaotic horizons explode or vanish, which needs short horizons, multiple shooting or per-frame forces. Gradients of the semi-Lagrangian backtrace are only piecewise smooth.

### Moonshot: a differentiable planet: shallow water on the cubed sphere, with a learned closure, across GPUs

*wow 10/10 · moonshot*

Extend boundary types from wrap and clamp to glued domains. A cubed sphere is six N×N faces whose edges map onto each other by affine index permutations, so the same peeling pass that handles periodic boxes can generate the halo logic. When faces are spread across GPUs, it can derive the halo exchange too. Run the Williamson shallow-water test suite and the Galewsky jet instability. Then train a small CNN closure end to end through dozens of unrolled solver steps, so a coarse model tracks one 4× finer (the NeuralGCM idea, Kochkov et al., Nature 2024). Along the way, the CUDA backend runs on real NVIDIA hardware for the first time, across several GPUs.

- **Build:** (1) Glued-domain boundary types in anvil/boundary.py: edge maps with orientation flips, and corner handling. (2) Equiangular cubed-sphere metric terms as compile-time tables. (3) Finite-volume or spectral-element shallow-water equations with RK3 and hyperviscosity, in examples/planet/. (4) A `distribute` clause that spreads faces or tiles across devices. Halo exchanges are derived from the boundary types and overlapped with interior compute; the interior/boundary peeling already separates the two. (5) cuda.py gains NCCL send/recv, CUDA Graphs to cut launch latency, and shared-memory stencil tiles (Micikevicius 2009, 2.5D blocking). (6) A halo-aware per-face Conv2d. (7) Checkpointed training through time from the smoke idea. (8) A three.js globe renderer.
- **Demo:** The Galewsky et al. 2004 barotropic jet: a slight bump rolls up into a chain of vortices over six simulated days on a spinning globe in the browser. A coarse run drifts away, while the coarse run with the learned closure tracks the high-resolution reference. VALIDATION: Williamson et al. 1992 test case 2 (steady geostrophic flow with an exact solution; l1, l2 and l∞ error convergence), test case 5 (flow over a mountain) against NCAR's high-resolution spectral reference, and test case 6 (Rossby-Haurwitz wave). Mass conserved to round-off. Weak scaling from 1 to 8 GPUs.
- **Builds on:** Boundary types and domain peeling, the CUDA backend (never run on hardware), the Metal backend, checkpointed autodiff through loops, Conv2d, cost.py
- **Risk:** Very high: there is no NVIDIA hardware experience yet, cubed-sphere connectivity and corner handling are notoriously fiddly, multi-GPU work needs a cluster, and learned closures can go unstable in long rollouts.

### The compiler does von Neumann stability analysis: CFL errors before you run

*wow 9/10 · weeks*

A linear stencil in Anvil is index notation with constant affine offsets and compile-time coefficients, so the compiler can compute its Fourier symbol G(θ) = Σ c_o·e^{iθ·o}. `anvil check` could then report something like: "this time step is unstable: |G| = 1.36 at θ = (π, π); explicit Euler with the 5-point Laplacian needs α·dt/dx² ≤ 1/4". It could also give each scheme's order of accuracy and its modified equation (for example, the numerical diffusion added by upwinding), and show the amplification factor as an inlay hint that turns red while you edit DT. NumPy code is opaque to this kind of analysis. It needs a compiler that sees the stencil, and von Neumann analysis is a lecture students usually grind through by hand.

- **Build:** New anvil/numerics.py, run after elaboration. (1) Find runtime loops whose loop-carried canonical buffers (the SSA write-backs elaborate.py already creates) are updated by kernels that read the carried state only at affine offsets. (2) Linearize the update with simplify.deriv, the symbolic scalar derivative autodiff already uses, to get one coefficient per (field, offset). Nonlinear terms use frozen coefficients around a representative state. (3) Build the symbol per θ as a small matrix for systems (2×2 for Gray-Scott; RK stages composed). Sample θ ∈ [-π, π]^d and take the spectral radius. (4) Taylor-expand around θ = 0 to get the order of accuracy and the leading error terms (the modified equation). (5) Report through diagnostics.py with a caret on the time-step line, solving for the largest stable dt in terms of the program's consts. (6) ide.py's JSON gains `stability` records, so the VS Code extension shows max|G| and the dt bound as inlay hints. Sweeps with `--set DT=…`, and an optional `assert stable` statement.
- **Demo:** In VS Code, open a heat-equation file and drag `const DT` past dx²/(4α): the update line turns red with the exact bound. Run it anyway with `--check`. The NaN tracer stops at the same line, and the field blows up as a checkerboard, the θ = (π, π) mode the analysis predicted. VALIDATION: reproduce the textbook table (LeVeque, Strikwerda): FTCS heat r ≤ 1/2 in 1D and 1/4 in 2D, FTCS advection unconditionally unstable, upwind and Lax-Friedrichs |CFL| ≤ 1, Lax-Wendroff second order, leapfrog wave stability. Every predicted bound is checked empirically by bisecting dt with real runs.
- **Builds on:** Affine offsets in the kernel IR, simplify.deriv, loop-carried SSA buffers, diagnostics.py, ide.py and the VS Code inlay hints, --check NaN tracing
- **Risk:** Frozen-coefficient analysis without boundaries gives a necessary condition, not a sufficient one (GKS theory covers boundaries). Nonlinear schemes (Burgers, Navier-Stokes) only get local estimates. Finding 'the time step' in an arbitrary program may need a `step` annotation.

### Hear the shape of a drum: eigenmodes of drawn shapes, isospectral drums, and a drum designed to play a chord

*wow 9/10 · months*

Kac asked in 1966 whether you can hear the shape of a drum, and Gordon, Webb and Wolpert showed in 1992 that you can't. Draw any shape in the browser: Anvil computes the Laplacian's lowest eigenmodes on a masked grid (LOBPCG over a matrix-free stencil), synthesizes the sound of striking it, and plays it. Load the two Gordon-Webb-Wolpert (GWW) polygons and the class hears two different drums sound the same. Then run it backwards: eigenvalue gradients (Hellmann-Feynman, dλ = vᵀ·dA·v) let gradient descent reshape a drum until its overtones form a major chord.

- **Build:** (1) Masked domains from the boundary-types idea (`on mask`, Dirichlet outside the shape). (2) LOBPCG (Knyazev 2001) in anvil/solvers.anvil, with a small dense Rayleigh-Ritz step through `eigh` from the linear-algebra idea and multigrid preconditioning from the solvers idea. (3) An implicit adjoint for eigenpairs (Hellmann-Feynman for simple eigenvalues, with a warning near degeneracy). (4) Shape parameterization as a smoothed density or a level set. (5) Modal synthesis: a sum of damped sinusoids with amplitudes from the mode shapes at the strike point, written out by a new `save_wav` runtime routine. (6) An examples/draw page for drawing, striking and playing.
- **Demo:** Draw a circle, a square or your initials, then tap anywhere to strike it and hear it while the mode shapes animate as Chladni-like patterns. Load the GWW isospectral pair: the shapes differ and the sounds are indistinguishable. Click "tune to C major" and the shape morphs until λ₂/λ₁ and λ₃/λ₁ reach (5/4)² and (3/2)². VALIDATION: unit-disk eigenvalues equal the squared Bessel zeros (2.4048², 3.8317², 5.1356²), with an O(h²) convergence plot. The square gives π²(m² + n²). The GWW drums against Driscoll's 1997 high-precision eigenvalues (SIAM Review). scipy.sparse.linalg.eigsh on the same matrix.
- **Builds on:** Stencils with boundary types and masks, the solvers and implicit adjoints, eigh, examples/draw, the Metal backend
- **Risk:** The staircase boundary of a masked grid limits accuracy near curved edges (Shortley-Weller or cut-cell corrections help). Symmetric shapes have degenerate eigenvalues, where gradients are not well defined. It depends on three other ideas (boundary types, linear algebra, solvers).

### Boundary conditions in the type: `f32[N wrap, N clamp]`, and stencils that keep their shape

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Writing a centered stencil in Anvil is awkward today. I checked: `lap[i] = v[i] + v[i + 2] - 2 * v[i + 1]` fails to compile ("`i + 2` can be 9"), adding `where i < 6` fails too (a plain `v[i]` must cover all 8 elements), and the only form that works is `v[0:6][i] + v[2:8][i] - 2 * v[1:7][i]`, with the shifted origin tracked by hand. dream.anvil pads by scatter-adding into a zeros buffer. The proposal: make the boundary rule part of each dimension's type (wrap/periodic, clamp/Neumann, mirror, fill(c)/Dirichlet, using Halide's names). Then `u[i-1, j]` is legal, outputs keep their N×N shape, the compiler writes the halo handling, and a heat, wave, Gray-Scott or vorticity solver fits on a page. Each time step becomes one fused pass, where NumPy's np.roll makes four copies per Laplacian.

- **Build:** (1) Types: extend the type expressions in ast.py and eval_type in elaborate.py so each dimension carries a boundary tag. Tags pass through elementwise ops, slices and `|>`. (2) Range inference: in Elaborator.solve_ranges, a tagged dimension keeps its exact extent. The lo/hi arithmetic that raises "index out of bounds" today computes the interior box instead: the region where every affine access is in bounds. (3) New anvil/boundary.py peels each kernel's domain into the interior plus faces and corners (3^d regions for radius-1 stencils). Each region is an ordinary Kernel built with Affine.subst (i := i' + 1). In it, out-of-range loads are rewritten as affine loads (wrap: i-1+N, mirror: -i, clamp: 0) or as a Const (Dirichlet). Bounds proofs, autodiff.py, optimize.py fusion and the vec_out schedule in kernel.py then work unchanged. Autodiff handles both cases: wrap is a bijection, so its gradient is an ordinary reduction kernel, and clamp/mirror are non-injective, so they take the existing scatter path. (4) Metal and CUDA run one thread per output, so there the compiler emits a single kernel with select/imod index fixups instead of peeling (on a GPU, dispatches cost more than branches). (5) A stencil library, anvil/stencil.anvil: lap, ddx/ddy (central and upwind), div, curl, advect_upwind, WENO5 reconstruction. (6) Masked domains (`on mask`) for obstacles drawn by hand. (7) interp.py implements boundaries with numpy.pad modes as the reference for differential tests.
- **Demo:** Double slit in examples/draw: draw a wall with two slits, and a 2D wave equation (leapfrog) runs live in the browser. The fringe spacing is checked against λL/d. A second page runs Gray-Scott reaction-diffusion (Pearson 1993 patterns) seeded by your strokes. VALIDATION: the periodic heat equation matches the exact discrete mode decay exp(-4αt·sin²(kΔx/2)/Δx²) to round-off. An O(Δx²) convergence plot. Each boundary mode is compared with scipy.ndimage.laplace's 'wrap', 'nearest', 'reflect' and 'constant'. Gray-Scott at 1024² is timed against NumPy np.roll, Numba, JAX jit and Taichi CPU; the target is 10× NumPy.
- **Builds on:** Index-notation range inference and bounds proofs (elaborate.py solve_ranges), Affine.subst in ir.py, the scatter path in autodiff.py, the vec_out NEON schedule, the drawing app (examples/draw)
- **Risk:** Wide 3D stencils multiply the region count (WENO5 in 3D gives 125 regions), which hurts compile time. Rules are needed for how tags flow through reshape and transpose. Peeling creates many tiny kernels, which is awkward for thread granularity.

### Lattice Boltzmann wind tunnel: draw a shape, watch the vortex street, then let grad streamline it

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

D2Q9 lattice Boltzmann is a pure stencil plus a pointwise collision, which is the best case for whole-step fusion: one kernel per time step that reads and writes 9 floats per cell. Run it on Metal in the drawing app at interactive rates, with drag and lift read off live. Then differentiate the drag with respect to the drawn obstacle (a Brinkman-penalized density) and watch your blob reshape itself to cut drag. The performance target is FluidX3D-class memory-bandwidth efficiency on the same Mac from about 40 lines of Anvil, with `anvil cost` predicting the MLUPS (million lattice-cell updates per second) before the run.

- **Build:** (1) examples/physics/lbm.anvil: pull-scheme streaming `fn[q, y, x] = f[q, y - cy[q], x - cx[q]]` and BGK/TRT collision. (2) Const-table specialization in elaborate.py: a load from a compile-time constant tensor indexed by a small index variable is split per value, so offsets stay affine instead of becoming gathers. Stencil weight tables benefit too. (3) Boundary types from the boundary-types idea: periodic in y, Zou-He inflow and zero-gradient outflow, bounce-back on a mask. (4) SoA layout [9, H, W] for NEON vec_out and coalesced Metal access. (5) An f16 storage dtype: kernel.py widens f16 loads and stores to f32, Metal uses `half`. This is FluidX3D's FP16 compression, which halves memory traffic (Lehmann et al. 2022). (6) cost.py predicts MLUPS from bytes per cell against the M3 Max's bandwidth. (7) examples/draw/server.py streams frames. (8) Shape optimization over a few hundred steps uses the checkpointed time-loop autodiff from the smoke idea.
- **Demo:** A browser wind tunnel: draw a car, a wing or your name, and the vortex street appears. An FFT of the lift force reads off the Strouhal number. Click "streamline" and gradient descent on the shape's density lowers the drag live. VALIDATION: the Poiseuille channel profile converges at second order. A cylinder at Re = 100 gives St ≈ 0.16–0.17 (Williamson 1996). The Schäfer–Turek 1996 benchmark (2D-2) reference intervals for maximum drag, lift and St. Low-Re optimized shapes are compared with Pironneau's 1973 minimum-drag body. MLUPS is compared with FluidX3D's published Apple-silicon numbers.
- **Builds on:** The Metal backend (loop counters stay on the host, so a time step never waits on the GPU), the static arena, fusion, the examples/draw server, boundary types
- **Risk:** LBM is bandwidth-bound, so the gains come only from layout and fusion. f16 storage costs accuracy. Gradients over long horizons of unsteady flow are chaotic: keep horizons short and Re low. Interactive browser LBM demos already exist, so the novelty is the gradient and the performance.

### mpm88 in Anvil: deterministic snow, jelly and water, then a soft robot that learns to walk

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Taichi's well-known 88-line MLS-MPM simulator (Hu et al., SIGGRAPH 2018) needs three things: a particle-to-grid scatter-add at gathered offsets, a grid update with boundaries, and grid-to-particle gathers. Anvil already does the hard part. I checked that `grid[base[p] + a, base[p] + b] += w[p, a] * w[p, b] where a < 3, b < 3` compiles and runs natively today. Port it, aim to undercut Taichi's line count, and make every run bit-identical across thread counts (Taichi's atomic particle-to-grid step is not). Then differentiate through it the way DiffTaichi did.

- **Build:** (1) examples/physics/mpm88.anvil and mpm_snow.anvil, with snow plasticity using a closed-form 2×2 polar decomposition written in Anvil. (2) Prelude `bspline_weights`, and grid boundaries from the boundary-types idea. (3) Deterministic parallel particle-to-grid in kernel.py: every few steps, particles are counting-sorted by cell (a scatter-add histogram plus a new `cumsum` prefix-scan builtin), and each thread owns disjoint grid tiles with a fixed accumulation order. (4) On Metal and CUDA, a sorted segmented reduction (Gao et al., SIGGRAPH Asia 2018) instead of float atomics. (5) Terminal rendering with `show`, and a canvas renderer in examples/draw. (6) The DiffTaichi soft-robot controller (diffmpm), using the checkpointed time-loop autodiff from the smoke idea.
- **Demo:** `bin/anvil run examples/physics/mpm88.anvil` animates falling water in the terminal with `show`. The browser version lets you draw blobs of jelly, snow or water and drop them. Then a 2D jelly robot learns, by grad, an actuation pattern that walks it to the right. VALIDATION: from the same initial state and dt as Taichi's mpm88.py, particle positions agree to about 1e-5 after 50 steps. Total mass is exact, momentum is conserved without gravity, and APIC angular-momentum conservation (Jiang et al. 2015) is checked by a runtime assert. Output is bitwise-identical with 1 and 12 threads.
- **Builds on:** Scatter-add at gathered offsets with index names (verified working natively), gathers, the counter-based RNG, `show`, examples/draw, the Metal backend
- **Risk:** Particle-to-grid throughput on the CPU, and determinism on the GPU, both depend on particle sorting. It starts in 2D only. The differentiable robot is blocked on autodiff through run-time loops. Porting a famous demo is less novel than building a new one; the selling points are determinism and the line count.

### Every Greek from one backward pass: Monte Carlo option pricing with AAD, Sobol paths and Longstaff-Schwartz

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

`price = mean(payoff(paths))` followed by `delta, vega, rho, theta = grad(price, S0, sigma, r, T)` is adjoint algorithmic differentiation ("Smoking adjoints", Giles & Glasserman 2006). Banks run the same technique in large C++ codebases. Anvil's counter-based RNG regenerates the same paths for any bumped input, so finite-difference checks get common random numbers for free, and every number is bit-identical on any thread count. Students see grad agree with Black-Scholes to within three standard errors. They also see where it fails: the pathwise derivative of a digital option is zero, and the likelihood-ratio method fixes that.

- **Build:** (1) anvil/finance.anvil, a module used with `use`: GBM and Heston paths (Andersen 2008 QE scheme), with time steps as `static for`, so path, payoff and backward pass fuse into one register-resident NEON loop over paths and the RNG runs inline. (2) Black-Scholes closed forms need new `erf`, `norm_cdf` and `norm_icdf` unaries in backend/vecmath.py (NEON polynomials), cuda.py and metal.py. (3) A `sobol(dims)` source as a new kind of ir.Rand, lowered in kernel.py: Joe-Kuo 2008 direction numbers as a compile-time table, XORed over the set bits of the counter (vectorizable, and counter-based like the existing generator), with hash-based Owen scrambling (Burley 2020). (4) Brownian-bridge path construction as a constant lower-triangular matmul, which goes to the AMX unit through blas.py. (5) Longstaff-Schwartz: per-date least squares through a small unrolled Cholesky (or `lstsq` from the linear-algebra idea), in a run-time loop backward over exercise dates. (6) A CRR binomial tree in Anvil as a reference pricer.
- **Demo:** A %%anvil Jupyter notebook (or browser page) with sliders for S0, K, σ and T. It shows the MC price ± 3 SE, five Greeks from one backward pass, the Black-Scholes closed form, and bump-and-revalue with common random numbers. A timing bar shows AAD at about 3× the cost of one pricing, against 101× for finite-difference deltas on a 100-asset basket. A Sobol-vs-pseudorandom error plot shows roughly O(1/N) against O(1/√N). VALIDATION: European Greeks against closed forms. Geometric Asian (Kemna-Vorst 1990) as a closed form and as a control variate. A barrier closed form with the Brownian-bridge crossing correction. The American put against Table 1 of Longstaff-Schwartz 2001 and a 5000-step binomial tree. QuantLib's MC engines as the reference library, and speed against NumPy and JAX.
- **Builds on:** Reparameterized `~ normal` sampling, the counter-based RNG, `grad` with respect to any tensor, `static for`, fusion, anvil.function and the %%anvil magic
- **Risk:** Kinks and discontinuities (digital and barrier payoffs) need smoothing or likelihood-ratio estimators. Gamma needs second derivatives, and higher-order grad doesn't exist yet. Long-dated paths in f32 lose accuracy (this pairs with the f64 idea).

### Complex numbers and an FFT written in Anvil, then spectral solvers and a Fourier neural operator trained on them

*wow 8/10 · months*

Add a `c64` dtype that is lowered to f32 pairs before autodiff, so gradients of complex code are ordinary real calculus and there is no Wirtinger convention to get wrong. Then write the FFT itself in the prelude: a Stockham radix-4 FFT is log₄(n) index-notation stages with affine indices and compile-time twiddle tables, and the staging interpreter plans it from the static size, much as FFTW generates codelets. With an FFT, a spectral-methods course fits in one file: Kuramoto-Sivashinsky chaos with ETDRK4, 2D turbulence, nonlinear Schrödinger solitons. The same program also generates its own training data for an FNO surrogate.

- **Build:** (1) anvil/complex.py, a pair-lowering pass shared with df64 and interval from the precision idea. C64 buffers become [..., 2] f32 views (zero-copy through the existing affine offsets), and complex mul, exp, abs and conj expand into real Expr trees. Literals such as `1j`. (2) anvil/fft.anvil: `fft`, `ifft`, `rfft`, `irfft` and `fft2` as compile-time-recursive Stockham stages, with twiddles fused into the stages. Pointwise spectral operations are fused in too: the i·k derivative, the 2/3 dealiasing mask, and ETDRK4 coefficients precomputed at compile time by contour integrals (Kassam-Trefethen 2005). (3) A new backend/fft.py modelled on blas.py, choosing vDSP on the CPU, MPSGraph FFT on Metal or cuFFT on CUDA when they are faster. (4) An FNO SpectralConv2d model in the prelude: rfft2, keep the low modes, a complex einsum `out[b, o, kx, ky] = sum W[i, o, kx, ky] * xh[b, i, kx, ky]`, then irfft2. (5) A PINN baseline once nested grad exists.
- **Demo:** "Three ways to solve Navier-Stokes", in one file with one browser page. A pseudo-spectral solver gives the ground truth. An FNO is trained on data the same program generates on the GPU, with no dataset files. A PINN is the third entry. Vorticity animations play side by side, with error over time and cost per prediction (the FNO is orders of magnitude faster at inference). VALIDATION: the FFT against numpy.fft (relative error about 1e-6 in f32, Parseval checked by assert) and its gradients against JAX. Taylor-Green vortex analytic decay. The NLS soliton keeps its sech shape and speed. KS matches Kassam-Trefethen's published figure and code. FNO error against the table in Li et al. (ICLR 2021) on their ν = 1e-3 setup. Speed against vDSP and numpy.fft.
- **Builds on:** Zero-copy affine views (real and imaginary parts are views), compile-time recursion (as in checkers' depth-limited search), inline NEON sin/cos, a prelude written in Anvil, blas.py's vendor-call pattern, the Metal backend
- **Risk:** A hand-written FFT won't match vDSP or cuFFT at every size (non-powers of two need mixed radix or Bluestein). Complex lowering doubles the kernel count unless the halves fuse. FNO training on the CPU is slow without a GPU FFT.

### Solvers with implicit adjoints: matrix-free CG and multigrid you can differentiate, and a live bridge designer

*wow 8/10 · months*

Anvil can't differentiate through run-time loops, but a converged solver doesn't need a tape: by the implicit function theorem, its gradient is one more solve with the transposed operator (Blondel et al. 2022, JAXopt). Functions can already be passed as compile-time arguments and inlined (`apply_twice(sq, u)` works today), so `x = cg(op, b)` can take any Anvil function as `op`. It runs a real `while` loop to tolerance and still gets exact gradients. That opens up PDE-constrained optimization: the 88-line topology-optimization code (Andreassen et al. 2011), with sensitivities derived automatically, running live on the GPU.

- **Build:** (1) anvil/solvers.anvil: cg, pcg, bicgstab, gmres(m), newton, fixed_point and lobpcg, written in Anvil over matrix-free function arguments. (2) A geometric multigrid V-cycle in Anvil, with compile-time recursion over levels. Restriction and prolongation use stride-2 affine index notation that already works (`r2[i, j] = sum w[a, b] * r[2*i + a, 2*j + b]`). (3) A custom-adjoint hook in autodiff.py, general enough for user-written `fn … adjoint …` blocks. It records the solve and, in reverse, emits a solve with the transposed operator (the transpose of a linear kernel is its autodiff), then -λᵀ·∂op/∂θ by ordinary autodiff of one application of `op`. (4) FEM pieces: element stiffness Ke as a constant table, and a matrix-free K·u by gather, multiply and scatter-add over elements. (5) A density filter as a boundary-typed stencil. (6) OC and MMA (Svanberg 1987) updates as prelude `optimizer` blocks. (7) An examples/draw page for placing loads and supports.
- **Demo:** The bridge designer: click to pin supports and place loads on a 2D domain, and SIMP compliance minimization (volume fraction 0.4) grows a truss-like bridge in seconds on Metal. Drag a load and the bridge re-forms. VALIDATION: reproduce the 88-line MATLAB MBB beam (60×20 mesh, volfrac 0.5, penal 3, rmin 1.5), with the same topology and final compliance. The automatic sensitivities equal the hand-derived -p·x^(p-1)·uᵀKₑu. CG residual history against scipy.sparse.linalg.cg. A multigrid convergence factor near 0.1 per V-cycle on Poisson (Briggs, A Multigrid Tutorial). Implicit gradients against f64 finite differences.
- **Builds on:** Functions as compile-time arguments with inlining (verified), run-time while loops, gather and scatter-add, transposition of linear kernels by autodiff, user-defined optimizer blocks, the Metal backend
- **Risk:** f32 CG converges poorly on stiffness matrices with 1e-9 void-to-solid contrast; it needs multigrid preconditioning or f64. The implicit adjoint assumes convergence, so a solve that stops early gives wrong gradients. The custom-adjoint hook is new compiler surface.

### Halide inside Anvil: schedules, overlapped tile fusion, and a local Laplacian filter that competes with Halide

*wow 8/10 · months*

Today a consumer that reads its producer's neighbors (any stencil) forces the producer to be written to memory in full, because elementwise inlining can't duplicate work. Add Halide's compute_at: overlapped tiling, where each thread computes the producer for its tile plus a halo while it stays in L1. The tiling is chosen from the cost model or written by hand in a separate `schedule` block, and the algorithm stays untouched. Also add Gradient Halide's scatter-to-gather rewrite of stencil adjoints, so backward passes become vectorized reductions instead of scatters, with no atomics on GPUs.

- **Build:** (1) anvil/schedule.py: tile, split, reorder, vectorize, parallel, compute_at and store_at directives attached to named kernels. (2) An overlapped-tile-fusion pass in optimize.py that fuses chains of affine-offset stencil kernels into one tiled kernel, with per-thread scratch slabs from backend/arena.py and halo sizes taken from the static offsets. Tile loops in kernel.py. (3) Automatic grouping over cost.py's FLOP and byte model (in the style of Mullapudi et al. 2016 and Adams et al. 2019), plus `anvil tune` (in IDEAS.md, not built) to time candidates. (4) Scatter-to-gather in autodiff.py (Li et al., SIGGRAPH 2018): for unit-stride affine windows, `dx[i + u] += dy[i]·w[u]` becomes `dx[j] = Σ_u dy[j - u]·w[u]`, with boundary types handling the edges. (5) Shared-memory tiles in cuda.py and threadgroup memory in metal.py for the same schedules. (6) PNG/JPEG loading in the runtime.
- **Demo:** A photo-editor page in examples/draw: local Laplacian filters (Paris, Hasinoff & Kautz 2011, Halide's flagship app with about 100 stages over Gaussian and Laplacian pyramids) with a detail slider at interactive rates, plus a bilateral grid, unsharp mask and a camera RAW demosaic. Then train the weights of a demosaicking pipeline through the schedule (Gradient Halide). VALIDATION: outputs against Halide's apps/local_laplacian and OpenCV (PSNR > 50 dB). Runtimes against Halide's manual and autoscheduled versions and arm64 OpenCV on the same M3 Max. Memory traffic predicted by `anvil cost` against measured.
- **Builds on:** optimize.py's fusion passes, the static arena, cost.py, kernel.py's register tiling, the scatter path in autodiff.py, boundary types
- **Risk:** Halide has a decade of schedule engineering behind it, and matching its hand schedules is hard. Choosing between recomputation and locality needs a good cost model. Pyramids mean many sizes and many kernels, which slows compilation.

### Differentiable DSP: filter design by gradient, a DDSP synth of your whistle, exported as a C++ audio plugin

*wow 8/10 · months*

Teach signals and systems with grad. Fit an IIR filter cascade to a target frequency response by differentiating its analytic response H(e^{jω}) (complex arithmetic, no recurrence needed), and watch FIR filters designed by gradient approach the Parks-McClellan equiripple optimum as the p-norm of the loss grows. Then DDSP (Engel et al., ICLR 2020): a harmonic-plus-noise synthesizer trained with a multi-scale spectral loss learns your whistle in a minute. `anvil export` turns it into a C library, wrapped in a JUCE C++ plugin you play from a MIDI keyboard in a DAW.

- **Build:** (1) anvil/dsp.anvil on top of the FFT idea: windows, stft/istft, a mel filterbank, multi-scale spectral loss, `freqz` from coefficients. (2) Biquad cascades parameterized by pole radius and angle, with a sigmoid keeping poles inside the unit circle, so filters are stable by construction. (3) A harmonic oscillator bank whose phase comes from a new `cumsum` builtin, and filtered noise by frequency sampling. (4) A `wav()` loader and `save_wav`. (5) A real-time-safe mode for export.py: a per-instance state struct, no lock and no allocation (static memory planning already rules out malloc), plus a C++ header. (6) A JUCE project template in examples/plugin/ whose AudioProcessor calls the exported library once per 128-sample block. (7) Microphone recording in the drawing app.
- **Demo:** Whistle into the laptop mic, and in about a minute Anvil learns a DDSP model of it. Load the generated AU/VST3 plugin in Logic, Ableton or FL Studio and play your whistle from a MIDI keyboard, with each audio block well under its 2.7 ms real-time budget. VALIDATION: STFT against scipy.signal.stft and librosa. freqz against scipy.signal.freqz. Butterworth and Chebyshev analytic responses. Gradient-designed FIRs against scipy.signal.remez. DDSP reconstruction loss against Google's DDSP library on the same NSynth note.
- **Builds on:** `anvil export` (a C library with the weights inside), static memory planning (real-time safe), complex numbers and the FFT, training loops, the examples/draw server
- **Risk:** Audio threads are strict (export currently serializes calls with a lock). JUCE builds, plugin signing and DAW hosting on macOS are fiddly. Phase accumulation in f32 drifts over long notes.

### Moonshot: sparse formats as types and finite-element weak forms (TACO meets FEniCS), differentiable

*wow 8/10 · moonshot*

Put the storage format in the type: `A: f32[n, n] @csr(nnz)`, with nnz read from the mesh file at compile time the way idx headers are. Compile sparse index notation the way TACO does (Kjolstad et al., OOPSLA 2017): `y[i] = sum A[i, j] * x[j]` becomes a CSR loop, sparse-times-sparse products use merge lattices, and gradients keep the sparsity pattern. On top of that, a UFL-style weak-form layer (`a = integral(dot(grad(u), grad(v)) * dx)`) generates element kernels and deterministic assembly. Then the whole FEM-course pipeline of mesh, assembly, solve and adjoint-based optimization (dolfin-adjoint style) fits in about 40 lines and runs on NEON, Metal and CUDA.

- **Build:** (1) Ragged loops in ir.py (`for p in pos[i] .. pos[i+1]`), the first data-dependent extent in an IR where every extent is static today. (2) A new anvil/sparse.py: sparse access paths and merge-lattice lowering. (3) Sparse-aware autodiff: the adjoint of SpMV with respect to the values is a pattern-masked outer product. (4) A `mesh("part.msh")` Gmsh loader read at compile time. (5) P1/P2 Lagrange bases and quadrature tables generated by the staging interpreter. (6) Assembly as scatter-add with a static coloring computed at compile time, so it is deterministic and atomic-free on GPUs. (7) Solvers from the implicit-adjoint idea, with algebraic multigrid as a stretch. (8) CSR SpMV schedules in kernel.py, plus cuSPARSE and Accelerate Sparse BLAS vendor paths.
- **Demo:** Load a Gmsh mesh of a heat sink, solve steady heat conduction with P2 elements, then optimize the conductivity layout (or the fin shape) to minimize peak temperature with gradients from the automatic adjoint, live in the browser. VALIDATION: the method of manufactured solutions with O(h²) L2 convergence for P1 and O(h³) for P2. The same solutions as FEniCSx or Firedrake on the same mesh, to solver tolerance in f64. SpMV throughput against scipy.sparse and Accelerate Sparse. Adjoint gradients against finite differences.
- **Builds on:** Index notation with gathers and scatter-add, file headers read at compile time (idx/npy/csv), the solvers and implicit adjoints, f64
- **Risk:** Ragged, data-dependent loops break the 'every extent is static' rule that the backends, the arena and the bounds proofs rely on. TACO and Firedrake are each multi-year projects, so scope creep is likely.

### Molecular dynamics where forces = -grad(energy): melt a crystal, measure g(r), fit a potential

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

In hand-written MD codes, a force that disagrees with its energy is a classic silent bug. In Anvil you write the Lennard-Jones energy with the minimum-image convention, and `forces = -grad(E, pos)` derives the force kernel. The pair energy reads pos[i] and pos[j], so the gradient comes out as two reduction kernels, with no atomics. Energy conservation is a runtime `assert`, and the integrator is symplectic velocity Verlet. Then turn it around: differentiate a measured radial distribution function g(r) with respect to σ and ε to fit the potential.

- **Build:** (1) anvil/md.anvil: minimum image `d - box * round(d / box)`, cut-and-shifted LJ and Morse potentials, velocity Verlet, and a BAOAB Langevin thermostat (Leimkuhler-Matthews) on the counter-based RNG. (2) Cell lists with static capacity: counting sort through a scatter-add histogram plus a new `cumsum` builtin, and a neighbor table nbr[i, k] of fixed width K whose overflow flag is checked by an assert (JAX-MD's capacity design, a natural fit for static shapes). (3) g(r) by scatter-add histogram. (4) An all-pairs O(N²) n-body schedule with threadgroup-memory tiles added to metal.py (the classic GPU Gems 3 ch. 31 kernel). (5) A three.js point renderer fed by examples/draw/server.py. (6) Stretch: particle-mesh Ewald once the FFT idea lands.
- **Demo:** "Melt a crystal": 4,000 LJ atoms start in an FCC lattice and a slider raises the temperature. At the melting point the live g(r) loses its crystal peaks, and the energy-temperature curve shows the latent-heat jump. Then a 65k-body galaxy collision on the GPU. VALIDATION: the Johnson-Zollweg-Gubbins 1993 LJ equation of state. g(r) against LAMMPS or JAX-MD with identical parameters. NVE energy drift scales as O(dt²). The Kepler two-body period against the analytic value. Momentum conserved to round-off. Forces checked against finite differences.
- **Builds on:** grad of anything, scatter-add histograms, gathers, `round`, the counter-based RNG, assert, the Metal backend
- **Risk:** f32 energy drift over long runs (pairs with the f64 idea). Variable neighbor counts strain static shapes (capacity plus rebuilds). The pair-energy gradient must not materialize N² intermediates, so the pair kernel has to fuse with its gradient.

### Differentiable LAPACK with structure types (lower, spd, orthogonal), and Gaussian processes in ten lines

*wow 7/10 · months*

Add Cholesky, triangular solve, solve, QR, lstsq, eigh, SVD and logdet as builtins with adjoint rules, lowered to the vendor library on each backend. Make matrix structure part of the type (`L: f32[n, n] lower`, `S: spd`). Then `cholesky(S)` returns a `lower` matrix, `solve(L, b)` picks a triangular solve, `solve(Q: orthogonal, b)` becomes `Q.T @ b`, and a non-square `solve` is a compile error with a caret: Julia's LinearAlgebra dispatch, but static. With these, a GP with learned hyperparameters is ten lines and least squares is one.

- **Build:** (1) A `LibCall` statement in ir.py that generalizes the Gemm path in backend/blas.py, including its batch loops over leading dimensions. (2) backend/lapack.py: Accelerate LAPACK on the CPU (spotrf, strtrs, sgesv, sgeqrf, ssyevd, sgesdd, through the LAPACK interface added in macOS 13.3). Metal uses MPSMatrixDecompositionCholesky, MPSMatrixSolveTriangular and MPSMatrixDecompositionLU; eigh and SVD fall back to the CPU with an explicit host sync that `anvil cost` reports. CUDA uses cuSOLVER (potrf, syevd, gesvd) and cuBLAS trsm. interp.py uses numpy.linalg. (3) Adjoint rules in autodiff.py: blocked Cholesky (Murray 2016), triangular solve, eigh with a guard for degenerate eigenvalues, SVD (Townsend 2016), QR. (4) Structure tags on tensor values in values.py, with propagation rules: A @ A.T + c*eye is spd, cholesky gives lower, the Q of qr is orthogonal. `assume spd` is checked at run time under --check. `inv` warns "use solve".
- **Demo:** A browser GP playground: click to add points, and Anvil fits the RBF length scale, signal variance and noise by maximizing the log marginal likelihood (gradients flow through Cholesky and logdet), then redraws the mean ± 2σ band. A Bayesian-optimization loop (expected improvement, using norm_cdf from the finance idea) then hunts the minimum of a hidden function while the class watches where it samples. A second lesson fits a polynomial through the normal equations and through QR, showing how the normal equations square the condition number. VALIDATION: values against scipy.linalg. Gradients against jax.scipy.linalg and f64 finite differences. GP hyperparameters against scikit-learn's GaussianProcessRegressor optimum.
- **Builds on:** blas.py's vendor-call machinery and batching, per-kernel adjoint rules in autodiff.py, shape unification for signatures, diagnostics
- **Risk:** eigh and SVD gradients are ill-conditioned when eigenvalues are close. Vendor calls break fusion and make the GPU wait. Structure inference must never mark a non-SPD matrix as spd, because a wrong tag means silently wrong results.

### Precision is a type: f64, df64 for Metal, interval arithmetic, and compensated sums

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Scientific computing needs f64, and the Apple GPU has none. Make that a type error with a fix: `f64` under `--metal` fails at the declaration and suggests `df64`, double-float arithmetic (two f32s combined with Dekker/Knuth error-free transforms, about a 48-bit mantissa) that runs on any backend. Add an `interval` dtype that rounds outward. The classic numerics lecture then runs itself: Rump's polynomial gives a confidently wrong answer in f32 and in f64, while the interval result shows it cannot be trusted.

- **Build:** (1) ir.py gains F64, DF64 and IVAL dtypes. Buffer.nbytes becomes per-dtype (it is hard-wired to 4 bytes today), along with arena alignment, pyapi and export dtype maps. (2) kernel.py: the lane count depends on the dtype (NEON .2d gives two f64 lanes), with f64 versions of the exp/log/sin polynomials in vecmath.py and Accelerate dgemm in blas.py. (3) cuda.py uses `double`. (4) A table of which dtypes each backend supports, checked by the elaborator with a caret on the declaration. (5) A pair-lowering pass, shared with the complex-number idea, rewrites df64 and interval ops into f32 pairs before autodiff. TwoProd uses fma (error bounds from Joldes, Muller & Popescu 2017). Intervals widen outward by 1 ulp, or switch the AArch64 FPCR rounding mode on the CPU. (6) Reduction annotations `sum[kahan]` and `sum[f64]` (mixed-precision accumulation). (7) interp.py in float64, plus checks against mpmath. (8) `anvil gradcheck`, listed in IDEAS.md and not built, runs finite differences in f64.
- **Demo:** "Why numbers lie", one file. The Lorenz system integrated in f32, df64 and f64: the trajectories visibly split within a few dozen time units. Rump's f(77617, 33096) printed in f32 and f64 (both wrong) and as an interval (a wide enclosure that shows the computed result cannot be trusted). The finite-difference error V-curve (truncation against round-off, optimum h ≈ √ε) in f32 and in f64. And a Metal compile error for f64 that suggests the fix. VALIDATION: everything against mpmath at 50 digits. df64 errors stay within Joldes et al.'s bounds. A property test over random expressions checks that the interval always contains the mpmath value.
- **Builds on:** The dtype field on Buffer and Expr, vecmath polynomials, blas.py, the CUDA and Metal generators, the NumPy interpreter for differential testing
- **Risk:** The 4-byte element size is assumed in many places (the arena, pyapi, export, loaders). df64 is about 10–20× slower than f32. Intervals widen badly over long computations (the dependency problem), though that is itself the lesson.

## ecosystem-interop-and-deployment (16)

### anvil.torch.compile_step: a whole PyTorch training step as one Anvil program

*wow 9/10 · months*

Most of Anvil's 6.5x lead over PyTorch comes from compiling forward, backward and the optimizer together, so the Adam or SGD update runs inside the weight-gradient GEMM's epilogue. compile_step(model, loss_fn, optimizer) captures only the forward pass and loss from PyTorch. Anvil's own autodiff writes the backward pass, and the torch.optim optimizer is translated into the prelude's `optimizer` block. Parameters and Adam state remain torch tensors, updated in place through pointers. The aim is the REPORT.md speedups from PyTorch code with a three-line change.

- **Build:** anvil/torch_step.py captures loss_fn(functional_call(model, params, x), y) with make_fx, skipping AOTAutograd, then runs the shared aten table to produce an Anvil `fn` whose params get `minimize ... with ...`. Optimizers are mapped from param_groups: SGD with momentum and weight_decay becomes sgd. AdamW becomes adam(weight_decay), which prelude.anvil already implements as decoupled decay. torch's Adam, whose weight_decay is a coupled L2 penalty, becomes a new adam_l2 block. clip_grad_norm_ becomes clip_norm. lr is passed as a run-time scalar on every call so torch lr_schedulers keep working. A new extern buffer kind `inout` in ir.EXTERN_KINDS, plus a relaxed pyapi.check_inputs_unchanged, lets params and optimizer.state[p]['exp_avg' | 'exp_avg_sq' | 'step'] be read and written through pointer slots. optimizer.state_dict() therefore stays valid, and you can drop back to eager mid-run. A compile_loop(steps, X, Y, batch) variant also moves randperm batching into Anvil's `batches`, so Python leaves the loop entirely.
- **Demo:** Take benchmarks/vs_pytorch/torch/*.py, change each script by three lines to `step = anvil.torch.compile_step(model, F.cross_entropy, opt)`, rerun benchmarks/vs_pytorch/run.py, and regenerate REPORT.md with an 'unmodified PyTorch + Anvil' column beside eager, torch.compile and native Anvil. Target: at least 4x geomean over eager on the CPU with the same final accuracy (native Anvil gets 6.5x).
- **Builds on:** anvil/autodiff.py, minimize and the optimizer blocks in prelude.anvil, epilogue and horizontal fusion in optimize.py, pyapi pointer slots
- **Risk:** Runs match statistically rather than bitwise, because dropout and init draw from Anvil's RNG, not torch's Philox. Code between backward() and step() (grad hooks, custom clipping, gradient accumulation) cannot be captured and must fall back. nn.GRU and scaled_dot_product_attention have to decompose to their math forms. BatchNorm running statistics need state outputs. Per-call Python overhead limits small-model gains unless compile_loop is used.

### device="anvil": a lazy-tensor PyTorch device (torch_xla for Apple silicon)

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

Register Anvil as a real PyTorch device through PrivateUse1. Ops on `anvil` tensors are recorded, not executed. At each sync point (.item(), .cpu(), print, the end of optimizer.step) the whole trace, covering forward, autograd's backward and the optimizer's foreach updates, becomes one Anvil program that is fused, compiled, cached by graph hash and replayed. The user writes `model.to("anvil")` and leaves the training loop alone. Anvil fuses kernel IR no matter who produced the derivative, so the SGD update still lands in the weight-gradient GEMM epilogue.

- **Build:** anvil/torch_device/anvil_device.cpp is a C++ extension modeled on PyTorch's OpenReg reference out-of-tree backend (test/cpp_extensions). It calls torch.utils.rename_privateuse1_backend("anvil") and provides an allocator that hands out page-aligned unified memory, also wrapped as MTLBuffers, so CPU and Metal kernels share every tensor. TORCH_LIBRARY_IMPL(aten, PrivateUse1, m) records ops into a lazy graph, or the extension reuses Lazy Tensor Core in torch/csrc/lazy as torch_xla does. A boxed CPU fallback handles unsupported ops without copies, because memory is unified. At a barrier, the graph is hashed (op sequence, shapes, aliasing), lowered through the shared aten table into kernel IR, optimized by optimize.py and compiled by the AArch64 or Metal backend. A replay path reuses the compiled program and only rebinds pointer slots ('trace once, replay', as CUDA graphs do). anvil.torch.metrics() reports traces, cache hits, recompiles and fallbacks.
- **Demo:** Run the nine benchmarks/vs_pytorch/torch scripts with device = "anvil" against "cpu" and "mps". Report steps/s, recompiles (target: 1 per script after warmup) and fallback ops (target: 0). Target: at least 3x geomean over CPU eager with a one-line change to the scripts.
- **Builds on:** the aten lowering table from the Dynamo backend, optimize.py fusion, the arena memory planner, the Metal backend's shared-memory MTLBuffers
- **Risk:** It inherits torch_xla's known failure modes. Per-op tracing costs microseconds per op and can eat small-model wins unless replay skips tracing. Changing shapes or Python scalars cause recompilation storms. Data-dependent ops (nonzero, .item() inside the loop) force syncs. A C++ extension has to be maintained across PyTorch releases.

### anvil serve: an OpenAI-compatible server in one native binary

*wow 9/10 · months*

An LLM server whose model step is compiled Anvil. It has continuous batching over a fixed number of slots (so shapes stay static), a paged KV cache addressed with gathers, speculative decoding, and grammar-constrained output where the JSON-schema automaton runs inside the compiled step instead of on a Python thread. Nothing runs in Python, and weights are mmapped, so a process can go from exec to first token in tens of milliseconds. Python serving stacks take seconds just to import.

- **Build:** anvil/serve/ contains http.c (kqueue, HTTP/1.1 and SSE streaming, serving /v1/chat/completions, /v1/completions and /v1/models) and sched.c (admission, chunked prefill mixed with decode in one static-shape step as in Sarathi-Serve, and slot recycling). A new buffer kind `state` persists between calls of an exported library, holding KV pages and automaton states. Gather is extended to page addressing, `table[s, j // P] * P + j % P`. grammar.py compiles a JSON Schema or regex into a character DFA and per-state token bitmasks (an Outlines-style index), baked as const tables. Anvil applies them as `logits + (0 if allowed[state[s], v] else -1e9)` and advances the state by walking the sampled token's bytes. Prompt-lookup (n-gram) speculative decoding verifies k drafted tokens in one static step. `anvil export --serve` links it all into one binary.
- **Demo:** `anvil serve hf:Qwen/Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct --json-schema invoice.json`, driven by the unmodified `openai` Python client. Measure cold start to first token (target under 100 ms) against mlx_lm.server and llama.cpp's llama-server. Every response is valid JSON, and constrained decoding adds almost no cost per token. Report aggregate tokens/s at 1, 8 and 32 concurrent streams.
- **Builds on:** anvil export (C libraries with pointer slots), mmapped safetensors, the Hub templates, run-time-offset writes and gathers, the assembly runtime's thread pool
- **Risk:** Throughput depends on a fused flash-decoding attention kernel that Anvil does not have; today it materializes the scores. Nested JSON needs a pushdown automaton (as XGrammar uses), and a DFA only approximates it to a bounded depth. Per-state bitmasks over a 150k-token vocabulary cost about 19 KB each.

### torch.compile(backend="anvil"): Anvil as a Dynamo backend, with anvil.explain

*wow 8/10 · months*

Register Anvil as a torch.compile backend so that unmodified PyTorch models lower from FX graphs into Anvil's kernel IR and run as fused NEON/AMX or Metal code. ATen's strided views map exactly onto Anvil's affine offsets, so view, permute, expand, slice and as_strided cost nothing, and Dynamo already specializes on static shapes the same way anvil.function does. `anvil.explain(model, x)` prints the readable Anvil source the PyTorch model turned into, with each line annotated with the Python file:line it came from. You can put that compiler in front of a class.

- **Build:** anvil/frontends/aten.py maps the Core ATen opset (about 180 ops after torch._decomp.core_aten_decompositions) to OpsMixin calls in anvil/ops.py and to prelude functions. aten.convolution goes to the prelude's im2col Conv2d path. addmm, mm and bmm become reduction kernels that backend/blas.py routes to AMX. native_layer_norm, _softmax and gelu go to the prelude. anvil/frontends/fx.py walks the GraphModule. Parameters are frozen into const buffers through the pyapi.lower baking path, which folds BatchNorm into conv weights (Anvil has no batch norm yet) and lets the backend pre-pack weights for its GEMM tile. Unsupported ops are split off with torch.fx's CapabilityBasedPartitioner and stay eager. anvil/torch_backend.py registers through a `torch_dynamo_backends` entry point in pyproject.toml, so `backend="anvil"` works right after pip install, and options={"device": "metal"} selects the GPU. Calls go through pyapi.Compiled and its ~/.cache/anvil dylib cache, using the fast call path from the zero-copy idea. anvil.explain emits Anvil source text from FX, using nn_module_stack and stack_trace metadata for names and comments, and reports graph breaks and compile time.
- **Demo:** Inference for torchvision resnet18 and mobilenet_v2 and Hugging Face bert-base and distilgpt2 at batch 1, 8 and 32 on an M3 Max. One published table compares eager, inductor, anvil-CPU and anvil-Metal, and `anvil.explain(resnet18)` prints ResNet as about 60 lines of index notation. Before/after: REPORT.md's kernel timings predict roughly 4-5x on CPU convolutions (macOS PyTorch builds have no oneDNN) and about 1x on AMX-bound GEMMs. Publish the real numbers whatever they turn out to be.
- **Builds on:** anvil/ops.py OpsMixin, prelude.anvil layers, pyapi.lower (weight baking) and pyapi.Compiled (dylib cache), backend/blas.py AMX routing, the Metal backend
- **Risk:** The long tail of ops: in-place ops, and dtype promotion to f64, i64 and bool when Anvil has only f32 and i32. Dynamo graph breaks can split a model into fragments. Anvil compiles in 0.2-3 s against inductor's warm cache. Batch-1 wins require replacing the ctypes call path first (REPORT.md: 14.8 us against 5.7 us for eager).

### Anvil as a JAX platform: StableHLO import, then a PJRT plugin

*wow 8/10 · moonshot*

JAX hands every jitted function to its backend as StableHLO, and Anvil can be that backend. JAX differentiates before lowering, so lax.scan and while_loop reach the backend as plain loops, which become Anvil's run-time `while` blocks. JAX training loops therefore avoid Anvil's biggest language gap: it cannot differentiate through run-time loops. The end state is JAX_PLATFORMS=anvil running Flax and Equinox code on the Apple CPU (AMX) and GPU (Metal). Apple's experimental jax-metal plugin has lagged behind JAX releases and op coverage.

- **Build:** Stage 1, weeks: anvil/frontends/stablehlo.py is a dependency-free parser for the StableHLO text from jax.jit(f).lower(*args).as_text().
