It lowers dot_general to reduction kernels; broadcast_in_dim, transpose, reshape, slice and reverse to zero-copy Affine views; reduce(add/max/min/mul) to Reduction; gather and scatter to Gather and scatter-add; select, compare and convert to Select and casts; while and case to kernel IR while/if blocks. ir.py and the backends gain u32 logical shifts and xor, so JAX's threefry2x32 (generic integer StableHLO on a new platform) produces bit-identical jax.random streams. An anvil.jax.jit decorator calls the result through DLPack. Stage 2, the moonshot: pjrt/anvil_pjrt.cc is a C++ plugin implementing the PJRT C API (GetPjrtApi, a client, devices anvil:cpu and anvil:metal, unified-memory buffers, PJRT_Client_Compile). It deserializes the StableHLO portable artifact with the StableHLO C++ library, passes the text to the Python compiler (embedded CPython or a compile-server process) and dlopens the resulting dylib. It registers through the jax_plugins entry point.
- **Demo:** Train an Equinox MNIST CNN and a lax.scan char-RNN with JAX_PLATFORMS=anvil on Metal. Compare steps/s against XLA:CPU and jax-metal on the same M3 Max, and check jax.random draws bit-for-bit against XLA:CPU.
- **Builds on:** kernel IR's structured run-time control flow (while/if blocks), Affine views, Gather and scatter-add, blas.py and the Metal backend, pyapi's compile cache
- **Risk:** StableHLO and the PJRT C API change between JAX versions; pinning versions and using StableHLO's VHLO compatibility guarantees mitigate this. The plugin needs the MLIR/StableHLO C++ libraries, although the compiler itself stays dependency-free. JAX emits f64, i64, bool and u8 freely (keeping x64 off helps). Async dispatch and buffer-donation semantics have to be honored.

### Triton both ways: Anvil kernels as Triton, and Triton kernels on Apple silicon

*wow 8/10 · moonshot*

Triton is the GPU kernel language the ecosystem actually writes: Inductor emits it, and Liger-Kernel, Unsloth and FlashAttention ports ship in it. Emitting Triton from Anvil's kernel IR gives Anvil tensor-core matmuls on NVIDIA and AMD GPUs without writing a tiled CUDA scheduler. Reading `@triton.jit` source into Anvil runs those community kernels on a Mac, which Triton itself cannot do because it ships no macOS wheels.

- **Build:** Export: anvil/backend/triton.py turns elementwise and fused kernels into 1-D blocked programs with masks. Matmul-like reductions (a sum over the product of two dense affine loads, the pattern blas.py already detects) become tl.dot tiles with BLOCK_M/N/K autotune configs, with Anvil's fused epilogue (bias, GELU, even the SGD update) applied to the accumulator tile. Other reductions become tl.sum loops. input_precision="ieee" is the default so numerics match the interpreter. The host program comes from cuda.py's host generator, and Triton's AOT compiler (triton.tools.compile) produces C launchers, so the final binary needs no Python. Import: anvil/frontends/triton.py parses the decorated function's Python AST without installing triton and interprets tl.* symbolically. program_id becomes an outer domain var and tl.arange an inner domain var. Pointer arithmetic becomes Affine offsets or Gathers, masks become `where`, tl.dot and accumulating K-loops become reductions, and `other=` in tl.load becomes a select.
- **Demo:** Triton's own tutorial kernels (vector add, fused softmax, matmul, layer norm, fused attention) and Liger-Kernel's fused linear cross-entropy run on an M3 Max through Anvil's Metal backend and match their torch references. In the other direction, examples/transformer.anvil trains on an A100 through generated Triton. Report GEMM throughput as a fraction of tensor-core peak before (the CUDA backend's one thread per output) and after.
- **Builds on:** backend/cuda.py (host generator and kernel walker), blas.py's GEMM pattern detection, Affine and Gather in ir.py, the Metal backend
- **Risk:** Triton's block semantics (tensors of pointers, masks, atomics, tl.where on pointers) do not always flatten into affine comprehensions, so import will cover only a subset of kernels. Triton's Python API changes often. Export needs real NVIDIA or AMD hardware in CI.

### anvil pull / anvil push: Hugging Face models as readable Anvil

*wow 8/10 · months*

`anvil pull Qwen/Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct` downloads the weights and generates the architecture from its config.json as about 150 lines of readable Anvil: RMSNorm, RoPE, SwiGLU and grouped-query attention. In index notation, grouped-query attention is just `k[b, j, h // G, e]`. `anvil push` publishes trained Anvil models back to the Hub with their source, safetensors weights and prebuilt C libraries. The Hub becomes where Anvil models are published and shared, and a class can read Llama as math.

- **Build:** anvil/hub.py uses urllib only. It resolves URLs pinned to a revision sha, reads HF_TOKEN from the environment for gated repos, and caches in Hugging Face's own ~/.cache/huggingface/hub layout so transformers and Anvil share downloads. anvil/templates/ has one template per model_type (llama, mistral, qwen2, phi3, gemma2, gpt2, bert, vit), filled from config.json: dimensions, heads, KV heads, rope_theta, eps, tied embeddings. prelude.anvil gains rms_norm, RMSNorm, rope, swiglu and GQA attention. The KV cache is written with run-time-offset slice assignment, which already works (seq[T + k] = ...). anvil/tokenizer.py is a pure-Python byte-level BPE that reads tokenizer.json and applies chat templates. The CLI adds `anvil pull | new | chat | push`, and push writes a model card with library_name: anvil.
- **Demo:** `anvil pull Qwen/Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct && anvil chat`. The generated qwen2.anvil appears next to transformers' modeling_qwen2.py, its logits match transformers to 1e-4 on the same prompt, and tokens/s is measured against mlx_lm.generate on the same M3 Max.
- **Builds on:** safetensors-as-types, the prelude models (Linear, Embedding, LayerNorm), index notation with integer division in indices, `use`, anvil export
- **Risk:** Decoding is limited by memory bandwidth: without quantization and a fused attention/GEMV path, Anvil will trail MLX and llama.cpp in tokens/s. With static shapes, attention runs over the full context length with a mask until fused attention exists. Configs keep adding variants (rope scaling, sliding windows), so each template needs its own tests.

### Anvil speaks MLIR: linalg.generic and StableHLO export (train an Anvil program on a TPU)

*wow 8/10 · months*

An Anvil kernel, `out[I] = ⊕_J body(I, J)` over affine loads, is nearly isomorphic to MLIR's linalg.generic (indexing maps plus parallel and reduction iterators). Every Anvil program, including its compiler-generated backward pass and optimizer, can therefore leave as MLIR. linalg reaches IREE (Vulkan, CUDA, ROCm, WebGPU, RISC-V), and StableHLO reaches XLA and TPUs. Write the model on a Mac, then run the whole training step on a TPU.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/mlir_linalg.py turns kernels into linalg.generic on flat memrefs. Anvil's flat Affine offsets are already valid affine maps, and de-flattening them into multi-dimensional maps is an optimization. Gathers become memref.load inside the region with linalg.index. Structured control flow becomes scf.for, scf.while and scf.if, and runtime calls (print, loaders, checkpoints) become func.call into anvil_host.h, which CUDA and Metal already share. anvil/backend/stablehlo.py raises common patterns: a sum over the product of two loads becomes dot_general, stride patterns become transpose, broadcast_in_dim, slice or reshape, and max/sum reductions become reduce. Everything else falls back to gathers with iota-computed indices. Anvil's lowbias32 counter RNG is written as StableHLO integer ops, so random streams match the Mac bit for bit. anvil/frontends/linalg.py imports linalg.generic text back into kernel IR (from torch-mlir or IREE input pipelines), which gives Anvil's autodiff and backends to any MLIR frontend. The CLI adds `anvil export --mlir | --stablehlo`, emitting the training step as `(params, opt_state, batch) -> (params', opt_state', loss)`.
- **Demo:** examples/transformer.anvil's training step is exported as StableHLO and run on a free Kaggle or Colab TPU through the PJRT client JAX ships, with its loss curve overlaid on the M3 Max run. The same step also runs through IREE's Vulkan target.
- **Builds on:** the kernel IR (Kernel, Affine, Gather and structured blocks in ir.py), autodiff and minimize producing ordinary kernels, anvil_host.h
- **Risk:** XLA reorders reductions, so loss curves match within tolerance, not bitwise. The generic gather fallback is slow on TPUs. MLIR's textual syntax drifts across LLVM versions, so the exporter should target StableHLO's versioned portable artifacts.

### import anvil.numpy as np: unmodified NumPy and Array API code, fused

*wow 7/10 · months*

A lazy array that implements the Python Array API standard and NumPy's __array_ufunc__ and __array_function__ protocols. NumPy-style code builds a graph, and Anvil compiles it into fused kernels when a value is actually needed. This repo already shows NumPy losing 2x on the MNIST MLP and 11-21x on softmax and layer norm, because it makes one pass over memory per operation. The lazy array gives those wins to code nobody rewrote, including scikit-learn's estimators that support the Array API.

- **Build:** anvil/lazy.py records LazyArray nodes per op, including in-place ops (__isub__ for `W -= lr * g`). On materialization (np.asarray, float(), __bool__, printing, __array__) it flushes them into one Anvil function, lowered with OpsMixin and compiled through pyapi.Compiled. The cache key is graph structure plus shapes, so a training loop compiles once. anvil/numpy.py is the namespace, with __array_namespace__ for the 2023.12 standard. Linear algebra (svd, eigh, cholesky, solve) is added as new RTCalls into Accelerate's LAPACK in backend/aarch64.py and anvil_host.h, since Anvil has no decompositions. CI runs the official data-apis/array-api-tests suite.
- **Demo:** Run benchmarks/mnist_numpy.py with only its import changed; target within 1.5x of examples/mnist.anvil's epoch time. Also run sklearn's LinearDiscriminantAnalysis and Ridge with set_config(array_api_dispatch=True) on Anvil arrays. Before/after: wall time of the unmodified script, and the array-api-tests pass rate.
- **Builds on:** anvil/ops.py, pyapi.Compiled and its on-disk cache, optimize.py fusion, Accelerate (already linked for blas.py)
- **Risk:** Python control flow on array values forces flushes and splits graphs into fragments. NumPy's many dtypes (f64 is the default) do not fit an Anvil that has only f32 and i32, and a downcasting policy changes results. Cache misses from varying shapes recompile at about 0.2 s each.

### anvil to-torch: idiomatic PyTorch generated from Anvil, with the same random numbers

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Generate a clean torch.nn.Module and training loop from an Anvil program, for teams that must ship PyTorch and for students who must hand it in. Models become nn.Modules, index notation becomes einsum or broadcasting, and minimize becomes torch.optim. An optional port of Anvil's counter-based RNG to torch integer ops makes the initial weights and dropout masks identical, so the generated PyTorch reproduces the Anvil run. It also gives Anvil models torch.export, ExecuTorch, TorchServe and coremltools.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/torch_codegen.py works from the AST plus the elaborator's recorder (anvil/ide.py already records every name's shape, dtype and constant value). Each model becomes an nn.Module. A param with `~ normal(...)` becomes an nn.Parameter with the same init expression. The prelude's Linear, Conv2d, LayerNorm and Embedding become small custom modules with a state_dict mapper, since Anvil's Linear stores W as [fan_in, fan_out], unlike nn.Linear. Sum-of-products comprehensions become torch.einsum strings, `where` becomes arange masks, and window indices such as x[s*i + u] become unfold. `~ normal` becomes a reparameterized randn, `static for` becomes a Python loop, and batches becomes randperm. anvil/torch_rng.py implements lowbias32 with int64 torch ops. The CLI adds `anvil to-torch file.anvil -o file.py`, plus a generated parity test that loads Anvil's checkpoint.
- **Demo:** Regenerate benchmarks/vs_pytorch/torch/*.py from benchmarks/vs_pytorch/anvil/*.anvil and diff them against the hand-written versions. All 9 should reach the same accuracy, and with the shared RNG the first 100 losses should agree to 1e-5. Then torch.export the generated digit reader to an ExecuTorch .pte.
- **Builds on:** the parser and AST, the elaborator recorder from the editor work, prelude.anvil, the counter-based RNG in runtime.s and anvil_host.h
- **Risk:** Fused or unusual index expressions turn into unidiomatic torch (as_strided, gathers). Bitwise agreement beyond the RNG is impossible, because summation orders differ. The generated code has to stay readable, or nobody will maintain it.

### Zero-install Anvil: pip, Homebrew, Colab on real CUDA, and the browser

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Anvil is pure Python with no dependencies, which makes it easy to distribute widely. `pip install anvil-lang` and `brew install anvil` cover Macs. A Colab notebook runs the CUDA backend on a free NVIDIA T4 through `%%anvil --cuda`, which finally tests it on real hardware. The compiler and the NumPy reference interpreter both run under Pyodide, so a JupyterLite page can give a class shape errors and hover types with nothing to install.

- **Build:** pyproject.toml publishes a py3-none-any wheel named anvil-lang, with the console script and, later, the torch_dynamo_backends and jax_plugins entry points. `anvil doctor` checks for cc (the Xcode Command Line Tools), nvcc and Metal. A Homebrew tap holds Formula/anvil.rb. notebooks/colab_cuda.ipynb is the Colab notebook. jupyter.py gains `%%anvil --cuda`, and anvil.function works on CUDA through __cuda_array_interface__. A CI job runs tests/test_cuda.py and every example on a real GPU (Colab, Modal or a GitHub GPU runner) and diffs the output against the interpreter. A JupyterLite build uses --interp, with anvil check --json for diagnostics. The CUDA backend's --cuda-emulate C++ path is the CPU fallback on Linux and x86 (Kaggle, student laptops).
- **Demo:** An 'Open in Colab' badge on the README: install, train MNIST on a T4 with the CUDA backend, diff the output against the interpreter, and publish the first timings of Anvil against PyTorch on CUDA. Before/after: CUDA examples verified on NVIDIA hardware go from none to every example. Install to first training run takes under 30 s on a fresh Colab.
- **Builds on:** the dependency-free compiler, backend/cuda.py and its emulation mode, jupyter.py, interp.py, ide.py
- **Risk:** The CUDA backend has never run on hardware, and real bugs (unified-memory synchronization, atomics, occupancy) will appear the day it does. The interpreter under Pyodide is slow beyond toy sizes. Colab's terms and GPU availability change.

### Zero-copy everywhere: DLPack, strides as types, MLX/Metal buffer sharing, a ~1 us call

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

anvil.function accepts tensors from any framework (PyTorch, JAX, MLX, CuPy, NumPy) by pointer and returns results the same way. Strides become part of the compiled signature, so a transposed or sliced PyTorch view flows straight into Anvil's affine offsets with no .contiguous() copy, which Anvil gets almost for free because every load is already an affine offset. A small C call path fixes the one place PyTorch beats Anvil on the CPU: batch-1 latency.

- **Build:** anvil/pyapi.py arguments() accepts __dlpack__ and __dlpack_device__ (DLPack 1.0 versioned capsules with the read-only flag), __array_interface__, the buffer protocol and __cuda_array_interface__. It specializes on (shape, strides, dtype) instead of calling np.ascontiguousarray. ir.Buffer gets explicit strides (its strides are row-major only today), so input loads are built with them. anvil/_fastcall.c, compiled with cc on first use and adding no build dependency, is a METH_FASTCALL entry that writes the pointer slots, releases the GIL and calls anvil_entry, replacing ctypes. Results are exported as DLPack capsules or written into an out= tensor. On Metal, anvil_metal.h wraps host memory with newBufferWithBytesNoCopy (page-aligned, with a copy only as fallback), so CPU torch, NumPy and MLX arrays become MTLBuffers at no cost. anvil.mlx.kernel() emits Anvil's generated MSL through mx.fast.metal_kernel, so fused Anvil kernels run inside MLX's lazy graph.
- **Demo:** A pipeline goes from torch (a non-contiguous transposed view) to Anvil layer norm on Metal to MLX and back to torch, with a byte counter showing 0 bytes copied, against 3 full copies before. REPORT.md's batch-1 MLP row drops from 14.8 us (0.38x eager) to 4 us or less, ahead of eager's 5.7 us.
- **Builds on:** pyapi.py pointer slots (the input/output extern buffers), the Affine offsets every load already uses, anvil_metal.h shared-memory buffers
- **Risk:** Specializing on strides multiplies the number of compiled versions, so it needs a cap and a copy fallback. DLPack lifetime and ownership bugs are easy to introduce. Torch MPS tensors' MTLBuffer handles and stream ordering are not public API, so sharing with MPS needs a sync or a C++ shim.

### safetensors as types: compile-time headers, mmap, bf16 storage

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

`w = safetensors("model.safetensors")` reads the JSON header at compile time, so every tensor in a checkpoint has a type before anything runs. A port with a wrong shape fails to compile with an error naming the tensor, and the editor shows the checkpoint's contents on hover. The file is mmapped rather than loaded: a 1 GB model is ready in microseconds and shared through the page cache. bf16 and f16 weights stay half size in memory and are widened in registers.

- **Build:** anvil/builtins.py gets bi_safetensors (an 8-byte length, then a JSON header, read like read_npy_header), and anvil/values.py gets a CheckpointVal with attribute paths (w.encoder.layer[3].attention.query.weight) and typed `w["name"]` lookups. ide.py's recorder lists its tensors for hover. ir.py gains storage-only dtypes bf16 and f16 (Buffer.nbytes hard-codes 4 bytes today). backend/kernel.py widens on load (shll #16 for bf16, fcvtl for f16) and narrows on store (bfcvtn on M2 and later, fcvtn), and interp.py emulates both. A runtime mmap routine in runtime.s and anvil_host.h points `indirect` pointer slots (the ≥64 MB heap path in aarch64.py) at base + offset. save_safetensors(net, path) writes model-path names and __metadata__ (including a source hash), and load(net, st, rename=...) reads them. Weights stay mmapped instead of being baked by pyapi.lower, whose `b.init = ...tolist()` cannot handle a 500M-parameter model.
- **Demo:** With google/vit-base-patch16-224, a deliberately wrong port fails at compile time with the tensor's name and both shapes. The correct port goes from process start to first forward in milliseconds, against transformers' from_pretrained (REPORT.md: `import torch` alone takes about 1.5 s). Resident memory halves with bf16 storage.
- **Builds on:** compile-time header reading in bi_npy and bi_idx, save/load checkpoints, ir.indirect heap pointer slots, ide.py's recorder
- **Risk:** Half-precision storage touches every backend (NEON, Metal, CUDA, the interpreter) and the arena planner. Some files have unaligned offsets. Hugging Face checkpoint names need per-architecture mapping.

### Out through every door: ONNX (with Einsum) and LiteRT int8 export

*wow 6/10 · months*

Export Anvil models to ONNX and to LiteRT (.tflite) with no dependencies. Anvil is index notation and ONNX has an Einsum op, so any sum-of-products kernel has an exact, readable fallback when no named op matches. That puts Anvil-trained models into ONNX Runtime (Windows, Linux, the browser through onnxruntime-web, NVIDIA GPUs through TensorRT), and onto phones and microcontrollers through LiteRT.

- **Build:** anvil/export_onnx.py is a protobuf encoder of about 250 lines for ModelProto, GraphProto, NodeProto and TensorProto (opset 18). A raising pass, shared with the StableHLO exporter, recognizes MatMul and Gemm with bias, Softmax, LayerNormalization, Conv (from the prelude's im2col + GEMM pair) and Gelu. Whatever it does not recognize falls back: sum-of-products to Einsum, elementwise bodies to operator trees, affine views to Transpose/Slice/Reshape/Expand, gathers to Gather or GatherElements, and `where` masks to Range + comparison + Where. anvil/export_tflite.py is a flatbuffer builder for a subset of schema.fbs (FULLY_CONNECTED, CONV_2D, MAX_POOL_2D, SOFTMAX, ADD, MUL, RESHAPE), with full-integer int8 post-training quantization calibrated by running Anvil itself over a calibration set. The CLI adds `anvil export --onnx | --tflite`.
- **Demo:** The bin/draw digit reader, exported to ONNX, runs in onnxruntime-web (a drawing page with no server) and in ONNX Runtime's CUDA/TensorRT provider on an NVIDIA GPU. The same model runs as int8 .tflite on an Android phone. Measured: onnx.checker passes, ORT outputs agree within 1e-5 for every example, and the int8 file is 4x smaller and loses less than 0.5 points of MNIST accuracy.
- **Builds on:** anvil export's lowering (pyapi.lower, which bakes the weights), the prelude's Conv2d and Linear structure, interp.py for calibration
- **Risk:** Raising arbitrary fused kernels back into named ops is pattern matching that never ends. Einsum is slow in some runtimes. LiteRT's op set is narrow and has no general Einsum, so some models will not export to it.

### PyTorch's OpInfo as Anvil's test oracle, plus a Rosetta page

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

PyTorch's OpInfo database describes hundreds of operators, with sample inputs and reference results. Running it against Anvil's aten lowering gives the Dynamo backend a public coverage number and gives Anvil's autodiff a gradient checker; the nine-project comparison alone found a wrong gradient. The Rosetta page shows one model written in PyTorch, JAX, MLX, tinygrad and Anvil side by side. It compares outputs and gradients in ULPs, step times and lines of code, and records when each framework reports a deliberately planted shape bug.

- **Build:** tests/conformance/test_opinfo.py imports op_db from torch.testing._internal.common_methods_invocations, with the torch version pinned. It runs each float32 SampleInput through the aten table and pyapi, compares forward results with torch.testing.assert_close, and compares vector-Jacobian products from anvil/autodiff.py with torch.autograd under a random cotangent. docs/conformance.md is generated with a forward/grad/unsupported status per op. benchmarks/rosetta/ holds the GPT from benchmarks/vs_pytorch in five frameworks, all starting from one safetensors initialization, with a harness that reports the maximum ULP difference per layer and per gradient and generates an HTML page.
- **Demo:** The Rosetta page: five columns of code for the same GPT, one table of ULP distances and step times, and a row for when the shape bug is reported. Anvil reports it at compile time, with the line; the others report it at run time, after N seconds.
- **Builds on:** tests/test_gradcheck.py, the aten lowering table, anvil/autodiff.py, benchmarks/vs_pytorch
- **Risk:** OpInfo is an internal API that changes between releases. Many samples use dtypes and features outside Anvil's scope (f64, complex, int64, dynamic shapes), and they must be filtered out honestly. Tolerances for f32 reductions depend on summation order.

### The Anvil Zoo: ports with bisecting parity tests, student good-first-ports, and the paper pipeline

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

A zoo of Hugging Face and torchvision models ported to Anvil, each guarded by `anvil parity`. The tool runs the original PyTorch model and the Anvil port on the same weights and input, then names the first layer where they diverge, with its Anvil source line. Porting becomes a graded class assignment with the parity tool as autograder, the zoo becomes the way Anvil gains users, and the measured results become papers.

- **Build:** anvil/parity.py adds `anvil parity port.anvil --against hf:<repo> | torch:<file>:<Class>`. It registers torch forward hooks on every submodule. On the Anvil side, the --check machinery (a runtime routine that already scans every tensor a kernel writes) dumps intermediate tensors, labeled with model paths from the elaborator recorder. A name mapper works from safetensors keys. The output is the first divergent layer, its maximum absolute and ULP error, and the Anvil line. Each zoo entry has zoo/<model>/{model.anvil, port.toml (repo, revision, tolerance), PARITY.md}, and nightly CI on a macOS arm64 runner shows parity and speed badges. 'Good first port' issue templates come with a grading rubric. Paper targets: PLDI or OOPSLA for shape-typed index notation with compile-time reverse-mode autodiff over comprehensions and the optimizer fused into the GEMM epilogue; MLSys for whole-step compilation of unmodified PyTorch (from compile_step); CGO for the NEON/AMX schedules with a static arena; SIGCSE for teaching ML with a compiler students can read.
- **Demo:** `anvil parity zoo/vit/vit.anvil --against hf:google/vit-base-patch16-224` reports the first divergence at encoder.layer.0 layernorm_before (vit.anvil:41), because HF's config.layer_norm_eps is 1e-12 and the port uses the prelude's 1e-5. The class then fixes it live.
- **Builds on:** --check NaN tracing, the elaborator recorder, safetensors-as-types, benchmarks/vs_pytorch
- **Risk:** Parity has to be maintained against moving upstream checkpoints and transformers versions, so revisions must be pinned. Name mapping is per-architecture work. How many models the zoo can hold depends on features still on the roadmap (fused attention, half precision).

## security-privacy-and-trust (15)

### ε in the type: DP-SGD with a compile-time privacy accountant

*wow 9/10 · months*

`minimize loss with dp_sgd(clip=1.0, noise=1.1)` over a `private` dataset labels the trained model dp(ε=2.7, δ=1e-5), and the compiler computes that number at compile time: dataset size (from the idx header), batch size, epochs and noise are all constants. Going over a declared budget is a compile error that solves for the fix: 'spends ε=4.1 > 3.0: use 6 epochs, or noise ≥ 1.34'. Threat model: a white-box adversary holding the released weights who wants to learn anything about one training example; the label rules implement the post-processing and composition theorems. Opacus computes ε at run time, typically accounts shuffled batches as if they were Poisson-sampled (Chua et al., ICML 2024 measured the gap), draws noise from a non-cryptographic PRNG, and lets you print the private loss. Anvil makes each of those a compile error.

- **Build:** (a) Per-example gradients as a kernel-IR transform in autodiff.py. Backprop's weight-gradient kernel reduces over the batch Var, which is known from `batches`' first axis. That reduction is either un-reduced, or replaced by ghost norms, chosen per layer from cost.py's static FLOP model: Goodfellow 2015 for dense layers, and for im2col convs the ghost clipping of Li et al. (ICLR 2022), ‖Σ_t a_t b_tᵀ‖² = Σ_{t,t'}(a_t·a_t')(b_t·b_t'). The clip factor s[n] is then folded into dz, so dW = aᵀ(s⊙dz) in one backward pass (Bu et al.'s book-keeping, ICML 2023). This works for any user-written index-notation layer, not a hand-maintained module list. (b) Poisson subsampling with static shapes: masked batches of fixed capacity B_max, with the overflow probability charged to δ through a Chernoff bound. (c) Noise from a CSPRNG: AES-CTR on the ARMv8 aese/aesmc instructions in runtime.s, seeded from getentropy (today's RNG is a hash with default seed 0, so anyone with the seed can subtract the noise). It feeds a constant-time discrete Gaussian on fixed-point gradients (Canonne-Kamath-Steinke 2020), which defeats Mironov 2012 and Jin et al. S&P 2022 floating-point and timing attacks on samplers. (d) anvil/privacy.py: an RDP accountant for the sampled Gaussian (Mironov-Talwar-Zhang 2019) in pure Python, plus a PRV/PLD accountant (Gopi et al. 2021) for tight ε. `anvil privacy file.anvil` reports ε by line, like `anvil cost`, and an inlay hint shows ε after each loop. (e) dp_sgd and dp_adam are ordinary prelude.anvil optimizer blocks. In type-system terms the budget is a linear resource: each `minimize` consumes part of it.
- **Demo:** MNIST at ε=3 trains in seconds on the CPU, shown next to the non-private curve with the measured DP overhead (target under 1.5×). In VS Code, lower the budget and the error proposes a noise multiplier. Then the LiRA attack from the red-team arena, which identified training members of the plain model, falls to chance on the DP model.
- **Builds on:** autodiff.py Backprop/minimize/clip_gradients, `batches`, cost.py, the counter-based RNG, IDE inlay hints, the privacy-label pass
- **Risk:** Accountant bugs void the guarantee, so test against Google's dp_accounting and Opacus RDP tables. Ghost norms versus materialized gradients is tricky for attention. The noise sampler must itself be constant-time. DP accuracy on MNIST is lower (roughly mid-to-high 90s% at ε 1–3 in published results) and must be presented honestly.

### Red-team arena: attacks fast enough to run live in class

*wow 9/10 · weeks*

A set of attack labs that run in seconds because Anvil trains MNIST in under half a second: LiRA membership inference (Carlini et al. S&P 2022) with 64 shadow models; privacy auditing that measures an empirical lower bound on ε (Steinke, Nasr, Jagielski, NeurIPS 2023: one training run) and plots it against the compiler's upper bound from the DP type; extraction of the small GPT's hidden width by SVD of its logits (Carlini et al. ICML 2024: 256-way logits from a D=64 model have rank ≤ 65); adversarial patches (Brown et al. 2017) via `grad`; and a BadNets backdoor with Neural Cleanse detection. Each lab states its threat model and pairs it with an Anvil defense the compiler checks (DP type, an API type that returns only labels or rounded scores, certified radius, partition aggregation). PyTorch can run these attacks, but not as a live laptop demo in seconds with a typed defense to switch on.

- **Build:** examples/redteam/*.anvil, each attack in 30–80 lines (LiRA's per-example Gaussian fits are index notation; shadow models train stacked along an ensemble axis or in `--set` loops). A `bin/arena` web UI extending examples/draw/server.py with a log-scale ROC (TPR at 0.1%/1% FPR), the audit plot of empirical versus proven ε, the logit singular-value spectrum, and a patch editor. Canary-insertion utilities and a class scoreboard.
- **Demo:** Every student draws 20 digits in the browser. The class model trains on half the students. LiRA names who was in the training set at high TPR at 1% FPR, because individual handwriting is an outlier. Recompile with `private` and ε=2 and the ROC collapses to the diagonal, while the audit's measured ε stays below the type's proven bound. Then the SVD 'cliff' at 64 reveals the GPT's secret width from its API.
- **Builds on:** examples/draw server and page, dream.anvil (input gradients), transformer.anvil, fast CPU training, `--set` sweeps
- **Risk:** On a model that generalizes well, MNIST membership inference is weak, so the demo needs small or overfit training sets and outlier canaries (student handwriting). Attack code must be kept classroom-safe and scoped to the class's own models.

### Federated classroom with secure aggregation and distributed DP

*wow 9/10 · months*

Thirty laptops each train on their owner's handwriting, and the teacher's server only ever learns the sum of their updates. Bonawitz et al.'s secure aggregation (CCS 2017) does this: pairwise masks from X25519 key agreements cancel in the sum, and Shamir shares recover students who drop out. Distributed discrete Gaussian noise (Kairouz, Liu, Steinke ICML 2021) makes the sum itself DP, so even the server sees nothing about any individual. Threat model: an honest-but-curious server plus up to t colluding students trying to reconstruct someone's handwriting from their update by gradient inversion (Zhu et al. NeurIPS 2019, which the class runs live first). The compiler guarantees what hand-written FL code gets wrong: updates are typed private@student, quantized into Z_2^32 with a compile-time no-overflow proof (clip norm × clients × static shapes), and masked with CSPRNG output.

- **Build:** FedAvg rounds as Anvil programs: local `minimize` steps, then `update = W - W0`, clipping, and fixed-point quantization relying on i32's mod-2^32 wraparound. The no-overflow bound is checked by the elaborator. Client crypto in C++ (X25519, an AES-CTR PRG on ARMv8 AES instructions, Shamir sharing) linked through anvil_host.h or runtime.s routines. A teacher server (C++ or asyncio) and a dashboard extending bin/draw. The DLG gradient-inversion attack written in Anvil with `grad` with respect to a dummy image (dream.anvil's machinery).
- **Demo:** Round 1 without secure aggregation: the teacher runs DLG on one student's single-image update and their drawn '3' appears on the projector. Round 2 with it: every individual update the server holds looks like uniform noise, only the sum is meaningful, and the global model reads everyone's handwriting better each round.
- **Builds on:** i32 kernels, clip_norm in minimize, `grad` of inputs (dream.anvil), the bin/draw server, anvil.function
- **Risk:** Real crypto and dropout handling are fiddly and must be tested against known vectors. Classroom Wi-Fi and NAT complicate peer key exchange, so the server relays it. DLG reconstruction works well only for tiny batches, so the demo must be set up honestly.

### zkML where index notation is the proof: Anvil compiles its own sum-check prover

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

`anvil prove digits_reader.anvil --input img.npy` produces a succinct proof that a committed model computed this output on this input. It verifies in a browser in milliseconds and can hide the weights or the input. Threat model: a model provider who quietly substitutes a cheaper or backdoored model, or a party who must show it ran the approved model (exam grading, content moderation) without revealing the weights. The key observation is that index notation is already the language of sum-check: `y[i] = sum W[i, j] * x[j]` becomes ỹ(r) = Σ_{j∈{0,1}^k} W̃(r, j)·x̃(j), which Thaler's matrix-product protocol (CRYPTO 2013) proves with linear prover overhead. Each kernel becomes a GKR layer (as in zkCNN, CCS 2021, and zkLLM, CCS 2024), and nonlinear ops go through lookup arguments (Lasso, Setty-Thaler-Wahby). EZKL instead compiles ONNX into huge Halo2 circuits.

- **Build:** (1) int8/i16 fixed-point dtypes and a quantization pass (parser.parse_type, ir, kernel.py with NEON sdot/smmla, which is also a big inference speedup on its own). (2) anvil/zk/: arithmetic over Mersenne-31 (p = 2^31−1, the Circle-STARK/Stwo field, whose reductions fit NEON 32-bit lanes). The prover's multilinear-extension folds and sum-check rounds are themselves index-notation kernels, compiled by Anvil's own AArch64 and CUDA backends, so the compiler builds its own prover. (3) A transparent hash-based multilinear commitment (Basefold or FRI style), post-quantum with no trusted setup. (4) Verifiers in TypeScript/Wasm and in Anvil. (5) Stretch: a proof of one SGD step (zkPoT, Abbaszadeh et al. CCS 2024).
- **Demo:** Draw a 7 in bin/draw: the page shows '7' and a proof badge, and the browser verifies that the answer came from the model whose commitment is printed in the README. Change one weight on the server and verification fails. Classroom version: the teacher commits a grading model and every student verifies their own grade.
- **Builds on:** kernel IR index notation, autodiff-style IR transforms, the AArch64/CUDA backends as prover compilers, bin/draw
- **Risk:** A soundness bug in a home-grown proof system is catastrophic, so it needs a slow Python reference prover/verifier, known-answer tests and outside review. Quantization costs accuracy. Proof time beyond MNIST/CNN scale is uncertain. Zero-knowledge masking adds a second layer of complexity.

### Shards: ensembles as a tensor axis for PATE, poisoning certificates and exact unlearning

*wow 8/10 · months*

Train K small models in one compiled program: every parameter gains a leading model axis `[K, 784, 128]`, and each model sees a disjoint shard. Three guarantees come from that one structure. PATE (Papernot et al. ICLR 2017/2018) labels public data by a noisy teacher vote, giving a DP student. Deep Partition Aggregation (Levine & Feizi ICLR 2021) certifies that no poisoning of m training examples can flip a prediction whose vote margin exceeds 2m. SISA (Bourtoule et al. S&P 2021) unlearns an example by retraining only its shard from the last checkpoint before it, and Anvil's bit-determinism lets anyone check that the result is identical to retraining from scratch without that example. Threat model: data poisoners and backdoor injectors, inference attacks against teachers, and right-to-erasure requests (GDPR Art. 17). PyTorch isn't bit-reproducible, so its unlearning can't be checked.

- **Build:** An `ensemble K` modifier on model instantiation (elaborate.py instantiate/model_params) that adds the leading axis and turns `x @ W` into stacks of products (blas.py already sends those to Accelerate/AMX). A `shards(X, y, k)` primitive with hash-based example-ID assignment and a per-shard Fisher–Yates stream, so deleting one example perturbs one shard only. `anvil certify-poison` for DPA margins. PATE's Confident-GNMax with data-dependent RDP, plugged into the DP accountant. SISA slice checkpoints via save/load, and `anvil unlearn --remove ids.txt` replays only the affected slices and prints both hashes.
- **Demo:** 250 MNIST teachers trained as one fused program on a laptop CPU in about a minute. (a) A BadNets trigger poisoned into 30 images backdoors a single model, but the DPA ensemble certifies most test digits as immune to any 30 poisons. (b) A student asks to delete their handwriting: one shard retrains in seconds, and the new model's hash equals a full retrain from scratch without their data.
- **Builds on:** stacked-product routing in blas.py, `batches`/shuffle in runtime.s, save/load, deterministic multithreading
- **Risk:** Each shard sees little data, so the ensemble loses accuracy (the classic DPA/PATE trade-off). Memory grows with K × params. Exact unlearning holds only for the slice structure chosen before training.

### Constant-time inference, checked on the emitted assembly

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

`anvil build --ct` proves that no branch, no memory address and no variable-latency instruction in the binary depends on a `secret` input. A model serving private inputs (a medical scan, a password-strength model, a prompt's token ids) then leaks nothing through timing or cache side channels. Threat model: a co-located attacker measuring time or cache state (Prime+Probe, Flush+Reload) and, on M1–M3, the data memory-dependent prefetcher abused by GoFetch (Chen et al., USENIX Security 2024). Static shapes have already removed almost all data-dependent control flow, so Anvil can verify for whole networks what crypto libraries prove with great effort (ct-verif, Binsec/Rel). PyTorch's dispatcher, data-dependent fast paths and denormal slowdowns make the claim impossible there.

- **Build:** (a) Secret taint over the backend's machine IR (mir.py Ins defs/uses), seeded by the label pass, plus an independent C++ checker (tools/ctcheck, ~1k lines) that re-parses the final .s and repeats the analysis, so the guarantee doesn't rest on 17k lines of Python. Violations map back to source lines through kernel labels. (b) Rewrites: gathers with secret indices (Embedding `E[ids]`, cross_entropy's `logp[i, labels[i]]`) become oblivious one-hot reductions `sum_k (k == ids[n]) * E[k, d]`, a one-line index-notation rewrite in elaborate.py's gather path, and the gather's `b.lo` bounds check (kernel.py:407) disappears because the scan is in range by construction. `if`, `while`, `nonzero` and `break` on secrets are compile errors. (c) Instructions: fdiv/fsqrt (softmax, layer_norm) become frecpe/frecps and frsqrte/frsqrts Newton steps unless they appear on Arm's DIT list. FPCR.FZ stops subnormals from changing timing (Andrysco et al. S&P 2015). The runtime entry runs `msr DIT, #1`, which on M3 also disables the DMP. (d) A dudect (Reparaz et al. 2017) harness as an empirical cross-check in tests.
- **Demo:** examples/transformer.anvil with a secret prompt: the checker colors the embedding gather's load instruction red and points at `E[ids]` in the source. One flag later it passes (the one-hot embedding costs 256×64 MACs per token, negligible here). Then dudect's t-statistic stays under its threshold over millions of runs, while a deliberately early-exiting variant fails within seconds.
- **Builds on:** kernel.py schedules (already branch-free vecmath, fcsel argmax, bsl selects), mir.py, the runtime.s entry point, the privacy-label pass
- **Risk:** The guarantee holds only relative to a leakage model (branches, addresses, listed instructions). On the Metal GPU, GPU.zip-style data-dependent compression (Wang et al. S&P 2024) is outside software control, so the guarantee is CPU-only. Accelerate/AMX sgemm is a black box: it is constant-time only if it isn't data-dependent, so audit it or use Anvil's own GEMM under --ct.

### Label-directed secure computation: two laptops, one prediction, no shared secrets

*wow 8/10 · moonshot*

Annotate ownership (`input image: f32[28, 28] @alice`, `model net @bob`) and the compiler picks a cryptographic protocol for every kernel from the labels. Kernels whose inputs one party owns run locally in cleartext; linear layers use additive secret sharing over Z_2^64 with Beaver triples; ReLU and argmax use OT-based comparison (CrypTFlow2, CCS 2020) or function secret sharing (AriaNN). Neither party sees the other's secret. Threat model: semi-honest two-party computation, upgradable to malicious security with SPDZ-style MACs; the privacy labels decide where each value may live. Viaduct (Acay et al. PLDI 2021) showed protocol selection from information-flow labels for general programs; Anvil applies it to tensors, and static shapes give exact communication bytes and rounds at compile time (`anvil cost --mpc`), which CrypTen and other PyTorch-based secure ML frameworks can't predict.

- **Build:** anvil/mpc/: a protocol-assignment pass over kernel IR (an ILP or greedy search minimizing communication, as in Viaduct). Fixed-point i64 ring arithmetic and truncation in kernel.py (new dtype). Beaver triples from a trusted dealer for the class, and silent OT (Ferret, CCS 2020) as the real path. Comparison protocols for ReLU, max_pool and argmax. A C++ socket transport layered on anvil_host.h. Softmax replaced by a revealed argmax or a polynomial approximation. Shares the quantization layer with the zkML and FHE work.
- **Demo:** Student A draws a digit in bin/draw; student B's laptop holds the trained CNN. The two machines talk over the classroom Wi-Fi and A learns '7'. A packet sniffer on the projector shows only random-looking shares, and the live byte and round counters match what `anvil cost --mpc` predicted before the run.
- **Builds on:** privacy labels, i32 kernels, cost.py's static analysis, anvil_host.h, bin/draw
- **Risk:** It must be clearly separated from the existing FHE moonshot: MPC is the interactive, far faster alternative. Malicious security is hard. ReLU-heavy networks need many rounds, so Wi-Fi latency dominates.

### Privacy labels: information-flow types for tensors

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Every tensor carries a label from a lattice (public ⊑ private(images) ⊑ secret, plus consent or license tags such as consent(research)), and the compiler proves noninterference: nothing derived from private data reaches print, show, save, save_npy, an exported library or a Python return value except through an approved declassifier. Threat model: a careless or malicious program, or a `use`d library, leaking training data through training logs, checkpoints, crash messages (today `_anvil_rt_oob` prints the offending index value, so a bad `labels[i]` gather prints a label), loop counts, exit codes or timing. Static shapes and structured IR let Anvil check timing-sensitive noninterference as well: kernel run times don't depend on data, so the only timing leaks left are secret branches and secret gathers, and the compiler finds both. Jif-style languages generally leave timing open, and PyTorch can't tell which tensors came from the dataset.

- **Build:** `private`/`secret` modifiers in A.TypeExpr (parser.parse_type) and on loaders (`private images = idx(...)`); a `label` field on ir.Buffer; a new anvil/flow.py pass over the optimized-before kernel IR: each Store gets the join of its Loads' and Gather indices' labels; ir.If/While/For carry a pc label (Denning 1977, Volpano-Smith-Irvine 1996), so writes under a private branch are tainted, and a `while`/`break` on a private condition is an error because of the termination channel. Sinks are listed from ir.Print, RTCall(save/save_npy/show), ir.Check messages, `--check` NaN reports, pyapi output buffers and export.py baking. Errors print the flow path line by line using the diagnostics Label and call-site chains ('loss (line 18) ← net(x) (line 17) ← batches(images) (line 3)'). There are two declassifiers: the DP mechanisms from the DP-SGD idea and an explicit `declassify(x, "reason")`, which is listed by a new `anvil audit`. Elaborator.recorder/ide.py show labels in hovers and inlay hints. In type-system terms this is a type-and-effect system whose effect is the pc label.
- **Demo:** Mark the MNIST images `private` and the trainer fails with three errors, each with its source path: `print(loss)` inside the loop (your training log leaks), `if loss < 0.05: break` (the epoch count leaks), and `save(net, ...)` of a non-DP model. Next, a friendly `use "fancy_layers.anvil"` that folds the first training image into a printed 'init statistic' is caught at the line where it happens.
- **Builds on:** ir.Buffer and structured control-flow IR, elaborate.py values, diagnostics call-site chains, ide.py recorder and VS Code inlay hints
- **Risk:** Label creep: everything that is trained becomes private and only becomes usable through the DP declassifier. Loop-carried write-backs, optimizer state and in-place buffer reuse (storage aliasing) all have to be tracked soundly. Gathers with private indices need address labels as well as value labels.

### Sensitivity types: differential privacy for any index-notation query

*wow 7/10 · months*

Take DP beyond SGD. The compiler infers the ℓ1/ℓ2 sensitivity of any function of a private dataset to adding or removing one example (linear sensitivity types in the style of Fuzz, Reed & Pierce ICFP 2010, and Duet, Near et al. OOPSLA 2019), so `release(counts, eps=0.5)` picks and calibrates the Laplace, Gaussian or discrete mechanism by itself. Threat model: an analyst who runs many aggregate queries over private data (histograms, means, PCA, marginals) and combines the answers to reconstruct individual rows (Dinur–Nissim PODS 2003). Index notation makes sensitivity mechanical. In `counts[labels[i]] += 1`, each example i touches one output with weight 1, so ℓ1 = 1. In `mean clamp(x[i, j], 0, 1)`, static shapes give the exact n. Neither PyTorch nor NumPy knows which axis is a person.

- **Build:** anvil/sensitivity.py with per-kernel rules on ir.Kernel. A store whose offset is injective in the example Var touches at most one output per example. A reduction over the example Var adds up bounded per-example contributions. Elementwise ops propagate ranges from declared clamps; error messages suggest where a clamp is needed. Products with public weights scale by an operator norm bounded at compile time with power iteration. An explicit example axis in types, `f32[n private, 784]`. Mechanisms in prelude.anvil: laplace, gaussian, discrete_gaussian, and report-noisy-max as the exponential mechanism. Composition goes through the DP-SGD accountant. DP synthetic data via MST (McKenna et al., winner of the NIST 2018 challenge), written in Anvil: noisy 2-way marginals, then a graphical model fit by `minimize`.
- **Demo:** A live anonymous class survey loaded from csv. First, a reconstruction attack recovers every student's yes/no answer from exact subset-sum queries. Rerun against the compiled DP version and it recovers noise, while the DP histogram and correlation matrix stay useful. Finally, DP synthetic survey data drawn from noisy marginals.
- **Builds on:** index-notation range inference and scatter-add in elaborate.py, the csv loader, cost.py's per-line reporting, the label pass and the DP accountant
- **Risk:** Nonlinear code has unbounded sensitivity without user clamps. Conservative bounds add too much noise. Operator-norm bounds are only valid when the weights are public. The neighbor relation (add/remove vs replace) must be consistent across mechanisms.

### Proof-carrying code: a small checker proves every load and store stays in its buffer

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Every binary Anvil emits ships with a memory-safety certificate. `anvil verify`, a separate ~1,500-line C++ checker that shares no code with the compiler, confirms before loading that every load and store in every kernel and runtime routine stays inside the buffer it belongs to. Threat model: a bug in the 17k-line Python compiler (fusion, in-place reuse, arena overlap, tile edges) or a hostile shape or file turning a model into a read/write primitive inside the process that loads it, such as a server dlopen'ing anvil.function libraries (today the ~/.cache/anvil dylib is loaded by a 64-bit name hash with no integrity check) or an app linking an `anvil export` library. This is Necula's proof-carrying code (POPL 1997) and the RockSalt/VeriWasm approach (trust a tiny verifier, not the compiler), and static shapes make it complete instead of heuristic. PyTorch's C++/CUDA kernels can't offer anything like it.

- **Build:** anvil/backend/cert.py emits a certificate per kernel. For each pointer register it records the buffer symbol, base offset, per-loop stride and trip count; the backend already keeps one pointer per access, advanced in each latch and rewound on exit. It also records the parallel chunk contract ([start, end) ⊆ [0, total)), and for gathers the dominating bounds check. The checker (tools/pcc, C++20) parses the assembly, rebuilds loops from LoopMark comments, runs affine-plus-interval abstract interpretation over the x registers, and checks every memory operand against symbol sizes and the arena.py layout. Hand-written runtime.s routines carry annotated loop invariants. Accelerate sgemm calls get their lda and dims checked against buffer sizes. The pyapi cache verifies the certificate and a content hash before ctypes.CDLL. The checker itself is fuzzed with mutated assembly so it is shown to reject bad code.
- **Demo:** Plant an off-by-one in the GEMM tile-edge code in kernel.py that no example output reveals (the overrun lands in arena padding). `anvil verify` rejects it: 'kernel 37 (l1 @ W): store via x12 reaches byte 401,408 of a 401,408-byte buffer at tile column 124'. Then a class 'find the bug' round with three planted bugs.
- **Builds on:** kernel.py's pointer-per-access scheme, mir.py LoopMarks, arena.py, ir.Gather bounds, the anvil.function cache
- **Risk:** Spills and stack frames need modeling. runtime.s routines (inflate via libz, the csv parser) need hand-written invariants. Accelerate stays trusted. Keeping the certificate format in step with schedule changes is ongoing work.

### Certified robustness as compiler transforms: CROWN, α-CROWN, randomized smoothing

*wow 7/10 · months*

Derive certification from the kernel IR. `crown(net, x, eps)` propagates linear bounds backward through the network (CROWN, Zhang et al. NeurIPS 2018), which is reverse-mode autodiff with matrices and ReLU relaxations in place of vectors, so it reuses autodiff.py. α-CROWN (Xu et al. ICLR 2021; the α,β-CROWN family won VNN-COMP 2021–2024) tunes the relaxation slopes with `maximize bound over alpha` written in Anvil itself. `smooth(net, sigma)` gives randomized smoothing's certified ℓ2 radius (Cohen, Rosenfeld, Kolter ICML 2019) with Clopper–Pearson confidence. Threat model: a test-time adversary perturbing the input within an ℓ∞ or ℓ2 ball, which is exactly what dream.anvil does. The result is a certificate carried in the type, and certified training (CROWN-IBP, Zhang et al. ICLR 2020) puts the bound inside `minimize`; PyTorch needs the separate auto_LiRPA library and its op coverage.

- **Build:** anvil/bounds.py, a backward linear-bound transform over kernels. Linear and conv layers use the same transpose rules as autodiff. Elementwise ops get per-op linear relaxations (relu, sigmoid, tanh, exp, plus softmax/attention relaxations in the auto_LiRPA style). Concretization uses dual norms for ℓ∞ and ℓ2, with outward rounding so float error can't break soundness. Prelude functions: crown, alpha_crown, smooth, certify. A small branch-and-bound over ReLU splits as a stretch goal. A benchmark against auto_LiRPA on the VNN-COMP MNIST MLPs, CPU against CPU.
- **Demo:** In bin/draw a ring around the prediction shows the certified radius as you draw. Run dream.anvil's attack with EPS inside the ring and it fails; just outside, it succeeds. Then a CROWN-IBP-trained network shows a much larger ring at a modest accuracy cost.
- **Builds on:** autodiff.py transpose rules, simplify.deriv, maximize/over, normal sampling, dream.anvil, bin/draw
- **Risk:** CROWN needs O(neurons²) memory on wide layers. Relaxations for attention are loose. It must be positioned clearly beyond the existing interval-robustness work so it isn't duplicated.

### Confidential CUDA: attested training on H100s the cloud can't read

*wow 7/10 · moonshot*

Run the CUDA backend for the first time on real hardware, inside an NVIDIA H100 in Confidential Computing mode within a confidential VM (AMD SEV-SNP or Intel TDX, e.g. Azure NCCadsH100v5). Bind the GPU attestation report, the CVM measurement and the sealed-model passport hash into one signed statement: 'this binary trained on this encrypted dataset inside attested hardware, and the operator saw only ciphertext'. Threat model: a cloud operator or co-tenant with host root, hypervisor control or PCIe snooping trying to read training data or weights; the guarantee assumes NVIDIA/AMD hardware roots of trust. In CC mode every CPU–GPU transfer goes through encrypted bounce buffers; Anvil's static memory plan knows all of that traffic at compile time and can batch it, while dynamic frameworks pay for transfers they can't predict.

- **Build:** Bring up anvil/backend/cuda.py on real GPUs (shared-memory tiled GEMM, cuBLAS optional). Replace unified memory with an explicit device arena (arena.py on the GPU, which also fixes a listed weakness) and compile-time-scheduled H2D/D2H copies. Datasets encrypted with AES-GCM (idx/npy), whose key a small key broker releases only after verifying the NVIDIA GPU attestation (NRAS or the local verifier) together with the SNP/TDX report. A `--confidential` mode in anvil_host.h that refuses to start unless the GPU reports CC mode on. Output weights sealed to a key and signed with the passport.
- **Demo:** Train the MNIST CNN on an H100 CC VM using a dataset that is only ever decrypted inside the TEE, and print the joint attestation. Turn CC mode off and the key broker refuses to release the dataset key, so the run never starts.
- **Builds on:** cuda.py and anvil_cuda.h/anvil_host.h, arena.py, the CUDA emulation tests, the sealed-model passport
- **Risk:** Cloud access and cost. The CUDA backend has never run on a GPU, so bring-up comes first. CC tooling changes quickly. Bounce-buffer bandwidth costs throughput. Trust ultimately rests on vendor hardware.

### Sealed models: typed, signed, pickle-free checkpoints and a model passport

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Replace the current checkpoint format with a schema'd, content-hashed, signed one that the compiler type-checks at compile time, the way it already parses npy headers. Today the file holds 'EINW', a version and an element count per tensor, and nothing else; I confirmed in a scratch test that a model with `W: [4, 8]` saves, and a model with `W: [8, 4]` loads it, returns 1, and runs on scrambled weights. Threat model: the long line of torch.load pickle remote-code-execution bugs and model-hub malware (even `weights_only=True` had an RCE bypass, CVE-2025-32434), tampered weights, and silently mismatched checkpoints. Anvil's loader can never run code, because the format is pure data and the loader is a fixed routine. Every artifact also gets a passport: one SHA-256 over source, prelude, compiler version, flags, data-file hashes, weights and binary.

- **Build:** anvil/ckpt.py plus runtime.s routines for a safetensors-compatible layout (a JSON header with names, dtypes and shapes, then raw little-endian bytes), so Hugging Face/PyTorch safetensors load directly. The header is read at compile time in builtins.bi_load, so a mismatch is a compile error naming the parameter. Per-tensor SHA-256 is checked at load using the ARMv8 sha256h instructions in runtime.s. Ed25519 signatures: `anvil sign`, and `load(net, "w.st", signed_by="teacher.pub")`. Optimizer state goes in the checkpoint (a PLAN v0.2 item). `anvil passport` writes an in-toto/SLSA provenance statement and a CycloneDX ML-BOM covering datasets, prelude and toolchain (cc, Accelerate, libz). export.py stamps the passport hash into the dylib as a symbol.
- **Demo:** A 'pretrained digits model' pickle that opens Calculator when torch.load reads it, next to the same weights as an Anvil sealed file. Anvil rejects a one-byte tamper with a signature error naming the tensor, rejects the transposed checkpoint at compile time ('l1.W is [128, 784] in the file, [784, 128] in DigitNet'), and loads a PyTorch-made safetensors file directly.
- **Builds on:** _anvil_rt_save/_anvil_rt_load in runtime.s, compile-time npy header parsing in builtins.py, export.py, cli.cache_dir
- **Risk:** Hand-written crypto in assembly must pass test vectors; Ed25519 probably means vendoring a small audited C implementation, which ends the no-dependencies purity. Passports only matter if people check them.

### Capability-safe compile time and hermetic builds

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Today the elaborator is a full compile-time interpreter that reads any file a string literal names, with `~` expanded (bytes, csv, npy, idx), and `anvil export` bakes whatever the function reads into the shipped library. I confirmed in a scratch test that a `use`d model library whose body calls `bytes("fake_credentials")` produces an exported dylib whose output on an all-zero input spells the file's contents. Threat model: an untrusted library, pasted snippet or future comptime/macro code running with the user's full file access at build time, the build-time supply-chain class behind xz-utils and malicious setup.py. Make compile time capability-based: a file reads only what it was granted, every read is recorded with its hash, and the same inputs always give the same binary.

- **Build:** An object-capability `Caps` value threaded through Elaborator scopes: st_Use, builtins.bi_idx/bi_npy/bi_csv/bi_bytes, export.py. `use "layers.anvil"` grants nothing by default; `use "data_utils.anvil" with read("data/")` grants a directory. The main file's caps come from the CLI (`--allow-read=data/`, in the style of Deno or WASI preopens), defaulting to the program's own directory with no `~`. Paths are canonicalized with realpath and checked against symlink escapes. `anvil build --manifest` lists every file read with its SHA-256 and feeds the sealed-model passport. `anvil build --hermetic` re-runs compilation under a macOS sandbox-exec (Seatbelt) profile that denies network access and any path outside the manifest, as defense in depth. This is groundwork for a package manager whose lockfiles are content hashes.
- **Demo:** A friendly-looking fancy_layers.anvil whose GELU variant mixes `bytes("~/.aws/credentials")` into a bias. With today's Anvil, calling the exported library on zeros prints the secret. With capabilities, the build fails: 'fancy_layers.anvil:14 reads ~/.aws/credentials, but this file was granted no read access (granted: none)'.
- **Builds on:** elaborate.py st_Use (already restricted to declarations), builtins loaders, export.py baking, cli.py
- **Risk:** The checks live inside the compiler, so a compiler bug can bypass them; Seatbelt is the backstop. Examples that read ../README.md need grants. sandbox-exec is deprecated, though it still works.

### Break the loader: fuzzing the assembly runtime and the compiler

*wow 5/10 · days*

Anvil's file loaders (gzip idx, npy, csv number parsing, bytes, checkpoints) and its printing are hand-written AArch64 assembly, which AddressSanitizer can't instrument, and the VS Code extension runs the compiler on untrusted source at every keystroke. Threat model: a malicious dataset or checkpoint file shared on a course page or a hub that overflows a runtime buffer, and source that crashes or hangs the compiler. The goal is a fuzzed-clean runtime with a differential oracle: every loader must agree byte for byte with the NumPy interpreter's Python loaders, a guarantee PyTorch's C++ loaders don't have.

- **Build:** tests/fuzz/: libFuzzer harnesses in C++ (Homebrew LLVM) that link runtime.s and call _anvil_rt_load_csv, _load_npy, _load_idx, _load_bytes and _load on fuzzed files. Destination buffers sit flush against PROT_NONE guard pages at both ends (Electric Fence style), since ASan can't see inside the assembly. A differential check against interp.py's loaders through a persistent Python worker. AFL++ on the gzip/libz path. Grammar-aware fuzzing of the parser and elaborator from Hypothesis-generated Anvil programs: any exception other than AnvilError is an internal compiler error, and a 2 s timeout catches hangs in range inference. ASan/UBSan builds of anvil_host.h, used by the CUDA emulation and the Metal host. A CI job that runs each harness for N minutes.
- **Demo:** The 'break the loader' lab: students get the harness and a branch with three planted bugs (an off-by-one in the csv digit loop, an unchecked npy header length, an integer overflow in the idx dimension product). A leaderboard counts unique crashes, then each team fixes one and adds a regression test.
- **Builds on:** runtime.s loaders, interp.py reference loaders, the tests/ and cuda_stub emulation infrastructure
- **Risk:** It may find nothing serious, though it is still a good teaching lab. Fuzzing the compile-time Python header parsers needs a separate Atheris harness.

## data-and-datasets (16)

### Moonshot: FineWeb on a Mac, an end-to-end typed pretraining data pipeline

*wow 10/10 · moonshot*

Do in one Anvil program what today takes three ecosystems (Hugging Face's datatrove, tiktoken and a PyTorch DataLoader). The program reads raw FineWeb-Edu Parquet through a typed schema and deduplicates it with MinHash-LSH using FineWeb's own recipe (5-gram shingles, 112 hashes in 14 bands of 8). It filters with a quality classifier trained in Anvil, tokenizes with Anvil's BPE, and packs documents into fixed-length sequences with boundary masks. Those stream from the SSD on the E-cores into a GPT training on Metal, and on a real NVIDIA GPU once the CUDA backend has run on one. Every stage gets a receipt and is timed against the tool it replaces.

- **Build:** - Combines the schema/Parquet provider, the SIMD parsers, the tokenizer, streaming and receipts ideas.
- New: string/ragged columns (Arrow offsets plus bytes) with static upper bounds.
- 64-bit hashing primitives and a MinHash kernel: shingles hashed with bitwise ops, then one min-reduction per hash function using the existing `min` reduction.
- Band keys sorted with the new radix sort; union–find over duplicate clusters as a runtime routine.
- A fastText-style quality classifier over hashed bags of n-grams, trained on FineWeb-Edu's published scores.
- Document packing (best-fit decreasing) with document-mask types, so attention never crosses a document boundary.
- A pipeline driver: `anvil run pipeline.anvil`, with each stage cached by content hash.
- **Demo:** From about 10 GB of raw FineWeb-Edu Parquet to a GPT of roughly 30–50M parameters that writes coherent English, on one M3 Max, with one command. A stage table compares docs/s for dedupe, filtering and tokenizing, and tokens/s for training, against datatrove, tiktoken and PyTorch. A receipt ties the final weights to the exact shard hashes.
- **Builds on:** The transformer/GPT example, the Metal backend, scatter-add, min reductions, and ideas 1, 5, 6, 9 and 11 above.
- **Risk:** Compute: 6 × 30M parameters × 2B tokens ≈ 4×10^17 FLOPs, about a day on the M3 Max GPU at a realistic 5 TFLOP/s, while GPT-2 124M on 10B tokens would take weeks. It needs fused attention (another lens's idea) for long contexts. Strings and variable sizes strain the static-shape design more than anything else on this list.

### Leakage is a type error: information-flow typing for train/val/test

*wow 9/10 · weeks*

Kapoor & Narayanan (Patterns, 2023) found data leakage in 294 papers across 17 fields. Its most common forms are data-flow facts a compiler can see: preprocessing fitted on train+test, feature selection on all the data, early stopping on the test set, and duplicates across splits. Anvil would label every data buffer with its split in a security lattice, propagate the labels through the kernel IR (Denning's 1976 lattice model, as in Jif and FlowCaml), and reject any program where something `minimize` updates depends on held-out data. A test metric could reach only `print` or `report`, which act as explicit declassification.

- **Build:** - builtins.py: `split(table, [0.8, 0.1, 0.1], by=row.patient_id, seed=...)` returns tensors labelled train, val and test. The split is group-aware, so one patient never lands in two splits. The label lives on ir.Buffer (a new field) and on TVal.
- New anvil/flow.py: a taint pass over the kernel IR using Kernel.reads()/writes() (optimize.py already caches these sets). It adds control dependence for ir.While, If and break conditions, so implicit flows are tracked: `if val_acc > best: save(...)` is allowed and `if test_acc > best:` is not. It also covers compile-time values, such as a hyperparameter picked by a compile-time `if` on a test statistic.
- autodiff.py: reuse the dependency walk that already reports "the loss does not depend on `W`" to explain why a parameter depends on test data.
- diagnostics.py: a multi-span chain: "mean over `all_x` (line 4, includes the test split) → `normalize` (line 9) → loss (line 21) → `minimize` (line 22)".
- `report(expr)` for declassification, and `--allow-leak` for transductive setups.
- Duplicate rows across splits caught at compile time with row content hashes (shared with the receipts idea).
- **Demo:** A 'Leakage zoo' lecture: eight 15-line programs, one per category in Kapoor & Narayanan's taxonomy. The centrepiece is the selection-bias trap from ESL §7.10.2 (Hastie, Tibshirani & Friedman; also Ambroise & McLachlan, PNAS 2002). Take 50 samples and 5,000 noise predictors with random labels, pick the 100 predictors most correlated with the label using all the data, then cross-validate: NumPy/sklearn report about 3% error on pure noise. Anvil refuses to compile the program and points at the feature-selection line. With selection moved inside the fold, error goes back to the true 50%.
- **Builds on:** The dependency analysis in autodiff.py and the warnings about parameters the loss can't train, the kernel IR's read/write sets, and the call-chain labels in diagnostics.py.
- **Risk:** Over-strictness: legitimate patterns (unsupervised pretraining on unlabeled test inputs, transductive learning, test-time augmentation) need clean declassification syntax, or users will switch the check off. Implicit flows through run-time control are where IFC systems usually get noisy. Following labels across programs through `save`/`load` requires storing them in checkpoint files.

### `anvil data audit`: mislabeled examples, duplicates, leakage and drift in seconds

*wow 9/10 · weeks*

Anvil trains MNIST in under half a second. That makes it cheap to do in one command what cleanlab and fastdup take minutes for: cross-validated training, confident learning, near-duplicate search, split leakage and drift. The command reads the program's own model and data declarations, so there is nothing to configure. The headline results are the MNIST test set's published label errors and CIFAR-10's train/test near-duplicates, found in seconds on a laptop.

- **Build:** - cli.py: `anvil data audit file.anvil [--model Net] [--folds 5]`, and `anvil data fix`, which writes a cleaned dataset plus a receipt.
- New anvil/audit.py: generates an Anvil program from the user's model and `batches` loop that computes out-of-sample probabilities as K compiled training runs (one compilation, folds chosen with `--set FOLD=k`). It then applies confident learning (Northcutt, Jiang & Chuang, JAIR 2021): per-class thresholds, the confident joint C[ŷ, y*] as a scatter-add, prune-by-noise-rate, and a ranking by normalized margin.
- Near-duplicates: penultimate-layer embeddings (or raw pixels), L2-normalized. Test vs. train similarity is a 10k×60k×128 product, about 0.15 TFLOP, well under a second through cblas_sgemm on AMX (measured at 2.2 TFLOP/s for 1024³). A fused per-row `topk` reduction is new in ir.py, kernel.py (an insertion network held in registers, k ≤ 16) and Metal.
- Exact duplicates: 64-bit row hashes, using new bitwise ops (xor/shl/shr/and in ir.BINARY_OPS; NEON `eor`/`ushl`) and reusing kernel.py's lowbias hashing.
- Drift and balance: per-feature KS and PSI, a kernel MMD between splits, class counts, and the cross-validated confusion matrix.
- Output: a terminal summary with `show` thumbnails, an HTML report with image grids (given vs. predicted label), and issues.json. `anvil data prune --keep 20%` ranks examples by EL2N (Paul, Ganguli & Dziugaite, NeurIPS 2021).
- **Demo:** 'Find the mislabeled MNIST digits in three seconds': `anvil data audit examples/mnist.anvil` prints the top 25 suspects as ASCII digits with given and predicted labels. They are scored for precision and recall against the 15 MNIST test errors published at labelerrors.com (Northcutt et al., NeurIPS 2021 Datasets track). Second act, CIFAR-10: reproduce Barz & Denzler's finding ('Do we train on test data?', 2020) that 3.3% of test images have near-duplicates in the training set, and show how much accuracy those duplicates inflate. Both runs are timed against cleanlab+sklearn and fastdup.
- **Builds on:** Fast compiled training, `--set` (one compilation, several folds), AMX matrix products (backend/blas.py), scatter-add, `nonzero`, `show`, and the drawing app's renderer for HTML thumbnails.
- **Risk:** Confident learning is only as good as the model's calibration: a 97% MLP also flags digits that are ambiguous but correctly labeled, so the honest metric is precision@k against the published list. CIFAR needs a decent CNN, and convolutions are Anvil's weakest kernels. topk and the bitwise ops are new IR primitives that all four backends must implement.

### The classroom data engine: draw, label and retrain live, with active learning

*wow 9/10 · weeks*

Turn bin/draw into a multiplayer classroom. Students draw letters on their phones, and each drawing becomes a typed, hashed row in a growing dataset. Every few seconds the model fine-tunes on the new drawings plus an EMNIST replay buffer. The server decides what to ask for next: the classes the model is least sure about (BALD with MC dropout, Gal et al. 2017, or BADGE, Ash et al., ICLR 2020), and which ambiguous drawings to send to other students for labels. Disagreements are resolved with Dawid–Skene (1979) EM over each student's confusion matrix, about ten lines of index notation. A new class is a new enum variant: the classifier head grows by one row initialized by weight imprinting (Qi, Brown & Lowe, CVPR 2018), and recompiling takes under a second.

- **Build:** - examples/draw/server.py: LAN mode (a QR code for phones), per-student sessions, an append-only dataset shard with schema {img: u8[28,28], label: Letter, author: Student, t} plus content hashes, and a queue of label requests.
- examples/classroom.anvil: a fine-tuning loop (load, N steps on new data plus replay, save) run by the server, with MC-dropout uncertainty from the prelude's `dropout(training=true)` at inference time.
- Hot swap in anvil.function: weights read from an input buffer instead of baked in, so a new model goes live without recompiling.
- A teacher dashboard: per-class accuracy over time, the most uncertain classes, and a 'whose drawings helped most' board (leave-one-out on small fine-tunes, or TracIn-style gradient dot products with `grad`).
- A Dynabench-style adversarial round (Kiela et al., NAACL 2021): "draw a 'g' the model reads as a '9'", with the fooling drawings added to the training data.
- **Demo:** A 30-minute class in which 25 students teach the model a script it has never seen, such as Greek letters or symbols the class invents. Accuracy on the teacher's held-out drawings climbs on the projector in real time, the model keeps asking for the letters it is least sure of, and the final model ships with a receipt that names every drawing it was trained on.
- **Builds on:** bin/draw and its MNIST/EMNIST-style renderer, the letters CNN and the text reader, anvil.function, save/load, dropout, and recompiles that take a fraction of a second.
- **Risk:** Classroom logistics: getting phones onto one network, and moderating what gets drawn. Fine-tuning on tiny, skewed data causes catastrophic forgetting without careful replay. Hot-swapping weights needs a mode in pyapi.py that doesn't bake them in.

### Moonshot: differentiable Datalog over your data, with provenance semirings

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

Extend the semiring index notation to recursive rules (Datalog with semi-naive fixpoints) and attach provenance semirings (Green, Karvounarakis & Tannen, PODS 2007). The same query can then compute booleans, counts, probabilities or differentiable top-k proofs (Scallop: Huang et al., NeurIPS 2021; Li et al., PLDI 2023), and gradients flow into the neural networks that produce the facts. Neurosymbolic learning takes a few lines: networks read the pixels, rules do the reasoning, and only the final answer needs a label. Today's index notation can almost write the core of MNIST-addition already: `p_sum[n, s] = sum p_a[n, a] * p_b[n, s - a]`.

- **Build:** - parser/elaborate: `rule` declarations over typed relations (from the relational-algebra idea), with stratified negation and aggregation. Recursion compiles to a run-time `while` over delta relations (semi-naive evaluation).
- Semirings: probabilistic add-mult, max-times, and top-k proofs (a k-best semiring stored as a sorted fixed-size vector, so shapes stay static), each with autodiff rules. Recursion is differentiated by unrolling to a fixed depth with `static for`, or by implicit differentiation at the fixpoint.
- Facts from networks: `digit(img, d) :- p = net(img)[d]` binds model outputs as probabilistic facts.
- examples/neurosym/: MNIST-addition, multi-digit sums, visual sudoku.
- **Demo:** MNIST-addition, DeepProbLog's benchmark (Manhaeve et al., NeurIPS 2018): the digit network never sees a digit label, only pairs of images and their sum, and still learns to read digits to about 98%, in seconds rather than DeepProbLog's hours. Then in bin/draw, students write "23 + 19" by hand, and the model, trained only on equation results, reads it. A visual sudoku example shows the rules succeeding where SATNet needed help to ground its symbols (Chang et al. 2020).
- **Builds on:** Index notation with `where`, the min/max/argmax reductions and their gradients, `while` loops, `static for`, the digit and letter models, and bin/draw.
- **Risk:** Autodiff through run-time loops doesn't exist, so recursive programs need unrolling or implicit differentiation. Top-k proof semirings blow up on programs with many rules. It depends on the sparse relational machinery of the relational-algebra idea, so it is the last floor of a tall building.

### Types from data: schema type providers with records, enums, Option and refinement columns

*wow 8/10 · months*

`adult = csv("adult.data")` should give a typed table, not an f32 matrix. Every column gets a dtype, categorical strings become a compile-time enum, '?' cells become `Option`, and the editor shows the schema on hover. Three mistakes become compile errors that quote the offending line of the data file: using a column that may be missing without handling the gap, forgetting an enum variant in a `match`, and loading a test file whose labels don't match the training file. This is F#'s type providers (Petricek, Guerra & Syme, PLDI 2016, 'Types from data') applied to tensors, and Anvil is halfway there already: idx, npy and csv shapes are read from the file at compile time.

- **Build:** - parser.py/ast.py: `enum Embarked = S | C | Q`, `schema Passenger:` blocks, `T?` option types, refinements (`i32 in 1..3`, `f32 >= 0`), and `match` with an exhaustiveness check.
- values.py: TableVal (ordered columns of TVals, stored structure-of-arrays like Arrow); RecordVal (one batch of rows); EnumType, whose variant names are the compile-time vocabulary, so `onehot(row.sex)` and `Embedding(Workclass.size, 8)` take k from the type; OptVal (a value buffer plus a validity mask, with `??` and `is some`).
- builtins.py: bi_csv infers a type per column (int, float, enum, bool or missing; inferred on a sample, then verified on the whole file) and `csv(path, schema=S)` checks a declared schema. `parquet()` and `arrow()` providers read only the footer through a ~300-line pure-Python Thrift compact-protocol reader. The footer gives the schema, the row count and per-column min/max/null_count statistics, which prove refinements without scanning the data.
- elaborate.py: `for row in batches(table, size=64)` yields RecordVals; field access; checks that Options are handled; match exhaustiveness.
- runtime.s/anvil_host.h: a typed CSV reader that dictionary-encodes enum columns with the compile-time vocabulary and writes validity masks; Parquet PLAIN and RLE/bit-packed dictionary pages; a small snappy decoder.
- ide.py + editors/vscode: hover shows the inferred schema with null counts and vocabularies; inlay hints such as `row.age: f32[64]?`.
- interp.py reference semantics; tests on Titanic, Adult and Iris.
- **Demo:** UCI Adult Census with `train = csv("adult.data")` and `test = csv("adult.test")`. Anvil infers `workclass: Workclass?` (8 variants, 1,836 cells missing as '?') and trims the leading spaces in every field. It then refuses to compile, because adult.test's first line is `|1x3 Cross validator` and its labels are `<=50K.` and `>50K.` (the dataset's well-known trailing period): "error: column `income` in adult.test has variants `<=50K.`, `>50K.` that adult.data does not". After the fix, a 25-line typed model replaces about 60 lines of pandas plus sklearn ColumnTransformer preprocessing.
- **Builds on:** Compile-time file reading in builtins.py: bi_csv already scans the whole file for its shape and checks that fields are numbers, and the idx/npy headers are parsed at compile time.
