Also builds on strings indexed at run time (for printing enum values) and the IDE recorder (Elaborator.recorder, `anvil check --json`).
- **Risk:** Static shapes don't fit row filtering: a `where` on rows changes the row count, so filtering needs masks or a compile-time count. Parquet files need snappy or zstd, and Apple's libcompression provides neither. Inference rules have to be predictable (is a column of "1"/"0" bool or int?). Compiling against GB-sized CSVs is slow without the content-hash cache and the fast parser from the SIMD-parser idea.

### Tokenizers written in Anvil: BPE as scatter-add + argmax, raced against tiktoken

*wow 8/10 · months*

Training byte-pair encoding is a loop over three tensor operations Anvil already has: count adjacent pairs (a scatter-add, `counts[t[i] * V + t[i+1]] += 1`), take the argmax, and merge that pair (a compaction with `nonzero`). The plan is to write BPE training and byte-level encoding in Anvil, compile the merge table into a perfect-hash lookup at compile time, show the output is token-for-token identical to tiktoken (r50k, cl100k, o200k) and Hugging Face tokenizers on a gigabyte of FineWeb, and race them. Tokens get types: `Tokens<cl100k>` cannot be fed to an embedding trained on GPT-2's vocabulary, and the vocabulary size flows from the tokenizer file into `Embedding(vocab.size, d)`.

- **Build:** - New anvil/text.anvil: `bpe_train(bytes, merges=K)` in index notation. Pair counts are dense while V² fits; beyond that, pairs are counted by sorting packed i32 keys, using a new `sort`/`argsort` primitive (radix sort in runtime.s, bitonic sort on Metal).
- builtins.py: a `tokenizer("cl100k_base.tiktoken")` / `tokenizer("tokenizer.json")` type provider that reads the ranks at compile time, builds a CHD perfect hash from byte pair to rank as const data, and returns a TokenizerVal whose vocabulary is part of the type.
- Pre-tokenization: the GPT-2, cl100k and o200k regexes compiled at compile time into a DFA over UTF-8 bytes, including the one-character lookahead that `\s+(?!\S)` needs. The DFA is emitted as a table-driven loop with NEON `tbl` for byte classes; the \p{L}/\p{N} tables are generated from Python's unicodedata.
- Encoding: tiktoken's lowest-rank-first merge within each piece, with pieces spread over the thread pool. The existing `decode` extends from bytes to tokens.
- Bitwise ops in ir.py for hashing. Variable-length outputs as (values, count) with a static upper bound (tokens ≤ bytes).
- Differential tests against tiktoken and tokenizers on FineWeb, Wikipedia, code and adversarial Unicode (emoji ZWJ sequences, CJK, mixed scripts).
- **Demo:** In class: run `bpe_train` on the README and print each merge as it is learned ("t h → th", "th e → the", " the", "ing"), then train the GPT example on the output of its own tokenizer. For engineers: a MB/s table of Anvil vs. tiktoken (Rust) vs. HF tokenizers (Rust) on 1 GB of FineWeb-Edu, single-threaded and at 12 threads, next to the differential test's mismatch count, which must be 0.
- **Builds on:** Scatter-add in index notation, `nonzero` compaction, `bytes()`/`decode()`, the char-RNN and transformer examples, and compile-time file reading.
- **Risk:** Exact tiktoken parity has a long tail: Unicode category tables, the regex's possessive and lookahead behaviour, special tokens. Applying merges is data-dependent and branchy, so beating Rust may come from careful scalar code rather than NEON. Variable-length outputs clash with static shapes.

### An augmentation algebra: fused, replayable and learnable

*wow 8/10 · months*

Augmentations compose like functions, so they can be typed combinators the compiler reasons about. Geometric ops are coordinate maps, and their affine parts multiply into one matrix at compile time: crop ∘ flip ∘ rotate ∘ scale becomes a single resample. Photometric ops fuse into that resample, and everything runs inside the gather that builds each batch (the EMNIST trainer already shifts and transposes in one gather). Even RandAugment's random choice of operation becomes a per-example parameter vector fed to one kernel with no branches. With the counter-based RNG, every augmented view is a pure function of (seed, epoch, example), so any image the network saw can be replayed exactly. And because the warp is differentiable, the augmentation ranges themselves can be learned (Augerino, Benton et al., NeurIPS 2020).

- **Build:** - prelude.anvil: `augment` combinators (pad, random_crop, flip, rotate, scale, shear, translate, elastic (Simard et al. 2003), color_jitter, solarize, posterize, cutout) composed with `>>`. mixup and CutMix change the label type from `i32[n]` to soft labels `f32[n, k]`, so `cross_entropy` picks its soft-label form from the type rather than by accident.
- elaborate.py: affine coordinate maps composed at compile time; bilinear or nearest resampling emitted as a gather with floor and lerp (the IR has floor and computed gathers); per-example parameter tensors drawn from the counter RNG keyed by (epoch, example index) rather than by draw order.
- autodiff.py: gradients through bilinear sampling with respect to the warp parameters (scatter-add gradients already exist), for Augerino-style learned invariance, plus its regularizer that rewards wide ranges.
- cli.py: `anvil replay file.anvil --epoch 3 --step 17 --example 5` renders exactly what the network saw, with `show` or as a PNG.
- Benchmarks: images/s against torchvision.transforms.v2 RandAugment (PIL and tensor backends) and Kornia.
- **Demo:** Rotated-MNIST with Augerino: the network learns its own rotation range, plotted live. The range grows toward ±180° when the digits are randomly rotated and stays near 0° on upright digits, where 6 and 9 must stay distinct. Second: a CIFAR-10 pipeline with RandAugment and mixup that adds a few percent to step time, compared with torchvision on CPU, where augmentation is usually the bottleneck.
- **Builds on:** The gather-based shift and transpose in examples/letters.anvil and digits.anvil, the counter-based RNG (rand_key/lowbias in kernel.py), scatter-add gradients, and `show`.
- **Risk:** Per-example random gathers defeat the register-tiled schedules, so the fused kernel has to be fast enough not to become the bottleneck itself. Equalize and AutoContrast need a histogram per image (a two-pass reduction). Learned augmentation can collapse without the right regularizer.

### Decoding inside the graph: JPEG, PNG, WAV and mel spectrograms on NEON, Metal and CUDA

*wow 8/10 · months*

Train from raw files, with decoding as just more kernels. JPEG's 8×8 inverse DCT is two small matrix products (`pix[b,x,y] = sum C[u,x] * C[v,y] * Q[u,v] * F[b,u,v]`). Dequantization, chroma upsampling, YCbCr→RGB, resizing and normalization fuse into one kernel that feeds the first convolution. Huffman decoding runs on CPU threads, one image per thread, the same split nvJPEG and DALI use. A WAV file is a header the type provider reads at compile time, and an STFT is a strided view times a DFT matrix. The sample rate is part of the type, so mixing 16 kHz and 44.1 kHz audio is a compile error.

- **Build:** - builtins.py type providers: `images("imagenette/train/*/*.JPEG", size=[160, 160])` reads every SOF marker at compile time (dimensions, subsampling, baseline or progressive) and turns folder names into a class enum. `wav("speech_commands/**/*.wav")` reads the RIFF headers (rate, channels, length) and gives `f32[n, 16000] @16kHz`.
- Runtime (a new C file next to runtime.s, or assembly): baseline JPEG Huffman decoding with 9-bit lookup tables in the style of libjpeg-turbo, parallel over images on the thread pool. PNG through the zlib the runtime already links for gzread, plus row unfiltering (Paeth is serial within a row and vectorized across channels).
- Prelude: `idct8x8`, `ycbcr_to_rgb`, `resize` (separable filters as matmuls), `stft` (a strided view times a DFT matrix on AMX), `mel` (a filterbank matmul), `log_mel`.
- metal.py/cuda.py: the dense stages run on the GPU. This would be the CUDA backend's first workload on real hardware, measured against nvJPEG/DALI.
- Option: train directly on DCT coefficients and skip the IDCT (Gueguen et al., NeurIPS 2018, 'Faster Neural Networks Straight from JPEG').
- **Demo:** Keyword spotting on Google Speech Commands v2 (105,829 one-second WAVs, 35 words), trained from the raw files with log-mel features computed in the graph. `anvil export` turns it into a C library behind a live microphone page in the style of bin/draw: say "yes", "no", "up", "down". Second: Imagenette from raw JPEGs, with images/s compared against PIL-SIMD + torchvision and Apple's ImageIO.
- **Builds on:** Compile-time header reading, the zlib already linked by runtime.s (gzread), matrix products on AMX/MPS, `anvil export`, and the drawing app's server pattern.
- **Risk:** Huffman decoding is serial and branchy; decoding it on the GPU via self-synchronization (Weißenberger & Schmidt, ICPP 2018) is research-grade. Progressive JPEGs and unusual subsampling multiply the code paths. Static shapes force a fixed resize at load time. The speedup only matters where decoding is the bottleneck, which it isn't on small datasets, and the benchmark has to say so.

### Relational algebra is index notation: semirings, typed index domains and worst-case-optimal joins

*wow 8/10 · months*

`sum` over a shared index is a join, and a reduction over a key is a GROUP BY. Changing the semiring turns the same line into shortest paths (min, +), Viterbi (max, ×), log-space probabilities (logsumexp, +) or existence (or, and); this is the FAQ framework (Abo Khamis, Ngo & Rudra, PODS 2016). The idea adds sparse relations stored as sorted tries and compiles multiway joins with Generic Join / Leapfrog Triejoin (Veldhuizen 2014) into co-iteration loops (TACO's merge lattices, Kjolstad et al. 2017) with NEON set intersection. Index domains get types, as in Dex's index sets (`f32[User, Item]`), so swapping two axes is a type error. Autodiff then gives forward-backward and inside-outside for free (Eisner 2016: they are just backprop).

- **Build:** - Prelude/parser: user-definable `semiring` blocks (zero, one, add, mul), declared like `optimizer` blocks. Before the compiler reorders or parallelizes a reduction, it checks the semiring laws (associativity, distributivity, identities) on random values at compile time, QuickCheck-style.
- ir.py/kernel.py: generalized reductions on every backend, including logsumexp as a stable online reduction and user semirings. autodiff.py gets a gradient rule per semiring (max-plus argmax routing already exists).
- Sparse relations: `relation Follows(src: User, dst: User) = csv(...)` stored as CSR/trie buffers with degree statistics known at compile time. ir.py gets a co-iteration loop form (intersect sorted lists), implemented with galloping search and NEON merge intersection (Schlegel et al. 2011; Lemire et al. 2016). The variable order comes from the AGM bound computed on those statistics.
- Typed index domains: enum and dictionary-encoded key columns become index types; index variables carry their domain, and unification rejects `ratings[item, user]`.
- Shared primitives: `sort`, `unique`, segmented reductions.
- **Demo:** A lecture called 'SQL is einsum': the Titanic survival-rate GROUP BY, a three-table join, attention and Floyd–Warshall (min-plus), all written with the same `sum`. For engineers: triangle counting on SNAP's soc-LiveJournal1 (69M edges). Pairwise hash joins such as DuckDB's build a huge intermediate there, while a worst-case-optimal join is bounded by O(|E|^1.5); timed against DuckDB and Kùzu.
- **Builds on:** Index notation with `where`, the min/max/argmax reductions, scatter-add, gathers through data, and the shape-unification machinery (for typing index domains).
- **Risk:** Sparse co-iteration is a new kind of code generation alongside the dense register-tiled schedules. Umbra and Kùzu already implement worst-case-optimal joins well, so the honest claim is 'competitive, and also differentiable'. Random testing of semiring laws is evidence, not proof, and floating point isn't exactly associative anyway.

### Data as pure functions: typed synthetic generators and differentiable simulators

*wow 8/10 · months*

A dataset doesn't have to be a file. It can be a pure function from (seed, index) to a typed example, written in Anvil and fused into the training step: infinite, never stored, and exactly replayable. Because it is Anvil code, `grad` flows through it, so the simulator's own parameters can be trained without REINFORCE (the goal of Learning to Simulate, Ruiz et al., ICLR 2019). Stroke width, slant, jitter and noise shift until the synthetic data looks real to the network.

- **Build:** - Language: `source` declarations such as `source digits(i: i32) -> Example`, with the RNG keyed by i (kernel.py's counter-based keys), consumed by `batches(source, n=…)`. An Example is a record type from the schema idea.
- Prelude: a differentiable soft rasterizer for strokes (pixel = sigmoid((r − distance to a Bézier curve) / τ)), elastic distortions, physics sources (bouncing balls, pendulum, cart-pole), and algorithmic tasks for the transformer (modular arithmetic for grokking (Power et al. 2022), sorting, copying, addition with carries).
- Autodiff: gradients of an MMD or feature-statistics loss between synthetic and real batches with respect to the generator's `param`s, using a frozen feature network; `static for` unrolling for short simulations.
- Tests: example i is identical across thread counts and backends.
- **Demo:** 'Zero real images': a digit classifier trained only on Bézier digits drawn by Anvil from ten hand-placed 'font' templates, then scored on real MNIST. Next, the generator's parameters are trained against 100 unlabeled real digits, and the class watches the synthetic digits drift toward handwriting while accuracy on the real test set climbs. Bonus: grokking modular addition, where the whole dataset is a two-line pure function.
- **Builds on:** The counter-based RNG, reparameterized sampling (`~ normal(mu, sigma)`), gradients of anything (the dream example's input gradients), `static for`, and `show`.
- **Risk:** Differentiable rasterization through a min-distance is noisy, and the MMD objective can be gamed. How well synthetic-only training transfers is an empirical question, so the demo's accuracy can't be promised in advance. Long simulations need autodiff through run-time loops, which Anvil doesn't have.

### Schema-specialized SIMD parsers: CSV and JSONL at gigabytes per second

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Because the schema is known at compile time, Anvil can generate a parser for this specific file in the style of simdjson (Langdale & Lemire, VLDB Journal 2019). It classifies 64 bytes at a time with NEON `cmeq`/`tbl`, finds quoted regions with a carry-less multiply (`pmull` prefix-XOR), walks field boundaries with `rbit`/`clz`, and parses numbers with the Eisel–Lemire algorithm (Lemire, 'Number parsing at a gigabyte per second', 2021). Each column gets its own parser: integer columns never enter the float path, and enum columns hash straight into their compile-time vocabulary. The current csv() reads the whole file twice, once of them in Python at compile time.

- **Build:** - New anvil/backend/parse.py: a parser generator that emits AArch64 for a schema. Classification stage: `cmeq` against `,` `\n` `"`, with a movemask built from `shrn` narrowing. Quote masks via `pmull` (vmull_p64). Per-column field parsers: fast_float-style for f32, SWAR digit parsing for ints, and a compile-time perfect hash for enum strings. Validity masks for empty cells.
- Threads: split the file at newlines across the existing thread pool in runtime.s, guess the quote state for each chunk and stitch the chunks afterwards (Ge et al., SIGMOD 2019, 'Speculative distributed CSV data parsing').
- builtins.py: replace the full Python scan with schema inference on a sample plus a verification run of the native parser, cached by content hash so the second compile is instant.
- JSONL: the same classification stage drives a field extractor for the schema's keys (Hugging Face datasets dumps).
- benchmarks/data/: a parsing benchmark against pandas (C engine), pyarrow.csv, Polars and DuckDB's read_csv.
- **Demo:** HIGGS (UCI: 11 million rows × 29 numeric columns, about 8 GB of CSV). `higgs = csv("HIGGS.csv", schema=Higgs)` is meant to parse in a few seconds on 12 cores, typed and validated, and to beat or match Polars and DuckDB. The same program then trains the standard HIGGS MLP. One README table shows GB/s for each tool.
- **Builds on:** bi_csv's compile-time scan, the assembly runtime's _anvil_rt_load_csv and its thread pool, and the schema type provider for column types.
- **Risk:** Polars, DuckDB and simdcsv are heavily tuned, so on numeric files 'beats' may turn out to be 'matches'. RFC 4180 edge cases (CRLF, escaped quotes, newlines inside quotes) are where speed tricks break. Eisel–Lemire needs a 128-bit multiply table and must round exactly like strtof.

### Streaming corpora: memory-mapped shards, an E-core loader and an honest benchmark

*wow 7/10 · months*

Everything Anvil trains today fits in RAM. This idea adds a streaming source for corpora that don't: memory-mapped token shards (the llm.c/nanoGPT `.bin` format), WebDataset tars and Parquet row groups. They are decoded on the M3 Max's efficiency cores into a fixed ring of batch slots in the static arena, so the performance cores and the GPU never wait for data. A global shuffle over 10 billion tokens needs O(1) memory: a Feistel-network permutation of the index, a pure function of (seed, epoch, i), the same trick as the counter-based RNG. Then publish the benchmark that is rarely reported honestly: loader throughput and the share of step time spent waiting for data.

- **Build:** - builtins.py: `stream("fineweb_edu/*.bin", seq=1024)` and `stream("shards/*.tar", schema=S)`. The type provider reads every shard header at compile time (llm.c's magic number, version and token count), so the epoch length and shapes are static; `batches` consumes the resulting StreamVal.
- runtime.s: mmap with madvise(MADV_SEQUENTIAL/WILLNEED); producer threads at QOS_CLASS_UTILITY/BACKGROUND (pthread_set_qos_class_self_np) so macOS schedules them on E-cores; a single-producer/single-consumer ring using ldar/stlr acquire-release; a Feistel permutation kernel; prefetching of the next shard.
- elaborate.py: `for x in batches(stream)` makes the batch tensor a ring slot whose address rotates each step, using the ir.indirect pointer slots that already exist for large tensors and caller-owned arrays.
- metal.py: ring slots in storageModeShared MTLBuffers, so the GPU reads what the E-cores wrote without a copy.
- cost.py: `anvil cost` reports bytes per step from the data source and predicts whether a program will be input-bound.
- benchmarks/data/: tokens/s and stall % against PyTorch DataLoader (num_workers 0–8), WebDataset, Mosaic StreamingDataset and tf.data on the same files.
- **Demo:** Train the GPT example on FineWeb-Edu sample-10BT straight from the SSD, with a live status line under the loss: "loader 1.9 GB/s on 4 E-cores · stall 0.0% · P-cores 100%" (illustrative numbers). Then run the same thing with a PyTorch DataLoader, whose workers compete with the training threads for the same cores.
- **Builds on:** batches and shuffling in elaborate.for_batches, ir.indirect pointer slots, the assembly thread pool, Metal's shared memory, and anvil cost. The lazy streams and generators named in the brief are not in the code at HEAD 9fb5c57, so this would be the first file-backed stream.
- **Risk:** On macOS, E-core placement is a hint, not a guarantee. The static arena and static shapes make variable-length documents awkward; packing text into fixed-length sequences avoids the problem. Whether training is disk-bound or compute-bound depends heavily on the model, so the benchmark has to cover both cases.

### Data receipts: content hashes in the type, bit-exact reproduction

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

Anvil is already bit-identical across thread counts and runs, so it can promise what no framework does: this checkpoint came from exactly these bytes. Every loader hashes its file at compile time (cached by path, size and mtime), and the hash becomes part of the tensor's type (`f32[60000, 784] @ sha256:440f…`). Lineage flows through the program, and `save` and `export` write a receipt with the program hash, compiler revision, seed, backend and data hashes. If the source pins a hash, a changed file is a compile error. `anvil reproduce net.weights` re-runs the program and checks the weights bit for bit.

- **Build:** - builtins.py: hashing in every loader (hashlib at compile time, cached in ~/.cache/anvil/hashes by inode, size and mtime); pinning with `idx(path, sha256="…")`; `Dataset<sha256:…>` shown in errors and on hover.
- runtime.s: optional verification at run time with the ARMv8 SHA-256 instructions (sha256h, sha256h2, sha256su0/su1), fast enough to check every load.
- values.py/ir.py: Buffer.provenance (hashes, path, rows, schema) and a lineage pass shared with the leakage checker (anvil/flow.py).
- export.py and the checkpoint routines: receipts next to `.weights` files and in exported C headers (`#define DIGITS_DATA_SHA256 …`).
- cli.py: `anvil reproduce`, `anvil receipt show|diff`, `anvil data hash`.
- ide.py: provenance on hover, and a warning when two loaded files have the same hash (an accidental train = test).
- **Demo:** Two students train 'the same' MNIST model and get different accuracies. `anvil receipt diff a.weights b.weights` shows that one of them used a relabeled train-labels file (the cleaned output of `anvil data fix`). Then `anvil reproduce digits.weights` on a second Mac prints "bit-identical: 101,770 parameters, sha256 match".
- **Builds on:** Compile-time loader headers, determinism across thread counts (including the PYTHONHASHSEED fixes), save/load checkpoints, and anvil export.
- **Risk:** Bit-exact reproduction holds only per backend and per Accelerate version (AMX sgemm results may change with a macOS update), so receipts must record the BLAS and OS versions and state exactly what is promised. Hashing multi-GB data on every compile depends on a cache that can be trusted.

### Anvil inside the database: Arrow zero-copy and a C++ DuckDB extension

*wow 7/10 · months*

Meet data where it lives. `anvil.function` accepts Arrow record batches through the Arrow C Data Interface, with zero copies and the Arrow schema checked against the Anvil `schema` before the call. `anvil export --duckdb` wraps a compiled model in a generated C++ DuckDB extension: a vectorized scalar function for inference over DuckDB's 2,048-row chunks, and an aggregate that trains a model from a SQL query. The schema is the contract between C++ producers and Anvil trainers: `anvil schema --cpp Passenger` emits a C++ struct and an Arrow builder, so a C++ game or robot logger writes exactly the rows the trainer accepts.

- **Build:** - pyapi.py: accept any object with `__arrow_c_array__` or `__arrow_c_stream__` (PyArrow, Polars, pandas with Arrow dtypes, DuckDB results). Validity bitmaps become Option masks, dictionary arrays become enums, and fixed-size lists become tensor columns; arguments still go through pointer slots as today.
- export.py: a C++ template on DuckDB's C++ extension API around the exported C library. A ScalarFunction checks the SQL types against the Anvil schema at bind time. An AggregateFunction whose state is the training loop is fed one chunk at a time, which needs a resumable 'step' entry point in the export; it returns the weights as a BLOB.
- `anvil schema --cpp/--rust`: generates structs and Arrow writers from schema declarations.
- Tests with DuckDB and Polars.
- **Demo:** In DuckDB, `SELECT label, anvil_predict_digits(pixels) AS guess FROM read_parquet('mnist_test.parquet') WHERE guess <> label;` lists every misread digit at millions of rows per second. Then `SELECT anvil_train('mnist.anvil', pixels, label) FROM read_parquet('mnist_train.parquet')` trains inside the database. A C++ logger compiled against the generated header feeds a model trained the same afternoon.
- **Builds on:** anvil export (a C library with baked weights), anvil.function's zero-copy pointer slots, and the schema type provider.
- **Risk:** DuckDB's C++ extension API changes between releases, and its stable C API covers less. Training as an aggregate breaks DuckDB's assumption that aggregate states can be computed in parallel and combined: combining would mean model averaging, or the aggregate must be forced single-threaded. Arrow's variable-width types still need a ragged-type design.

### The compiler reads the data: narrow storage, compile-time evaluation of data-only code, data-directed kernels

*wow 6/10 · weeks*

Every loader today widens data to 4-byte f32/i32 in a scalar assembly loop. The EMNIST trainer therefore holds 2.2 GB of floats for 550 MB of bytes. The fix is to make the data's real types first-class: u8, i8, u16, f16 and bf16 for storage, computed in f32 registers. The decode step (`/ 255`, `- mean`) fuses into the first kernel that reads a batch. Code that depends only on data files (normalization statistics, vocabularies, class weights) runs at build time, and its cached result is baked into the binary. Column types choose kernels: `onehot(c) @ W` with c an enum becomes a row gather.

- **Build:** - ir.py: an item size per dtype (Buffer.nbytes is currently hard-coded to 4*numel), plus storage dtypes U8, I8, U16, F16 and BF16. parser.py stops rejecting `u8`, `f16` and `bf16`.
- backend/kernel.py: widening loads (`ldr q` + `uxtl/uxtl2` + `ucvtf` for u8, `fcvtl/fcvtl2` for f16, `shll #16` for bf16) and narrowing stores. arena.py aligns by item size. metal.py and cuda.py use `uchar4`/`half4` loads.
- runtime.s: the idx, npy and bytes loaders stop widening; u8 stays u8 and npy keeps its dtype.
- optimize.py: fold the decode into the consumer's load, including the shuffled-batch copy in elaborate.for_batches, so the gather moves bytes instead of floats.
- elaborate.py: compile-time evaluation of data-only code. An expression whose inputs are only loaded files and constants runs on the interpreter at compile time (as `anvil export` already does), is cached in ~/.cache/anvil by content hash, and becomes a const buffer.
- Type-directed rewrites: onehot(enum) @ W becomes a gather; a warning such as "column `x` is constant on all 60,000 rows"; integer columns in 0..255 stored as u8 automatically.
- **Demo:** `anvil build examples/letters.anvil` before and after: data memory drops from 2.19 GB to 0.55 GB, the shuffled-batch copy moves a quarter of the bytes, and the epoch time is measured. Second: a Criteo-style CTR model on the Kaggle Display Advertising data (26 categorical columns), where the compiler turns one-hot matmuls into embedding gathers and `anvil cost` shows FLOPs per step falling by orders of magnitude.
- **Builds on:** Compile-time idx/npy headers, the static arena (backend/arena.py), elementwise inlining in optimize.py, the way `anvil export` runs statements on the interpreter at export time, and anvil cost.
- **Risk:** Every backend and every schedule in kernel.py assumes 4-byte elements somewhere (offsets in elements vs. bytes, vector lane counts). f16 arithmetic, as opposed to f16 storage, is a separate and larger project. Evaluating large data-only expressions at compile time slows compiles unless the cache works.

## realtime-robotics-and-control (16)

### Learning to fly in seconds: a GPU-trained Crazyflie policy with RAM and cycle budgets checked at compile time, from one Anvil file

*wow 10/10 · moonshot*

One file holds a quadrotor simulator, domain randomization, an off-policy RL trainer and the policy. `anvil run --cuda` trains it with each environment's whole rollout fused into a single megakernel (one thread per world, state in registers, millions of steps per second). `anvil flash --target crazyflie` then compiles just the policy for the Crazyflie 2.1's STM32F405 (168 MHz, 192 KB RAM), with its RAM and cycles proven to fit the 1 kHz stabilizer tick. The bar to beat is RLtools' "Learning to Fly in Seconds" (Eschmann et al., about 18 s of training on a laptop), and this would be the CUDA backend's first run on a real NVIDIA GPU.

- **Build:** cuda.py: a persistent-rollout schedule. A new transformation in optimize.py fuses the host-side `for t in range(T)` loop around small per-environment kernels into one device loop with environment state in registers or shared memory. This is valid because shapes are static and the per-environment body has no cross-environment dependency. Add shared-memory tiles for the policy MLP (the counter-based RNG is already shared with the CPU), and validate every example on a real RTX GPU for the first time. A Cortex-M4F target via the portable C backend (cuda.py's emulation output as plain C) or a Thumb-2 emitter plugs into the Crazyflie firmware's app API, behind `anvil flash`. Pre-flight check: 10^8 randomized rollouts on the GPU produce a Clopper-Pearson bound on crash probability before anything is flashed.
- **Demo:** On stage: `anvil run quad.anvil --cuda` trains in seconds on a cloud GPU and prints "policy: 4.1 KB RAM, <= 62 us of the 1,000 us stabilizer tick, crash rate < 1e-6 (95% CI, 10^8 rollouts)". Then `anvil flash`, and a $250 Crazyflie takes off on the learned policy, hovers, and recovers when thrown.
- **Builds on:** the never-run CUDA backend, the compiled-gym idea, export.py, and the WCET and real-time ideas
- **Risk:** Quadrotor sim-to-real needs carefully identified motor, thrust and delay models; that is where RLtools spent most of its effort. The CUDA backend has never run on a GPU, and a drone crash in class is very visible.

### `deadline` as a checked property: static WCET from the emitted machine code

*wow 9/10 · months*

Every function can carry a cycle bound that the compiler proves, for example `fn attitude(s: [13]) -> [4] deadline 250us on cortex-m7@480MHz`. Tools like aiT and OTAWA have to recover loop bounds and aliasing from C binaries after the fact. Anvil already knows every loop trip count, every buffer address (the static arena) and the exact instruction stream it emitted. A missed deadline becomes a compile error that points at the line using up the budget, and the editor shows a bound such as "<= 212 us" next to every function.

- **Build:** New anvil/wcet.py walks the machine IR after register allocation (backend/mir.py Seq/LoopMark/Ins). It costs each basic block with a per-target timing model and multiplies by the static trip counts. The timing models go in anvil/backend/timing/{cortex_m7,cortex_m55,cortex_a53,apple_p}.py: issue width and latency/throughput tables (Arm's Cortex-M7 cycle timings, Dougall Johnson's Firestorm tables for Apple). Memory model: fixed latency for TCM on MCUs. Cached cores get a Ferdinand/Wilhelm must/may LRU cache analysis, which is tractable because arena.py gives every load a known address range. Fork/join costs of the thread pool are bounded from runtime.s. Parser/ast.FnDecl get a `deadline <dur> [on <target>]` clause, and the elaborator records it. `anvil wcet file.anvil` in cli.py prints a per-line cycle table in the style of `anvil cost`. ide.py adds the bound to hover and inlay JSON. `--measure` runs each kernel N times under PMU cycle counters (DWT->CYCCNT on Cortex-M, kperf on M-series) and reports how tight the bound is.
- **Demo:** Live in VS Code, widen the hidden layer of a quadrotor attitude policy. At width 192 the `deadline 1ms` squiggle turns red: "line 14: Linear(192,192) is 71% of 1,000 us; bound 1,140 us". Flash the 160-wide version to a Cortex-M7 board. A scope traces a GPIO toggled at the start and end of the loop: measured 801 us against a proven 846 us, projected next to the compiler output.
- **Builds on:** anvil/cost.py (static FLOPs and bytes per line), backend/mir.py, backend/arena.py, the VS Code extension's inlay hints, and the existing TinyML/MCU backend idea for an in-order target
- **Risk:** Apple's out-of-order cores have no published timing model, so on the Mac the bound is a calibrated estimate, not a proof. A sound WCET is realistic only on in-order or cache-less targets (Cortex-M/R, A53), and those need a backend Anvil does not have yet.

### Differentiable MPC: a fixed-iteration QP solver with a compile-time timing bound that you can backprop through

*wow 9/10 · months*

An `mpc` block compiles a model-predictive controller into an OSQP-style ADMM solver with a fixed iteration count, so its cost per tick is a compile-time number. The constant KKT factorization is computed at compile time and baked into the binary, the way CVXGEN and acados generate code. Unlike them it is differentiable, either unrolled or implicitly through the KKT conditions (OptNet, Amos & Kolter 2017; differentiable MPC, Amos et al. 2018). So `minimize closed_loop_cost over (Q, R, residual_dynamics)` tunes the controller by gradient through a 500-step closed-loop rollout.

- **Build:** Language: `mpc Name(horizon=H):` with `state x: [nx]`, `control u: [nu]`, `dynamics`, `cost` and `subject to` clauses. The elaborator desugars it into a QP with static sparsity. New anvil/qp.anvil: ADMM with OSQP's updates and a fixed rho. The LDL^T factorization of constant KKT matrices is evaluated by the staging interpreter at compile time (the same path export.py uses to bake weights) and emitted as constants. autodiff.py gets an `implicit`/`custom_vjp` hook that differentiates the solution of an equation by solving the adjoint KKT system instead of unrolling, reusable later for deep equilibrium models. Linear MPC comes first, then SQP-style nonlinear MPC with dynamics linearized by `jacobian`. cost.py and wcet.py report solver FLOPs and cycles per tick.
- **Demo:** A cart-pole swing-up, in 3D in the browser or on a physical rig. An MPC with hand-picked weights wobbles. Then 60 s of `minimize` through the closed loop learns Q, R and a residual dynamics MLP, and the tuned controller runs at 10 kHz with a printed bound: "40 ADMM iterations, 18,400 FLOPs, <= 31 us". acados (no gradients) and cvxpylayers (gradients, far slower per solve) run beside it on the same task.
- **Builds on:** elaborate.py's compile-time staging, autodiff.py, minimize and optimizer blocks, `static for`, and the control.anvil linear algebra idea
- **Risk:** Fixed-iteration ADMM may not reach tolerance on hard or saturated instances, so the timing guarantee trades against solution accuracy. Implicit gradients are also wrong at an unconverged point.

### Featherstone in index notation: differentiable rigid-body dynamics from URDF

*wow 9/10 · months*

`robot = urdf("panda.urdf")` is read at compile time the way `idx()` reads data, so the kinematic tree is a constant. RNEA, CRBA and the articulated-body algorithm then unroll over the bodies into straight-line spatial-algebra code. That is the code-generation trick behind Pinocchio's single-digit-microsecond dynamics, but the gradients come from Anvil's autodiff instead of hand derivations (Carpentier & Mansard 2018). With soft contact (MuJoCo-style convex compliant contact) it becomes a differentiable simulator, batched across thousands of worlds on Metal and CUDA.

- **Build:** builtins.py: compile-time loaders `urdf(path)` and `mjcf(path)` that produce parent arrays, joint types, inertias and joint transforms as constants (like the npy header parsing). New anvil/rigid.anvil: 6D spatial vectors and Plücker transforms in index notation, plus `fk`, `rnea`, `crba`, `aba`, contact Jacobians, and semi-implicit Euler and RK4 integrators, all as `static for` over the body tree. Frames use the Lie-group types idea. A leading world dimension lets `world[b]` run 4,096 environments per kernel on the Metal and CUDA backends. benchmarks/rigid/ compares against Pinocchio (C++), RBDL, MuJoCo/MJX and Brax on the same machine.
- **Demo:** A 7-DoF Franka Panda in a three.js page swings to hit a target. Gradient-based trajectory optimization through 500 simulated steps converges in about a second. A sidebar reads "ABA: 1,946 FLOPs, 0 branches, 0 allocations, <= 1.4 us; 4,096 worlds on Metal: 3M steps/s", next to Pinocchio's and MuJoCo's timings on the same machine.
- **Builds on:** index notation, `static for`, autodiff.py, the Metal and CUDA backends, compile-time loaders in builtins.py, and the Lie-group types idea
- **Risk:** Contact is where differentiable physics gets hard: gradients through stiff or discontinuous contact are useless or explode. Long horizons as `static for` also hit the slow-compile weakness, so this depends on the `scan` idea.

### Synchronous multi-rate control: `at 1 kHz` blocks, clock types and a proven schedule

*wow 9/10 · months*

Make Anvil a synchronous dataflow language for robots, in the tradition of Lustre/SCADE (used in Airbus fly-by-wire software) and Esterel: `at 1 kHz: x = ekf.update(imu)`, `at 100 Hz: u_ref = mpc(x)`, `at 10 Hz: goal = planner(camera)`. Signals carry clock types, so a 100 Hz value used in a 1 kHz block must be explicitly held or interpolated. The compiler takes WCETs from the `deadline` idea, proves the task set schedulable with rate-monotonic response-time analysis (Liu & Layland 1973; Joseph & Pandya 1986), and generates a static cyclic executive with wait-free double buffers in the arena between rates.

- **Build:** parser.py/ast.py: `at <rate>:` blocks and the stream operators `pre`, `fby`, `hold` and `sample`. New anvil/clocks.py: a clock calculus (Lustre-style clock inference) that rejects rate mismatches with a suggested fix. New anvil/schedule.py: per-core response-time analysis from wcet.py bounds plus the thread pool's fork/join costs, producing either a frame table (hyperperiod and minor frames) or fixed-priority task assignments. Backends: a generated cyclic executive in runtime.s (driven by mach_wait_until on macOS, a timer ISR on MCUs) and in export.py's C output. `anvil schedule file.anvil` draws the hyperperiod as a Gantt chart in the terminal.
- **Demo:** A simulated quadrotor with a 1 kHz attitude loop, 200 Hz position MPC and a 20 Hz perception net. `anvil schedule` prints the Gantt chart with slack per task. A student enlarges the perception net and gets a compile error: "task `perception` (20 Hz) misses its deadline: response time 61 ms > 50 ms; move it to core 2 or shrink line 31". After the fix, the live sim shows the attitude loop's timing unaffected by the slow task.
- **Builds on:** the `deadline` WCET idea, arena.py (double buffers are just more arena buffers), the runtime.s thread pool, control-flow lowering in elaborate.py
- **Risk:** A schedulability proof is only as sound as the WCETs behind it, which on the Mac are estimates. Synchronous-language semantics (clock calculus, causality analysis) is a deep design space that could take over the project.

### Certified learned controllers: interval types, abstract interpretation of the IR, provable regions of attraction

*wow 9/10 · months*

Run the same kernel IR on boxes and zonotopes instead of numbers, as a non-standard interpreter next to interp.py. That gives refinement types like `obs: f32[4] in [-1, 1]` and compile-time `verify forall` statements. With CROWN-style linear bounds (Zhang et al. 2018; α,β-CROWN has won VNN-COMP) plus branch-and-bound, Anvil can certify that a neural Lyapunov function decreases everywhere in a region (Chang, Roohi & Gao 2019) and that N-step reachable sets avoid an unsafe set. A Simplex-style shield hands control to a verified LQR outside the certified region, and the same ranges prove that int8/Q15 fixed-point code cannot overflow on MCUs without an FPU.

- **Build:** New anvil/absint.py: interval, zonotope and CROWN linear-relaxation domains over ir.Kernel expressions (Binary/Unary/Select/Acc/Gather), tightened by input splitting. Range syntax `f32[...] in [lo, hi]` is parsed and checked in elaborate.py, and the range facts also discharge bounds checks currently deferred to run time. `verify forall x in Box(...): V(step(x, pi(x))) < V(x) - eps` runs at compile time with branch-and-bound and prints either a certificate or a concrete counterexample. Prelude: `shield(policy, fallback, region)`. A `--fixed q15` lowering in the C backend derives its scaling from the proven ranges.
- **Demo:** An inverted pendulum's state space is drawn as a heatmap in the browser. Train a policy and a neural Lyapunov function together, press verify, and the certified region of attraction grows tile by tile as a green area while branch-and-bound runs. Kick the pendulum outside that region and the shield visibly hands control to LQR. A counterexample state found by the verifier is replayed in the sim, and the pendulum really does fall.
- **Builds on:** interp.py (the IR already has a non-backend interpreter), ir.py's small expression language, simplify.py, `assert`, and LQR from the control.anvil idea as the verified fallback
- **Risk:** Verification scales badly with network size and state dimension (branch-and-bound is exponential), so demos must stay small: 2 to 6D state and small networks. Loose bounds can make the certified region disappointingly small.

### A tiny vision-language-action model with a compile-time-proven control rate

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

A 10 to 30M-parameter VLA: a small ViT encoder, a few instruction tokens, and an action-chunking transformer head as in ACT (Zhao et al. 2023), or a few-step diffusion policy. Token counts are static, so the forward pass costs a fixed number of FLOPs and Anvil can promise "a 16-step action chunk every 40 ms" while a 500 Hz loop interpolates the chunk. PyTorch-based stacks such as LeRobot, or OpenVLA at around 6 Hz on a 4090, cannot promise a rate at all.

- **Build:** Flash-style fused attention in kernel.py (NEON) and metal.py, which fixes a listed weakness, plus f16/bf16 dtypes and faster GPU convolutions for the patch embedding (the MPS gap). New examples/vla/: a fixed instruction vocabulary and tokenizer, and an ACT head with temporal ensembling, trained on Metal on LeRobot SO-100 datasets exported to npy. Deployment uses the multi-rate idea (`at 25 Hz: chunk = vla(cam, text)`, `at 500 Hz: u = interpolate(chunk, t)`) with wcet.py bounds on the 25 Hz task. A serial driver in the runtime talks to the Feetech servos.
- **Demo:** Type "put the red block in the cup" and a $200 SO-100 arm does it. A live timeline shows every 25 Hz inference finishing inside its slot (bars of remaining slack) and the 500 Hz servo loop never late. A toggle runs the same model through PyTorch MPS: the timeline shows missed slots and the arm's motion turns jerky.
- **Builds on:** examples/transformer.anvil (attention in index notation), the Metal backend, the multi-rate scheduler idea, the real-time runtime idea
- **Risk:** Even a tiny VLA that generalizes needs substantial data and training compute. Fused attention, fast GPU convolutions and half precision are all missing today, and all three are on the critical path.

### Anvil to FPGA: sub-microsecond policies with exact cycle-count latency

*wow 9/10 · moonshot*

Static shapes and fixed trip counts are exactly what high-level synthesis wants. The kernel IR can be lowered to a pipelined datapath whose latency is not merely bounded but known exactly: "this policy: 187 cycles at 200 MHz = 935 ns, initiation interval 1". That is the hls4ml niche (Duarte et al. 2018; LHC trigger systems that answer in under 1 us) and the regime of motor and power-electronics current loops at 100 kHz to 1 MHz, generated from the same source that trained the network.

- **Build:** New anvil/backend/hls.py lowers kernel IR to Vitis or Bambu HLS C++, with PIPELINE/UNROLL/ARRAY_PARTITION pragmas derived from the static schedule. Next, a direct RTL emitter (Amaranth or Verilog) for fully unrolled small MLPs in fixed point, with bit widths taken from the certified-controllers range analysis. `anvil cost --target ice40|ecp5|artix7` estimates DSP slices, LUTs and BRAM. An open-source flow (yosys + nextpnr) targets cheap boards (iCEBreaker, Tang Nano 20K, ULX3S). A cocotb testbench checks the hardware bit-exactly against a fixed-point mode of interp.py.
- **Demo:** A $30 FPGA board closes a learned current loop on a small BLDC motor at 1 MHz (or balances a ball on a resistive touch plate). The scope shows input-to-output latency as a flat 935 ns line with zero jitter, matching `anvil cost`'s prediction to the cycle.
- **Builds on:** the kernel IR and its static schedules, cost.py (extended to hardware resources), the certified-controllers range analysis for fixed-point widths, the C++ generation in cuda.py
- **Risk:** Fixed-point quantization of a trained policy and timing closure on cheap FPGAs are each a project of their own. HLS tools are either proprietary or immature, and floating point barely fits on small FPGAs.

### Black-box flight recorder: bit-exact replay and gradient forensics for controllers

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

An Anvil program's whole state is one static arena at fixed offsets, and its random numbers are counter-based. So the complete state of a controller at each tick is a known number of bytes that can be ring-buffered almost for free. When the robot does something stupid, `anvil replay crash.bin --tick 48213` reproduces that tick bit-for-bit on the laptop and lets you step through it line by line. It then runs `grad(action, observation)` at the failing tick to show which sensor drove the decision.

- **Build:** arena.py: split buffers into state (params, loop-carried values, optimizer state, RNG counter) and scratch, and emit a manifest of name, offset, shape and source line. runtime.s: `_anvil_rt_record` copies the state region into a ring buffer. Its size is static, so `anvil cost` reports recorder bandwidth, for example "1.9 KB/tick = 1.9 MB/s at 1 kHz". Recording is triggered on assert failure, on NaN (reusing the `--check` scanner) or on a signal. New anvil/replay.py loads a snapshot into interp.py or a recompiled binary, verifies the outputs are bit-identical, and opens the REPL at that tick. `anvil replay --why` builds an input-gradient program over the recorded inputs, reusing the machinery of the dream example.
- **Demo:** A browser-simulated self-balancing robot running a learned policy gets kicked and falls over. Click replay: the 200 ticks before the fall re-execute with a matching hash on every tick, and a saliency chart shows the policy reacting to a saturated gyro channel. Patch the policy, rerun it on the same recorded tape, and the robot recovers.
- **Builds on:** arena.py's static layout, the counter-based RNG in backend/kernel.py, `--check` NaN tracing, interp.py, examples/dream.anvil's input gradients
- **Risk:** Bit-exact replay holds only for the same target and code. Replaying an MCU log on the M3 needs an identical float operation order (no FMA-contraction differences) across backends, or replay becomes approximate.

### Lie-group types: SO(3), SE(3) and quaternions with on-manifold gradients

*wow 8/10 · months*

Rotations and poses become types instead of 3x3 arrays: `R: SO3`, `T: SE3`, `exp`/`log`, composition `T1 ∘ T2`, and action on points `T ⋅ p`. The type checker rejects `R1 + R2` ("rotations do not add; compose them"), and the gradient with respect to a rotation is a 3-vector in the tangent space. `minimize` picks the retraction `R <- R ∘ exp(-lr·g)` from the parameter's type, so rotations never drift off the manifold. LieTorch, jaxlie, Sophus and manif offer this as libraries; here the compiler enforces it.

- **Build:** ir.py/values.py: a group-type layer over f32 storage (quaternion or 3x3 for SO3, [R|t] for SE3), checked in elaborate.py like shapes are today. A `group` declaration form in the prelude defines compose, inverse, exp, log, adjoint and act in Anvil, so users can add SE2, Sim3 or SO(n). autodiff.py expresses cotangents of group-valued inputs through left and right Jacobians. Optimizer blocks get a `retract` hook dispatched on the parameter's type. Library code: an error-state Kalman filter (ESKF) for IMU orientation and preintegration on top of control.anvil. Stretch: an event-camera front end that processes fixed batches of 1,024 events, so the static cost gives the maximum event rate the pipeline sustains.
- **Demo:** Each student opens a web page on their phone, which streams gyro and accelerometer data (DeviceMotion) to the teacher's laptop. An Anvil ESKF on SO3 estimates every phone's orientation at 1 kHz and renders 30 tumbling 3D phones. A toggle swaps in a naive 3x3-matrix filter: its phones visibly shear and grow as det(R) drifts. A CPU-budget bar shows the fixed per-update cost times 30 phones.
- **Builds on:** shape types and unification in elaborate.py, optimizer blocks in prelude.anvil, `grad`, and the control.anvil filters idea
- **Risk:** This is the first real type beyond shapes and f32/i32, and it touches broadcasting, indexing, Python interop and the export ABI. Keeping index notation usable on group values without breaking the abstraction is a real design problem.

### `scan` with checkpointed adjoints: differentiate 10,000-step rollouts within a fixed memory budget

*wow 8/10 · months*

A functional `scan(step, x0, inputs)`, like JAX's lax.scan, has a static trip count but compiles to a single loop instead of a `static for` unroll. Its gradient uses Griewank's binomial checkpointing (revolve) with a tape whose size is computed at compile time and placed in the arena: `anvil cost` reports "tape 1.2 MB, recompute 1.8x forward", and `scan(..., memory=2MB)` picks the trade-off. This closes the biggest language gap (no autodiff through loops that run at run time) without giving up the no-allocation guarantee. It also unlocks system identification: fitting a physics model plus a neural residual to real sensor logs (universal differential equations, Rackauckas et al. 2020).

- **Build:** parser/elaborate.py: `scan` as a builtin that takes a function argument. This is a first, limited form of first-class functions: functions passed to builtins and elaborated once per call site. ir.py: a `Scan` statement (a body block plus carried buffers). autodiff.py: a reverse pass for Scan that emits a backward loop with checkpoint slots and recompute segments, with the revolve schedule computed at compile time in Python. arena.py sizes the tape from that schedule, optimize.py treats Scan bodies like loop bodies, and cost.py reports tape memory and recompute FLOPs. Prelude: `odeint(f, x0, ts, method="rk4")` built on scan.
- **Demo:** Record 20 s of encoder data from a DC-motor pendulum, or a phone swinging on a string with its IMU. `minimize` fits mass, friction and a 2x32 residual MLP through all 20,000 steps in seconds with a 512 KB tape (storing everything would take 160 MB). An LQR designed on the fitted model then balances the real pendulum. Compile time is about 0.3 s, against minutes for the unrolled version.
- **Builds on:** ir.For/While, autodiff.py's ad_region, arena.py, cost.py, and `static for` (which scan replaces for long horizons)
- **Risk:** Scan bodies containing `minimize`, random draws or data-dependent control flow complicate the reverse pass and keeping the RNG stream aligned. The first version should allow only pure kernels in the body.

### Frame-budget character and vehicle controllers: learned motion matching and physics characters as a C++ engine plugin

*wow 8/10 · months*

Games impose a hard budget of 16.6 ms per frame for everything, and learned character controllers run for every character on every frame. Anvil can compile Learned Motion Matching (Holden et al. 2020, Ubisoft La Forge: three small MLPs that replace a motion database of gigabytes) and DeepMimic/AMP-style physics policies (Peng et al. 2018/2021) into a GDExtension or Unreal plugin whose per-character cost is known at compile time. The engine can then say "400 characters fit in 2 ms on 4 cores" before you ship.

- **Build:** anvil/export_cpp.py targets: `--godot` (a GDExtension C++ class bound to `_physics_process`, with a batched API so all characters run as one batched kernel) and `--unreal` (an AnimGraph node or a Chaos physics callback). A `budget 2ms per 400` annotation is checked with wcet.py. New examples/motion_matching.anvil trains the decompressor, stepper and projector networks on the open LaFAN1 dataset. A physics-character trainer runs on the Featherstone dynamics idea, and a vehicle variant learns a drift or racing-line controller for arcade cars.
- **Demo:** A Godot scene with 500 motion-matched characters walking under joystick control and an on-screen meter showing the Anvil controllers' proven and measured share of the frame. Keep spawning characters and the meter predicts the overrun before it happens. Personal tie-in: AI golf-cart opponents for the Bogey Run cart game that learned to take corners in the cart-feel lab's physics.
- **Builds on:** export.py with baked weights, batched and threaded kernels, the Featherstone dynamics idea, and the WCET and real-time ideas
- **Risk:** Most of the work is the content pipeline (retargeting LaFAN1, animation blending, engine integration), not compiler work. Players judge learned motion quality harshly.

### Pendulum Arena: a class tournament refereed by the compiler

*wow 8/10 · weeks*

Every student submits `controller.anvil` (PID, LQR, MPC or a learned policy) for a cart-pole, a reaction-wheel pendulum and a drone hover task. The server compiles each one and rejects any that miss `deadline 1ms` or exceed a 16 KB RAM budget, using the compiler's own error message. It runs every submission against the same counter-based-RNG disturbance sequences (bit-identical, so the comparison is fair) and posts a leaderboard with 3D replays. The final round flashes the top three onto a physical pendulum in front of the class.

- **Build:** New apps/arena/: a small Python server in the bin/draw style that compiles submissions with anvil.function and gates them with anvil cost (later wcet.py). Scenario files are written in Anvil (hand-written cart-pole and pendulum equations at first, Featherstone dynamics later), with disturbance schedules seeded per round. A three.js viewer reads recorded trajectories in the flight-recorder format. Homework grader for "prove your controller meets 1 kHz": the submission must compile under `deadline 1ms on cortex-m4@120MHz` and stabilize from 20 seeded initial states. A starter kit ships PID, LQR (from control.anvil) and a policy-gradient trainer.
- **Demo:** The leaderboard on the projector, with LQR, learned and MPC columns and each row's proven cycle bound and RAM. One student's oversized network is rejected live ("line 9: 1,384 us > 1,000 us"). The finals run on a roughly $150 reaction-wheel pendulum kit, where the scoreboard's predictions of who survives the kick test are checked against reality.
- **Builds on:** bin/draw's browser-plus-server pattern, anvil.function, anvil cost, counter-based RNG determinism, `--set`
- **Risk:** Until the WCET idea lands, the deadline check is only a FLOP-based estimate. The physical round needs an MCU flash path and hardware that behaves like the sim, so the sim-to-real gap can make the finals noisy.

### Real-time mode: a `realtime` effect, a jitter-free runtime, and a latency-tail shootout

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

An effect system checks that a `realtime fn` never allocates, prints, loads files, sleeps or makes a syscall. `anvil build --realtime` then produces a binary that pre-faults and mlocks its arena, pins its threads to P-cores with Mach's THREAD_TIME_CONSTRAINT_POLICY (the class Core Audio threads use; SCHED_FIFO + mlockall + isolcpus on Linux), sets flush-to-zero so denormals cannot cause spikes, and keeps workers spinning in `wfe` without backoff. Then publish a full latency distribution: 100 million batch-1 policy ticks under background load, p50 through max, Anvil against PyTorch eager, TorchScript, ONNX Runtime, ExecuTorch and TFLite.

- **Build:** elaborate.py: each function gets an effect set computed from the RTCalls it emits (load_idx/load_csv/save/print/input/sleep are io, clock is time). A `realtime` modifier on FnDecl rejects io effects with a diagnostic at the offending call site. Gather bounds the compiler cannot prove become explicit clamps or compile errors, so the tick has no fault path. runtime.s: `_anvil_rt_realtime_init` (mlock the arena and touch every page, thread_policy_set, set FPCR.FZ) and a spin-only worker mode. blas.py: realtime functions use the in-house NEON GEMM schedules from kernel.py instead of Accelerate, whose internal threading and allocation are unbounded. export.py `--realtime` emits `<name>_init()` and `<name>_tick()`. New benchmarks/latency/: an HDR-histogram harness with stress-ng or a parallel LLVM build as interference, and dtrace/perf syscall and page-fault counters.
- **Demo:** A live dashboard with two log-scale latency histograms side by side, updating while a large compile runs in the background. Anvil's maximum stays within about 2x its ~3 us median. PyTorch's p99.99 jumps into milliseconds from GC and allocator stalls. Counters under the Anvil histogram read "syscalls in tick: 0, page faults: 0".
- **Builds on:** runtime.s thread pool (already wfe/sev based), arena.py, export.py, the 5.7 us batch-1 call measured in benchmarks/vs_pytorch/REPORT.md
- **Risk:** macOS is not an RTOS: interrupts and the kernel can still preempt the thread. The honest claim is "no self-inflicted jitter" plus measured tails. Hard real-time guarantees are reserved for MCU and RTOS targets (Zephyr, PREEMPT_RT).

### control.anvil: Kalman, EKF, UKF, LQR and fixed-size linear algebra written in Anvil

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

The estimation and control canon becomes textbook-readable Anvil that compiles to branch-free, allocation-free code with exact FLOP counts. A UKF's sigma points are one index-notation line, an EKF's Jacobian is `jacobian(f, x)`, and LQR is a Riccati iteration. Fixed-size Cholesky, LU and triangular solves unroll into straight-line code (an n=12 Cholesky is about 650 FLOPs and no branches), the way Eigen's fixed-size matrices work in C++, and that is ideal input for a WCET analyzer.

- **Build:** builtins.py: `cholesky`, `solve`, `tri_solve` and `inv` for static n up to about 32, lowered to unrolled kernels (blocked kernels for larger n), with reverse-mode rules in autodiff.py (Murray 2016 for Cholesky; the adjoint of a solve is another solve). `jacobian(f, x)` uses a new forward-mode pass (the `jvp` item in IDEAS.md section 5), or n reverse passes for small n. Add `atan2`, `asin` and `acos` to UNARY_MATH and backend/vecmath.py. New anvil/control.anvil: `model Kalman(A, B, H, Q, R)` with predict/update (Joseph-form covariance), `ekf_update`, `ukf_update` (Merwe scaled sigma points), a square-root variant, `dlqr`/`dare` (Riccati with a fixed iteration count, or the doubling algorithm), and `pid` with anti-windup. `anvil cost` reports FLOPs per filter step.
- **Demo:** A browser page tracks a ball thrown across the webcam (or a noisy simulated radar track) with KF, EKF and UKF side by side. Each draws its covariance ellipse, and a sidebar shows each filter's roughly 12 lines of source with its proven cost, for example "UKF step: 4,212 FLOPs, 0 branches, 3.1 KB arena". Students drag the Q and R sliders and watch the ellipses change.
- **Builds on:** prelude.anvil (a standard library written in Anvil), index notation, `grad`, `static for`, `anvil export`
- **Risk:** With f32 only, Riccati iterations and covariance updates can lose positive-definiteness. Square-root forms help, but some users will want an f64 dtype that no backend supports yet.

### `anvil export --ros2 / --ros2-control / --px4`: generated C++ robot-stack nodes whose update provably never allocates

*wow 7/10 · weeks*

A ros2_control controller's `update()` must never allocate or block, and C++ robotics teams enforce that by code review and hope. Anvil can generate a controller plugin where it holds by construction, with the proven cycle cost printed in the plugin metadata. One command turns an Anvil function into a ROS 2 component node, a ros2_control controller plugin, or a PX4 uORB work-queue module, with message bindings derived from the function's typed signature.

- **Build:** New anvil/export_cpp.py next to export.py, with C++17 templates for: an rclcpp component (realtime_tools::RealtimePublisher, pre-allocated messages); a ros2_control ControllerInterface/ChainableControllerInterface plugin (on_configure, on_activate, update), whose state and command interfaces map from named, typed arguments such as `fn update(q: joint_position[7], dq: joint_velocity[7]) -> effort[7]`; and a PX4 module (ModuleBase + ScheduledWorkItem, uORB subscriptions). It also generates package.xml, CMakeLists.txt and pluginlib XML. Message mappings are written as annotations in Anvil (`from "sensor_msgs/JointState"`). The code builds with the realtime flags from the real-time-mode idea, and a generated `colcon test` uses osrf_testing_tools_cpp memory tools to assert that update() never calls malloc.
- **Demo:** A UR5 or Franka in Gazebo runs an Anvil learned impedance controller at 1 kHz next to the stock C++ PID controller. ros2_control's timing statistics and the malloc test both show zero allocations in update. The same Anvil source is then exported as a PX4 module and flies a SITL quadrotor.
- **Builds on:** export.py (a C library with weights baked in), the realtime effect and runtime idea, wcet.py bounds for the metadata, and the portable C output of cuda.py's emulation path as the bridge to Linux targets
- **Risk:** ROS 2 and PX4 build systems, message generation and version churn (Humble, Jazzy, PX4 releases) are an ongoing maintenance burden. Everything here also needs Linux backends (x86-64 or AArch64) that Anvil does not have yet.
